## VIII A Climax / 八 高潮
===Original===

Mr. Earlforward woke up after what seemed to him a very long sleep, feeling appreciably better. He had less pain; at moments he had no pain. And his mind, he thought, was surprisingly clear and vigorous. He had ideas on all sorts of things. Most invalids got their perspective awry﻿—he knew that﻿—but his own perspective had remained absolutely true. Rising out of bed for a moment he found that he could stand without difficulty, which was yet another proof of his theory that people ate a vast deal too much. The doctor had been utterly wrong about him. The doctor had made a mystery about ordinary chronic indigestion. The present attack was passing, as the sufferer had always been convinced it would. A nice old mess of a complication they would have made of it at the hospital! Or more probably he would have been bundled out of the place with contumely as a malingering fraud! He straightened the bed a little, and then, slipping back into it with a certain eagerness, he began to concert plans, to reorganize and resume his existence.
The day was darkening. Four o’clock, perhaps. Elsie? Where was that girl? She ought to be coming. Had she got a bit above herself? Thought she was the boss of the whole place, no doubt, and could do as she chose! An excellent creature, trustworthy, devoted.﻿ ﻿… And yet﻿—in some things they were all alike. Give them an inch and they’d take an ell. He must be after her. Now what was it he had noticed, or thought he had noticed, when he was last awake? Oh, yes! That was it. His keys. He had missed them from the top of the chest of drawers. He peered in the gloom. They were there right enough. Perhaps hidden before by something else. The room had been tidied, dusted, while he slept. He didn’t quite care for that, but he supposed it couldn’t be helped. Anyhow, it showed that she was not being utterly idle. Of course the girl was not going to bed properly, but she had ample opportunity to sleep. With the shop closed she had practically nothing to do.﻿ ﻿…
“Fibroid growth.” Fibroid﻿—like fibre, of course. He scarcely understood how a growth could be like fibre; but it was a name, a definition, and therefore reassuring. Much better than “cancerous,” at the worst! An entirely different thing from cancer! But he was dreadfully concerned, frightened, for Violet. If she died﻿—not that it was conceivable﻿—but *if* she died, what a blank! Sickening!

===Chinese===

厄尔福沃德先生睡了一场——在他感觉里——极长的觉，醒来时明显好了些。疼痛轻了；有几阵完全不疼。而且他自觉脑子出奇地清明、有劲。各种事情他都有见解。大多数病人会把远近比例看歪——这他懂——可他自己的比例始终分毫不差。他下床站了片刻，发觉站立毫不费劲，这又给他那条理论添了一个证据：人吃得太撑太撑了。医生对他完全是误判。医生把普普通通的慢性消化不良弄成了一桩玄案。眼下这场发作正在退去，正如患者一向笃信的那样。若进了医院，他们准把这病搅成一团漂亮的老乱麻！再不然，多半是把他当装病的骗子，骂骂则则地轰出门去！他把床铺理了理，随后带着几分急切重新钻回被窝，开始筹划算计，把自己的日子重新组织起来、继续下去。
天色在暗下去。四点钟了吧，也许。埃尔西呢？那丫头哪儿去了？她该来了吧。是不是翘了尾巴？八成自以为是这宅子里的总管，想怎么着就怎么着了！人是个极好的人，靠得住，尽心尽力。……可话又说回来——有些地方，她们全是一路货。得寸进尺，蹪鼻子上脸。他得盯紧她。上回醒来时他注意到、或自以为注意到什么来着？哦，对！是那么回事。他的钥匙。他发现钥匙不在五斗柜顶上了。他在昏暗里眎眼张望。钥匙好端端在那儿呢。兴许先前叫别的东西挡住了。他睡着这阵子，屋子被打扫过、掉过尘了。这他不大乐意，可转念想，也无可奈何。至少这证明她没全然闲着。当然，这丫头没好好上床睡觉，可她有的是空子补觉。店铺关着，她实在无事可干。……
「纤维性增生。」纤维——自然像纤维了。增生怎么个像纤维法，他不大明白；可这是个名目，一个定义，因此叫人宽心。比起「癌性的」——往最坏里说——好太多了！同癌是全然不同的两回事！可他为薇奥莱特担着老大的心，怕得慌。她若是死了——这不可想象——可她*万一*死了，那将是怎样一片空白！叫人呕心！

===Original===

No! He could not contemplate it. Yet simultaneously in his mind was a little elusive thought: as a widower, freed from the necessity of adapting himself to another, and of revealing to another to some extent his ideas, intentions, schemes﻿—what freedom! The old freedom! And he would plunge into it as into an exquisite, warm bath, voluptuously. He would be more secretive, more self-centred, more prudent, more fixed in habit than ever! A great practical philosopher, yes! In no matter what event he would discover compensations. And there were still deeper depths in the fathomless pit of his busy mind, depths into which he himself would do no more than glance﻿—rather scared.
Elsie came in and saw a sinister sick man, pale as the dying, shrunk by starvation, with glittering, suspicious little eyes.
“Oh! So you’ve come, miss!” He wished that he had not said “miss.” It was a tiny pleasantry of reproof, but too familiar. Another inch, another ell!
“Why! You’ve been making your bed again!” she exclaimed.
But she exclaimed so nicely, so benevolently, that he could not take offence. And yet﻿—might she not be condescending to him? Withal, he enjoyed her presence in the bedroom. Her youth, her reliability, her prettiness (he thought she was growing prettier and prettier every day﻿—such dark eyes, such dark hair, such a curve of the lips), and her physical power and health! Her mere health seemed miraculous to him. Oh! She was a godsend.﻿ ﻿… She had said nothing about Violet. Well, if she had had news she would have told him. He hesitated to mention Violet. He could wait till she began.
“I’ll run and make you some food,” she said.
“Here! Not so fast! Not so fast!” he stopped her.
He was about to give an order when, for the second time, he noticed that her apron was wet in several places.
“Why is your apron all wet?” he demanded sharply.
“Is it?” she faltered, looking down at it. “So it is! I’ve been doing things.” (She appeared to have dropped the “sir” completely.)
The fact was that she had been sponging Joe.
Mr. Earlforward became suspicious. He suspected that she was wasting warm water.
“Why are you always running upstairs?” he asked in a curious tone.
“Running upstairs, sir?”
(Ha! “Sir.” He was recovering his grip on her.)
She blushed red. She had something to hide. Hordes of suspicions thronged through his mind.
“Well, sir, I have to go to the kitchen.”

===Chinese===

不！这事他想都不敢想。可与此同时，他心里却溜过一丝捉不住的念头：做回鳏夫，从此不必迁就另一个人，不必把自家的主意、盘算、计划向人透出一星半点——何等的自由！旧日的那种自由！他要纵身跳进这自由里，像跳进一池精美温热的浴，恣意享用。他会比从前更守密、更自我、更精明、更一丝不苟地固守成习！一位了不起的实践哲学家，没错！无论落到什么境地，他都能找出补偿来。而在他那颗忙碌心智的无底深坑里，还有更深的深处——那些深处，他自家也只肯瞟上一眼——心里发毛。
埃尔西走进来，看见一个面目不善的病人，苍白得像垂死的人，饿得脱了形，一双发亮的小眼睛满是无端的疑心。
「哦！您可算来了，小姐！」说完他又后悔不该说「小姐」。这本是句小小的责备玩笑，却失之亲昵。得了一寸，又要一尺！
「呀！您又自己铺床了！」她叫道。
可她叫得那样和善、那样好脾气，他恼不起来。然而——她这份和气，会不会是屈尊俯就？话虽如此，他仍喜欢她在卧室里待着。她的年轻，她的可靠，她的好看（他觉得她一天比一天好看——这样的黑眼睛，这样的黑头发，这样的唇线），还有她的体力和康健！单是她那份健康，在他眼里就近乎神迹。哦！她真是天赐。……她只字没提薇奥莱特。也罢，若有消息她早说了。他自己不便先提薇奥莱特。他可以等她起头。
「我这就跑去给您弄点吃的。」她说。
「站住！别急！别急！」他拦住她。
他正要吩咐，忽然第二次注意到，她的围裙好几处是湿的。
「你围裙怎么全是湿的？」他厉声问。
「是吗？」她结巴着低头看。「还真是！我方才在干活儿。」（那个「先生」，她像是彻底戒掉了。）
实情是，她方才在给乔（Joe）擦身。
厄尔福沃德先生起了疑心。他疑心她精蹋热水。
「你怎么老是往楼上跑？」他用一种古怪的山气道。
「往楼上跑，先生？」
（哈！「先生」。他对她的钳制又回来了。）
她涨红了脸。她有事瞒着。成群结队的疑心涌过他的脑海。
「是这样，先生，我总得上厨房去呀。」

===Original===

“I don’t hear you so often in the *kitchen*,” said he drily.
It was true. And all footsteps in the kitchen could be heard overhead in the bedroom. He suspected that she was carrying on conversations from her own bedroom window with new-made friends in the yard of the next house or the next house but one, and giving away the secrets of the house. But he did not utter the suspicion; he kept it to himself for the present. Yes, they were all alike.
“You haven’t inquired, Elsie, but I’m much better,” he said.
“Oh! I can *see* you are, sir!” she responded brightly.
But whether she really thought so, or whether she was just humouring him, he could not tell.
“Yes. And I’m going to get up.”
“Not today you aren’t, sir,” she burst out.
He said placidly:
“No. Tomorrow morning. And I think I shall put on one of my new suits and a new shirt. I think it’s about time. I don’t want to get shabby. Just show them to me.”
Elsie was evidently amazed at the suggestion. And he himself did not know why he had made it. But, at any rate, it was not a bad idea. He fancied that he might feel better in a brand new suit. He indicated the right drawers to her, and one by one she had to display on the bed the carefully preserved garments which he had bought for a song years ago and never persuaded himself into the extravagance of wearing. The bed was covered with new merchandise. He thought that he would have to wear the clothes some time, and might as well begin at once. It would be uneconomic to waste them, and worn or unworn they would go for far less than a song after his death. He must be sensible; he must keep his perspective in order. He regarded this decision to have out a new suit as a truly great feat of considered sagacity on the part of a sick man.
Elsie with extreme care restored all the virgin clothes to their drawers except one suit and one shirt, which for convenience she put separately into Mrs. Earlforward’s wardrobe. As all the suits were the same and all the shirts were the same, it did not matter which suit and which shirt were selected. But this did not prevent him from choosing, and hesitating in his choice.
Elsie seemed to be alarmed by the scene﻿—he could not understand why.
“Of course,” he said, “being new they’ll hang a bit looser on me than my old suit; that’s all wrinkled up. I’m not quite so stout as I was, am I?”

===Chinese===

「可我怎么不常听见你在*厨房*里。」他干巴巴地说。
这是实情。厨房里的一切脚步声，楼上的卧室里都听得见。他疑心她是从自己卧室的窗口，同隔壁或隔一家的院子里的新朋友搭话，把宅子里的机密漏出去。但这层疑心他没说出口；暂时只搁在自己肚里。是啊，全是一路货。
「你还没问呢，埃尔西——不过我好多了。」他说。
「哦！我*瞧*得出来，先生！」她脆生生地应道。
可她是真这么想，还是只顺着哄他，他捉摸不透。
「是的。我要起床了。」
「今天可不行，先生。」她冲口而出。
他平平静静地说：
「不。明儿早上。我想我要穿一身新衣裳、一件新衬衫了。我看是时候了。我不想穿得寒酸。拿来给我瞧瞧。」
埃尔西对这提议显然大为惊讶。他自己也不明白为什么提出它来。不过无论如何，主意不坏。他想象自己穿上簇新一身或许会感觉好些。他给她指了对的抽屉，她只好把那些保存得仔仔细细的衣裳一件件摊到床上——那是他多年前贱价买来、却始终没说服自己奢侈到上身的东西。床上铺满了新货。他想这些衣裳总有一天要穿，不如即刻开始。精蹋了不经济；而且穿过没穿过，等他一死，卖出去的价钱连贱价都远不如。他得讲理性；得把远近比例摆正。于是他把「取出一身新衣来穿」这个决定，看作一个病人深思熟虑的睿智、一桩真正了不起的功业。
埃尔西小心翼翼地把那些未沾身的新衣悉数放回抽屉，只留一身衣裳一件衬衫，图方便，单独放进了厄尔福沃德太太的衣柜。所有衣裳全是一个样式，所有衬衫全是一个样式，挑哪一身哪一件本无区别。可这挡不住他挑三拣四、举棋不定。
埃尔西似乎被这一幕骇着了——他不懂为什么。
「当然喽，」他说，「衣裳是新的，上身会比我那身旧的松些；旧的那身全皱巴巴了。我不如从前那么富态了，是吧？」

===Original===

Elsie turned round to him from the wardrobe with a nervous movement, and then quickly back again. The fading light glinted for a second on a teardrop that ran down her cheek. This teardrop annoyed Mr. Earlforward; he resented it, and was not in the least touched by it. He had not perceived the extraordinary pathos in the phrase “not quite so stout,” coming from a man who had never been stout (or slim either), and who was now a stick, a skeleton; he thought she was merely crying because he had lost flesh. As if people weren’t always either putting on flesh or losing it! As a fact, Elsie had not felt the pathos of the phrase either, and her tears had no connection whatever with Mr. Earlforward’s wasting away. Nor had they sprung from the still more tragic pathos of his caprice about a new suit. In depositing the chosen suit in Mrs. Earlforward’s wardrobe Elsie had caught sight of the satin shoe which on the bridal night she had tied to the very bedstead whereon the husband was now lying alone. She thought of the husband lying alone and desperately ill and desperately determined not to be ill, and the wife far off in the hospital, and of her own helplessness, and she simply could not bear to look at the shabby old shoe﻿—which some unknown girl had once worn in flashing pride. All the enigma of the universe was in that shoe, with its curved high heel perched lifeless on a mahogany tray of the everlasting wardrobe. Elsie had never heard of the enigma of the universe, but it was present with her in many hours of her existence.
Mr. Earlforward said suddenly:
“Was the operation going to be done this morning or this afternoon?” He knew that the operation had been fixed for the morning, but he had to account to Elsie for his apparent lack of curiosity.
“This morning, sir.”
“We ought to be getting some news soon, then.”
“Well, sir. That’s just what I was wondering. I don’t hardly think as they’ll send up﻿—not unless it was urgent. So I suppose it’s gone off all right.” A pause. “But we ought to know for certain, sir. I was thinking I could run out and get someone to go down and find out﻿—I mean someone who *would* find out and tell us all about it﻿—not a child. I dare say a shilling or two﻿—”
With her experience Elsie ought not to have mentioned money, but she was rather distraught. The patient reacted instantly.

===Chinese===

埃尔西正对着衣柜，闻声神经质地朝他转过身，又飞快转了回去。渐暗的天光在她颊上一颗泪珠里闪了一秒。这颗泪珠惹恼了厄尔福沃德先生；他嫌恶它，丝毫没被它打动。他没有品出「不如从前富态」这句话里那非同寻常的哀感——这话出自一个从来不曾富态（也不曾清瘦）的男人之口，而此人如今已是根柴棍、一副骨架；他只当她是因为他掉了肉才哭。好像人不总是在长肉掉肉似的！而实情是，埃尔西也未曾品出这句话的哀感，她的眼泪同厄尔福沃德先生的消瘦毫无干系。也并非源于他要穿新衣那份任性里更悲惨的哀感。把挑中的衣裳放进厄尔福沃德太太衣柜时，埃尔西一眼瞥见了那只缎面鞋——新婚之夜，她亲手把它系上的，正是眼下丈夫孤身躺着的这张床架。她想着孤零零躺着、病得凶险又拼死不肯认病的丈夫，想着远在医院里的太太，想着自己的无能为力，她实在不忍再看那只寒酸的旧鞋——某个不知名的姑娘，曾穿着它出尽风头。宇宙全部的谜，都在那只鞋里，弯弯的高跟了无生气地架在衣柜永恒的红木托板上。埃尔西从没听说过「宇宙之谜」，可在她一生的许多时辰里，它都陪着她。
厄尔福沃德先生突然说：
「手术是定在今儿上午还是下午？」他明知手术定在上午，可总得给埃尔西一个交代，免得显得自己漠不关心。
「今儿上午，先生。」
「那咱们该快有消息了。」
「是呢，先生。我也正犯嘀咕呢。我想他们不大会上门送信——不到火烧眉毛不会来。所以我琢磨着，手术准是顺顺当当的。」顿了顿。「可咱们总该问个准信儿，先生。我想，我可以跑出去找个合适的人去一趟医院打听——我是说，找个*真*能打听明白、回来一五一十告诉咱们的——可不能找个孩子。我估摸着，一两个先令也就——」
以埃尔西的阅历，本不该提钱字；可她实在心乱。病人立刻就有了反应。

===Original===

It was evident to him that Elsie had old friends in the Square, or near by, upon whom she wanted to confer benefits through the medium of her employer’s misfortunes. They were always bent on lining their pockets, those people were. He was not going to let them pick up shillings and florins as easily as all that. His shop was perforce closed; his business was decaying; his customers would transfer their custom to other shops; not a penny was coming in; communism was rife; the political and trade outlook was menacing in the extreme; there was no clear hope anywhere; he saw himself as an old man begging his bread. And the girl proposed gaily to scatter shillings over Riceyman Square for a perfectly unnecessary object! She had not reflected at all. They never did. They were always eager to spend other people’s money. Not their own! Oh, no! He alone had kept a true perspective, and he would act according to his true perspective. He was as anxious as anybody for news of the result of the operation and Violet’s condition; but he did not see the need to engage an army of special messengers for the collecting of news. An hour sooner or an hour later﻿—what difference could it make? He would know soon enough, too soon if it was to be bad news; and if it was to be good news a little delay would only increase joy.﻿ ﻿… And, moreover, you would have thought that even the poorest and most rapacious persons would not expect money for services rendered in a great crisis to the sick and the bedridden.
“I see no reason for doing that,” he said placidly and firmly. “Let me think now﻿—”
“Shall I run down there myself? It won’t take me long.”
She was ready in the emergency, and in deference to his astounding whims, to take the fearful risks of leaving the two men alone together in the house. Suppose Joe should rise up violent? Suppose Mr. Earlforward should begin in his weakness to explore the house? He was already suspecting something; and she knew him for the most inquisitive being ever born. She trembled. Still, she was ready to go, and to run all the way there and all the way back.
“Oh, no!” he forbade positively. “That won’t do at all.” He was afraid to lose her. He, so seriously ill (he was now seriously ill again!), to be left by himself in the house! It was unthinkable. “Look here.

===Chinese===

在他看来，事情再明白不过：埃尔西在广场一带或左近有旧相识，想借着东家遭难这个由头给他们送好处。那种人，整天就惦记着往自己腰包里接钱。他可不能让他们这么轻巧地把先令、两先令银币捡了去。他的铺子被迫关着门；生意正在败落；主顾们正把生意转到别家铺子；进项一个便士也没有；共产主义甚嚣尘上；政局和行市的远景凶险已极；放眼望去没有一线明确的指望；他看见自己成了个讨饭的老头。而这丫头倒好，提议欢欢喜喜地往莱斯门广场（Riceyman Square）上撒先令，为一件全无必要的事！她根本没过脑子。她们从来不过脑子。她们总是热心花别人的钱。自己的？那可不！唯独他一个人把真实的比例守住了，他也要照这真实的比例行事。手术结果如何、薇奥莱特情形如何，他同谁一样急着知道；可他看不出有什么必要雇一支特别信使大军来收集消息。早一个钟头晚一个钟头——能差出什么来？他很快就知道了；若是坏消息，知道得越早越糟；若是好消息，晚一点知道反倒加几分喜。……再者说，你就是想想，再穷、再贪的人，总不至于指望在病人和卧床者的大难当头帮了个忙，还要收钱吧。
「我看没这个必要。」他平和而坚决地说。「让我想想——」
「要不我自己跑一趟？用不了多大工夫。」
情急之下，她已打定主意——哪怕顺着东家那些叫人瞠目的怪念头——甘冒把两个男人单独留在家里的可怕风险。万一乔暴起伤人怎么办？万一厄尔福沃德先生撑着病体开始满宅子探查怎么办？他已经在起疑了；她深知他是天底下头一号的好打听。她发着抖。可她还是准备去，去时一路跑，回时一路跑。
「哦，不行！」他断然不准。「那绝对不成。」他怕失去她。他，病得这样重（他此刻又病重了！）——被独自掠在宅子里！想都不敢想。「听着。

===Original===

Step across to Belrose’s” (Belrose﻿—the man who had purchased Violet’s confectionery business). “I hear he’s got the telephone now. Ask him to telephone for us to the hospital. Then we shall know at once.”
“We don’t do much with them,” Elsie objected, diffident. The truth was that the Earlforward household bought practically nothing at Belrose’s, Belrose’s not being quite Violet’s “sort of shop” under its new ownership.
Mr. Earlforward almost sat up in his protest against the horrible suggestion contained in Elsie’s remark. What! Would Belrose say: “No, you don’t deal with me, and therefore I won’t oblige you by telephoning to the hospital to find out whether Mrs. Earlforward is alive or dead”? A monstrous notion!
“Don’t be silly,” he chid her gravely. “Do as I tell you and run down at once.”
“And would you like me to ask them to telephone for another doctor for you while I’m about it? There’s Dr. Adhams, he’s in Myddelton Square too. They do say he’s very good.”
“When I want another doctor I’ll let you know, Elsie,” said Mr. Earlforward with frigid calm. “There’s a great deal too many doctors. What has Raste done for me, I should like to know?”
“You wouldn’t *let* him do anything,” said Elsie sharply.
He had never heard her speak with less benevolence. Of course he was entitled to give her a good dressing-down, and it might even be his duty to do so. But he lacked confidence in himself. Strange, but he was now in the last resort afraid of Elsie! She was like an amiable and tractable animal which astonishingly shows its teeth and growls.
“Leave the door open,” he muttered.
As Elsie descended to the shop there was a peremptory and loud rat-tat, and then a tattoo on the glass of the shop door. It frightened her. She thought naturally of the possibility of bad news by special messenger or telegraph from the hospital. But Mrs. Perkins’s boy Jerry was at the door. He wore his uniform, of which the distinguishing characteristics were a cap with brass letters on the peak and a leathern apron initialled in black. In King’s Cross Road an enormous motor-lorry throbbed impatiently in attendance upon the gnome.
“Here’s yer umbrella, Elsie,” said Jerry proudly. “I thought you might be wanting of it.”
He made no inquiry as to sick persons.

===Chinese===

你到贝尔罗斯（Belrose）家铺子跑一趟」（贝尔罗斯——盘下薇奥莱特糖果店的那个人）。「听说他如今装了电话。请他替咱们往医院打个电话。那咱们立刻就知道消息了。」
「咱们同他们家不大来往。」埃尔西怯怯地拦了一句。实情是，厄尔福沃德家几乎不在贝尔罗斯铺子里买东西——换了新东家之后，那铺子已不大是薇奥莱特眼中「那一路的店」了。
厄尔福沃德先生几乎从床上坐起来，以示抗议埃尔西话里那可怕的暗示。什么！难道贝尔罗斯会说：「你们不照顾我生意，所以我也不赏这个脸，不替你们打电话问医院，厄尔福沃德太太是死是活随她去」？荒唐透顶的念头！
「别犯傻，」他正色训她。「照我说的办，这就下楼去。」
「那趁这趟，您要不要我请他们顺便再打电话，另请位大夫？阿达姆斯（Adhams）医生就也住在米德尔顿广场（Myddelton Square）。人家都说他医道很好。」
「我要另请大夫，自会告诉你，埃尔西。」厄尔福沃德先生冷冷地说。「这世上的医生实在太多了。我倒想问问，拉斯特（Raste）给我治出什么来了？」
「您*不让*他治嘛。」埃尔西没好气地说。
他从没听过她说话这么不客气。按理他有权把她狠狠训一顿，这没准还正是他的本分。可他对自己没了底气。说来奇怪，事到临头，他如今竟怕埃尔西！她像一头温驯和善的牲口，忽然龇出牙、低吼起来，叫人措手不及。
「门开着。」他咕哝一句。
埃尔西下楼到店堂时，店门上响起一声不容分说的叩门，接着在玻璃上一阵急敲。把她吓了一跳。她自然而然想到：兴许是医院派专差或电报送坏消息来了。可门口站着的是珀金斯（Perkins）太太家的少爷杰里（Jerry）。他穿着他那身制服——标志是一顶帽檐缀铜字的帽子和一条黑字绣姓名缩写的皮围裙。国王十字路（King's Cross Road）上，一辆巨大的载重汽车轰隆隆不耐烦地候着这个土地爷。
「你的伞，埃尔西。」杰里挺自豪地说。「我寻思你兴许用得着。」
他一句也没打听病人的事。

===Original===

He was only interested in the romantic fact that he had used the vast resources of his company to restore the umbrella to his queen, carrying it all day through all manner of streets in his long round, and finally persuading that important personage the motor-driver to stop at Riceyman Steps on no business of the company’s. Elsie took the umbrella from his dirty little hands, which were, however, no dirtier than his grinning face, and he ran off almost before she could thank him.
“Jerry!” she summoned him back, and he came, risking the wrath of the driver. “Come along tonight, will yer, after ye’ve done? Rap quiet on the door. I might want yer.”
“Right O, Elsie!” He was gone. The lorry was gone.
Elsie went upstairs again with the umbrella, not because the umbrella would not have been safe in the shop, but because she felt that she must give another glance at Joe before she left the premises. It was an unconsidered movement. She had forgotten that Mr. Earlforward’s bedroom door was open.
“Elsie,” he called out, as she passed on the landing, “who was that?”
Her tired and exasperated brain worked with extraordinary swiftness. She decided that she could not enter into a long explanation concerning the umbrella and Jerry. Why should she? “He” was already suspicious.
“Postman,” she answered, without the slightest hesitation, lying as glibly and lightly as a born, lifelong liar, and continued her way upstairs. She was somehow vaguely, indirectly, defending the secrecy of Joe.
In her room she put the umbrella in its paper again under her bed, gazing at Joe as she did so. Joe was very ill. She had given him two doses of quinine (which Dr. Raste, making Elsie ashamed of her uncharitable judgments on him, had had sent direct from a chemist’s within an hour and a half of his departure), and she was disturbed that the medicine had not produced an immediate and marked effect on the patient.
Joe had got one arm through the ironwork at the head of the bed, and was tearing off little slips of the peeling wallpaper in the corner. She took hold of his hot hand, and silently guided it back through the ironwork on to the bed.
“Shall I give you another dose?” she suggested tentatively, with brow creased.
He nodded. He knew malaria and he knew quinine; and, fortified by his expert approval, she gave him another dose.

===Chinese===

他只醉心于那桩罗曼蒂克的事实：他动用了公司的庞大资源，把伞物归原主、奉还他的女王——他揣着它跑了一整天的长路，穿街过巷，末了还说动汽车司机那位要人，掠开公司的事务，在莱斯门台阶（Riceyman Steps）停了车。埃尔西从他脏兮兮的小手里接过伞——那双手，倒也不比他咧着的笑脸更脏——她还没来得及道谢，他已跑远了。
「杰里！」她把他唤回来，他冒着司机发火的风险折返。「今儿晚上收了工，过来一趟，成不成？轻轻敲门。我兴许用得着你。」
「没得说，埃尔西！」他人没了。车也走了。
埃尔西带着伞又上了楼——并非伞放在店堂里不稳妥，而是她觉得出门前必须再看乔一眼。这是没过脑子的举动。她忘了厄尔福沃德先生的卧室门开着。
「埃尔西，」她走过楼梯口时他喊，「方才是谁？」
她那颗又累又烦的脑子，转得出奇地快。她断定，关于伞和杰里，没法展开一场长篇解释。何必呢？「他」已经起疑了。
「邮差。」她答道，没有半分迟疑，撒谎撒得像天生的、职业的骗子那样溜、那样轻，说罢继续上楼。不知怎么，隐隐约约、曲曲折折地，她这是在护着乔的秘密。
回到自己房里，她把伞重新包上纸塞回床底，一面直望着乔。乔病得很重。她已经喂过他两剂奎宁（拉斯特医生临走不到一个半钟头，就让人从药房直接送了来——这叫埃尔西为自己刻薄他的那些想法害臊），只是药没在病人身上立竿见影，她心里发慌。
乔的一条胳膊穿过了床头铁栏，正抠着墙角剥落的壁纸，一条条撕下来。她握住他滚烫的手，不出声地把它引回铁栏里、放回床上。
「再给你服一剂好不好？」她皱着眉，试探着说。
他点点头。他懂疟疾，也懂奎宁；得了这行家的首肯，她胆气一壮，又喂了他一剂。

===Original===

Both of them had the belief that if five grains of a medicine did you ten percent of “good,” ten grains would assuredly do you twenty percent of good, and so on in proportion.
“I’m coming in again in a minute or two. I’ve just got to go across the Steps on an errand,” she said, and kissed him. Both of them had also the belief that her kisses did him good; and this conviction was better founded than the other one. She had said nothing to him about Mrs. Earlforward’s operation. He had learnt only that Elsie was mistress because Mrs. Earlforward was in hospital; the full story might have aggravated his mental distress.
“Elsie!” It was Mr. Earlforward’s summons as she crossed the landing on her way down.
She put no more than her face﻿—a rather mettlesome face﻿—into the room.
“What do you keep on going upstairs for?”
Yes. He suspected. With strange presence of mind she replied promptly:
“I’ve just been up for the key of the shop, sir. I left it up in my room. I can’t go out and leave the shop door on the latch, can I?”
“Well, bring me all the letters.”
“Oh, very well. Very well!” She was hostile again.
This time she shut the bedroom door, ignoring his protest. Then she went upstairs once more and locked her own door on the outside and carried off the key. At any rate, if in some impossible caprice he should take it into his head to prowl about the house in her absence, he should not pry into her room. He had no right to do so. And she was absolutely determined to defend her possession of Joe. A moment later she bounced into Mr. Earlforward’s bedroom, and carelessly dropped all the letters on to the bed﻿—a regular shower of envelopes and packets.
“There!” she exclaimed, on a hard and inimical note, as if saying: “You asked for them. You’ve got them. And I wash my hands of it all.”
Mr. Earlforward saw that he must walk warily. She was a changing Elsie, a disagreeably astonishing Elsie. He did not quite know where he was with her.
As she emerged from the shop into the Steps a young woman with a young dog, stopping suddenly, addressed her in soft, apprehensive, commiserating accents:
“How is Mr. Earlforward this evening?”
“He seems to think as he’s a bit better, ’m, thank you, *in himself*,” Elsie answered brightly.

===Chinese===

他们俩都信这么个理：一味药五格令若能给你百分之十的「好处」，十格令保管给你百分之二十，依此类推，童叟无欺。
「过一两分钟我就再进来。我得出去到台阶对面办点事。」说着她吻了他。他们俩还都信，她的吻对他有好处；这条信念倒比那条更有根据。厄尔福沃德太太手术的事，她一个字没对他说。他只知道太太进了医院、埃尔西当家；全部实情若说给他听，只怕加重他心里的苦。
「埃尔西！」她下楼穿过楼梯口时，厄尔福沃德先生唤住了她。
她只把一张脸——一张憋着股劲的脸——探进房门。
「你一趟趟老上楼做什么？」
是了。他起疑了。她以惊人的镇定，应声答道：
「我刚上去取店门的钥匙，先生。我把它落在房里了。我总不能出门时让店门虚掩着，对吧？」
「那么，把信全给我拿来。」
「哦，好啊。好啊！」她又来了火气。
这一回她不管他抗议，径自带上了卧室门。然后她又上楼一回，把自己房门反锁，掠走钥匙。这样一来，哪怕他一时犯了什么说不通的怪念头，趁她不在满宅子逡巡，也窥不进她的房间。他没有这个权利。而捍卫自己对乔的所有权，她是铁了心的。片刻之后，她一阵风闯进厄尔福沃德先生的卧室，把一沓信随手掠在床上——信封信裹撒了半床。
「喏！」她硬邦邦、气冲冲地掠下一句，仿佛在说：「是你要的。给你了。往后一概与我无干。」
厄尔福沃德先生看出来了，自己得处处留神。这是一个变了样的埃尔西，一个变得叫人发怵的埃尔西。同她打交道，他有些吃不住底了。
她从店堂出来走上台阶时，一位牵着条小狗的年轻太太忽然站住，柔声细语、又是关切又是怜惜地开了口：
「厄尔福沃德先生今晚怎么样了？」
「他自己觉得好些了呢，太太，谢谢您，*他自个儿觉得*。」埃尔西脆生生地答。

===Original===

She was uplifted by the mere concern in the voice, and at once felt more kindly towards her master, was indeed rather ashamed of her recent harshness to him.
Dusk had now fallen, and she could not see very clearly, but the next instant she had recognized both the woman and the dog. Quite a lady! A sort of a sealskin coat! Gloves! Utterly different from the savage creature of the previous night. The dog, too, was different. A dog lacking yet in experience of the world, and apt to forget that a dog’s business is to keep an eye on its guardian if it sets any store on a quiet and safe existence; but still well disposed towards its guardian, and apparently in no fear of her. More remorse for Elsie.
“Oh! I’m *so* glad!﻿ ﻿… And Mrs. Earlforward?”
“Oh, ’m! We haven’t heard. We’re expecting news.”
“I do hope everything’ll be all right. Operation﻿—internal trouble, isn’t it?”
“Yes, ’m.”
“Yes. So I heard. Well, thank you. Good night. Skip﻿—Skip!”
Skip was the disturber of repose, and he responded, leaping. The two disappeared round the corner.
It was wonderful to Elsie how everybody knew, and how kind everybody was. She was touched. The woman had given her the illusion that the whole of Clerkenwell was filled with anxiety for the welfare of her master and her mistress. Her sense of responsibility was intensified. If the whole of Clerkenwell knew that she was secretly harbouring her young man in her bedroom!﻿ ﻿… She went hot. The complexity of her situation frightened her afresh.
Belrose’s was at its old royal game of expending vast quantities of electric current. The place had just been lighted up, and had the air of a popular resort; it warmed and vitalized all the Steps by its radiance, which seemed to increase from month to month. What neither Mr. Earlforward nor anybody else of the old Clerkenwell tradition had ever been able to understand or approve was the continual illumination of the upper storeys. And yet the solution of the mystery was simple, and lay in a fact with which most of the district was familiar. Belrose had “gone in for wholesale.” Elsie entered the shop very timidly, for she regarded her errand as “presuming,” and in the midst of all her anxieties she had diffidence enough to be a little ashamed of it.

===Chinese===

单是那嗓音里的关切，就把她的心抬高了些；她随即对东家也和软下来，当真为方才对他的粗声粗气有点害臊。
暮色已合，她看不十分清，可下一瞬她就认出了这太太和这狗。好一位体面太太！海豹皮样式的大衣！戴着手套！同昨儿夜里那个凶神恶煞判若两人。狗也不同了。这是条还没见过世面的狗，动不动忘了狗的本分——狗若想图个安稳太平，就该时刻盯牢自己的监护人；不过它对监护人尚存善意，瞧着也不怕她。埃尔西愈发过意不去。
「哦！那可*太*好了！……厄尔福沃德太太呢？」
「唉，太太！还没有信儿。正等着呢。」
「千万盼着顺顺当当。手术——是里头的毛病，对吧？」
「是的，太太。」
「嗯，我也这么听说。好，谢谢。晚安。斯基普（Skip）——斯基普！」
斯基普这位安眠的捣乱分子应声一跃。一人一狗在拐角处不见了。
人人都知道，人人都这样好心——埃尔西觉得真是奇妙。她心里发热。这位太太给了她一种错觉：整个克拉肯韦尔（Clerkenwell）都在为她的东家和主母的安危悬着心。她的责任感陡然重了。可倘若整个克拉肯韦尔都知道了，她在卧室里偷偷窝藏着自己的情郎！……她脸上烧起来。处境的盘根错节又把她吓了一跳。
贝尔罗斯铺子又玩起了它那挥霍巨量电流的老派王者游戏。店堂刚点上灯，一派热闹场所的气象；它的光辉把整条台阶都烘暖了、照活了，而且那光辉仿佛一月盛过一月。楼上楼层彻夜通明——这一层，无论厄尔福沃德先生还是克拉肯韦尔老规矩的任何人，从来理解不了也赞成不了。可这谜底其实简单，本区大多数人都心里有数：贝尔罗斯「做起了批发」。埃尔西怯生生地走进店堂，因为她觉得这趟差事有些「僭越」；心乱如麻之中，她还有足够的羞怯，为此有点抬不起头。

===Original===

The shop was most pleasantly warm; its warmth was a greeting which would have overpowered some folk; and there was a fine rich odour of cheese and humanity. Also the shop was full. You could scarcely move in it. The stock was plenteous, and the character of the stock had changed. Advertised brands of comestibles of universal consumption were far less prominent than under previous regimes, and there was a great deal more individuality. The travellers and the collectors of advertised brands now called at the establishment with a demeanour different from of old; they had to leave their hard-faced, bullying manner on the doorstep. Two enormous and smiling young, mature women stood behind the counter. Their magnificently rounded façades were covered with something that was only white on Saturdays and Wednesdays, and certainly was not white tonight. Like the shop itself the servers were neither tidy nor clean; but they were hearty, gay and active, and they had authority, for one of them was Mr. Belrose’s sister, and the other Mrs. Belrose’s sister; nevertheless, they looked like sisters; they both had golden, rough hair and ruddy complexions, and the same experienced, comprehending, jolly expression, and fat, greasy hands.
There were four customers in the shop, of course all women, and the six women seemed to be all chatting together. The interior was the interior of a shop in full swing, but it showed in addition the better qualities of a bar parlour whose landlord knows how to combine respectability with freedom of style. Miss Belrose, who was nearest the door, smiled benignantly at Elsie on her entrance, as if saying: “You are one of us, and we are yours.”
When two outgoing customers squeezed themselves between Elsie and a pile of cheeses, and her turn came to be served, Elsie suddenly discovered that she could not straight away execute Mr. Earlforward’s command. She had a feeling that shops did not exist in order to supply telephone accommodation gratis to non-customers, and she was simply unable to articulate the request; nor did the extreme seriousness of the case inspire her to boldness. She asked for a quarter of a pound of cheese, and was immediately requested to name any cheese that she might fancy, the implication being that no matter what her fancy it could and would be satisfied on the most advantageous terms.

===Chinese===

店堂里暖得叫人周身舒泰；这份暖意本身就是一声招呼，能把有些人整个儿淹没；还有一股奶酪和人味儿交织的浓香。店堂也挤满了。人在里头几乎转不开身。货色充足，而货色的路数也变了。先前各任东家手底下那些贴广告的通用吃食，如今远不如从前显眼，个性倒是多出许多。跑街的和收广告牌子的如今登这家的门，做派也同从前两样；那副铁面霸道的架势，得掠在门槛外头。柜台后头站着两位体量惊人、笑容满面的少妇。她们那宏伟圆润的前襟上罩着的东西，只有星期六和星期三才是白的，今晚肯定不是。同店堂本身一样，这两位售货员既不整洁也不算干净；可她们热诚、快活、麻利，而且有威权——一位是贝尔罗斯先生的妹妹，一位是贝尔罗斯太太的妹妹；饶是如此，两人看着倒像亲姐妹：一样的金色乱发，一样的红脸膛，一样那种见过世面、心照不宣、乐呵呵的神气，还有一样肥腻的手。
店里有四位顾客，自然全是女人，六个女人听着像是凑在一处闲聊。这内堂是一间生意鼎盛的铺子内堂，却又兼有上等酒吧间的优点——东家懂得怎样把体面同洒脱合到一处。贝尔罗斯小姐离门最近，见埃尔西进来，慈眉善目地朝她一笑，仿佛在说：「你是我们一路人，我们也是你一路人。」
两位要走的顾客从埃尔西同一摞奶酪之间挤出去，轮到她买了，她这才猛然发现，厄尔福沃德先生的吩咐，她没法张口就办。她总觉得，铺子的存在不是为了给非主顾免费提供电话方便；那句话她怎么也吐不出口；而事情再十万火急，也没能给她壮起胆来。她开口要了四分之一磅奶酪，人家立刻请她指名任何一款她中意的奶酪——言下之意，无论她中意哪一款，都能以最合算的条件让她中意到底。

Now Elsie did not want any cheese; she wanted nothing at all. Mrs. Earlforward, before vanishing into the hospital, had bought for the master a generous supply of invalid foods, which, for the most part refused by the obstinate master, would suffice Joe for several days, and of all such eatables as Belrose’s sold Elsie had in hand enough also for several days.
She said “Cheddar,” reacting quite mechanically to the question put; and then she was confronted with another problem. She had no money, not a penny. It would be necessary for her to say, “I must run back for some money,” and having said that to return and somehow manoeuvre Mr. Earlforward’s keys off the chest of drawers and rifle the safe once more. And already he was suspicious! How could she do it? She could not do it. But she must do it. She saw the cheese weighed and slipped into a piece of paper. The moment of trial was upon her.
Then the back door of the shop opened﻿—she recognized the old peculiar, familiar sound of the latch﻿—and a third enormous, white-clad, golden-haired, jolly, youngish woman appeared in the doorway. This was Mrs. Belrose herself, and you at once saw, and even felt, that her authority exceeded the authority of her sister and her sister-in-law. Mrs. Belrose was a ruler. As soon as she saw Elsie her gigantic face softened into a very gentle smile of compassion, a smile that conveyed nothing but compassion, excluding all jollity. She raised a stout finger and without a word beckoned Elsie into the back room and shut the door. The ancient kitchen-parlour was greatly changed. It was less clean than Elsie had left it, but it glittered with light. More cheeses! And in the corner by the mantelpiece was the telephone. And through the window Elsie saw an oldish, thin little man moving about in the yard with a lantern against a newly erected shed. Still more cheeses﻿—seemingly as many cheeses as Mr. Earlforward possessed books! The oldish man was Mr. Belrose, guardian and overlord of the three women, and original instigator of this singular wholesale trade in cheeses which he had caused to prosper despite the perfect unsuitability of his premises and other difficulties. Individuality and initiative had triumphed.

===Chinese===

其实埃尔西一点奶酪也不要；她什么都不想要。厄尔福沃德太太住进医院之前，给东家买了丰盛的病人吃食；那执拗的东家多半不肯沾唇，这些够乔吃上好几天；而贝尔罗斯铺子卖的那类吃食，埃尔西手头也备着好些天的量。
她机械地应对了那句问话，说「切达」；随即又迎面撞上另一个难题。她身无分文。那她就得说「我得回去取钱」，说了再折回去，想方设法把厄尔福沃德先生的钥匙从五斗柜顶上弄到手，再去攝一回保险柜。可他已经在起疑了！这怎么做得成？做不成。可又非做成不可。她眼看着奶酪过了秤、包进了一张纸。考验的时辰压到了她头上。
这时铺子的后门开了——那声门闩响，老旧、特别、耳熟——门口出现了第三位体量惊人、白衣金发、乐呵呵的半老妇人。这位正是贝尔罗斯太太本人；而且你一眼就看出、甚至体感到，她的威权盖过她的小姑和她的小妯娌。贝尔罗斯太太是位统治者。她一看见埃尔西，那张巨大的脸便柔下来，化作一个极温柔的怜悯的笑——那笑里只有怜悯，不含一丝嬉笑。她抬起一根粗指头，一言不发，把埃尔西招进后屋，随手带上门。这间老厨房客厅大变了样。它不如埃尔西先前留下时干净，却灯火煌煌。又是奶酪！壁炉边的角落里安着电话。隔着窗，埃尔西望见一个上了年纪、瘦小的男人提着灯笼在新搭的棚子边转悠。还是奶酪——奶酪之多，怕是赶得上厄尔福沃德先生的书了！这个上了年纪的男人就是贝尔罗斯先生——三个女人的保护人和太上皇，也是这桩奇特奶酪批发生意的始作俑者；尽管店面全然不合用、又多般掣肘，他硬叫它兴旺了起来。个性与进取心得胜了。

===Original===

People asked one another how the Belroses had contrived to build up such a strange success, but they had only to look at the mien and gestures of the Belroses to find the answer to the question.
“How are you getting on, my dear?” demanded Mrs. Belrose, who had scarcely spoken to Elsie in her life before.
“Master wished me to ask you if you’d mind telephoning to the hospital, ’m,” said Elsie, after she had given some details.
“Of course I will. With the greatest pleasure.”
Mrs. Belrose grabbed at the tattered telephone-book, and whetting her greasy thumb whipped over the pages rapidly.
“Where’s them Saints now? Oh! ‘Saintsbury’s.’ ‘Saint.’ ‘St. Bartholomew’s Football and Cricket Ground.’ I expect that’s for the doctors and students. ‘St. Bartholomew’s Hospital.’ This is it. Here we are. City 510.﻿ ﻿… Oh, dear! oh, dear! ‘No telephone information given respecting patients.’ Oh, dear, oh, dear!” She looked at Elsie. “Never mind,” she went on brightly. “We can get over that, I should think.”
She obtained the number and got into communication with the reception office of the hospital.
“I want you to be kind enough to give a message to Mrs. Violet Earlforward from her husband. She’s in your hospital for an operation.﻿ ﻿… Oh, but you must, please. He’s very ill. But he’s a bit better, and it will do Mrs. Earlforward ever so much good to know.﻿ ﻿… Oh, *please*! Yes, I know, but they can’t send anyone down. Oh, you don’t count rules when it’s urgent. It might be life and death. But you can telephone up to the ward. You’re starred, so you must have a private exchange. Oh, yes. To *oblige*. Yes, Earlforward, Violet. And you might just ask how she is while you’re about it. You *are* good.”
She held the line and waited, sitting down on a chair to rest herself. And to Elsie:
“They’re very nice, really, at those hospitals, once you get on the right side of them. I suppose *you*’ve got about all you can do?”
“Well, there isn’t much nursing, and the shop’s closed.”
“Oh, yes, and the Steps do look so queer with it closed. Somehow it makes it look like Sunday. Doctor has been today, I suppose?”
“Yes, ’m. This morning,” said Elsie, and stopped there, not caring to divulge the secret of Mr. Earlforward’s insane obstinacy.
“Yes. I’m here. I’m listening. Oh, dear! Oh, dear! She’s﻿—Oh, dear! Owing to what? ‘Under﻿—nourishment’?﻿ ﻿… He’s rung off.”
Mrs.

===Chinese===

人们彼此打听，贝尔罗斯一家是怎么经营出这样一桩离奇的成功来的；可他们只需看看贝尔罗斯们的风度手势，答案就在眼前。
「日子过得怎么样啊，亲爱的？」贝尔罗斯太太问道——她这辈子几乎没同埃尔西说过话。
「东家想请您行个方便，替咱们往医院打个电话，太太。」埃尔西先说了些原委，然后道。
「当然可以。万分乐意。」
贝尔罗斯太太一把抓过翻烂了的电话簿，把油拇指蘸湿了，咝咝地翻。
「圣什么的在那页来着？哦！『圣茨伯里』。『圣』。『圣巴塞洛缪足球板球俱乐部』。八成是给大夫和学生用的。『圣巴塞洛缪医院』。就是这个。找着了。城市区510。……哎呀！哎呀！『有关病人情况，恕不提供电话咨询』。哎呀，哎呀！」她看看埃尔西。「不要紧，」她又爽利起来。「这个咱们能想法子绕过去，我瞧着。」
她接通了号码，同医院的接待处搭上了话。
「劳驾您行个好，替一位丈夫给薇奥莱特·厄尔福沃德太太带句话。她正在贵院动手术。……哦，可您务必帮这个忙，求您了。他病得很重。不过他好些了，让他知道消息，对厄尔福沃德太太也大有好处。……哦，*求您了*！是，我知道，可他们家没人能来。哎呀，事到紧急，哪还顾得上章程。这是人命关天的事。您可以往病房打电话嘛。您是带星标的，肯定有自己的交换机。哎，对。*帮个忙*。对，厄尔福沃德，薇奥莱特。顺便也请问一声她怎么样了。您*真*是个好人。」
她占着线等，坐到一张椅子上歇脚。又对埃尔西说：
「那些医院里的人，说真的，都挺好——只要你摸着了他们的顺毛。*你*嘛，怕是手头的事儿也够你受的了吧？」
「嗐，护理上没多少事，铺子又关着。」
「可不是，铺子一关，台阶那儿瞧着可真怪。不知怎么，倒像礼拜天了。大夫今天来过吧，我猜？」
「来过，太太。上午。」埃尔西说到这儿打住，不愿把厄尔福沃德先生那股疯魔般的执拗抖搂出去。
「在，我在。我听着呢。哎呀！哎呀！她——哎呀！因为什么？『营养——不良』？……他把电话挂了。」
贝尔罗斯

===Original===

Belrose sniffed as she hung up the receiver.
“Oh, Elsie! Your poor mistress has died under it. She died about half an hour ago. According to what *they* say, she might have pulled through, but she hadn’t strength to rally owing to﻿—undernourishment.﻿ ﻿… Well, I’m that cut up!” Mrs. Belrose cried feebly.
Elsie stared at her and did not weep.
“Ought I to tell him, ’m?”
“Oh, yes, you must *tell* him. There’s no sense in hiding them things﻿—especially as he’s a little better. He’s *got* to know. And he’d be very angry, and quite rightly, if he wasn’t told, *and* at once.”
“I’ll go and tell him.”
“Would you like me to come with you?”
“You’re very kind, ’m,” said Elsie, cunning even in disaster. “I can manage. He’s very peculiar, but I know how to manage him. There won’t be nothing to be done till tomorrow, anyway.”
She had another and a far more perilous secret to keep, that of Joe. Therefore she dared not admit a stranger to the house. Of course, soon she would have to admit strangers﻿—but not tonight, not tonight! She must postpone evil.
Mrs. Belrose lifted her immense bulk and kissed Elsie, and then Elsie cried. Saying not a word more, she turned, opened the door, and passed through the shop, rapt, totally ignoring the servers and the quarter of a pound of cheese.
“Tomorrow,” she said to herself, “I shall tell her” (Mrs. Belrose) “all about Joe. She’ll understand.” The mere thought of Mrs. Belrose was a refuge for her. “But missis can’t be dead. It was only yesterday morning﻿—”
“Leave me alone. Leave me!” breathed Henry Earlforward in a dismaying murmur when she gave him the news. She obeyed.

===Chinese===

太太挂上听筒，吸了吸鼻子。
「哦，埃尔西！你那可怜的主母没能挺过来。半个钟头前去的。照*他们*的说法，她本有可能挺过去的，可她没有力气缓过来，因为——营养不良。……唉，我这心里刀割似的！」贝尔罗斯太太有气无力地喊。
埃尔西直愣愣望着她，没有哭。
「我该告诉他吗，太太？」
「哦，该，你*必须*告诉他。这种事瞒着没道理——何况他还好些了。他*必须*知道。若不告诉他，还*不马上*告诉他，他会大发脾气——而且发得完全在理。」
「我这就去告诉他。」
「要我陪你去吗？」
「您心眼儿真好，太太，」埃尔西说——纵在大祸里也透着精明。「我一个人应付得来。他那人古怪，可我知道怎么拿捉他。横竖到明天也没什么要办的事。」
她还压着另一桩、凶险得多的秘密——乔的秘密。所以她不敢放生人进宅。当然，很快她就不得不放生人进门了——可今晚不行，今晚不行！她能做的，是把祸事往后推。
贝尔罗斯太太抬起她那庞大的身躯，吻了吻埃尔西；这一回，埃尔西哭了。再没说一句话，她转身开门，穿过店堂，神思恍惚，全然顾不上那两位售货员，也顾不上那四分之一磅奶酪。
「明天，」她自言自语，「我把乔的事一五一十告诉她」（指贝尔罗斯太太）。「她会懂的。」单是想到贝尔罗斯太太，她就有个避难处。「可太太不会死的。明明昨天早上还——」
「别管我。别管我！」她把消息告诉他时，亨利·厄尔福沃德用一声叫人心惊的低语吐出这句话。她依了他。

## XIV A Man’s Private Life / 十四 一个男人的私生活
===Original===

One morning in November, at a little past eight o’clock, Mrs. Arb, watching from behind the door of her yet unopened shop, saw Mr. Earlforward help Elsie to carry out the empty bookstand and set it down in front of the window, and then, with overcoat, muffler and umbrella, depart from Riceyman Steps on business. Mrs. Arb immediately unlocked her door, went out just as she was﻿—hatless, coatless, gloveless, wearing a white apron﻿—locked her door, and walked across to Mr. Earlforward’s. Elsie had already begun to fill the bookstand with books which overnight had been conveniently piled near the entrance of the shop.
“Good morning, Elsie. Dull morning, isn’t it? Is master up yet?” said Mrs. Arb vivaciously, rubbing her hands in the chilly, murky dawn, and brightening the dawn.
“Oh, ’m! He’s gone out. I don’t expect him back till eleven. It’s one of his buying mornings, ye see.”
“Oh, *dear*, dear!” Mrs. Arb exclaimed, with cheerful resignation. “And I’ve only got ten minutes. Well, I haven’t really got that. Shop ought to be open now. But I thought I’d let ’em wait a bit this morning.”
She glanced anxiously at her own establishment to see whether any customer had come down the steps from the square. But, in truth, as she had now sold the business, and the premises, and was to give possession in a few weeks, she was not genuinely concerned about the possible loss of profit on an ounce or two ounces of tea. She wandered with apparent aimlessness into Mr. Earlforward’s shop.
“Did you want to see him particular, ’m?”
“I won’t say so particular as all that. So you look after the shop when Mr. Earlforward is out, Elsie?”
“It’s like this, ’m. All the books is marked inside, and some outside. If anybody comes in that looks respectable, I ask ’em to look round for themselves, and if they take a book they pay me, and I ask ’em to write down the name of it on a bit of paper.” She pointed to some small memorandum sheets prepared from old unassorted envelopes which had been cut open and laid flat, with pencil close by. “If it’s some regular customer like, that *must* see Mr. Earlforward himself, I ask ’em to write their names down. And if I don’t like the look of anybody, I tell ’em I don’t know anything, and out they go.”
“What a good arrangement!” said Mrs. Arb approvingly. “But if you have to attend to the shop, how can you do the cleaning and so on?”

===Chinese===

十一月的一个早晨，八点刚过，阿尔布太太从她那尚未开门的铺子门后张望，看见厄尔福沃德先生帮着埃尔西把空书架抬出去、安在橱窗前，随后披上大衣、围好围巾、拿起伞，离开莱斯门台阶办事去了。阿尔布太太立刻开了自家的锁，原样走了出去——没戴帽子，没穿外衣，没戴手套，只系着条白围裙——锁上门，穿过街到厄尔福沃德先生的铺子里来。埃尔西已经开始往书架里填书，那些书是头天夜里顺手堆在店门口附近的。
「早晨好，埃尔西。今儿个天真阴沉，是不是？东家起来了吗？」阿尔布太太活泼地说，在阴冷昏暗的清晨里搓着手，倒把清晨搓亮了几分。
「哦，太太！他出门了。我看他十一点前回不来。今儿是他收书的日子，您瞧。」
「哎呀，真是的！」阿尔布太太叫道，认命认得心甘情愿。「我只有十分钟。喍，其实十分钟也没有。铺子这会儿早该开门了。不过我想，今儿早上就叫他们多等一会儿吧。」
她焦心地朝自家铺子掠了一眼，看看有没有顾客从广场那头下了台阶来。可实情是，她既已把生意连同店面都盘了出去，几星期内就要交房，一兩盎司茶叶可能折掉的那点利，她并不真正放在心上。她装出一副漫无目的的样子，晃进了厄尔福沃德先生的店堂。
「您是有要紧事找他吗，太太？」
「倒也说不上多要紧。这么说，厄尔福沃德先生出门的时候，铺子就归你照管了，埃尔西？」
「是这样的，太太。所有的书里头都标了价，有的外头也标了。进来的客人要是瞧着体面，我就请他们自己随便看；他们拿了书就付钱给我，我请他们把书名写在一张小纸条上。」她指了指几张记事纸——那是用拆开摊平的旧杂信封改的——旁边掠着铅笔。「要是哪位老主顾，非见厄尔福沃德先生本人不可的，我就请他们把大名留下。要是哪个人的模样我瞧着不对，我就说我什么都不知道，他们也就走了。」
「安排得多妥当！」阿尔布太太赞许道。「可你既要照管铺子，打扫收拾那些活儿又怎么做得过来？」

===Original===

Elsie’s ingenuous, kind face showed distress; her dark-blue eyes softened in solicitude.
“Ah, ’m! There you’ve got me. I can’t. I can only clean the shop these mornings, and not much of that neither, because I must keep my hands dry for customers.”
Mrs. Arb, vaguely smiling to herself, trotted to and fro in the gloomy shop, which had the air of a crypt, except that in these days crypts are usually lighted by electricity, and the shop was lighted by nature alone on this dark morning. She peered, bending forward, into the dark spaces between the bays, and descried the heaps of books on the floor. The dirt and the immense disorder almost frightened her. She had not examined the inside of the shop before﻿—had, indeed, previously entered it only once, when she was in no condition to observe. Mr. Earlforward had never seized an occasion to invite her within.
“This will want some putting straight,” she said, “if ever it is put straight.”
“And well you may say it, ’m,” Elsie replied compassionately. “He’s always trying to get straight, ’specially lately, ’m. We did get one room straight upstairs, but it meant letting all the others go. Between you and me, he’ll never get straight. But he has hopes, and it’s no use saying anything to him.”
“I suppose you can do this room, too, on his buying mornings,” said Mrs. Arb, peeping into Mr. Earlforward’s private back room from which the shop and the shop-door could be kept under observation.
“Oh, ’m! He wouldn’t let me. He won’t have anything touched in that room.”
“Then who does it?”
“He does it himself, ’m﻿—when it is done.”
“Does he!” murmured Mrs. Arb in a peculiar tone.
The bookshelves went up to the ceiling on every side. The floor was thickly strewn with books, the table also. Chairs also. The blind lay crumpled on the book-covered windowsill. The window was obscured by dirt. The ceiling was a blackish-grey. A heavy deposit of black dust covered all things. The dreadful den expressed intolerably to Mrs. Arb the pathos of the existence of a man who is determined to look after himself. It convicted a whole sex of being feckless, foolish, helpless, infantile, absurd. Mrs. Arb and Elsie exchanged glances. Elsie blushed.
“Yes. I’m that ashamed of it, ’m!” said Elsie. “But you know what they are!”
Mrs. Arb gave two short nods.

===Chinese===

埃尔西那张实心眼、好心肠的脸露出为难；她那双深蓝的眼睛关切地柔和下来。
「唉，太太！这可问住我了。做不来。这样的上午我只能打扫店堂，还打扫不了多少——因为待客的手得保持干爽嘛。」
阿尔布太太心不在焉地含着笑，在昏暗的店堂里小步来回蹀——这店堂有一股地下墓室的气息，只是如今的墓室大都用电灯照明，而这个昏暗的早晨，店堂全凭天光照明。她俯身朝书架隔出的暗处张望，望见地板上一堆堆的书。那污秽、那铺天盖地的凌乱，几乎把她吓着了。她以前从没细看过店堂内部——其实统共只进来过一回，而且那回她也无心细看。厄尔福沃德先生从不曾抓住时机请她进来看看。
「这得好好归置归置了，」她说，「如果还有归置过来的一天。」
「您算说对了，太太，」埃尔西怜惜地答道。「他老想理出个头绪，尤其这阵子。楼上我们倒是把一间屋理利索了，可那意味着别的屋全掠下不管。咱们私下说，他永远理不利索。可他抱着指望呢，您说什么也是白说。」
「他收书的上午，这间屋大概也归你打扫吧。」阿尔布太太说着，朝厄尔福沃德先生那间私室后屋掠了一眼——从那里，店堂和店门都可以监视。
「哦，太太！他不许我沾手。那间屋里的东西，什么都不许动。」
「那谁打扫？」
「他自己动手，太太——若当真扫过的话。」
「是嘛！」阿尔布太太用一种特别的声气扇咕道。
书架四面顶到天花板。地板上密密铺着书，桌上也是。椅子上也是。窗帘皱成一团堆在铺满书的窗台上。窗玻璃被污垢糊得不明。天花板是黑灰色。一层厚厚的黑尘罩着一切。这间可怕的巢穴，把一个打定主意自己照料自己的男人的生存之哀，赤裸裸地摆在阿尔布太太面前，叫人不忍直视。它给整个男性定了罪：无能、愚蠢、无助、幼稚、荒唐。阿尔布太太同埃尔西交换了个眼色。埃尔西红了脸。
「是呢。我都替他臊得慌，太太！」埃尔西说。「可男人就那副德行，您是知道的！」
阿尔布太太短促地点了两下头。

===Original===

She moved her hand as if to plumb the layer of dust with one feminine finger, but refrained; she dared not.
“And do you do his cooking, too?” she asked.
“Well, ’m. He gets his own breakfast, and he makes his own bed﻿—it’s always done before I come of a morning﻿—and he cleans his own boots. I begin his dinner, but, seeing as I go at twelve, he finishes it. He gets his own tea. I must say he isn’t what you call a big eater.”
“Seems to me it’s all very cleverly organized.”
“Oh, it is, ’m! There’s not many gentlemen could manage as he does. But it’s a dreadful pity. Makes me fair cry sometimes. And him so clean and neat himself, too.”
“Yes,” said Mrs. Arb, agreeing that the contrast between the master and his home was miraculous, awful, and tragic.
“I suppose I’d better not go upstairs as he isn’t here, Elsie?”
The two women exchanged more glances. Elsie perfectly comprehended the case of Mrs. Arb, and sympathized with her. Mrs. Arb was being courted. Mrs. Arb had come to no decision. Mrs. Arb desired as much information as possible before coming to a decision. Women had the right to look after themselves against no matter what man. Women were women, and men were men. The Arb-Earlforward affair was crucial for both parties.
“Oh! I think you might, ’m. But I can’t go with you.” Sex-loyalty had triumphed over a too-strict interpretation of the duty of the employed to the employer. A conspiracy had been set up.
Mrs. Arb had to step over hummocks of books in order to reach the foot of the stairs. The left-hand half of every step of the stairs was stacked with books﻿—cheap editions of novels in paper jackets, under titles such as *Just a Girl*, *Not Like Other Girls*, *A Girl Alone*. Weak but righteous and victorious girls crowded the stairs from top to bottom, so that Mrs. Arb could scarcely get up. The landing also was full of girls. The front room on the first floor was, from the evidence of its furniture, a dining room, though not used as such. The massive mahogany table was piled up with books, as also the big sideboard, the mantelpiece, various chairs. The floor was carpeted with books. Less dust than in the den below, but still a great deal. The Victorian furniture was “good”; it was furniture meant to survive revolutions and conflagrations and generations; it was everlasting furniture; it would command respect through any thickness of dust.

===Chinese===

她抬起手，仿佛想用一根女人的手指探探那层尘的深浅，又忍住了；她不敢。
「他的饭也是你做吗？」她问。
「这么说吧，太太。他的早饭他自己弄，床他自己铺——我早上来之前总是铺好了的——靴子他自己刷。他的午饭我起个头，可我十二点就走，剩下他自己收尾。下午茶他自己弄。我得说一句，他可不是您说的那种大肚汉。」
「依我看，这一套组织得够巧的。」
「可不是嘛，太太！能像他这么操持的先生可不多。可也真叫人惋惜。有时候我都得掉泪。他人自己又那么干净齐整。」
「是啊。」阿尔布太太应道，心里认同：主人的齐整同他的家宅之间那反差，真是离奇、骇人、悲惨。
「他既然不在，我最好还是别上楼去吧，埃尔西？」
两个女人又交换了几个眼色。埃尔西完全明白阿尔布太太的处境，也同情她。阿尔布太太正被人追求。阿尔布太太还没拿定主意。阿尔布太太想在拿主意之前尽量多掌握些底细。女人有权保护自己，无论对上哪个男人。女人是女人，男人是男人。阿尔布同厄尔福沃德这桩事，对双方都是生死攸关。
「哦！我看可以，太太。可我不能陪您去。」同性别的忠心战胜了对雇员忠于东家这条义务的过分拘泥的解释。一场共谋就此缔结。
阿尔布太太只得跨过一坨坨的书才走到楼梯脚下。楼梯每一级的左半边都码着书——纸面封皮的廉价小说，书名诸如《不过是个姑娘》《不似旁的姑娘》《孤身姑娘》。那些柔弱却正气、终得胜利的姑娘们挤满了楼梯，从顶到底，阿尔布太太几乎挤不上去。楼梯口也满是姑娘。二楼临街那间屋，从家具看是间餐室，尽管没派上这用场。厚重的大红木桌上堆满书，大餐具柜上、壁炉台上、好几把椅子上也是。地板上铺的书倒成了地毯。比起楼下的巢穴灰尘少些，可依旧厚厚一层。维多利亚时代的家具是「上等货」；那是造出来要挺过革命、大火和世世代代的家具；是永恒的家具；蒙上多厚的尘，也自有一股叫人肃然的气派。

===Original===

The back room, with quite as large a number of books as the front room, but even less dust, was a bedroom. The very wide bed had been neatly made. Mrs. Arb turned down the corner of the coverlet; a fairly clean pillow-slip, no sheets, only blankets! She drew open drawers in a great mahogany chest. Two of them were full of blue suits, absolutely new. In another drawer were at least a dozen quite new grey flannel shirts. A wardrobe was stuffed with books.
Coming out of the bedroom, she perceived between it and the stairs a long, narrow room. Impossible to enter this room because of books; but Mrs. Arb did the impossible, and after some excavation with her foot disclosed a bath, which was full to the brim and overflowing with books. Now Mrs. Arb was pretty well accustomed to baths; she was not aware of the extreme rarity of baths in Clerkenwell, and hence she could not adequately appreciate the heroism of a hero who, possessing such a treasure, had subdued it to the uses of mere business. Nevertheless, her astonishment and amaze were sufficiently noticeable, and she felt, disturbingly and delightfully, the thrill of surprising clandestinely the secrets of a man’s intimate personal existence.
Then she caught the sound of dropping water; it was on the second-floor, in a room shaped like the bathroom, a room with two shelves, a gas-ring, and a sink. The water was dropping with a queer reverberation on to the sink from a tap above. There were a few plates, cups, saucers, jugs, saucepans, dishes; half a loaf of bread, a slice of cooked bacon; there was no milk, no butter. His kitchen and larder! One gas-ring! No fireplace! Mrs. Arb was impressed.
The other rooms on the second-floor were full of majestic furniture, books and dust. One of them had recently been cleaned and tidied, but dozens of books still lay on the floor. She picked up a book, a large, thick volume, for no other reason than that the cover bore a representation of a bird. It was a heavy book, with many coloured pictures of birds. She thought it was quite a pretty thing to look at. By accident she noticed the price pencilled inside the front cover. £40. She was not astonished nor amazed﻿—she was staggered. Mrs. Arb had probably not read ten books since girlhood. To her, reading was a refuge from either idleness or life. She was never idle, and she loved life. Thus she condescended towards books.

===Chinese===

后屋的书同前屋一样多，灰尘却更少；那是间卧室。那张极宽的床铺得整整齐齐。阿尔布太太翻开床罩一角；枕套还算干净，没有床单，只有毯子！她拉开一只大红木柜的抽屉。两抽屉满满的全是蓝色套装，簇新。另一只抽屉里至少有一打崭新的灰法兰纶衬衫。衣柜里塞满了书。
出得卧室，她发现卧室同楼梯之间夹着一间狭长的屋子。书多得进不去人；可阿尔布太太办成了这桩办不到的事，用脚扒拉了几下，扒出一座浴缸——满满一缸书，还往外漫。阿尔布太太对浴缸本是司空见惯；她不知道浴缸在克拉肯韦尔是何等稀罕之物，因而无从充分领略这位英雄身上的英雄气概——坐拥这样一件宝贝，竟把它降服来当纯粹的生意家什。饶是如此，她的惊愕也够分明的了；她感到一阵战栗，心慌意乱又暗自欢喜——她偷窥到了一个男人私生活最深处的秘密。
接着她听见滴水声；在顶层，一间同浴室一个模样的屋里——两块搁板，一只煤气灶圈，一个水槽。水从上方的龙头里滴下来，落在水槽上，声响古怪地嗡嗡回荡。有几只盘子、茶杯、茶托、水壶、锅子和盆；半条面包，一片熟咸肉；没有牛奶，没有黄油。这就是他的厨房兼食品柜！一只煤气灶圈！没有壁炉！阿尔布太太怔住了。
顶层其余几间屋满是大气的家具、书和灰尘。有一间新近打扫收拾过，可地上仍躺着几十本书。她拾起一本——又大又厚的一册——理由不过是封面上画着一只鸟。这本书沉甸甸的，有许多鸟类的彩图。她觉得这东西看着还挺美。无意间她掠见封里用铅笔标着的价钱：四十镑。她不是吃惊，不是惊讶——她是被击懵了。阿尔布太太打少女时代起读过的书怕不到十本。于她，读书是逃避无所事事或逃避人生的去处。她从不闲着，又热爱人生。于是她对书籍，向来是居高临下的。

===Original===

That any book, least of all a picture-book of birds, could be worth £40 had not occurred to her mind. (And this one lying on the floor!) Instantly, in spite of her common sense, she thought for a brief space of all the books in the establishment as worth £40 apiece! Before returning down the book-encumbered stairs, she paused on the top landing. Her throat was coated with the dust which she had displaced in her passage through the house. Her hands were very dirty and very cold﻿—they shone with cold. No fire could have burnt in any of those rooms for years. She dared not touch the handrail of the staircase, even with her fingers all dirty. She paused because she was disconcerted and wanted to arrange the perplexing confusion of her thoughts. The more she reflected the better she realized how strange and powerful and ruthless a person was Mr. Earlforward. She admired, comprehended, sympathized, and yet was intimidated. The character of the man was displayed beyond any misunderstanding by the house with its revelations of his daily life; but there was no clue to it in his appearance and deportment. She was more than intimidated﻿—she was frightened. Withal, the terror﻿—for it amounted to terror﻿—fascinated her. She went down gingerly, hesitating at every step.﻿ ﻿… At the bottom of the lower flight she heard, with new alarm, the bland voice of Mr. Earlforward himself. He was talking with a customer in his den.
“I’ll slip out,” she very faintly whispered to Elsie, who was sweeping near the stairs. Elsie nodded﻿—like a conspirator. But at the same moment Mr. Earlforward and his customer emerged from the back room, and Mrs. Arb was trapped.
“I didn’t notice you come in,” said the bookseller most amiably. “What can I do for you?”
“Oh, thank you, but I only stepped across to speak to Elsie about something.”
The lie, invented on the instant, succeeded perfectly. And Elsie, the honestest soul in Clerkenwell, gave it the support of her silence in the great cause of women against men.
“I’m glad to see you in here,” said Mr. Earlforward gently, having dismissed the customer. “It’s a bit of luck. I’d gone off for Houndsditch, but I happened to meet someone on the road, and nothing would do but I must come back with him. Come in here.”
He drew her by the attraction of his small eyes into the back room. Books had been tipped off one of the chairs on to the floor.

===Chinese===

一本书，尤其一本画鸟的图画书，竟能值四十镑——这样的念头她从没有过。（何况这本还躺在地板上！）一瞬间，她全然不顾常识，把这家铺子里的书统统想成了每本四十镑！在走下那书满为患的楼梯之前，她在顶层的楼梯口站了站。一路穿宅而过扬起的灰尘糊了她的喉咙。她的两手又脏又冷——冷得发亮。那些屋子里怕是有好几年没生过火了。楼梯扶手她都不敢碰，哪怕自己手指头已经全是脏的。她站住，因为她心乱，想把脑子里那团纷乱的线头理一理。越想，她越看清厄尔福沃德先生是个多么古怪、多么厉害、多么无情的人。她敬他，懂他，怜他，可又怕他。这座宅子连同它披露的他的日常起居，把此人的性子摆得明明白白，无可误会；可这份性子在他的容貌举止里却寻不出半点线索。她何止是怕——她是吓着了。饶是如此，这份惊怖——它已够得上惊怖——偏偏又迷住了她。她小心翼翼地下楼，一步一猶豫。……下到底层楼梯脚，她带着新的惊慌，听见了厄尔福沃德先生本人那温润和软的嗓音。他正在巢穴里同一位顾客交谈。
「我溜出去。」她声音轻得不能再轻地对正在楼梯边扫地的埃尔西说。埃尔西点点头——像个同谋。可就在这一刻，厄尔福沃德先生同顾客从后屋走了出来，阿尔布太太被堵了个正着。
「我没瞧见您进来。」书商和和气气到了极处。「有什么要我效劳的？」
「哦，谢谢，我只是过来找埃尔西说句话。」
这句临时编出的谎，效果圆满。而埃尔西——克拉肯韦尔头一号实心眼的人——也在女人对男人那桩大业里，用她的沉默为它撑了腰。
「真高兴在这儿见到您。」送走顾客后，厄尔福沃德先生柔声道。「运气不错。我本来动身去豪恩斯迪奇了，路上正巧碰上个熟人，他非要我陪他回来不可。请到这边来。」
他凭那双小眼睛的吸力，把她引进了后屋。有一把椅子上的书已被拂到地上。

===Original===

She sat down. Surely Mr. Earlforward was the most normal being in the world, the mildest, the quietest, the easiest! But the bath, the kitchen, the blankets, the filth, the food, the £40 book, and all those new suits and new shirts! She had never even conceived such an inside of a house! She could hardly credit her senses.
“I’ve wanted to see you in here, in this room,” said Mr. Earlforward in a warm voice. And then no more.
She could not withstand his melting glance. She knew that their intimacy, having developed gradually through weeks, was startlingly on the point of bursting into a new phase. The sense of danger with her, as with nearly all women, was intermittent. The man was in love with her. He was in her hands. What could she not do with him? Could she not accomplish marvels? Could she not tame monsters? And she understood his instincts; she shared them. And he was a rock of defence, shelter, safety!﻿ ﻿… The alternative: solitude, celibacy, spinsterishness, eternal self-defence, eternal misgivings about her security; horrible!
“I must be opening my shop,” she said nervously.
“And I must be getting away again, too,” he said, and put on his hat and began to button his overcoat. Nothing more. But at the door he added: “Maybe I’ll come across and see you tonight, if it isn’t intruding.”
“You’ll be very welcome, I’m sure,” she answered, modestly smiling.
She was no better than a girl, then. She knew she had uttered the deciding word of her fate. She trembled with apprehension and felicity. He was a wonderful man and an enigma. He inspired love and dread. As the day passed her feeling for him became intense. At closing time her ecstatic heart was liquid with acquiescence. And she had, too, a bright, adventurous valour, but shot through with forebodings.

===Chinese===

她坐下了。厄尔福沃德先生分明是天下最正常不过的人，最温良、最安静、最随和！可那浴缸、那厨房、那毯子、那污秽、那饭食、那四十镑的书，还有那一柜子新套装新衬衫！这样的一座宅子内部，她连想都不曾想过！她几乎不敢相信自己的感官。
「我一直想请您到这儿来，到这间屋里坐坐。」厄尔福沃德先生说，嗓音是热的。然后便没了下文。
她抵挡不住他那叫人融化的眼波。她知道，他们这份亲昵经过几个星期渐渐发酵，此刻正惊人地濒临迸入一个新阶段。危险感在她身上——如同在几乎所有女人身上一样——时有时无。这个男人爱着她。他掠在她手心里。她还有什么不能叫他做的？她岂不能成就奇迹？岂不能驯服怪物？何况他的天性她懂；她也共有那些天性。而且他是一块岩石，是防卫、庇护、安稳！……另一条路呢：孤寂，独身，老处女的命，永无休止的自我防卫，永无休止地对自身安全的扇咕；可怕！
「我得回去开铺门了。」她紧张地说。
「我也得再动身了。」他说着，戴上帽子，开始扣大衣扣子。再没有别的话。可到了门口他又添一句：「今儿晚上，若不嫌打扰，我兴许过来看看您。」
「您只管来，一定受欢迎。」她答道，笑得矜持。
这么说，她同个小姑娘也差不了多少。她知道自己吐出了决定命运的那一个字。她又惶又喜，微微发着抖。他是个奇妙的人，一个谜。他激起爱，也激起惧。这一天过去，她对他的情感愈燃愈烈。到打烊时分，她那颗出神的心已顺化成一泓柔水。她也不乏一股明亮的、敢闯的胆气——只是那胆气里，缕缕缠着不祥的预感。

## II After Breakfast / 二 早饭之后
===Original===

By chance Violet went down into the shop just after the first-post delivery and just before Henry came. She was always later in the shop on Monday mornings than on other mornings because on that day she prepared the breakfast herself and also attended personally to other “little matters,” as she called them. Henry had already been into the shop, for such blinds as there were had been drawn up, and he had replenished the bookstand, but too soon for the letters. She noticed the accumulation of dirt in the shop, very gradual, but resistless. Although the two women cleaned the shop, and, indeed, the whole establishment, section by section, with a most regular periodicity, they could not get over the surface fast enough to cope with the unceasing deposit of dirt. And they could not cope at all with, for instance, the grime on the ceiling; to brush the ceiling made it worse. In Henry’s eyes, however, the shop was as clean as on the wedding night, and he was as content with it as then; he deprecated his wife’s lamentations about its condition. Certainly no one could deny that it still was cleaner than before her advent, and anyhow he could never again have tolerated another vacuum-cleaning, with its absurd costliness; he knew the limits of his capacity for suffering.
Violet unlocked the door and let in the morn, and shivered at the tonic. This act of opening the shop-door, though having picked up the milk she at once closed the door again, seemed to mark another stage in the process which Elsie had begun more than two hours earlier; it broke the spell of night by letting in not only the morn but dailiness. She gathered the envelopes together from the floor, and noticed one with a halfpenny stamp, which she immediately opened﻿—furtively. Yes, it was the gas bill for the September quarter, the quarter which ought to be the lightest of the year. And was not! She deciphered the dread total; it affected her like an accusation of crime, like an impeachment for treason. She felt guilty, yet she had done her utmost to “keep the gas down.” What would Henry say? She dared not let him see it.﻿ ﻿… And the electricity bill to follow it in a few days!﻿ ﻿… Unquestionably Elsie was wasteful. They were all alike, servants were, and even Elsie was not an exception.
At that moment Henry limped down the stairs. Violet hid the bill and envelope in the pocket of her pinafore-apron.

===Chinese===

碰巧，薇奥莱特在头一班邮差投递之后、亨利下来之前，先到了店堂。逢星期一早晨，她到店堂总比别的早晨晚，因为那天她要亲自备早饭，还要亲自打理她所谓的另外几桩「小事」。亨利已经来过店堂——那几面仅有的窗帘已经拉起，书架也补过了书——只是来得太早，没赶上信。她注意到店堂里积攒的尘垢，那积攒极缓，却势不可当。两个女人虽按部就班、周期精准地把店堂、其实是把整座宅子分区包干地打扫，却追不上那无休无止的落尘——擦过一遍表面的速度总赶不上尘垢沉降的速度。而有些地方她们根本无从下手，比如天花板上的乌垢；一刷，反而更糟。可在亨利眼里，店堂还同新婚之夜一样干净，他也同那时一样心满意足；妻子为店容发的悲叹，他不以为然。当然，谁也不能否认，比起她进门之前，店堂眼下确实干净些；而且无论如何，再来一回吸尘器大扫除、花那笔荒唐的钱，他是说什么也受不了的；他清楚自己受苦的极限。
薇奥莱特开了店门，把清晨放了进来，被这剂提神药激得一哆嗦。开拒门这个动作——虽然拾起奶罐后她又立刻关上了门——似乎标志着一个新阶段，接续了埃尔西两个多钟头前开始的那一进程；它放进来的不只是清晨，还有日常，一并破了夜的魔咒。她把信封从地上拢起来，注意到一枚贴半便士邮票的，立刻拆开了——鬼鬼祟祟地。果然，是九月份那一季的煤气账单——那一季本该是一年里最省的。偏偏不是！她辨读出那个可怕的总额；它落在她身上，像一桩罪行的指控，像一桩叛国的弹劾。她自觉有罪，可她已经竭尽所能地「省煤气」了。亨利会怎么说？她不敢给他看。……过几天电费单还要接踵而来！……毫无疑问，埃尔西太糟蹋了。仆人全是一个样，连埃尔西也不例外。
正在这时，亨利一瘸一拐地下了楼。薇奥莱特把账单连同信封塞进了围裙的口袋。

===Original===

“Here are the letters,” she said, seizing the little milk-can and moving forward to meet him. “Just put a match to the stove, will you? I’m late.”
She went on towards the stairs.
“We surely shan’t want the stove today,” he stopped her. “We haven’t needed it yet. It’s going to be a beautiful day.”
She had had the fire laid in the stove more than a week ago, perceiving, with her insight into human nature, that a fire laid is already half lighted.
“That’s all very well for you﻿—for you to talk like that,” she laughed, hiding her disquiet with devilish duplicity under a display of affectionate banter. “You’re going out, but *I* have to keep shop.”
He was dashed.
“Well, you’ll see later on. I won’t light it now, at any rate. You’ll see later on. Of course you must use your own judgment, my dear,” he added, courteously, judicial, splendidly fair.
“Elsie,” said Violet, peeping into the bathroom on her way upstairs. “Do you really need that geyser full on all the time?” She spoke with nervous exasperation.
“Well, ’m﻿—”
“I don’t know what your master will say when he sees the gas-bill that’s come in this very moment. I really don’t. I daren’t show it him.” She warningly produced the impeachment.
“Well, ’m, I must make the water hot.”
“Yes, I know. But please do be as careful as you can.”
“Well, ’m, I’ve nearly finished.” And Elsie dramatically turned off the gas-tap of the geyser.
The gloomy bathroom was like a tropic, and the heat very damp. Linen hung sodden and heavy along the line. The panes of the open window were obscured by steam. The walls trickled with condensed steam. And Elsie’s face and arms were like bedewed beetroot. But to Violet the excessive warmth was very pleasant.
“You didn’t have any tea this morning,” said she, for she had noticed that nobody had been into the kitchen before herself.
“No, ’m. It’s no use. If I’m to get through with my work Monday mornings I can’t waste my time getting my tea. And that’s all about it, ’m.”
Elsie, her brow puckered, seemed to be actually accusing her mistress of trying to tempt her from the path of virtue. The contract between employers and employed in that house had long since passed, so far as the employed was concerned, far beyond the plane of the commercial.

===Chinese===

「信在这儿。」她说，一把提起小奶罐，迎着他走上前去。「劳驾，给炉子点个火，好不好？我今晚了。」
她径自朝楼梯走去。
「今天咱们肯定用不着炉子。」他拦住她。「到现在还没用上呢。今天会是个大晴天。」
一个多星期前她就把炉里的柴炭摆好了——凭着她对人性的洞见：火摆好了，就等于点着了一半。
「你倒是说得好——说得好听。」她笑了，拿一层恩爱打趣作幌子，把心下的不安藏得滴水不漏、狡猾透顶。「你是要出门的人，*我*可得守着铺子。」
他蔫了。
「好啦，待会儿你瞧着办。反正这会儿我不点。待会儿再说。当然，亲爱的，你务必自己拿主意。」他补上一句，彬彬有礼，一派法官风度，公道得无懈可击。
「埃尔西，」上楼途中，薇奥莱特朝浴室里掠了一眼。「那热水器你真需要一直开足吗？」她说话带着神经质的火气。
「这个，太太——」
「你东家等会儿看到这煤气账单——这会儿刚送到的——会说什么，我可不知道。真不知道。我都不敢给他看。」她亮出那份弹劾状，以示警告。
「可是太太，我总得把水烧热呀。」
「是，我知道。可请你千万尽量省着点。」
「好的，太太，我快弄完了。」埃尔西说着，极有戏剧效果地拧上了热水器的煤气阀。
阴暗的浴室像个热带，热得潮乎乎的。晾衣绳上挂着的布件湿沉沉地垂着。敞开的窗玻璃蒙满了蒸汽。墙上淌着凝成的水珠。埃尔西的脸蛋和胳膊活像淋了露水的红甜菜。可对薇奥莱特来说，这份过度温暖舒服极了。
「你今儿早上没喝茶。」她说——她早注意到，在她之前没人进过厨房。
「没有，太太。喝了也白喝。要想星期一早上把活儿干完，我就不能把工夫耗在喝茶上。就这么回事，太太。」
埃尔西眉头一皱，倒像是当真在怪主母诱她偏离勤勉的正道。在这座宅子里，就受雇的一方而言，那份雇佣契约早就远远越出了做买卖的层面。

===Original===

The employers gave £20 a year; the employed gave all her existence, faculties, energy; and gave them with passion, without reserve open or secret, without reason, sublimely.
“It’s her affair,” muttered Violet as she mounted to the kitchen to finish preparing breakfast. “It’s her affair. If she chooses to work two hours on a Monday morning on an empty stomach, I can’t help it.” And there followed a shamed little thought: “It saves the gas.”
When the breakfast tray was ready she slipped off her blue apron. At the bedroom door she set the tray down on the floor and went into the bedroom to put on the mantle which she had already worn that morning as a seamstress in bed. Before taking the tray again she called out to Elsie:
“Your breakfast’s all ready for you, Elsie.”
Mr. Earlforward was waiting for her at the dining room table. He wore his overcoat. In this manner, at his instigation, they proved on chilly mornings that they could ignore the outrageous exactions of coal trusts and striking colliers.
“What’s that?” demanded Henry with well-acted indifference as he observed an unusual object on the tray.
“It’s a boiled egg. It’s for you.”
“But I don’t want an egg. I never eat eggs.”
“But I want you to eat this one.” She smiled, cajolingly.
Useless! She was asking too much. He would not eat it.
“It’ll be wasted if you don’t.”
It might be; but he would not be the one to waste it. He calmly ate his bread and margarine, and drank his tea.
“I do think it’s too bad of you, Harry. You’re wasting away,” she protested in a half-broken voice, and added with still more emotion, daringly, defiantly: “And what’s the use of a husband who doesn’t eat enough, I should like to know?”
A fearful silence. Thunder seemed to rumble menacingly round the horizon; nature itself cowered. Henry blushed slightly, pulling at his beard. Then his voice, quiet, bland, soothing, sweet, inexorable:
“Up to thirty, eat as much as you can. After thirty, as much as you want. After fifty, as little as you can do with.”
“But you aren’t fifty!”
“No. But I eat as much as I want. I’m the only judge of how much I want. We’re all different. My health is quite good.”
“You’re thinner.”
“I was getting stout.”
“I prefer you to *be* a bit stout﻿—much. It’s a good sign in a man.”
“Question of taste,” he said with a humorous, affectionate glance at her.
“Oh, Harry!” she exclaimed violently. “You’re a funny man.”

===Chinese===

雇主一年出二十镑；受雇的一方交出的却是她全部的生存、全部的心力、全部的气力；交得满腔热忱，不留一手——明的暗的都不留，没有道理，轰轰烈烈。
「她自己的事，」薇奥莱特上楼到厨房去备早饭，一路咕哝，「她自己的事。她非要饿着肚子在星期一早上干两个钟头，我有什么法子。」跟着冒出一个叫她脸红的小念头：「省煤气。」
早饭托盘备齐，她褪下蓝围裙。到卧室门口，她把托盘掠在地板上，进卧室去穿上那件晨衣——今天早上她坐在床上做针线时就披过它。重新端起托盘之前，她朝埃尔西喊了一声：
「你的早饭都给你备好了，埃尔西。」
厄尔福沃德先生在餐室桌旁等她。他穿着大衣。照他的主张，每逢寒冷的早晨，他们就以这副装束证明：煤炭托拉斯的横征暴敛、罢工矿工的要挟，他们全都可以置之不理。
「那是什么？」亨利掠见托盘上一件不寻常的物件，用演得很到位的漠然发问。
「煮鸡蛋。给你的。」
「可我不要鸡蛋。我从来不吃鸡蛋。」
「可我要你把这只吃了。」她笑着哄他。
没用！她要得太多了。他不吃。
「你不吃就糟蹋了。」
糟蹋也未必；反正糟蹋的不会是他。他安安静静吃他的面包人造黄油，喝他的茶。
「你这样真是太过分了，哈里。你一天天消下去。」她半哽着嗓子抗议，又添上一句，情绪更激动，又大胆又挑衅：「倒要请教，吃不饱的丈夫有什么用处？」
一阵骇人的寂静。雷声仿佛在天边隐隐滚着威胁；连大自然都缩了起来。亨利微微红了脸，捻着胡须。随后他开了口，平静、温润、安抚、甜美，且不容更改：
「三十以前，能吃多少吃多少。三十以后，想吃多少吃多少。五十以后，能少吃就少吃。」
「可你还不到五十！」
「没到。可我按想吃的量吃。想吃多少，只有我一个裁判。人人各不相同。我的健康好得很。」
「你瘦了。」
「我原先在发福。」
「我倒宁愿你*发*点福——发得足足的。男人发福是吉相。」
「口味问题。」他说着，诙谐而恩爱地看了她一眼。
「哦，哈里！」她激动地喊。「你这个人真逗。」

===Original===

Then she laughed.
The storm had dissipated itself, save in Violet’s heart. She knew by instinct, by intuition, beyond any doubt, that Henry deprived himself in order to lessen the cost of housekeeping﻿—and this although by agreement she paid half the cost out of her separate income! The fact was, Henry was just as jealous of her income as of his own. She trembled for the future. Then for safety, for relief, she yielded to him in her heart; she trusted; her hope was in the extraordinary strength of his character.
Mr. Earlforward ate little, but he would seldom hurry over a meal. At breakfast he would drink several cups of tea, each succeeding one weaker and colder than the last, and would dally at some length with each. He was neither idle nor unconscientious about his work; all that could be charged against him was leisureliness and a disinclination to begin; no urgency would quicken him, because he was seriously convinced that he would get through all right; as a rule, his conviction was justified; he did get through all right, and even when he didn’t nothing grave seemed to result. He loved to pick his teeth, even after a meal which was no meal. One of the graces of the table was a little wineglass containing toothpicks; he fashioned these instruments himself out of spent matches. He would calmly and reflectively pick his teeth while trains left stations without him and bargains escaped him. Violet, actuated by both duty and desire, would sit with him at meals until he finally nerved himself to the great decision of leaving the table and passing on to the next matter; but as she never picked her teeth before her public, which was himself, she grew openly restive sometimes. Not, however, this morning. No, this morning she would not even say: “I know you’re never late, dear, but﻿—”
When they did arrive in the shop Elsie, having had her breakfast and changed her apron, had already formally opened the establishment and put the bookstand outside in front of the window. The bookstand, it should be mentioned, could now be moved, fully loaded, by one person with ease, for brilliant Violet had had the idea of taking the castors off the back legs of an old armchair and screwing them on to two of the legs of the bookstand, so that you had merely to raise one end of the thing and it slid about as smoothly as a perambulator.

===Chinese===

说着她笑起来。
风暴散了——只在薇奥莱特心里没散。她凭本能、凭直觉、不容置疑地知道：亨利克扣自己，为的是压下持家的开销——尽管依约一半开销由她从自己的私产里付！实情是，亨利守她的钱，同守他自己的钱一样紧。她为将来发抖。随后，为了安稳，为了松口气，她在心里向他让了步；她信他；她的指望，寄托在他性格里那股非凡的坚劲上。
厄尔福沃德先生吃得少，可他极少赶着一顿饭。吃早饭时他要喝上几杯茶，一杯比一杯淡、一杯比一杯凉，每杯都要细细消磨一阵。他干活既不偷懒也不敷衍；能指摘他的只有一点：慢性子，外加不肯开工；再急的事也催不动他，因为他一本正经地笃信自己总能办妥；而通常这笃信也应验；他确实总能办妥，即便没办妥，好像也不出什么大乱子。他爱剔牙，哪怕刚吃完一顿算不上饭的饭。桌上的一件雅设是一只盛牙签的小酒杯；那些牙签是他拿烧过的火柴棍亲手做的。火车丢下他出站、便宜买卖从他手边溜走，他都能心平气和、若有所思地剔着牙。薇奥莱特出于本分也出于情意，每顿饭都陪他坐着，直坐到他终于鼓足勇气作出那个重大决定——离席、转入下一桩事；可她当着她的观众——也就是他——是从不剔牙的，于是有时便公然坐不住。不过今早没有。没有，今早她连这句都不说：「我知道你从不误事，亲爱的，不过——」
等他们两人到店堂时，埃尔西已吃过早饭、换了围裙，正式开了铺门，把书架搬到橱窗外安好了。书架如今——这里该补一句——装满了书一个人也能轻松挪动，因为聪明的薇奥莱特出了个主意，把一把旧扶手椅后腿上的小轮子卸下来，装到书架的两条腿上；这么一来，只需抬起一头，它便滑来滑去，顺溜得像辆婴儿车。

===Original===

Do not despise such achievements of the human brain; such achievements constituted important events in the domestic history of the T. T. Riceyman firm; this one filled Violet with exultation, Henry with pride in his wife, and Elsie with wondering admiration; Elsie never moved the bookstand without glee in the ingenious effectiveness of the contrivance.
Violet, despite the chill, had removed her mantle. She could not possibly wear it in the shop, whatever the temperature, because to do so would be to admit to customers that the shop was cold. Nor would she give an order to light the stove; nor would she have the stove lighted when the master had gone forth on his ways; after the stifled scene at breakfast she must act delicately; moreover, she contemplated a further dangerous, desperate move which might be prejudiced if she availed herself of Henry’s authorization to use her own judgment in regard to the stove. So she acquired warmth by helping Elsie with the cleaning and arranging of the shop for the day. The work was done with rapidity.﻿ ﻿… Customers might now enter without shaming the management. An age had passed since Elsie, preceding the dawn, arose to turn night into day. Looking at it none could suppose that the shop had ever been sheeted and asleep, or that a little milk-can was but recently squatting at the foot of its locked door. Mysterious magic of a daily ritual, unperceived by the priest and priestesses!
Mr. Earlforward was writing out the tail-end of a long bill in the office. He could not use his antique typewriter for bills, because it would not tabulate satisfactorily. He wore his new eyeglasses, memorial of Violet’s sole victory over him. She had been forced to make him a present of the eyeglasses, true; but he did wear them.
“My dear,” he summoned her in a rather low voice, and she hastened to him, duster in hand. “Here’s this bill for Mr. Bauersch; £148 18s.” He blotted the bill with some old blotting-paper which spread more ink than it absorbed. “And here’s the stamp. I haven’t put it on in case there’s any hitch. I asked him if he’d mind paying in cash. Of course he’s a very big dealer, but you never know with these New Yorkers, and he’s sailing tomorrow, and I’ve not done any business with him before. He said he wouldn’t mind at all.”
“I should hope not, indeed!”

===Chinese===

别小看人脑的这类成就；在 T.T.莱斯门这爿商号的家政史上，这类成就便是重大事件；这一件让薇奥莱特心花怒放，让亨利为妻子骄傲，让埃尔西叹服不已；埃尔西每回挪那书架，都要为这装置的巧妙灵便欢喜一阵。
薇奥莱特不顾寒意，把晨衣脱了。在店堂里她说什么也不能穿它，无论气温几何——穿了，便等于向顾客承认店里冷。她也不肯下令生炉子；主人出门之后，她也不肯让人把炉子生上；早饭后那场撔出的口角之后，她行事须得细腻；何况她正盘算着下一步危险的孤注一掷，若先动用了亨利「炉子由你自行裁夺」的授权，那步棋便要走坏。于是她靠帮埃尔西做当日店堂的清洁整理来取暖。活干得飞快。……如今顾客进来，不至于叫管店的难堪了。自埃尔西赶在天光之前起身、把黑夜翻成白昼，仿佛已过去了一个纪元。看这店堂，谁也想不起它曾蒙着布沉睡过，也想不起一只小奶罐方才还蹲在落锁的拒门口。每日一度的仪式自有神秘的法力——行礼的祭司和女祭司们自己却浑然不觉！
厄尔福沃德先生正在账房里誊一份长账单的尾巴。开账单他没法用那台老古董打字机——它列表总列不利索。他戴着新配的眼镜，那是薇奥莱特对他唯一一场胜利的纪念碑。这副眼镜确是她被迫当礼物送给他的，不假；可他到底戴了。
「亲爱的。」他压低嗓子唤她；她掸子还拿在手里，赶紧过去。「这是鲍尔施（Bauersch）先生的账单，一百四十八镑十八先令。」他用几张旧吸墨纸吸账单——那纸洇出去的墨比吸掉的还多。「邮票在这儿。我没贴上，怕万一有反复。我问他介不介意付现款。他当然是个大客商，可这些纽约人你摸不透，他明天就上船，我同他又没做过生意。他说一点也不介意。」
「他要介意才没天理呢！」

===Original===

said Violet, who, nevertheless, was well aware that the master had asked for cash, not from any lack of confidence in the great Bauersch, but because he had a powerful preference for cash; the sight of a cheque did not rouse Henry’s imagination.
“It’s all ready,” said Henry, pointing to two full packing-cases in front of his desk.
“But are we to nail them up, or what?”
“I haven’t fastened them. He might want to run through them with the bill.”
“Yes,” agreed Violet, who nevertheless was well aware that the master had not fastened them because he had postponed fastening them till too late.
“He’ll take them away in a car; probably have them re-packed with his other purchases. I hear he’s bought over twenty thousand pounds’ worth of stuff in London these last three weeks.”
“Oh, my!”
“And you can put the money in your safe till I get back.”
Henry stood up, took his hat from the top knob of the grandfather’s clock, and buttoned his overcoat. He was going to a book auction at Chingby’s historic salerooms in Fetter Lane. For years he had not attended auctions, for he could never leave the shop for the best part of a day; he had to be content with short visits to ragged sub-dealers in Whitechapel and Shoreditch, and with such offers of “parcels” as came to him uninvited. He always bought cheap or not at all; but he would sell cheap, with very rare exceptions. If he picked up a first edition worth a pound for two shillings he would sell it for five shillings. Thus he had acquired a valuable reputation for bargains. He was shrewd enough﻿—shrewder than most﻿—and ready to part with money in exchange for stock. Indeed, his tendency was to overstock his shop. Violet’s instinct for tidiness and order had combated this tendency, whose dangers he candidly admitted. He had applied the brake to buying. No longer was the staircase embarrassed with heroic and perfect girls in paper dust-jackets! And save in the shop and the office all floors had been cleared of books. A few hundred volumes, in calculated and admired disorder, still encumbered the ground-floor and the lower steps of the staircase, to the end explained by the master to his wife on the morrow of the honeymoon. Stock was now getting a little low, and the master went to certain sales with his wife’s full encouragement. He was an autocrat, but where is the autocrat who can escape influence?

===Chinese===

薇奥莱特说。可她心里明镜似的：东家要现款，不是对鲍尔施大人有什么不放心，而是他对现款有一股强烈的偏爱；支票的模样，唤不起亨利的想象力。
「都齐了。」亨利指着书桌前两只装满的货箱说。
「可咱们要不要把它们钉上，还是怎么的？」
「我没封。他兴许要对着账单翻验一遍。」
「是。」薇奥莱特应道。可她心里同样明镜似的：东家没封它们，是因为拖来拖去，拖过了头。
「他会开车来拉走；多半要同他买的别的东西重新打包。听说这三个星期，他在伦敦收了价值两万多镑的货。」
「老天爷！」
「钱你先收进你的保险柜，等我回来。」
亨利站起身，从落地大钟的顶球上取下帽子，扣上大衣。他要去费特巷（Fetter Lane）钦比（Chingby）老拍卖行参加一场图书拍卖。多少年来他没进过拍卖场了——他从来离不开铺子大半天；他只得满足于去白教堂和肖尔迪奇找那些寒酸的二级书商小转一圈，再等着人家不请自来的「成包」报价。他买货向来掠便宜，不便宜宁可不买；卖货倒肯贱卖，极少例外。两先令收进一册值一镑的初版，他会五先令出手。就这样，他挣下了一块值钱的金字招牌：到他这儿能捡漏。他精明——比大多数人都精明——也舍得掏钱换货。老实说，他的毛病是把铺子堆得太满。薇奥莱特爱整洁讲条理的天性一直在同这个毛病作斗争，这毛病的危险他自己也直言不讳。他给进货踩了刹车。楼梯上再不见那些裹着纸书衣、又英勇又完美的姑娘们碍手碍脚了！除店堂和账房，各层楼的地板都已清空了书。只有几百册书——乱得有心计、乱得叫人叹赏——仍堆在一层和楼梯下段，其用意，主人在蜜月次日对妻子讲明过。眼下存货有点薄了，主人去参加几场拍卖，得了太太的全副鼓励。他是个独裁者，可天底下哪个独裁者逃得过潜移默化？

===Original===

“Now do take care of yourself, darling,” Violet murmured, almost in a whisper. “And if you go to that A.B.C. shop be sure to order some cold beef. What does it matter if you do miss a few lots?”
“I’ll see.”
They parted at the shop-door on a note of hard, cheerful indifference: note struck for the sake of the proprieties of a place of business﻿—and utterly false. For Henry loved his wife to worry about him, and Violet’s soul was heavy with apprehensions. She saw herself helpless in a situation growing ever more formidable.

===Chinese===

「千万保重自己，亲爱的。」薇奥莱特几乎耳语般地说。「要是去那家 A.B.C. 连锁店，一定叫份冷牛肉。错过几件拍品又有什么要紧？」
「再说吧。」
他们在店门口分手，分手的调子硬邦邦、喜洋洋、满不在乎：那是做买卖的地方讲究的体面——全然是假的。因为亨利巴不得妻子为他操心，而薇奥莱特的心沉甸甸压满了忧惧。她眼看自己在一步比一步更凶险的局面里，束手无策。

## IV Afternoon / 四 午后
===Original===

When Henry came home, limping, taciturn and absorbed, in the afternoon, Violet examined him carefully with her glance, and, asking no questions, gave him the written list of the day’s transactions, which he always wanted, and which today was quite a good one. He, on his part, asked no questions either﻿—he said not an inquiring word even about the visit of Mr. Bauersch; the name and the sum noted on the list sufficed his curiosity for the moment. (Out of compassion for his fatigue, Violet said not a word about his trickery in the matter of the removal of Mr. Bauersch’s books.) After a sale he would sit down to his desk and study the catalogue marked with his purchases, and he would transfer the details into a special book; he must do this before anything else. Violet went upstairs, leaving him alone in the office to guard the shop.
She went upstairs to the kitchen and to her conspiracies and to the secret half of her double life which had recently commenced. Although apparently she had accomplished little in the way of modifying the daily routine of the establishment and household, that little amounted spiritually to a great deal. And it had been done almost without increased expense﻿—save for gas. Her achievement generally was symbolized and figured in the abolition of the thermos flask from which Henry was used to take his tea, made many hours earlier when the gas was “going.” The abolition of the thermos flask had been an event in the domestic annals. (Henry afterwards sold the contrivance for half a crown.) Violet would have tea set on the table in the dining room; she would have fresh tea; she refused to drink thermos-preserved tea; she would have plates and bread and margarine on the table. And, considering that tea﻿—now served immediately on the closing of the shop﻿—was the last meal or snack of the day in that abode, none could fairly accuse her of innovating in an extravagant manner. Still, the disappearance of the thermos flask was regarded by everybody in the house as the crown of a sort of revolution. Such was the force of the individuality of Mr. Earlforward, who had scarcely complained, scarcely argued, scarcely protested! He had opposed simply his quiet blandness and had yielded﻿—and the revolutionary yet marvelled at her own courage and her success, and had a sensation akin to being out of breath.

===Chinese===

下午，亨利回到家——一瘤一拐，寡言，心事重重。薇奥莱特拿眼色细细察看他，不发一问，只把当日交易的流水单递给他——那是他每回必看的，今天这一张业绩相当不错。他呢，也不问什么——连鲍尔施先生来过的事也没探一句；单子上记的名字和数目，暂时够喂饱他的好奇心了。（出于对他劳顿的怜惜，搬走鲍尔施那些书时他耍的那点花招，薇奥莱特一字未提。）做完一桩买卖，他总要坐到书桌前，翻看标了他所购之物的拍卖册，把细目誊进一本专册；这件事必须先于一切。薇奥莱特上了楼，把他独自留在账房里看店。
她上楼去厨房，去进行她的密谋，去会她那新近开始的二重生活里见不得人的一半。表面上，她在这宅子这家务的日常运转上改动无几；可就精神而论，那寥寥几笔抵得上许多。而且几乎没多花钱——煤气除外。她的功业，大体上可以由保温瓶的废除来象征和代表：从前亨利的茶总在几个钟头前煤气「正烧着」时沏好，再从保温瓶里倒出来喝。废除保温瓶，在家宅史册上是一桩大事。（事后亨利把那玩意儿卖了两先令六便士。）薇奥莱特要把茶摆在餐室桌上；要现沏的茶；隔瓶温着的茶她拒不上口；桌上要有盘子、面包和人造黄油。再说，这顿茶——如今铺门一关即刻开饭——已是这座宅邸一天里最后一顿正餐兼点心；谁也不能公道地指控她革新得奢侈。饶是如此，保温瓶的消失，在宅中每个人的眼里，仍算得一场小小革命加了冕。厄尔福沃德先生的个人力量就有这般大——而他几乎没抱怨过，几乎没争辩过，几乎没抗议过！他只拿他那一份安静的温润对抗，然后就让步了——以致这位革命家至今还惊讶于自己的胆量和成功，还带着一种近乎透不过气的眩晕。

===Original===
