Of course to keep the cash thus was to lose interest, but you couldn’t have it both ways. And the cash was so beautiful to behold.

===Chinese===

那一小堆一小堆的纸币银币，仿佛在控诉某人刑事级别的疏忽，几乎是在邀请共产主义来掀翻社会结构。夫妻二人仍被那桩共产凶案——共产分子离莱斯门台阶这么近（共产分子自然是杀人犯——凡不幸同他们意见相左的人，他们一律杀掉）——和埃尔西那些恶交游重重地震着；至于薇奥莱特，她又被更多的东西激荡着：她悟出亨利买报、布告新闻那番蓄意的戏剧性；悟出这个神秘男人守时待机的功力；还有他在炉火与钞票两桩馈赠上的慷慨；还有他深情的拥抱——这一切，都作用在牛排事件那场火的余烬上。说真的，薇奥莱特——这位精明、鲜亮、精力充沛、敢作敢为的世故女子——正处在一种颤栗而纷乱的情绪里，其烈度连她自己都几乎不曾意识到。亨利掏出保险柜钥匙时，她出奇地松了一口气；她那双发亮的眼睛仿佛在说：「这钱在桌上掠得太久了。快藏起来，快！看在老天份上，一刻也别再耽搁，藏稳它！」
开了保险柜，亨利抽出两只抽屉（他的柜比薇奥莱特的大得多，四屉）掠在桌上。一只装满一镑券，另一只装满十先令券，张张都同新的一样。脏票子他从不过手几天，通常收进当天就能换出一张干净的。装国库券那只抽屉底，躺着一只大页八开的亚麻信封——早年挂号邮寄来的。它鼓胀着钞票。他把鲍尔施先生那一沓漂亮的票子硬塞了进去。他慢条斯理、一丝不苟地料理余下的钱；薇奥莱特猜着这两屉现款共有多少。几百，或者几千镑；她估不出。一幅奇妙而安人心神的景象。她从前见过，但没在这样庄严的场合、也没见得这样全。它安了她的心，抵了共产主义。那一窖紧紧掠住，共产分子到头来能拿你怎样？当然，现金这样存着，利息就没有了；可鱼与熊掌不可兼得。何况现金这样好看。

===Original===

﻿ ﻿… Stocks! Dead flesh! Bodily desires, appetites!﻿ ﻿… Negligible! This lovely cash satisfied the soul. Ah, how she admired Henry! How she shared his deepest instincts! How she would follow his example! How right he was﻿—always!
He said suddenly, but with admirable calm:
“Of course if things do come to the worst, as they certainly will, in my opinion, all this will be worth nothing at all!” “This” was the contents of the two drawers. “Nothing. Or just as much as a Russian rouble. If some of those fellows across the road in Great Warner Street get their way a five-pound note won’t buy a loaf of bread. I’m not joking. It’s happened in other countries and it’ll happen here. And the first thing will be the banks closing. And then where will you be, with your gilt-edged securities? Where will you be then? But I’ll tell you one thing that communism and socialism and murder and so on won’t spoil, and it’ll always be good value.”
He took a third drawer out of the safe, lifting it with both hands because of its weight, and put it on the table. It was full of gold sovereigns. Violet had never seen this gold before, nor suspected its existence. She was astounded, frightened, ravished. He must have kept it throughout the war, defying the Government’s appeal to patriots not to hoard. He was a superman, the most mysterious of supermen. And he was a fortress, impregnable.
“Nothing like it!” he said blandly, running his fingers through the upper sovereigns as through water that tinkled with elfin music.
She too ran her fingers through the gold. A unique sensation! He had permitted it to her as a compensation for her silly sufferings in regard to the steak. She looked down, moved.﻿ ﻿… With regret she saw him put the drawers back and close the safe. They stayed a very long time in the office. Henry had clerical work to do, and she helped him, eagerly, in a lowly capacity.﻿ ﻿… The crumpled newspaper was carefully folded. The light was extinguished. They climbed the dark stairs, leaving behind them the shop, with the faint radiance near the window from the gas-lamp. She slipped. She grasped his arm. He knew the stairs far more intimately than she did. On the first landing she exclaimed:
“Now, has that girl fastened the dining room windows? Or hasn’t she?”
She had new fears for the security of the house.

===Chinese===

股票！死的肉身！肉体的欲求、口腹！……不值一顾！这可爱的现款才餍足灵魂。啊，她多钦佩亨利！她多么共有他最深的本能！她要怎样亦步亦趋！他多么正确——永远正确！
他突然开口，却平静得可敬：
「当然，事情若真到了绝地——依我看必然到——这一切就一文不值了！」这一切指那两只抽屉的内容。「一文不值。或者说，同一张俄国卢布差不多。马路对面大华纳街那些家伙若得了势，一张五镑券买不来一条面包。我不是说笑。别国出过的事，这儿也要出。头一件事就是银行关门。到那时你在哪儿？掠着你那些金边证券？到那时你又在哪儿？可我告诉你一样东西，共产主义、社会主义、凶杀暴乱等等，都精蹋不了它；它永远保值。」
他双手从柜里抬出第三只抽屉——因它沉——掠上桌。满满一屉金镑。这批金子薇奥莱特从未见过，连想都没想过它的存在。她惊，她惧，她迷醉。整个大战期间他必是一直藏着它，公然违抗政府恳请爱国者勿囤积的号召。他是超人，超人里最神秘的一位。而且他是一座堡垒，攻不破。
「没有比它更靠得住的！」他温润地说，手指插进上层金镑里，像插进一片叮咚着精灵音乐的活水。
她也把手指插进金子里。独一无二的体验！这是他赏她的，补偿她为牛排受的那些傻罪。她低下头，感动了。……眼看他把抽屉放回、锁上柜门，她怅然若失。他们在账房里又待了很久。亨利有案头的活计，她热切地帮着，居于下位。……揉皱的报纸被细心叠好。灯灭了。他们摸黑上楼，把店堂留在身后——橱窗近旁还浮着煤气灯淡淡的微光。她滑了一下。她抓住他的胳膊。这楼梯他比她稔熟得多。到了第一个楼梯口，她叫起来：
「哎，那丫头关餐室的窗了没有？到底关没关？」
对宅子的安危，她又添了新的忧惧。

===Original===

Not surprising that he had previously breathed no word as to the golden contents of his safe! What a proof of confidence in her that he had let her into the dangerous secret! Suppose that the truth should get about? Burglars! Homicides! (Madame Tussaud’s!) She shut her knowledge up with triple locks in herself. They passed into the dining room, groping. The windows had been duly fastened. There was plenty of light through them. The upper windows of the confectioner’s nearly opposite, her old shop, were blazing as usual with senselessly extravagant illumination. That business would not last long. She had been fortunate to get the last instalment of her money. The purchaser was a middle-aged man with a youngish wife. Fatal combination! Violet had not found him directly through her advertisement in the *News of the World*, but through one of those business-transfer agents who had written to her about the advertisement. How right Henry had been in insisting that she should not pay the agent’s commission until she had received the last instalment of the purchase-money! Henry had told her that most business-transfer agents were quite honest, but that a few weren’t, because it was a calling that could be embraced without any capital and therefore specially tempting to the adventurer. Henry knew all those things.
A tramcar thundered up King’s Cross Road, throwing sparks from its heels and generally glowing with electricity. It was crammed and jammed with humanity﻿—exhausted pleasure-seekers returning home northwards from theatre, music-hall, cinema and restaurant. Pathetic creatures; stupid, misguided, deluded, heedless, improvident﻿—sheltered in no strong fortress, they! Violet thought of the magic gold.
“Come. Come to bed,” she said. “It’s very cold here after the office.”
He obeyed.

===Chinese===

先前他对柜中之金只字不提，何足为奇！竟让她进了这个危险的秘密，这是何等的信任！万一真相传出去呢？盗贼！杀人犯！（杜莎夫人蜡像馆！）她把这桩知情，用三重锁锁进了自己心里。他们摸黑进了餐室。窗已妥当关好。窗外透进充足的光。斜对面那家糖果铺——她从前的铺子——上层窗口照例灯火狂燃，亮得毫无理性。那桩生意长不了。她运气好，尾款已收齐到手。买家是个中年男人，带着个年轻老婆。要命的组合！薇奥莱特不是通过她在《世界新闻报》上的广告直接找到他的，是通过一位就那广告来函的铺面转让捎客。亨利坚持：不收到最后一期货款，就不付捎客佣金——他多么正确！亨利对她说过，铺面转让捎客大多相当诚实，但有几个不老实；因为那是个无需本钱就能入行的行当，对冒险家诱惑格外的大。这些事亨利全知道。
一辆电车轰隆隆碾上国王十字路，脚跟甩着火花，通身电光流转。车里人挤人塞满了人——寻欢寻到精疲力竭的人们，正从剧院、杂耍场、电影院和餐馆往北回家。可怜的生灵；愚蠢、误入歧途、受蒙蔽、不知留心、不知积攒——没有坚固堡垒庇身的，正是他们！薇奥莱特想起了那有魔力的金子。
「来。上床吧。」她说。「账房里待过，这儿冷得很。」
他从命了。

## VI Henry’s Plot / 六 亨利的计谋
===Original===

When Violet awoke the next morning at the appointed time for waking, and heard the familiar muffled sounds of Elsie’s activity, she was tempted to stay in bed; she had not had a good night, and she felt quite disturbingly unwell; indeed, her physical sensations, although not those of acute pain, alarmed her by a certain fundamental quality involving the very basis of her vitality. But she resisted the temptation, apprehensive of the results, on herself and on the household organism, of any change of habit. The upset would be terrible if she failed in her daily role; Henry would maintain his calm, but beneath the calm “what a state he would be in!” She knew him (she said to herself). “I shall be better on my feet, and I shall worry less.” So she arose to the cold room and to the cold water. Henry was quite bland and cheerful, and said that he had slept well. It was his custom to get up as soon as Violet had washed. He did not get up.
“Aren’t you going to get up? I’ve finished here.” She was folding the towel.
“I think I shall stay where I am for a bit,” he announced with tranquillity.
It was just as if he had given her a dizzying blow. This, then, was the beginning of the end. She crossed the room to the bed, and gazed at him aghast.
“Now, Vi!” he admonished her, pulling at his short beard. “Now, Vi!”
There was so much affection, so much loving banter, in his queer tone, that her glance fell before his, as it had not fallen for months. She covered her exposed throat with her cold, damp hands.
“I shall send for the doctor at once,” she announced with vivacity, all her body tingling in sudden energy.
“You’ll do nothing of the sort,” he said. “I’ve told you I’m all right. But I’ll promise you one thing. Next time the medicine-man comes to see you he shall see me as well, if you like.﻿ ﻿… Now”﻿—he changed his tone to the practical﻿—“you can attend to everything in the shop. Surely it can manage without me for a day or two.”
“ ‘A day *or two*’!” she thought. “Is he taking to his bed permanently? Is that it?”
“And I shall save a clean shirt,” he said reflectively.
“But, darling, if you’re all right, why must you stay in bed? Please, please, do be open with me. You never are﻿—if you know what I mean.” She spoke with a plaintive and eager appeal, as it were girlishly.

===Chinese===

第二天早晨，薇奥莱特在既定的起床时分醒来，听见埃尔西活动的熟悉闷响，真想赖在床上；她一夜没睡好，浑身上下说不出的难受，难受得叫人心慌；说真的，她身上的感觉虽不是剧痛，却带着一种根本的质素，牵动她生命力的根基，叫她自己惊心。可她抵住了诱惑——她害怕任何习惯的变动落到她自己、落到这个家庭有机体上的后果。她若有一日演砸了自己的日常角色，那乱子就大了；亨利会端着镇静，可镇静底下「他还不知乱成什么样呢」！她懂他（她对自己说）。「起来走动反倒好些，也少操心。」于是她起身，走进冷屋，就着冷水。亨利满面温润欢愉，说他睡得好。他的习惯是：薇奥莱特一洗漱完便起。他没起。
「你不起床吗？我这儿完事了。」她正叠毛巾。
「我想我就在这儿再躺一阵。」他安然宣布。
这就像给了她一记天旋地转的重击。这么说，末日的开头到了。她穿过屋子走到床边，惊骇地盯着他。
「好了，薇！」他捻着短须告诫她。「好了，薇！」
他那古怪的声调里，情意那么多、恩爱打趣那么多，她的目光在他面前垂了下去——几个月都不曾垂过。她用冰冷潮湿的手掩住裸露的喉咙。
「我这就去请医生。」她宣布，精神一振，浑身发热。
「你什么也不要去做。」他说。「我说过我没事。可我答应你一件事。下回那郎中来瞧你，只要愿意，也让他瞧瞧我。……好了——」他把调门转回实际——「店堂里的事你全照管得来。少我一两天，铺子总还转得动。」
「『一两天』！」她想。「他是要长卧不起了？是这个吗？」
「还能省下一件干净衬衫。」他若有所思地说。
「可是亲爱的，你既没事，为什么非躺在床上？求你，求你，对我敞亮些吧。你从来不敞亮——你懂我的意思。」她的话带着哀恳，带着急切的央求，几乎是少女腔。

===Original===

Her face, with an almost forgotten mobility, showed from moment to moment the varying moods of her emotion; tears hung in her eyes; and she was less than half-dressed. She looked as if she might sob, shriek, and drop in a hysterical paroxysm to the floor.
“Something has to be done about that thief of an Elsie,” Henry very calmly explained. “Of course, I could put a lock on the cage, but that might seem stingy, miserly, and I should be sorry if anybody thought we were that. Besides, she’s a good sort in some ways. She’s got to be frightened; she’s got to be impressed. You send her in to me. You can talk to her yourself as much as you like afterwards, but send her in to me first. I’ll teach her a lesson.”
“How? What are you going to say to her?”
“I shall tell her we’ve had the doctor, and make out I’m very ill indeed. And we’ll see if that won’t shake her up! We’ll see if she’ll keep on picking and stealing after that! That ought to sober her down. And it will, too. Something must be done.”
Violet was amazed at this revelation of his mentality. She had a new source of alarm now. No doubt the plan would work; but what a plan! How *funny*! (She meant morbid.) Could she cross him? Could she deride the plan? She dared not. She dared not trifle with a man in his condition. And the worst was that he might, after all, be only pretending to pretend he was very ill. He might really be very ill.
“Elsie,” she said shortly in the kitchen, “go to your master. He wants to speak to you.”
“Is he in the office already, ’m?”
“No, he isn’t in the office already. He’s in bed. Now run along, do!”
As soon as Elsie was gone, Violet examined the hanging larder. The ravage was appalling. Where in heaven’s name did the girl stow the food? Well might the doctor say that she was well nourished. A good thing if she *was* to be frightened! She deserved it.﻿ ﻿… Ah! Violet did not know which way to turn in the moil of Henry’s illness, Henry’s morbidity, her own unnamed malady, and Elsie’s shocking and incredible vice.
Elsie entered the bedroom with extreme apprehension, as for an afflicting solemnity. She thanked God she had had the wit to remove her working apron. Mr. Earlforward was staring at the ceiling. Nothing of him moved except his eyelids, and he appeared not to notice her presence. She waited, twitching her great, red hands. Violet had seemed like a girl before him.

===Chinese===

她的脸带着一种几乎被遗忘的灵动，一时一时映出情绪的流转；泪珠悬在眼里；她身上衣裳还没穿齐一半。她那样子，仿佛随时会啜泣、尖叫、歇斯底里一阵发作瘫倒在地。
「埃尔西那个小贼，总得治一治。」亨利极平静地解释。「当然，我可以给食橱上把锁；可那显得小气、显得抠，叫人当咱们是那路人，我会难受。再说她有些方面不坏。得吓她一吓；得叫她受点震动。你把她打发进来。回头你自己爱怎么同她谈都行，可先打发她进来。我来教训她一顿。」
「怎么教训？你打算对她说什么？」
「我就告诉她，咱们请过医生了，再装出我病得很重。咱们瞧瞧这一下镇不镇得住她！瞧瞧她往后还偷不偷嘴！这准能叫她收敛。准能。总得有个办法。」
他的心术这样一番亮底，叫薇奥莱特大为骇异。她如今又添了一桩新的惊惧。这计划没有不灵的；可这是怎样一个计划！多么*邪性*！（她指的是病态。）她能违逆他吗？能讥笑这计划吗？她不敢。同他这样状况的男人，她不敢轻慢。而最糟的是：他装作「装病」——装到最后，未必真是装。他可能真的病得很重。
「埃尔西，」她在厨房里简短地说，「去你东家那儿。他有话对你说。」
「他已经在账房了，太太？」
「不，他没在账房。他在床上。快去吧，听话！」
埃尔西一走，薇奥莱特便查验那只挂着的食橱。劫后的惨状骇人。老天爷，这丫头把吃食塞到哪里去了？难怪医生说她营养良好。吓她一吓倒是好事！她活该。……唉！亨利的病、亨利的心病、她自己那叫不出名的毛病、埃尔西那骇人听闻难以置信的恶癖——乱麻一团里，薇奥莱特不知该先顾哪头。
埃尔西满怀惶恐走进卧室，像赴一场降灾的庄严典礼。她感谢上帝，自己幸而灵机一动，解下了干活的那条围裙。厄尔福沃德先生正眼盯着天花板。除了眼皮，他浑身不动，仿佛没察觉她在场。她候着，一双又大又红的手直扭动。方才在他面前，薇奥莱特已像个姑娘。

===Original===

But here was the genuine girl. Elsie’s hard experience of life and disaster fell away from her. She was simple and intimidated. Youthfulness was her chief characteristic as she stood humbly waiting. Her candid youthfulness accused the room of age, decay and distemper.
“Elsie, has Mrs. Earlforward told you anything?”
“No, sir.”
“Listen.” He still did not shift his eyes from the ceiling. “We had the doctor in yesterday afternoon.” Elsie’s heart thumped. Had the doctor betrayed her meddling? “He came to buy a book, and we kept him.” Elsie thought the worst was over. “I’m very ill, Elsie, and I shall probably never get up again. Do you think it’s right of you to go on stealing food as you do, with a dying man in the house?” He spoke very gently.
Elsie gave a sob; she was utterly overwhelmed.
“Now you must go. I can’t do with any fuss, Elsie!” He stopped her at the door. “Do we give you enough to eat? Tell me at once if we don’t.”
“Yes, yes. Quite enough!” Elsie cried, almost in a shriek, hiding her face in her hands. Her condition was so desperate that she had omitted the ceremonial “sir.” The rushing tears ran between her fingers as she escaped. She sat a long time in the kitchen sobbing, sobbing for guilt and sobbing for sorrow at her master’s fate.

===Chinese===

可这儿站着的才是真正的姑娘。埃尔西那一身世路的辛酸与灾殃，簌簌剥落。她变得单纯，变得怯惧。她谦卑侍立时，最主要的特征就是年轻。她那坦白的年轻，控诉着这间屋子的老迈、朽坏与失调。
「埃尔西，厄尔福沃德太太同你说过什么吗？」
「没有，先生。」
「听着。」他的眼仍不离天花板。「昨天下午，咱们请医生进过家门。」埃尔西的心咚咚直撞。医生把她多嘴的事抖出来了？「他来买书，咱们把他留住了。」埃尔西以为最坏的过去了。「我病得很重，埃尔西，多半再也起不来了。家里躺着一个快死的人，你还照旧偷嘴，你觉得对吗？」他说得极温和。
埃尔西呜咽一声；整个人被击垮了。
「现在你走吧。大惊小怪我可受不了，埃尔西！」他在门口拦住她。「咱们给你的吃的够吗？不够就立刻告诉我。」
「够，够。很够了！」埃尔西喊道，几乎是尖叫，双手掩住脸。她的处境狼狈到把例行的「先生」都丢了。夺路而逃时，奔涌的泪水从指缝间直流。她在厨房里坐了很久很久，抽喍着——为罪疚抽喍，也为东家的命运悲恸抽喍。

## III International / 三 国际交易
===Original===

Violet was attending to another customer when Mr. Bauersch came into the shop. She ignored him until she had sped the first customer, who happened also to be “in the trade.” According to Violet’s code, all customers were equal in the sight of the shopkeeper, and although the first customer was shabby and dirty, and carried for his acquisitions a black stuff sack which he slung over his shoulder, Violet would not abate one comma of her code. Nevertheless, while ignoring, she appraised Mr. Bauersch, whom on his previous visits she had only glimpsed once. She was confirmed in her original lightning impression that he bore a resemblance to Henry. He was of about the same age and build; he had the same sort of pointed beard, and the same mild demeanour; and also his suit was of the same kind and colour of cloth as Henry wore on Sundays. But what a different suit from Henry’s! It had a waist. Violet did not like that. Unaware that Mr. Bauersch clothed himself in London, she attributed the waist to the decadent eccentricity of New York. Nor did she like the excessive width of the black ribbon which held Mr. Bauersch’s pince-nez. Nor did she like the boldly exposed striped shirt﻿—(nobody except Violet and Elsie ever saw even the cuffs of Mr. Earlforward’s shirt, to say nothing of the front)﻿—nor the elegant, carefully studied projecting curve of the necktie. In short, Mr. Bauersch failed utterly to match Violet’s ideal of a man of business.
She turned to him at last, as he was strolling about curiously, and greeted him with the hard, falsely genial, horrible smile of the suspicious woman who is not going to be done in the eye in a commercial matter. This was not at all the agreeable Violet of the confectioner’s shop. And the reason for the transformation was that she had a husband to protect, that the prestige and big transactions of the great Bauersch made her nervous, and that Mr. Bauersch was from New York. Violet, I regret to say, had fixed and uncharitable notions about foreigners. Mr. Bauersch acknowledged her greeting with much courtesy, and with no condescension whatever.
“My name’s Bauersch, Mrs. Earlforward,” said he. (Why should he so certainly assume that she was Mrs. Earlforward?)
“Oh, yes!” she murmured, simpering. “You’ve called about the books, I suppose?”

===Chinese===

鲍尔施先生进店时，薇奥莱特正应酬着另一位顾客。她把他掠在一边，直到送走头一位——那位恰巧也「是同行」。按薇奥莱特的信条，在店主眼里天下顾客一律平等；头一位顾客虽然寒酸肮脏，收的书装在一只斜挎肩头的黑布袋里，薇奥莱特的信条也不肯打一个逗号的折扣。话虽掠着，她却把鲍尔施先生掂量了一遍——他前几回来，她只匆匆掠见过一次。她头一回那种闪电式的印象得到了证实：此人同亨利有几分相像。年纪身量相仿；同款的山羊尖须，同样温文的做派；连那身西装，料子同颜色也同亨利礼拜天穿的那套一样。可同亨利那套又是多么不同的西装！它收腰。薇奥莱特不喜欢收腰。她不知道鲍尔施先生的衣裳是在伦敦置办的，便把这腰身归咎于纽约的颓诞怪气。她也不喜欢夹鲍尔施先生夹鼻镜的那条黑缎带——宽得过火；也不喜欢那件条纹衬衫的大胆外露——（除了薇奥莱特和埃尔西，谁也没见过厄尔福沃德先生衬衫的袖口，更不必说前襟）——也不喜欢领带那精心讲究的、漂亮的外凸弧线。总之，鲍尔施先生全然配不上薇奥莱特心中生意人的理想。
等他正好奇地四处踱看时，她终于转向他，迎以一个多疑女人的僵硬、假热络、可怕的笑——那女人打定主意，生意场上休想叫她着了道儿。这同糖果铺里那个可人的薇奥莱特，已判若两人。变脸的缘故：一，她有丈夫要保护；二，鲍尔施大家的威名和大手笔叫她发紧；三，鲍尔施先生是纽约来的。很遗憾，对外国人，薇奥莱特抱着一套刻板而不厚道的成见。鲍尔施先生答她的问候，礼数周全，且毫无居高临下。
「我姓鲍尔施，厄尔福沃德太太。」他说。（他凭什么这般笃定她就是厄尔福沃德太太？）
「哦，是！」她抿嘴一笑，喃喃道。「您是为那些书来的吧，我猜？」

===Original===

Her tone indicated that there was just a chance of his having called about the gas or the weather.
“Yes. Are they all ready for shipping?” (What did he mean by “shipping”? They were ready for him to take away, ready for dispatch.)
She nodded vaguely.
“Those are the cases, no doubt,” said Mr. Bauersch, pointing to the office, and walking into it without invitation.
“People aren’t supposed to come in here,” said Violet, smiling harshly, as she followed him.
He examined the packing of the cases rather negligently, and then turned to the shelves and adjusted his pince-nez.
“Mr. Earlforward left the bill. I don’t know whether you’d like to check the volumes.”
Mr. Bauersch appeared to be a man of few words. In another minute he had paid down the money in Bank Notes and Treasury Notes. Violet counted and temporarily locked the money away in a drawer of the desk. Strange that this reassuring episode did not soften her attitude!
“May I go and explore a little upstairs?” asked Mr. Bauersch, while she was preparing the receipt.
Evidently Henry, as sometimes he did to customers, had given Mr. Bauersch the freedom of the house during Violet’s absence. The house was still very full of books, and free exploration was good for trade; but Violet the house-mistress objected to free exploration.
“I’m afraid I can’t go up with you now,” said she. “I’m all alone in the shop.”
“I quite see.” Mr. Bauersch accepted the rebuff with grace, and turned back again to the shelves, and then to the mounds of books on the floor.
Having receipted the bill, Violet ahemmed in the direction of the absorbed Mr. Bauersch, who ignored the signal. Then two young women entered the shop, and Violet decided to punish Mr. Bauersch by attending to them. They wanted “sevenpennies.” There were no sevenpennies, and Violet spent at least five minutes with them, making a profit of one penny on the sale of a soiled copy of *The Scapegoat*; she displayed no impatience, and continued to chat after the deal was done and finished; she seemed to part from them with lingering pain.
“How much is this?” Mr. Bauersch demanded, somewhat urgently, holding out a volume; he had come into the shop.
The book was a copy of an eighteenth-century Dutch illustrated edition, octavo, of La Fontaine’s Tales. Violet, looking at it, inspected it. She did not know what the book was.

===Chinese===

她的口气暗示：他也未尝没有一星可能是为煤气或天气来的。
「是。都备好待运了吗？」（他这个「运」是什么意思？它们备好了等他提走，备好了待发，如此而已。）
她含含糊糊点了点头。
「想必就是那几只箱子。」鲍尔施先生指着账房说，也不等人请，径自走了进去。
「这儿按说是不让人进的。」薇奥莱特跟着进去，笑得生硬。
他马马虎虎验了验箱子的包装，转身对着书架，扶了扶夹鼻镜。
「厄尔福沃德先生留了账单。您要不要清点一下卷数，我不晓得。」
鲍尔施先生像是个话少的人。不出一分钟，他已把钞票和国库券如数付讫。薇奥莱特点清，先把钱暂锁进书桌抽屉。怪的是，这桩叫人安心的事，竟没能软化她的态度！
「我能上楼稍稍转转吗？」她正备着收据，鲍尔施先生问。
显然，亨利像他有时待客那样，趁薇奥莱特不在，给了鲍尔施先生满宅随便走的权利。宅子里书还堆得很满，随便转转于生意有益；可宅子女主人薇奥莱特，反对随便转转。
「眼下恐怕不能陪您上去。」她说。「店堂里就我一个人。」
「明白。」鲍尔施先生风度十足地受了这一软钉子，又转回书架前，再看地板上一堆堆的书。
账单签讫，薇奥莱特朝浑然忘我的鲍尔施先生那边咳了一声，他只当没听见。这时两位年轻女子进店，薇奥莱特决定惩治鲍尔施先生一下：专心应酬她们去。她们要「七便士书」。七便士书没有；薇奥莱特陪着她们耗了至少五分钟，卖出一本污损的《替罪羊》，赚了一便士；她毫无不耐，交易完结了还接着聊；同她们分手时，简直像难舍难分。
「这本什么价？」鲍尔施先生伸着一册书，语气颇急地发问；他已走进店堂。
那本书是十八世纪荷兰插图本《拉封丹故事集》，八开。薇奥莱特看了看，又细察一番。她不知道这是部什么书。

===Original===

But Henry had taught her some general principles: for instance, that any book printed before 1600 is “worth money,” that any book of verse printed before 1700 is worth money, and that most illustrated books printed before 1800 are worth money. Also she had learnt to read Roman date numerals. Indeed she had left Elsie out of sight in the race for knowledge. The price of the book was marked in cipher, inside the front-cover﻿—ten shillings. In Elsie’s viceroyalty all prices had been marked in plain figures﻿—largely for the convenience of Elsie. But under Violet plain figures were gradually being abolished; there was no need for them, and they were apt to interfere with Violet’s freedom of action in determining prices to suit the look and demeanour of customers.
“A pound,” she answered.
“Put it in, please.” Mr. Bauersch pulled out a Treasury Note. “We won’t haggle. Now I must have these cases sent down to the American Express Company’s at once, please, at once. I’ll have the books checked there. I’ve got a pile of stuff collected there, and they must leave London tonight, sure.”
“Mr. Earlforward told me you would take the cases away with you in your car.”
“Me take them away with me!﻿ ﻿… Well, in the first place, I’ve come in a taxi. And in the second place, I couldn’t put those in a car. And they won’t hold in a taxi either. I’ll be glad if you’ll send them down.”
“I’m very sorry, but I don’t see how I can send them. I haven’t anybody here, as I’ve told you.” She was unhelpful, adamantine.
“Mr. Earlforward isn’t in?” Mr. Bauersch’s tone had begun to roughen in impatience.
“Oh no!” She swept aside such an absurd impossibility. “But I’m sure he understood you were taking them away.” (She perceived, however, that Henry had involved her in this difficulty in order to escape the cost of delivery.)
“Do you know where he is?”
“I couldn’t say exactly; he might be at a sale at Chingby’s.”
“Well, will you mind telephoning to him and saying﻿—”
“We don’t have the telephone here,” she replied, with cold triumph, remembering Henry’s phrase, “those New Yorkers.”
“Well, can you send to a garage and get a van or something for me?”
“I couldn’t unless I went myself.”
“Well, where *is* the nearest garage?”
“I’m sure I couldn’t tell you.”
Using words in a sense in which Violet had never heard them used, Mr. Bauersch dashed out of the shop to speak to his taxi-driver.

===Chinese===

可亨利教过她几条大原则：比如，一六〇〇年以前印的书「都值钱」；一七〇〇年以前印的诗集都值钱；一八〇〇年以前印的插图本多半值钱。她还学会了认罗马数字的纪年。说真的，在求知的赛跑里，她已把埃尔西甩得没了影。那本书的价钱用暗码标在前封里——十先令。埃尔西摄政那阵子，所有价钱都用明码——多半为埃尔西安便。可到了薇奥莱特治下，明码正渐次废止；用不着了，况且它们常常碍手碍脚——妨碍薇奥莱特看客人的相貌做派、随行就市的自由。
「一镑。」她答。
「请一并包上。」鲍尔施先生抽出一张国库券。「我们不还价。现在，这几只箱子必须立刻送到美国运通公司——立刻。我要在那边清点。我有一大批货汇集在那儿，它们今夜必须离开伦敦，一定。」
「厄尔福沃德先生告诉我，您会用汽车把箱子带走。」
「我用汽车带走！……第一，我是坐出租汽车来的。第二，那些箱子轿车装不下。出租汽车也装不下。麻烦您派人送一下吧，感激不尽。」
「非常抱歉，我看不出怎么个送法。这儿一个人手也没有，我告诉过您了。」她寸步不让，铁石心肠。
「厄尔福沃德先生不在？」鲍尔施先生的口气因不耐而渐粗。
「当然不在！」她把这荒唐的不可能挥到一边。「可我拿得准，他原以为您会带走的。」（不过她也看穿了：亨利为躲那笔送货钱，把她拖进了这道难题。）
「您知道他在哪儿吗？」
「说不太准；也许在钦比拍卖场。」
「那么，麻烦您给他打个电话，就说——」
「我们这儿没有电话。」她答，带着冰冷的凯旋——记着亨利那句话：「这些纽约人」。
「那么，能不能到车库替我雇辆货车什么的？」
「办不成，除非我亲自跑一趟。」
「那最近的*车库究竟在哪儿？*」
「我实在说不上来。」
鲍尔施先生甩出一串话——那些字的用法，薇奥莱特闻所未闻——冲出店堂，找他的出租汽车司机去了。

===Original===

He returned in ten minutes. In the meantime Violet had hammered the lids on the two cases. In possession of both the money and the books she had maintained all her tranquillity. Mr. Bauersch had come back with a Ford van in addition to his taxi. He led the driver of the taxi and the driver of the van into the office, and instructed them to remove the cases.
“The receipt, if you please,” he said dryly to Violet, who handed him the receipt, but showed none of the clemency due from a conqueror to the defeated.
Mr. Bauersch moralized (to himself) about English methods.
“Why *do* they hate the sight of a customer?” he asked himself, puzzled. “I’ll never come into this damned store again!” he said to himself.
But he well knew that on his next visit he would come into the damned shop again, because the shop had the goods he wanted, and didn’t care whether he bought them or not. If he could have ruined the shop by never coming into it again he would perhaps have ruined the shop. But it was the shop’s cursed indifference that spiritually beat him and ensured the triumph of the astonishing system.

===Chinese===

十分钟后他回来了。这一向，薇奥莱特已把两只箱盖钉死。钱和书双双在握，她的镇定自始至终未失分毫。鲍尔施先生带着他的出租汽车，外加一辆福特货车回来了。他把出租司机和货车司机领进账房，吩咐他们搬箱子。
「收据，劳驾。」他干巴巴地对薇奥莱特说。她把收据递过去，却不肯显半分胜利者对败军该有的宽仁。
鲍尔施先生（在心里）对英式做派发起感慨。
「他们到底*为什么*一见顾客就恨？」他纳闷地自问。「这该死的铺子，我再也不来了！」他对自己说。
可他心里雪亮：下回再来伦敦，他还是要进这家该死的铺子——因为货在他这儿要什么有什么，而铺子呢，他买不买压根不在乎。倘若「从此不来」能叫铺子垮掉，他没准真叫它垮掉。可偏偏是这铺子那份可恨的漠然，在精神上打败了他，保住了那套惊人体系的凯旋。

## VII Malaria / 七 疟疾
===Original===

“I suppose I must just do the best I can, sir,” said Elsie on the landing outside the bedroom. She smiled timidly, cheerfully and benevolently.
The doctor looked at her, startled. It seemed to him that in some magic way she had vanquished the difficulties of a most formidable situation by merely accepting and facing them. She did not argue about them, complain about them, nor expatiate upon their enormity. She was ready to go on living and working without any fuss from one almost impossible moment to the next. During his career in Clerkenwell Dr. Raste had become a connoisseur of choice examples of practical philosophy, and none better than he could appreciate Elsie’s attitude. That it should have startled him was a genuine tribute to her.
“Yes, that’s about it,” he said nonchalantly, with the cunning of an expert who has seen an undervalued unique piece in an antique shop. “Well, good morning, Elsie. Good morning.”
He was in a hurry; he had half a hundred urgent matters on his professional conscience. What could he do but leave Elsie alone with her ordeal? He could not help her, and she did not need help in this particular work, which was, after all, part of her job at twenty pounds a year and food given and stolen. She was beginning to see the top of his hat as he descended the stairs. The stupid, plump, practical philosopher wanted to call him back for an affair of the very highest importance, and could not open her mouth, because Mr. Earlforward’s desperate plight somehow inhibited her from doing so.
“Doctor!” she exclaimed with a strange shrillness as soon as he had passed from her sight into the shop.
“What now?” demanded Dr. Raste sharply, afraid that his connoisseurship should have been mistaken and she would stampede.
She ran down after him. His gaze indicated danger. He did not mean to have any nonsense.
“I suppose you couldn’t just see Joe for a minute?” she stammered, with a blush. This now faltering creature had a moment earlier been calmly ready to do the best she could in circumstances which would scarcely bear looking at.
“Joe? What Joe?”
“Your old Joe. He’s here, sir. Upstairs. Came last night, sir. He’s very ill. I’m looking after him too. Master doesn’t know.”
“What in God’s name are you talking about, my girl?” said the doctor, moved out of his impassibility.

===Chinese===

「我想，我只能尽力而为了，先生。」埃尔西在卧室外的楼梯口说。她笑了，怯怯的，欢欢喜喜，又和和气气。
医生望着她，吃了一惊。在他看来，她竟以某种魔法，单凭接受和直面，就降服了一个极其凶险的局面里的重重困难。她不同它们争辩，不抱怨，也不铺陈它们何等吓人。她随时准备不声不响地继续生活、继续干活，从一个几乎不可能的时辰，撑到下一个。在克拉肯韦尔行医这些年，拉斯特医生成了实践哲学精品的鉴赏家；埃尔西这副姿态，没有人比他更懂得欣赏。它竟能叫他吃惊——这本身就是对她真正的礼赞。
「嗯，就是这么回事。」他淡漠地说，带着行家的狡黠——那行家在古董铺里瞧见一件被低估的孤品。「好，早安，埃尔西。早安。」
他赶时间；他的职业良心上压着五十桩急事。他还能怎样？只能留埃尔西独自面对她的磨难。他帮不了她；而这桩特殊的活计她也无需人帮——毕竟这也是她那份年俸二十镑、管饭（给的加偷的）的差事的一部分。他下着楼梯，她已只能望见他的帽顶。这个愚蠢、丰腴的实践哲学家，想把他唤回来，为一件天大的要事；可她张不开口——厄尔福沃德先生那走投无路的险境，不知怎么封住了她。
「医生！」他刚从她视线里走进店堂，她就迸出一声古怪的尖喊。
「又怎么了？」拉斯特医生厉声问，唯恐自己这鉴赏看走了眼、她要惊溃。
她追着他跑下楼。他的目光示着警。他不容许任何胡闹。
「您能不能……就看乔一眼，一分钟？」她结结巴巴，红着脸。这个此刻支支吾吾的人，方才还安安静静地预备在一个几乎不敢细看的处境里尽力而为呢。
「乔？哪个乔？」
「您从前的乔。他在这儿，先生。在楼上。昨儿夜里来的，先生。他病得厉害。我也在照看他。东家不知道。」
「你到底在说什么呀，我的姑娘？」医生说，那份不为所动被撼动了。

===Original===

She told him the facts, as though confessing a mortal sin for which she could not expect absolution.
“I really haven’t a minute to spare,” said he, and went upstairs with her to the second-floor.
By the time they got there Elsie had resumed her self-possession.
The doctor, for all his detached and frigid poses, was on occasion capable, like nearly every man, of being as irrational as a woman. On this occasion he was guilty of a perfectly indefensible prejudice against both Elsie and Joe. He had a prejudice against Elsie because he was convinced that had it not been for her affair with Joe, Joe would still have been in his service. And he was prejudiced against Joe because he had suffered much from a whole series of Joe’s successors. For the moment he was quite without a Joe. Also he resented Elsie having a secret sick man in the house﻿—and that man Joe﻿—and demanding so unexpectedly his attention when he was in a hurry and overfatigued by the ills of the people of Clerkenwell. He would have justly contemned such prejudices in another, and especially in, for example, his wife; and it must be admitted he was not the godlike little being he thought he was. Fortunately Joe was in a state which made all equal before him.
“Oh, dear! I do so ache, and I’m thirsty,” the second patient groaned desperately, showing no emotion﻿—surprise, awe or shame﻿—at sight of the doctor and employer whom he had so cruelly wronged by leaving him in the lurch for inadequate reasons originating in mere sentiment. He had been solitary for half an hour and could not bear it. He wanted, and wanted ravenously, something from everybody he saw. The world existed solely to succour him. And certainly he looked very ill, forlorn, and wistfully savage in the miserable bed in the miserable bedroom of the ex-charwoman. He looked quite as ill as Mr. Earlforward, and to Elsie even worse.
“It’s malaria,” said the doctor in a casual tone, after he had gone through the routine of examination. “Temperature, of course. He’ll be better in a few days. I’ve no doubt he had it in France first, but he never told me. When they brought back troops to France from the East, malaria came with them. All the north of France is covered with mosquitoes, and they carry the disease. I’ll send down some quinine. You must feed him on liquids﻿—milk, barley-water, beef-tea, milk-and-soda. Hot water to drink, not cold.

===Chinese===

她把实情说了，像在忏悔一桩不望赦免的死罪。
「我实在一分钟也匀不出。」他说，还是跟她上楼到了顶层。
到得那里，埃尔西已恢复了镇定。
这位医生尽管惯作超然冷淡的姿态，偶尔也同几乎每个男人一样，能同女人一样不讲道理。这一回，他就对埃尔西和乔两个都怀着一种全然站不住的偏见。他偏忌埃尔西，因为他认定：若不是她同乔那一段，乔如今还在他手下。他偏忌乔，则因为乔的一连串后任叫他吃够了苦头。眼下他正压根没有乔。再者，埃尔西竟在宅里私藏病人——藏的还是乔——又这样出其不意地讨他的注意，而他正赶时间、正被克拉肯韦尔众生的病痛累得半死，这也叫他恼。这样的偏见若出在别人身上——尤其出在比如他太太身上——他会义正辞严地鄙薄；必得承认，他并不是他自以为的那尊小神。幸而乔的状态，使一切人在他面前平等了。
「哎哟！疼死我啦，渴死我啦。」第二位病人凄凄地呻吟，见了这位医生兼旧东家，竟无动于衷——不惊，不敬，也不愧——虽说他曾纯凭一点情由，为着些站不住的理由，狠心把东家掠在难处，一走了之。他独处了半个钟头，独处不下去了。他见了谁，就向谁饿狼似的要。这世界的存在，单为救济他一人。而且他瞧着确实病得厉害：躺在那个前打杂女佣寒碜的卧室、寒碜的床上，凄惶落魄，蛮野又可怜。他瞧着同厄尔福沃德先生病得一般重；在埃尔西眼里，还更重。
「疟疾。」例行检查做完，医生随口判道。「发烧，自然。几天就好。我拿得准他先是在法兰西染上的，只是从没告诉我。当年他们把军队从东方调回法国，疟疾就跟着回来了。整个法国北部蚊子铺天盖地，病就是它们带的。我打发人送些奎宁下来。只准喂流食——牛奶、大麦茶、牛肉汁、牛奶兑苏打水。喝的要热水，不要凉的。

===Original===

And you ought to sponge him down twice a day.”
Elsie, listening intently to this mixture of advice and information, could not believe that Joe’s case was not more serious than the doctor’s manner implied. Well implanted in her lay the not groundless conviction that doctors were apt to be much more summary with the sick poor than with the sick rich. And she was revisited by her old sense of this doctor’s harsh indifference. He had not even greeted his former servant, had regarded him simply as he would regard any ordinary number in a panel.
“You won’t have a great deal to do downstairs. In fact, scarcely anything,” the doctor added, who apparently saw nothing excessive in leaving two patients in charge of one unaided woman, she being also housekeeper, shopkeeper, and domestic servant.
“Of course you can send him to the hospital if you care to,” said the doctor lightly. “I dare say they’d take him in.” He was, in fact, not anxious to insist on Joe’s removal, thinking that he had already sufficiently worried the hospital authorities about the dwellers in Riceyman Steps.
To send Joe to the hospital would have relieved Elsie of the terrific responsibility which she had incurred by bringing him unpermitted into the house. But she did not want to surrender him. She hated to part with him. And privately, when it came to the point, she shared Mr. Earlforward’s objection to hospitals. Joe might be neglected, she feared, in the hospital; he might be victimized by some rule. She had no confidence in the nursing of anybody except herself. She was persuaded that if she could watch him she might save him.
“I think I can manage him here, sir,” she smiled. But it was a reserved smile, which said: “I have my own ideas about this matter and I don’t swallow all I hear.”
Dr. Raste began to put on his gloves; in the servant’s room he had not taken off his hat, much less his overcoat. She escorted him downstairs. At the shop-door he suddenly said:
“If he *does* want another doctor there’s Mr. Adhams﻿—other side of Myddelton Square.” His features relaxed. This remark was his repentance to Elsie, induced in him by her cheerful and unshrinking attitude towards destiny.
“You mean for master, sir?”
“Yes. *He* may be able to do something with him. You never know.”

===Chinese===

还有，每天该给他擦身两回。」
埃尔西凝神听着这忠告夹见闻的一锅，不能相信乔的病就只医生那副做派暗示的这么轻。她心里扎着一条并非无据的信念：医生待穷病人，总比待富病人草率得多。这位医生那份冷硬的漠然，又旧地重游地爬回她心头。他连自己从前的仆人都不曾打个招呼；看他，就像看诊号牌上随便一个寻常号。
「楼下你也不会有多少事。其实，几乎没事。」医生补一句——两个病人掠给一个孤立无援的女人，而这女人还兼着管家、女仆、店伙，他显然不觉得有半点过分。
「当然，你若愿意，也可以送他进医院。」医生轻轻巧巧地说。「我料他们肯收。」实则他并不急于坚持送走乔——为莱斯门台阶这几口人，他已经把医院当局麻烦得够多了。
把乔送进医院，本可以卸掉埃尔西那副可怕的担子——未经许可把人带进宅子，这担子是她自己惹上的。可她不肯交出他。她舍不得同他分开。而且私下里，事到临头，她同厄尔福沃德先生一样对医院怀有成见。她怕乔在医院里受冷落；怕他吃哪条规程的亏。除了她自己，谁的护理她都信不过。她笃信：只要她能守着他，兴许就能救下他。
「我想我在这儿照应得过来，先生。」她笑了。那是个有保留的笑，说的是：「这件事我有我自己的主意，听见的不全咽下去。」
拉斯特医生开始戴手套；在仆人房里他连帽子都没摘，更不必说大衣。她送他下楼。到了店门口他忽然说：
「他若*当真*要另请大夫，还有阿达姆斯先生——米德尔顿广场那一边。」他的五官松了下来。这句话是他向埃尔西的悔罪——她那直面命运、毫不畏缩的欢快，引出了他这份悔意。
「您是说为东家请，先生？」
「是。*他*兴许有法子治他。天知道。」

===Original===

“I’m sure I’m very much obliged, sir,” said Elsie eagerly, her kindliness springing up afresh and rushing out to meet the doctor’s spark of feeling. He nodded. He had not said whether or not he would call again to see Joe, and she had not dared to suggest it. She shut the door and locked herself in the house with the two men.

===Chinese===

「真是万分感激您，先生。」埃尔西热切地说，她的善意重新蹿起，迎着医生那一星情感的火花涌了上去。他点点头。再来复诊乔的话，他说了没有，她没敢提。她关上门，把自己同那两个男人一道锁进了宅子。

## V Midnight / 五 午夜
===Original===

Elsie, straight from the street, sat down on the edge of her creaking bed on the second-floor and looked at her best boots, which had lost their polish during the course of the afternoon and were covered with dust. She had paid various brief calls, and in her former home in Riceyman Square she had taken off her jacket and put on a pinafore-apron and vigorously helped with housework in arrear. But most of the time she had spent in walking certain streets. Though she ought to have been tired﻿—what with the morning’s labour, the calls, the episode in the pinafore, the long walking﻿—she had almost no sensation of bodily fatigue. Her mind, however, was exhausted by the monotony of thinking one importunate thought, which refused to be dismissed, and which indeed she did not sincerely want to dismiss.
When, on her way upstairs, she had spoken to Mrs. Earlforward at the door of the dining room, she had hoped that her employer would say: “There’s someone been inquiring for you,” or, “Elsie, *that man* has come pestering again.” But no! Nothing but a colourless, preoccupied “Good night.” An absurd hope, naturally! She knew it was an absurd hope﻿—and yet would not let it go. She had had the same silly hope upon entering each of the houses which she had visited. She had had it constantly as she walked the streets, examining every distant male figure. The silence of Dr. Raste had nearly killed it, but it could not be killed; it had more lives than a cat.
She had been sitting on the bed for a century when a church clock struck. Eleven! Still another hour! Why exactly an hour? Well, midnight was midnight. She must give him till twelve. An hour was an enormous period, full of chances. Suddenly she bent to take off her boots. They were not comfortable, never had been, but she took them off for another reason: so that she might move about noiselessly. She extinguished the candle and passed into the empty front room, and after some struggles with the front window posted herself at the side window. It was unfortunate that the window giving on to Riceyman Steps simply would not open on just this night, for if Joe came he would probably come by way of the steps, having first called at the house in the Square to get news of her. Nevertheless, he might come along King’s Cross Road en route for the Square.
King’s Cross Road was preparing to go to sleep for the night. No lorries.

===Chinese===

埃尔西从街上一回来，就坐在顶层她那张吱呀作响的床沿上，看着自己那双最好的靴子——一下午过去，鞋面已没了光，蒙满了尘。她串了几处短门；在莱斯门广场的旧居，她脱下外衣、系上围裙，铆足劲帮着清了清积欠的家务。可大部分时间，她都花在反复走那几条街上。她本该累了——一早的劳作，几处拜访，围裙那一出，长长的步行——可她几乎不觉身上的乏。倒是她的脑子，被一个念头的单调磨得精疲力竭：那念头缠死人不放，她其实也并没真心想打发它走。
上楼时她在餐室门口同厄尔福沃德太太搭了话，那时她盼着东家说一句：「有人来找过你。」或者：「埃尔西，*那人*又来缠了。」可没有！只有一声心不在焉、没滋没味的「晚安」。自然，荒唐的指望！她知道这是荒唐的指望——却偏撜不了手。她迈进每一户拜访的人家时，都怀着同一个傻指望。走在街上，她时时怀着它，把每一个远处的男人身影都端详一遍。拉斯特医生的沉默险些弄死它，可它弄不死；它比猫的命还多。
她在床沿坐了一个世纪，一座教堂的钟敲响了。十一点！还有一个钟头！为什么非得一个钟头？嗐，午夜就是午夜。她必须给他留到十二点。一个钟头是老大一段光阴，满是机会。她突然俯身脱靴。靴子并不舒服，从来就不舒服；可她脱它另有缘故：好让自己走动起来没有声。她吹灭蜡烛，走进空着的前屋，同前窗搏斗了一番，末了在侧窗边站定了岗。真不巧，朝莱斯门台阶那扇窗偏偏今夜打不开；乔若来，多半会走台阶那头——先去广场那座宅子打听她的消息。不过，他也可能沿着国王十字路来，往广场去。
国王十字路正预备入睡。没有货车。

===Original===

Not a taxi﻿—even in the daytime taxies were few in King’s Cross Road. A tramcar, two tramcars crammed with passengers. A few footfarers, mostly couples. The Nell Gwynn Tavern was dark, save for a window in the top storey where the barmaids slept. Down to the left a cold, vague glare showed the locality of the loading yard of the big post office. She could not see the pavement beneath the window; thus she might miss him. Cautiously and silently she opened the window wider. The bulb-pots were on the sill. Mrs. Earlforward had forgotten to bring them in. Elsie brought them in. (A transient, sympathetic thought for Mrs. Earlforward in her trouble.) She leaned her body out of the window, and felt the modest feather of her hat brush against the window-frame. She could see everything perfectly now﻿—north and south. No wanderer could escape her vision. At intervals, not a sign of either vehicle or footfarers! The road would be utterly deserted, and the street lamps seemed to be wasted. Then a policeman; he never looked up, never suspected that Elsie had her eye on him. Then a tramcar, empty save for a few woeful figures, a vast waste of tramcar.
She fancied she saw him approaching from the direction of the police-station. No, not a bit like him. She fancied she heard a sound in the room behind her. Incredible that her first notion should be that Joe had somehow entered the house and meant to surprise her with a long hug; and that the far more obvious explanation of surveillance by Mr. or Mrs. Earlforward should come to her only second! But so it was. Neither was correct. In the excited tension of her nerves she had merely imagined the sound. This delusion made her ashamed of her infatuated vigil. She had withdrawn into the room, but after a moment, despite shame, she resumed her post.
The night was calm and not very cold, but no frost would have driven her inside. The sky was thickly clouded; she did not raise her eyes to it. Weather did not exist for her. Another tramcar thundered past; she did not hear it﻿—only saw it. And, as a fact, nobody in the house ever heard the tramcars nor felt, save rarely, the vibrations which they caused. Elsie was far gone now in her madness, and yet more sane every minute.

===Chinese===

一辆出租也没有——白天国王十字路的出租都寥寥。一辆电车，两辆电车，塞满了乘客。几个步行人，多半成双成对。内尔·格温酒馆黑了，只有顶层一扇窗还亮——酒吧女招待们睡的地方。左下方一片冷而模糊的白光，标出大邮局装卸场的方位。窗底下的人行道她望不见；这样她可能错过他。她小心无声地把窗再推开些。球茎盆在窗台上。厄尔福沃德太太忘了收进。埃尔西把它们收了进来。（对困厄中的厄尔福沃德太太，闪过一瞬怜惜的念头。）她把身子探出窗外，觉出帽子上那枚素净的羽毛擦着了窗框。这下她南北两头都看得清清楚楚。再没有哪个夜行人逃得过她的眼睛。隔一阵，路上车辆行人一个不见！道路彻底空了，路灯像是白白点着。然后是一个警察；他从没抬头，从没疑心埃尔西正盯着他。然后又是一辆电车，除了几个愁苦的身影空空荡荡——好大一辆空耗着的电车。
她恍惚看见他从警察局那头走来。不，一点不像。她又恍惚听见背后屋里一声响。难以置信：她的头一个念头竟是乔不知怎么进了宅，要来个长长的拥抱吓她一跳；而那个明显得多的解释——厄尔福沃德先生或太太在监视——竟第二个才来！可就是这样。两个都不对。神经绷得太紧，她纯粹幻听了一声。这错觉叫她为自己的痴守害了臊。她已缩回屋里，可片刻之后，臊归臊，她又上了岗。
夜里无风，也不很冷；可纵有霜冻也撵不进她屋里去。天上云层厚厚的；她没抬眼看。天气于她并不存在。又一辆电车轰隆驶过；她没听见——只看见。而实情是，这宅里从没有人听得见电车，连它们引起的震动也极少觉得。埃尔西此刻疯得很深了，却又一分钟比一分钟清明。

===Original===

She felt herself in Joe’s arms, heard herself murmuring to him﻿—and he mute and passionate; and at the same time she well realized that she was merely indulging herself in foolishness. She was happy in the expectation of bliss, and wretched in the assurance of its impossibility.
The church clock began to strike. Could a whole hour have gone by? It seemed more like a quarter of an hour. She had her great sorrow, and superimposed on it a childish regret that the expectant watching was over; she had enjoyed the vigil, and it appeared now that no balm whatever remained to her. Reluctantly she drew in her body and shut the window softly, shutting out the last vestige of hope, and carrying with her, as she padded back to her bedroom, the full sense of her unbelievable silliness. Her mind swerved round to Mrs. Earlforward’s ordeal; her heart overflowed with benevolence towards Mrs. Earlforward, and with a sublime determination to stand by Mrs. Earlforward in any crisis that might arise. She forgot herself for a space, and became tranquil and cheerful and uplifted.
Then she felt hungry. Since midday she had eaten little, having refused offers of meals on her visits, and accepted only snacks, lest she might deplete larders already very inadequate. She took the candle into the kitchen cautiously, but also with a certain domination; for at nights the entire second-floor was her realm. She opened the kitchen window and the cage, and procured for herself more of the diminished cheese and one or two cold potatoes and a piece of bread crust. Then she arranged the side-flap of sacking on the cage to protect it against possible rain. She ate slowly, enjoying with deliberation each morsel. After all, she had one positive pleasure in life. She knew she was wicked; she knew she was a thief; she did not defend herself by subtle arguments. Of late she had been stealing more and more, and had received no reproach. She thought “they” had given up taking stock of the larder. She was becoming a hardened criminal.

===Chinese===

她感到自己在乔的怀里，听见自己朝他喃喃低语——而他无言，火热；同时她又明明白白知道，自己不过在纵情犯傻。她因盼着至福而欢喜，又因确知其必不能而悲苦。
教堂的钟敲响了。整整一个钟头竟过完了？倒像一刻钟还不到。她有她的大悲恸；悲恸之上又覆了一层孩子气的怅惘——那场悬望的守望结束了。她本是享受这守夜的；可现在看来，香膏一丝也没给她剩下。她老大不情愿地收回身子，轻轻关上窗，把最后一线希望关在了外头；蹑手蹑脚回卧室去时，带着对自己那份难以置信的傻气的全部自知。她的心思一转，转到了厄尔福沃德太太的磨难上；她的心满溢出对厄尔福沃德太太的善意，满溢出一种崇高的决心：无论临到什么危局，都同厄尔福沃德太太站在一起。有一阵她忘了自己，变得安宁、快活、昂扬。
然后她觉出饿来。自晬午起她吃得很少——拜访时人家留饭都辞了，只受些点心，怕掏空本就不充裕的人家食橱。她端着蜡烛进了厨房，小心翼翼，却也带着几分君临；一到夜里，整个顶层便是她的疆土。她开了厨房的窗，开了窗外食笼，给自己又弄了些已见少的奶酪、一两个凉土豆、一块面包皮。随后她把食笼的麻袋布侧帘掩好，防备下雨。她慢慢吃，一口一口，细细受用。说到底，她此生还有一样实实在在的乐子。她知道自己坏；知道自己是个贼；她不靠巧辩替自己开脱。近来她越偷越多，也没挨一句责备。她想，「他们」是不再盘食橱的账了。她正在成为一名惯犯。

## VI Mrs. Arb’s Case / 六 阿尔布太太的境况
===Original===

You would have thought, while Mrs. Arb was talking to Mr. Earlforward, that the enigma of the universe could not exist in her presence. Yet as soon as she was alone it was there, pervading the closed little shop. By letting Mr. Earlforward out she had let the enigma in; she had relocked the door too late. She stood forlorn, apprehensive, and pathetically undecided in the middle of the shop, and gazed round at the miserable contents of the shop with a dismayed disillusion. Brightness had fallen from her. Impossible to see in her now the woman whose abundant attractive vitality had vitalized Mr. Earlforward into a new and exalted frame of mind!
She had married, raising herself somewhat, in her middle twenties, a clerk of works, popular not only with architects, but with contractors. Mr. Arb had been clerk of works to some of the very biggest erections of the century. His vocation carried him here and there﻿—wherever a large building was being put up; it might be a provincial town hall, or a block of offices in London, or a huge hydro on some rural countryside, or an explosives factory in the middle of pasture land. And Mr. Arb’s jobs might last any length of time, from six months to three or four years. Consequently he had had no fixed residence. As there were no children his wife would always go about with him, and they would live in furnished rooms. This arrangement was cheaper than keeping a permanent home in London, and much more cheerful and stimulating. For Mr. Arb it had the advantages (with the disadvantages) of living with a wife whose sole genuine interest, hobby, and solicitude was her husband; all Mrs. Arb’s other social relations were bound to be transitory and lukewarm. When Mr. Arb died he left a sum of money surprisingly large in view of the fact that clerks of works do not receive high salaries. Architects, hearing of the nice comfortable fortune, were more surprised than contractors. A clerk of works has great power. A clerk of works may be human.
Mrs. Arb found herself with an income but no home, no habit of home life, and no masculine guidance or protection. She was heart-stricken, and﻿—what was worse﻿—she was thoroughly disorganized. Her immense vitality had no outlet. Time helped her, but she lived in suspense, undecided what to do and not quite confident in her own unaided wisdom.

===Chinese===

同厄尔福沃德先生说话那会儿，你会以为宇宙之谜在她跟前根本无法存在。可她一独处，它就来了，弥漫了这间紧闭的小铺。放厄尔福沃德先生出去，她把谜放了进来；重新落锁，已迟了一步。她凄惶地、忧心忡忡地、可怜地拿不定主意，站在店堂当中，灰心地环视着铺子里寒酸的陈设，一片幻灭。光亮从她身上褪尽了。此刻再无从辨认，这就是那个曾以丰盈迷人的生机、把厄尔福沃德先生激荡进一番崭新昂扬心境的女人！
她二十五六上结的婚，算是略略高攦：嫁了个监理工程师——不单建筑师们喜欢他，包工商们也喜欢。本世纪几项最大的营造工程，阿尔布先生都当过监理。他的行当把他带东带西——哪里起了大建筑，哪里就有他：也许是外省的一座市政厅，也许是伦敦的一幢写字楼，也许是乡间一座大型水疗旅馆，也许是牧草地正中一座炸药厂。他的差事长短不定，短则六个月，长则三四年。所以他从不曾有个固定的居所。两人没有孩子，太太总是跟着他走，住带家具的公寓。这样安排，比在伦敦常年撑一个家便宜，也快活得多、来劲得多。对阿尔布先生，这安排有它的好处（也有坏处）：同一位太太过日子，而这位太太唯一的真兴趣、真癖好、真牵挂，就是丈夫；太太其余的一切社会关系，注定短暂而温吞。阿尔布先生去世时，留下了一笔数目惊人可观的钱——须知监理工程师的薪水并不算高。建筑师们听说了这笔舒适的家私，比包工商们还吃惊。监理工程师手握大权。监理工程师也未尝不食人间烟火。
阿尔布太太发觉自己守着一份进项，却没有家，没有家居生活的习惯，也没有男人的指引和庇护。她伤了心；更糟的是，她整个儿乱了套。她那巨大的生机无处宣泄。时间帮了她，可她活在悬置里：做什么拿不定主意；单凭自己那份不借助人的聪明，她又信不足。

===Original===

An incredible letter from a solicitor announcing that she had inherited the confectioner’s business and premises and some money in Riceyman Steps shook and roused her. These pleasant and promising things had belonged to her grandmother’s much younger half-sister, whom she had once helped by prolonged personal service in a great emergency. The two had not met for many years, owing to Mrs. Arb’s nomadic existence; but they had come together at the funeral of Mr. Arb, and had quarrelled magnificently, because of Mrs. Arb’s expressed opinion that the old lady’s clothes showed insufficient respect for the angelic dead. The next event was the solicitor’s letter; the old lady had made a deathbed repentance for the funeral costume. Mrs. Arb abandoned the furnished rooms in Fulham, where she had been desiccating for two years, and flew to Clerkenwell in an eager mood of adventure. She did not like Clerkenwell, nor the look of the business, and she was beginning to be disappointed, but at worst she was far happier and more alive than she had ever been since Mr. Arb’s death.
She had, nevertheless, a cancer﻿—not a physical one: the secret abiding terror lest despite all her outward assurance she might be incapable of managing her possessions. The more she inherited, the more she feared. She had a vision of the business going wrong, of her investments going wrong, and of herself in poverty and solitude. This dread was absurd, but not less real for that. It grew. She tried to counter it by the practice of severe economy.
The demeanour of Mr. Earlforward, and his gift, had suddenly lightened her horizon. But the moment he departed she began saying to herself that she was utterly silly to indulge in such thoughts as she had been thinking, that men were not “like that,” that men knew what they were about and what they wanted﻿—and she looked gloomily in the fancy mirror provided by a firm of cocoa manufacturers and adorned with their name at the top and their address at the bottom.
She put pieces of gauze over the confectionery in the window and over the two bony remnants of ham, placed the chair seat downwards on the counter, and tilted the little table against the counter; then extinguished the oil-lamp, which alone lit the shop, and went into the back room, lighted by another similar oil-lamp.

===Chinese===

一封令人难以置信的律师来信，说她继承了莱斯门台阶的一爿糖果生意连同店面、外加一笔钱——这信把她震醒、把她振奋了。这些又可人又前程可期的东西，原属于她外婆那位年轻得多的小姨——从前一次大难临头，阿尔布太太曾长期亲身伺候过她。因阿尔布太太的逐水草而居，两家多年没有见面；却在阿尔布先生的葬礼上聚了头，还轰轰烈烈吵了一大架——起因是阿尔布太太放言，老太太那身衣裳对升天的亡者敬意不足。接下来便是律师的信；老太太临终为那身葬礼行头悔了罪。阿尔布太太掠下富勒姆那间带家具的公寓——她在那里枯萎了两年——怀着跃跃欲试的冒险心绪，飞到了克拉肯韦尔。她不喜欢克拉肯韦尔，也看不上那桩生意的模样，失望已然开头；可就算往坏里说，她也比自阿尔布先生死后任何时候都快活、都更有生气。
然而她身上长着一枚癌——不是肉体上的：是一种秘而不宣、盘踞不去的恐惧，怕自己尽管外表笃定，骨子里却料理不动这份产业。继承得越多，她越怕。她有一幅幻象：生意一败涂地，投资一败涂地，她自己沦入贫穷与孤零。这份惧怕荒唐，却并不因荒唐而减少半分真实。它在长。她试行以厉行节俭来抵抗它。
厄尔福沃德先生的做派，连同他的馈赠，霜然照亮了她的地平线。可他前脚一走，她就开始对自己说：方才沉溺的那些念头傻透了；男人不是「那样的」；男人心里有数，知道自己要什么——她闷闷地照着那面花哨的镜子——一家可可厂商奉送的，上端饰着厂商名号，下端印着地址。
她给橱窗里的糖果和那两片瘦骨嶙峋的火腿残躯盖上纱布，把椅子面朝下扣上柜台，小桌斜倚柜台立好；随后熄了那盏独照店堂的油灯，走进后屋——屋里点着另一盏同样的油灯。

===Original===

In this room, which was a parlour-kitchen, and whose principal table had just been scrubbed, Elsie, a helot withdrawn from the world and dedicated to secret toil, was untying her sack apron preparatory to the great freedom of the night.
“Oh, Elsie﻿—you did say your name was Elsie, didn’t you?”
“Yes’m.”
“I should take it very kindly if you could stay a bit longer this evening.”
Elsie was dashed; she paused on the knot of the apron-string.
“It’s a quarter of an hour past my time now, ’m,” she said apologetically and humbly.
“It is? So it is. Well, not quite.”
“I had an engagement, ’m.”
“Couldn’t you put it off for this once? You see, I’m very anxious to get straight after all this mess I’ve been in. I’m one that can’t stand a mess. I’ll give you your supper﻿—I’ll give you a slice of ham﻿—and sixpence extra.”
“I’m sure it’s very kind of you, ’m, but﻿—”
Mrs. Arb coaxed, and she could coax very effectively.
“Well, ’m, I always like to oblige.” Elsie yielded, not grudgingly nor with the air of conferring a favour, but rather with a mild and pure kindliness. She added, coaxing in her turn: “But I must just run out half a minute, if you’ll let me.”
“Oh, of course. But don’t be long, will you? Look, here’s your half-day and the extra sixpence. Take it now. And while you’re out I’ll be cutting the ham for you. It’s a pity I’ve turned out the shop lamp, but I dare say I can see if I leave this door open.” She gave the girl some silver.
“I’m sure it’s very kind of you, ’m.”
Mrs. Arb cut an exceedingly thin slice of ham quite happily. She had two reasons for keeping Elsie; she wanted to talk to somebody, and she felt that, whether she talked or not, she could not bear to be alone in the place till bedtime. Her good spirits returned.

===Chinese===

这间屋是客厅兼厨房；主桌刚擦洗过。埃尔西——一个遁出人世、献身隐秘劳作的黑劳士——正解她那条麻袋布围裙，预备迎接夜里那场大解放。
「哦，埃尔西——你说过你叫埃尔西，是吧？」
「是，太太。」
「今儿晚上你要能再多留一阵，我就感激不尽了。」
埃尔西愣了；解围裙带的手停在结上。
「这会儿已过钟点一刻钟了，太太。」她又赔罪又谦卑地说。
「是吗？可不是。嗐，也不算多。」
「我还有个约，太太。」
「就这一回，不能推一推吗？你瞧，折腾了这一通乱摊子，我急着理出个头绪。我这人见不得乱。晚饭我管你——给你一片火腿——另加六便士。」
「您真是太好心了，太太，可——」
阿尔布太太哄劝起来；她哄劝的本事可不小。
「好吧，太太，我总归乐意效劳。」埃尔西让了步——不勉强，也不带施恩的架子，倒带着一种温和纯粹的善意。她又反过来央告：「可我得出去半分钟，您行个方便。」
「哦，当然。可别去久了，好不好？瞧，这是你歇班半天工钱，外加那六便士。这就拿上。你出去这会儿，我把火腿给你切出来。店里的灯可惜熄了，不过门这么开着，想必还看得见。」她递给姑娘几枚银币。
「您真是太好心了，太太。」
阿尔布太太欢欢喜喜切下一片薄得不能再薄的火腿。她留埃尔西有两个缘故：她想找个人说话；还有，无论说不说话，她都受不了独自守在这地方直到就寝。她的好兴致回来了。

## IV No Verdict / 四 无定论
===Original===

“What do you think of Mr. Earlforward’s health?” Violet demanded peremptorily, in the bedroom. Her features were alive with urgent emotion. She almost intimidated the doctor.
“Ha!” he retorted defensively, with an explosive jerk. “I haven’t examined him. I have﻿—not﻿—examined him. He strikes me as undernourished.”
“And he is. He refuses food.”
“But why does he refuse food? There must be some cause.”
“It’s because he’s set on being economical. He’s got drawers full of money, and so have I﻿—at least, I’ve got a good income of my own. But there you are. He won’t eat, he won’t eat. He won’t eat enough, do *what* I will.”
“Is that the only reason?”
“Of course it is. He’s never had indigestion in his life.”
“Um! Your maid, what’s her name, seems to be pretty well nourished, at all events.”
“Have you been seeing her?” Violet inquired sharply, her suspicion leaping up.
The doctor appreciated his own great careless indiscretion, and answered with admirable deceitful nonchalance:
“I noticed her one day last week in passing. At least, I took it to be her.”
Violet left the point there.
The electric light blazed down upon them; it had no shade; not a single light in the house had a shade. It showed harshly, realistically, Violet half leaning against the foot of the bed, and Dr. Raste, upright as when in uniform he used to give orders in Palestine, on the rag hearthrug. Violet’s baffled energy raged within her. She had at hand all the materials for tranquil happiness﻿—affection, money, temperament, sagacity, an agreeable occupation﻿—and they were stultified by the mysterious, morbid, absurd, inexcusable and triumphant volition of her loving husband. Instead of happiness she felt doom﻿—doom closing in on her, on him, on the sentient house.
“My husband is a miser. I’ve encouraged him for the sake of peace. And so now you know, doctor!”
An astounding confession to a stranger, a man to whom she had scarcely spoken before! But it relieved her. She made it with gusto, with passion. She had begun candour with Elsie in the morning; she was growing used to it. The domestic atmosphere itself had changed within six hours. That which had been tacitly denied for months was now admitted openly. Truth had burst out. A few minutes earlier﻿—vain chatter about hospitals, trifling and vain commercial transactions, make-believes, incredible futilities, ghastly nothings!

===Chinese===

「厄尔福沃德先生的健康，您怎么看？」卧室里，薇奥莱特不容分说地质问。她五官间全是急切的情绪，几乎把医生镇住了。
「哈！」他护身似的应战，一蹦一蹦地迸字。「我没给他查体。我——没有——查体。依我看，他是营养不足。」
「正是。他拒绝进食。」
「可他为什么拒绝进食？总有个原因。」
「就因为他打定了主意省钱。他一抽屉一抽屉全是钱，我也一样——至少我自己有一份可观的进项。可没用。他不肯吃，不肯吃。任我*怎么*办，他也不肯吃饱。」
「就这一个原因？」
「当然。他这辈子从没闹过消化不良。」
「唔！你家女仆，叫什么来着，倒是营养得挺好。」
「您见着她了？」薇奥莱特厉声追问，疑心蹿起。
医生掠出了自己这话失言失得有多冒失，便以可敬的、骗人的闲淡作答：
「上星期哪天路过，我瞧见过一眼。至少，我当那是她。」
薇奥莱特没有再追。
电灯直射下来，照着他们；灯上没有罩；这宅里没有一盏灯有罩。它生硬地、写实地照出：薇奥莱特半倚着床脚，拉斯特医生立在破炉毯上，笔挺——像当年在巴勒斯坦穿军装下令时那样笔挺。薇奥莱特那受挫的精力在心里烧。安稳幸福的材料她样样在手——恩爱，钱财，性情，精明，一份称心的营生——却尽被她亲爱的丈夫那股神秘、病态、荒唐、不可原谅而又节节得胜的意志作废了。幸福没有来，来的是劫数——劫数正朝她、朝他、朝这座有知觉的宅子合围。
「我丈夫是个守财奴。为了太平，我一直纵着他。现在您都知道了，医生！」
对一位陌生人、一个她从前几乎没说过话的男人，坦白到这个地步，骇人听闻！可这叫她松快。她说得酣畅，说得激昂。早晨她已对埃尔西开了诚；她渐渐惯了。家庭空气本身，六个钟头里已经变了。几个月来心照不宣加以否认的，如今公开承认了。真话破门而出。而几分钟之前——还是关于医院的空谈，琐屑而虚浮的买卖，做戏，难以置信的无谓之举，可怖的虚无！

===Original===

And now, the dreadful reality exposed! And at that very moment Henry in his office, to maintain to himself the frightful pretence, was squandering the remains of his vitality in the intolerably petty details of business.
“Well,” said Dr. Raste primly﻿—the first law of his actions was self-preservation﻿—“there isn’t a great deal to be done until you can persuade him to have professional advice.﻿ ﻿… And you? What is it with you? You don’t look much better than your husband.”
“Oh, doctor!” Violet cried, suddenly plaintive. “I don’t know. You must examine me. Perhaps I ought to have come to you before.”
At this point the light went out and they were in darkness.
“Oh, dear!”﻿—a sort of despair in Violet’s voice now﻿—“I knew that lamp would be going soon.” The fact was that the lamps in the house generally had begun to go. All of them had passed their allotted span of a thousand burning hours. Two in the shop had failed. Henry possessed no reserve of lamps, and he would not buy, and Violet had not yet wound herself up to the resolve of buying in defiance of him. Once a fuse had melted. For two days they had managed mainly with candles. Violet, irritated, went forth secretly to buy fuse wire. She returned, and with a half-playful, half-resentful gesture threw the wire almost in his face; but it had happened that during her absence he had inserted a new fuse made from a double thickness of soda-water-bottle wire which he had picked up from somewhere. His reproaches, though unspoken, were hard for her to bear.
The doctor promptly struck a match, and Violet lit the candle on the night-table.
“I’m afraid I can’t examine you by *that* light,” said the doctor.
“Oh, *dear*!” She nearly wept, then masterfully took hold of herself. “I know!” She rushed to the bathroom, stood on the orange-box, and detached the bathroom lamp, and returned with it to the bedroom. “Here! This will do.”
The doctor climbed on to a chair. As soon as he had fixed the new lamp Violet economically blew out the candle; and then, quaking, she yielded up her body, in the glacial chill of the room, for the trial and verdict which would reassure or agonize her. However, she was neither reassured or agonized; there was no verdict.
When Dr. Raste redescended the dark stairs the shop lay in darkness and the bookseller was wheeling in the bookstand.

===Chinese===

而现在，可怕的现实亮出来了！而就在此刻，亨利还在账房里，为着独自撑住那可怕的假面，把残余的精力挥霍在琐碎得难以忍受的生意细节里。
「这个嘛，」拉斯特医生端着架子说——他行事的第一法则是自保——「在您劝动他接受正规诊治之前，没有多少可做的。……您呢？您怎么了？您气色不比您丈夫强多少。」
「哦，医生！」薇奥莱特叫起来，忽然哀声哀气。「我不知道。您得给我查一查。兴许我早该来找您了。」
正说着，灯灭了，两人陷进黑暗。
「哎呀！」薇奥莱特的声音里此刻带着一种绝望。「我就知道那灯泡快完了。」实情是，宅里的灯泡已普遍开始寿终。只只都烧满了一千小时的额定寿数。店堂里已熄了两只。亨利没有备泡，又不肯买；薇奥莱特呢，还没把自己拧到不顾他、决意去买的那一步。有一回保险丝熔了。整整两天，他们主要靠蜡烛对付。薇奥莱特憋着火，偷偷出门买了保险丝回来，半噌半闹地朝他脸上摔过去；不巧她出门那阵，他已从哪儿捡来汽水瓶的铁丝，双股拧成一根，换上了新保险丝。他一句责备没有，可那份无言的数落，她受不住。
医生赶紧划了根火柴，薇奥莱特点上床头柜的蜡烛。
「这样的光，我怕没法给您查。」医生说。
「哎呀，真是！」她几乎哭出来，随即硬生生拿住了自己。「有了！」她冲进浴室，站上橙木箱，卸下浴室的灯泡，捧回卧室。「喏！这个行。」
医生爬上一把椅子。新灯泡一装好，薇奥莱特为省蜡烛吹熄了烛火；随后，她哆嗦着，在屋子冰窖般的寒气里，交出自己的身体，听候那场裁定——或叫她宽心，或叫她痛断肝肠。然而两样都没有；没有裁定。
拉斯特医生重新摸黑下楼时，店堂一片黑，书商正把书架往里推。

===Original===

The doctor entered the still lighted office to get his two parcels, which he arranged on his left side exactly as before.
“Oh?” said Mr. Earlforward, approaching him. It was an interrogation.
“I should prefer not to say anything at present,” the doctor announced in loud, prim, clearly articulated syllables. “There may be nothing abnormal, nothing at all. At any rate, it is quite impossible to judge under existing conditions. I shall call again in a week or ten days﻿—perhaps earlier. No immediate cause for anxiety.”
He had been but little more communicative than this to Violet herself. He was inhuman again﻿—for his patients. Within him, however, glowed the longing to see his child’s eyes kindle when he presented her with the Globe Shakespeare for her very own.
That night, contrary to custom, Henry went to bed earlier than Violet. He stated that he felt decidedly better, but that he had finished all his bookkeeping and oddments of work, and that it would be a pity to keep the office fire alive for nothing. Violet, in her mantle, had to darn a curtain in the front room. When she went into the bedroom and switched on the light she saw him, with the counterpane well up to his chin, lying flat on his back, eyes shut, but not asleep. He had the pallor of a corpse, and the corpse-like effect was enhanced by the indications of his straight, thin body under the clothes. She stood bent by the side of the bed and looked at him, as it were passionately, but vainly trying by the intensity of her gaze to wrench out and drag up from hidden depths the inaccessible secrets of his mind.
Though saying little to her about her trouble he had behaved to her through the evening with the most considerate kindliness. He had caressed her with his voice. And about her trouble she had not expected him to say much. He had a very inadequate conception of the physical risks which women by nature are condemned to run. And she had never talked much in such directions, for not only was he a strangely modest man, but she deliberately practised the reserve which he himself practised. She argued, somewhat vindictively: “He tells me nothing. I will tell him nothing.” Moreover, the doctor’s calm noncommittal attitude had given Henry an exceptional occasion to exercise his great genius for postponement. Never would Henry go halfway to meet an ordeal of any sort.

===Chinese===

医生走进还亮着灯的账房取他那两个包裹，照旧安置在左侧。
「哦？」厄尔福沃德先生迎上来。这是一句盘问。
「目前我宁可不置一词。」医生一字一顿，高声而端整地宣布。「可能什么异常也没有，什么也没有。无论如何，在眼下这种条件下无从判断。一周或十天后我再来——也许更早。暂无立需焦虑之由。」
对薇奥莱特本人，他透的口风也只比这多一点。他又成了非人——对他的病人。然而他心里燃着一份渴望：要把环球版莎士比亚赠给女儿、归她独有时，看她眼睛点亮。
那天夜里，一反常例，亨利比薇奥莱特先上了床。他说自己分明好些了，又说账目和零星活计都已了结，账房的火再白白烧着可惜。薇奥莱特裹着晨衣，还得在前屋补一蝠窗帘。她进卧室开灯时，看见他被子直拉到下巴，仰面平躺，闭着眼，却没睡。他一副死人的苍白；被子底下那笔直瘦削的身形，又把这死人相添了几分。她俯身立在床边望着他，近乎痴狂地、却徒劳地想凭目光的烈度，从深藏的底层里，把他心里那些无法企及的秘密拧出来、拽上来。
对她的难处，他话说得少；可一晚上待她，体恤周到到了极处。他用嗓音爱抚她。她的难处，她也本没指望他多说什么。女人天生命定要担的那些身体上的风险，他的概念淡薄得很。这类方向她也从不多谈——一来他是个出奇拘谨的男人，二来她有意奉行着他自己奉行的缄默。她多少带着点狠劲地讲理：「他什么都不告诉我。我也什么都不告诉他。」再者，医生那副平静的不置可否，又给了亨利一个天赐良机，施展他那门拖延的大天才。任何一种磨难，亨利是决不肯迎上去半步的。

===Original===

Lastly, his reactions were generally slow. Fear, anxiety, seemed to come late to him.
He opened his eyes. She gave him one of the long kisses which he loved. Could he guess (she wondered) that her kiss was absentminded that night, perfunctory, a kiss that emerged inattentive to him from the dark, virginal fastnesses of her being, which neither he nor any other would or could invade. With intention she pressed her lips on his.
“Come to bed,” he murmured gently, “and get that light out.”
Half undressed she looked carefully at herself in the mirror of the perfectly made, solid, everlasting Victorian wardrobe. Yes, her face showed evidence of illness; it frightened her. No, she was merely indisposed; she was frightening herself. She had no pain, or extremely little. She thought, as she regarded herself in the glass, how inscrutable, how enigmatic, how feminine she was, and how impossible it was for him to comprehend her. She felt superior to him, as a complex mind to a simple one. She thought that she, far better than he, could appreciate the significance of the terrible day. She was overwhelmed by it. Situations were evolving one out of another. Nothing had happened, and yet all was changed. The night was twenty years away from the morning.
“Do you know about that girl?” he asked with soft weariness when she had slipped into bed and the light was out.
“No. Elsie? What?”
“She’s eaten two-thirds of the cheese in the cage﻿—at least two-thirds. Must have eaten it before she went out.”
The “cage” was the wire-netted larder hung outside the kitchen window. Henry had taken to buying cheese, because it was as nourishing as meat, and cheaper. He had “discovered” cheese as a food﻿—especially a food for servants. Violet said no word, but she sighed. She was staggered, deeply discouraged, by this revelation of Elsie’s incredible greed and guile; it was a blow that somehow finished her off.
“Yes,” Henry went on, and his mild voice passed through the darkness into Violet’s ear with an uncanny effect. “I happened to go up into the kitchen just before I came to bed.” (And he had not rushed back to tell her of the calamity. He had characteristically kept it to ripen in his brain. And how characteristic of him to wander ferreting into the kitchen! Naught could escape his vigilance.) “Did you see her when she came home?”
“Yes. She went straight to bed.”
A silence.

===Chinese===

末了一条：他的反应素来慢。恐惧、忧惧，到他身上似乎总迟到。
他睁开眼。她给了他一个他喜爱的长吻。他猜不猜得出（她纳闷）——那晚她的吻心不在焉，例行公事；一个吻，从她生命幽暗的、处女般的禁地里漫不经心地浮出来给他，而那禁地，他也好，旁人也罢，谁也不能、谁也不会闯入？她有意地把唇压在他唇上。
「上床吧，」他轻轻咕哝，「把灯关了。」
褪了一半衣裳，她对着那做工完美、敦实、永恒的维多利亚衣柜的镜子，细细端详自己。是的，她脸上带着病的证据；这把她吓着。不，她只是不适；她在自己吓自己。她不疼，或疼得极微。她望着镜中的自己想：她是多么不可测、多么像谜、多么女人；他要懂她，是多么不可能。她自觉高于他，如一颗复杂的心之于一颗简单的心。她想，这可怕一天的分量，她远比他掠得出。她被它压倒了。局面正一个变出一个。什么都没发生，可一切都变了。这夜晚同早晨之间，隔着二十年。
「那丫头的事你知道吗？」她钻进被窝、灯灭之后，他带着软软的倦意问。
「不知道。埃尔西？怎么了？」
「食笼里的奶酪，她吃掉了三分之二——至少三分之二。准是出门前吃的。」
「食笼」就是挂在厨房窗外的铁丝网食橱。亨利开始买奶酪，因为它同肉一样养人，又便宜。他把奶酪「发现」成了一种食物——尤其是一种给仆人的食物。薇奥莱特没说话，只叹了口气。埃尔西这骇人听闻的贪馋与狡诈一曝光，她被打懵了、深深泄了气；这一击，不知怎么，把她最后一点气力也了结了。
「是啊，」亨利接着说，他那温吞的嗓音穿过黑暗落进薇奥莱特耳朵，效果瘆人。「上床前我恰巧上了趟厨房。」（出了这等灾祸，他也没有跑回来报信。他照他的性子，把它存在脑子里催熟。而溜进厨房东嗅西探，又多么是他的性子！什么也逃不过他的哨探。）「她回来时你见着了吗？」
「见着了。她径直上床睡了。」
一阵沉默。

===Original===

“Something will have to be done about that girl,” he said at length.
“What does he mean?” thought Violet, alarmed anew. “Does he mean we must get rid of her? No, that would be too much.” But she was not afraid of the extra work for herself which getting rid of Elsie would entail. She was afraid of being left to live all alone with Henry. She trembled at such a prospect.

===Chinese===

「那丫头，总得想个办法。」他终于说。
「他什么意思？」薇奥莱特想，又惊起来。「是说必须打发她走？不，那也太过分了。」不过，打发走埃尔西要她自己多操的劳，她倒不怕。她怕的是掠下她、独个儿同亨利过活。想到那样的前景，她直打颤。

## VIII On the Landing / 八 楼梯口
===Original===

During the day Henry had asked several times for bulletins as to Elsie’s consumption of food, and he received them with satisfaction, but also with a certain sardonic air new in Violet’s experience of him. This demeanour was one of the things that disquieted Violet. Another was that, contrary to his habit of solicitude for her, he made absolutely no inquiry as to her own health, though he surely ought to have been ever so little disturbed about it. And another was that he no longer showed his customary quiet pleasure in being worried over her. After taking some soft food he demanded a toothpick, and had employed himself with it in the most absurd way for quite an hour. In answer to her questions he said blandly again and again that he was all right. Soon after nightfall he insisted that the electricity should be switched off. Violet refused, as she was determined to watch him carefully. He said that the light hurt his eyes. She took the paper lining from a tray in her wardrobe and fashioned a shade for the lamp﻿—the first shade ever known in that house.
At ten o’clock, feeling cold and ill, she undressed and got into bed, but kept the light burning. Henry was perfectly tranquil. The trams seemed to make a tremendous uproar. She could not sleep, but Henry apparently dozed at intervals. Then she had a severe shock. He was violently sick.
“What’s this? What’s this?” he murmured feebly and sadly.
He did not know what it was; but Violet, who had witnessed a deal of physical life during her peregrinations with the clerk of the works, knew what it was. It was what Violet’s varied acquaintances had commonly called, in tones of awe on account of its seriousness, the “coffee-grounds vomit.” It was, indeed, a sinister phenomenon.
Henry had dropped back exhausted. His forehead was wet, and his hair damp with perspiration. Also he seemed to be terrorized﻿—he who was never afraid until hours or days after the event! At this point it was that Violet went out of the bedroom to send Elsie for the doctor.
As soon as Elsie was gone Violet dressed. She still felt very cold and ill. The minutes dragged. Henry lay inert. His aspect had considerably worsened. The facial emaciation was accentuated, and the pallor of the ears and the lips, and even his beard and hair were limp as if from their own fatigue.

===Chinese===

白天，亨利几次三番追问埃尔西进食情况的简报；听得满意，却又带着一股讥诮气——这做派在薇奥莱特对他的经验里是新的。这副神情是叫薇奥莱特不安的事情之一。另一件：他素来对她关切，如今对她自己的健康却绝口不问——虽说照理他总该挂那么一点点心。又一件：被人惦记时他惯常的那份安静欢喜，也不见了。吃了些软食之后，他要了根牙签，拿它消遣了足足一个钟头，那用场荒唐透顶。任她怎么问，他只是温温吞吞、一遍一遍地说自己没事。天黑不久，他坚持要关电灯。薇奥莱特不依——她打定主意要细看他。他说灯光刺眼。她便从衣柜里一只托盘上揭下衬纸，给灯糊了个罩——这座宅子有史以来的第一只灯罩。
十点钟，她自觉又冷又病，脱衣上了床，灯却留着。亨利安安静静。电车仿佛闹出震天的喧腾。她睡不着；亨利倒像是时时打着盹。随后她重重吃了一惊。他剧烈地呕吐了。
「这是什么？这是什么？」他微弱而悲切地喃喃。
他不知道这是什么；薇奥莱特却知道——跟着监理工程师东奔西走那几年，肉身上的事她见得多了。这正是薇奥莱特那些三教九流的相识们常说的「咖啡渣样呕吐」——说到它，因为性命攸关，人人带着敬畏的口气。这实在是个凶象。
亨利力竭般瘫了回去。他额头上是湿的，头发浸着汗。而且他像是被吓破了胆——他这个人，出了事也要几个钟头甚至几天之后才知道怕的！就在这当口，薇奥莱特走出卧室，打发埃尔西去请医生。
埃尔西一走，薇奥莱特便穿了衣裳。她仍觉又冷又病。一分钟一分钟磨着。亨利躺着不动。他的形容已大为恶化。脸上的消瘦愈发分明，耳朵和嘴唇没了血色，连他的须发都蒨蒨的，仿佛它们自己乏透了。

===Original===

Elsie’s greed was now an infinitesimal thing in Violet’s mind, and the importance attached to it struck her as wildly absurd. Yet she had a strange, cruel desire (which she repressed) to say to Henry: “Your bluff has failed! Your bluff has failed! And look at you!” She thought of the approaching Christmas, for which she had secretly been making plans for merriment; she had meant to get Elsie’s aid, because she knew that Elsie had in her the instincts of fancy and romance. Pathetic! She thought of her anger at Elsie’s indiscretion in telling a customer that the master would never get up again. Ridiculous anger! He never *would* get up again; and what did it matter if all Clerkenwell knew in advance? The notion of Henry spending money on the cure of his damaged knee seemed painfully laughable. His dread, genuine or affected, of communism, seemed merely grotesque. She saw a funeral procession, consisting of a hearse and one coach, leave Riceyman Steps. The coffin would have to be carried across the space from the shop-door to the main road, as no vehicle could come right to the door. Crowds! Crowds of gapers!
Then she heard a noise below. Elsie, who had run all the way to Myddelton Square and all the way back, tapped with tremulous eagerness.
“He’s coming, ’m.” She was panting.
Dr. Raste arrived, but only after an interval of nearly half an hour, which seemed to Violet like half a night. The fact was that, despite much practice, he could not dress in less than about twenty minutes; nor was it his habit to run to his patients, whatever their condition. He came with the collar of his thick overcoat turned up. Violet met him on the landing; she had shut the bedroom door behind her. He was calm; he yawned; and his demeanour hovered between the politely indifferent and the politely inimical. He spoke vaguely, but in his loud tone, in reply to Violet’s murmur: “I was afraid you weren’t coming, doctor.”
Violet had by this time lost her sense of proportion. She was incapable of bearing in mind that the doctor lived daily and nightly among disease and death, and that he was more accustomed to sick people than to healthy. She did not suspect that in the realism of his heart he regarded sick people and their relations in the mass as persons excessive in their fears, ruthless in their egotism, and cruel in their demands upon himself.

===Chinese===

埃尔西的贪嘴，此刻在薇奥莱特心里已缩成一件微末小事；先前加之于它的那份郑重，想来简直荒诞。可她心里有一股古怪而残忍的欲望（她按住了）——想对亨利说：「你的虚张垮了！你的虚张垮了！瞧瞧你自己这副样子！」她想起渐渐临近的圣诞——她一直暗暗筹划着如何热闹一场；她本想拉埃尔西帮忙，因为她知道埃尔西骨子里有花巧与罗曼司的天性。可怜！她又想起自己对埃尔西失言的恼怒——她竟对一个顾客说东家再也起不了床。可笑的恼怒！他是再*也*起不了床了；全克拉肯韦尔提前知道，又有什么要紧？亨利肯花钱治那条坏膝盖的念头，显得辛酸可笑。他对共产主义的恐惧——不管是真是假——只显得怪诞。她看见一支出殡的行列，一部灵车加一辆马车，驶离莱斯门台阶。棺材得靠人抬着走过店门到大路之间那段路，因为车进不到门口。人群！黑压压看热闹的人群！
然后她听见楼下一阵响。埃尔西一路跑到米德尔顿广场、又一路跑回，激动得发着抖来叩门。
「他来了，太太。」她喘着。
拉斯特医生到了——但隔了将近半个钟头，在薇奥莱特感觉里像半宿。实情是：任凭演练再多，他穿好一身衣裳也少不下二十分钟；而且无论病人什么状况，跑着去从不是他的习惯。他来了，厚大衣的领子竖着。薇奥莱特在楼梯口迎他；身后的卧室门已带上。他镇静；他打了个呵欠；他的做派在客气地淡漠同客气地敌对之间悬着。薇奥莱特低声说「我还当您不来了呢，医生」，他含含糊糊、却嗓门洪亮地回了一句。
薇奥莱特此时已失了分寸感。她想不起来：这位医生日日夜夜就住在疾病与死亡中间；比起健康人，病人于他更司空见惯。她也料不到：在他那颗写实的心里，他大体上把病人和病人家属看作一群恐惧过头、自我得冷酷、对他索求得残忍的人。

===Original===

She had no conception that to him a night-call was primarily a grievance and secondarily an occasion to save life or pacify pain. She might have credited that fifty percent of his night-calls were unnecessary, but she could never have guessed that he had already set down this visit to Riceyman Steps as probably the consequence of a false, foolish, feminine alarm. She began to explain to him at length the unique psychology of the sufferer, as though the doctor had never before encountered an unwilling and obstinate patient. The doctor grew restless.
“Yes. Just so. Just so. I’d better have a look at him.”
“I haven’t dared to tell him I’ve sent for you,” said Violet piteously, reproachful of the doctor’s inhumanity.
“Tut-tut!” observed the doctor, and opened the bedroom door.
He sniffed on entering, glanced placidly at Henry, then at the fireplace, and then went to the window and drew the curtains and blind aside.
“I should advise you to have a fire lighted at once, and we’ll open the window a bit.”
He put his hat carefully on the chest of drawers, but did not even unbutton his overcoat or turn down his collar. Then he removed his gloves and rubbed his hands. At last to Henry:
“Well, Mr. Earlforward, what’s this I hear?”
No diplomacy with the patient! No ingenious excusing of his presence! The patient just had to accept his presence; and the patient, having no alternative, did accept it.
“Shall I light the fire now, ’m?” asked Elsie timidly at the door.
“Yes,” said the doctor shortly, including both the women in his glance.
“But won’t she be disturbing you while you’re﻿ ﻿…” Violet suggested anxiously. She was afraid that this unprecedented proceeding would terribly upset Henry and so make him worse.
“Not at all.”
“I don’t think we’ve ever had this fire lighted,” said Violet, to which the doctor deigned no reply.
“Run along, Elsie. Take your things off and be quick. The doctor wants a fire immediately.”
Before the doctor, changed now from an aggrieved human being into a scrupulously conscientious professional adviser, had finished his examination, the room was half full of smoke. Violet could not help looking at Elsie reproachfully as if to say: “Really, Elsie, you should be able to control the chimney better than this﻿—and your master so ill!”

===Chinese===

她更无从想到：夜间出诊于他，头一重是委屈，第二重才是救人或镇痛的时机。他半夜出诊五成纯属多余——这她或许肯信；可她万万猜不到，这趟莱斯门台阶之行，他早已在心里记作一场虚妄、愚蠢、娘儿们式警报的后果了。她开始对他长篇大论地讲解这位患者独一无二的心理，仿佛医生从未见过不肯配合、执拗顽固的病人似的。医生坐立不安起来。
「是。正是。正是。我还是看看他去。」
「我还不敢告诉他我请您来了。」薇奥莱特可怜巴巴地说，怪着医生的不通人情。
「啧啧！」医生说着，开了卧室门。
进门他吸了吸鼻子，平静地掠一眼亨利，再掠一眼壁炉，然后走到窗前，拉开窗帘和遮帘。
「我劝你们立刻生个火，窗子也开一条缝。」
他把帽子端端正正掠上五斗柜，大衣扣子却不解一颗，领子也不翻下。然后他脱了手套，搓了搓手。终于对亨利开口：
「怎么样，厄尔福沃德先生，我听说这是怎么了？」
对病人毫无外交！对他自己的到场，也没有半句巧妙的托词！病人只管接受他到场；病人别无选择，也就接受了。
「太太，这就生火吗？」埃尔西在门口怯怯地问。
「生。」医生简短地说，一瞥把两个女人都扫进去。
「可她生火的时候，会不会搅了您……」薇奥莱特不安地提议。她怕这桩空前之举会把亨利搅得心神大乱，病上加病。
「毫不。」
「这炉子，我记得我们从来没生过。」薇奥莱特说。这句医生不屑置答。
「快去吧，埃尔西。把外衣脱了，麻利点。医生要火，立刻就要。」
医生已从一位受了委屈的人，变成一位一丝不苟、尽职尽责的职业顾问；他的检查还没做完，屋里已是半屋子烟。薇奥莱特忍不住朝埃尔西递去责备的眼色，仿佛在说：「真是的，埃尔西，这烟囱你总该调度得比这强——东家还病成这样呢！」

===Original===

The patient coughed excessively, but everyone knew that the coughing was merely his protest against the madness of lighting a fire.
“I’m too hot,” he muttered. “I’m too hot.”
And such was the power of autosuggestion that he did in fact feel too hot, though the fire had not begun to give out any appreciable heat. He privately determined to have the fire out as soon as the doctor had departed; a limit must be set to folly after all. However, Henry was at once faced with a great new crisis which diminished the question of the fire to a detail.
“I can’t come to any conclusion without washing out the stomach,” said Dr. Raste, turning to Violet, and then turning back quickly to Henry: “You say you’ve no pain there? You’re sure?” And he touched a particular point on the chest.
“None,” replied Henry.
“The fellow is lying,” thought the doctor. “It’s amazing how they will lie. I bet anything he’s lying. Why do they lie?”
Nevertheless, the doctor could not be quite sure. And he had a general preference for not being quite sure; he liked to postpone judgment.
“*I* don’t mind having my stomach washed out,” Henry murmured blandly.
“No, of course not. I’ll telephone to the hospital early tomorrow, and Mrs. Earlforward will take you round there in a cab.” And to Violet: “You’ll see he’s well covered, won’t you?”
“I will,” Violet weakly agreed.
“But I don’t want to go to any hospital,” was Henry’s second protest. “Why can’t you do the business here?”
“Impossible in a house!” the doctor announced. “You can only do that sort of thing where you’ve got all the apparatus and conveniences. But I’ll make it all smooth for you.”
“Oh, no! Oh, no! Not to a hospital!”
The doctor said callously:
“I doubt whether you realize how ill you are, my friend.”
“I’m not *that* ill. When should I come out again?”
“The moment you are better.”
“Oh, no! No hospital for me. There’s two of them here to nurse me.”
“Your wife is not in a condition to nurse you. You must remember that, please.﻿ ﻿… Better get him there by eleven o’clock. I shall probably be there first. I’ll give you the order﻿—to let you in.”
Henry ceased to cough; he ceased to feel hot. His condition suddenly improved in a marvellous way. He had been ill. He admitted now that he had been chronically ill. (He had first begun to feel ill either just before or soon after the eating of the wedding cake on his bridal night.)

===Chinese===

病人咳个不停；可谁都明白，这咳嗽不过是他对生火这桩疯举的抗议。
「太热了，」他咕哝，「太热了。」
自我暗示的力量就有这么大：火还没散出半点像样的热，他倒真觉出热来了。他私下打定主意，医生一走就把火弄灭；胡闹总得有个限度。然而，亨利随即迎头撞上一场新的重大危机，炉火的问题顿时缩成了一桩细节。
「不洗胃，我下不了任何结论。」拉斯特医生说着转向薇奥莱特，又飞快转回亨利：「你说那儿不疼？拿得准？」他按了按胸口一个特定的点位。
「不疼。」亨利答。
「这家伙在撒谎。」医生想。「他们就是能撒谎，真叫人叹为观止。我赌什么他都行，他在撒谎。他们为什么要撒谎？」
饶是如此，医生也不能拿稳。而拿不准，本就是他普遍的偏好；他喜欢缓下断语。
「洗胃嘛，*我*倒不介意。」亨利温温吞吞地低语。
「当然不介意。明儿一早我给医院打电话，厄尔福沃德太太雇车送您过去。」又对薇奥莱特说：「您看着他穿戴严实了，行吗？」
「行。」薇奥莱特有气无力地应承。
「可我不要去什么医院。」亨利第二次抗议。「这事儿干吗不能在这儿办？」
「在住家宅子里，办不成！」医生宣布。「那种事，只有在器械设备齐全的地方才做得。不过我会替您把一切铺排顺当。」
「哦，不！哦，不！别送医院！」
医生冷冷地说：
「我只怕您还没意识到自己病得多重，我的朋友。」
「我没病到*那个*份上。我什么时候能出来？」
「一好就出来。」
「哦，不！我不进医院。家里这不有两个人护理我么。」
「您太太的身体护理不了您。这一点请您记牢。……十一点前把他送到最好。我多半先到。我给您入院令——放你们进去。」
亨利不咳了；也不觉热了。他的病情霜然间奇迹般好转。他是病过。他此刻承认，他是慢性地病着。（初觉不适，是在新婚之夜吃婚礼蛋糕之前不久、或之后不久——两下里总居其一。）

===Original===

But he was now better, much better. He was aware of a wonderful amelioration, which surprised even himself. At any rate, he would not go into a hospital. The enterprise was too enormous and too perilous. Once in, when would he get out again? And nurses were frightful bullies. He would be helpless in a hospital. And his business? It would fall to ruin. Everything would get askew. And the household? Astounding foolishness would be committed in the house if he lost his grip on it. He could manage his business and he could manage his household; and nobody else could. Besides, there was no sound reason for going into a hospital. As for washing out his stomach, if that was all, give him some mustard and some warm water, and he would undertake to do the trick in two minutes. The doctor evidently desired to make something out of nothing. They were all the same. And women were all the same, too. He had imagined that Violet was not like other women. But he had been mistaken! She had lost her head﻿—otherwise she would never have sent for the doctor in the middle of the night. The doctor would undoubtedly charge double for a night visit. And the fire, choking and roasting him! He saw himself in the midst of a vast general lunacy and conspiracy, and he alone maintaining ordinary common sense and honesty. He felt the whole world against him; but he could fight the whole world. He had perfect confidence in the fundamental hard strength of his nature.
Then he observed that the other two had left the room. Yet he did not remember seeing them go. Elsie came back, her face smudged, to watch the progress of the fire, which was no longer smoking.
“Where’s your mistress, my girl?”
“She’s talking to the doctor on the landing, sir.”
“You see,” the doctor was saying in a low voice to Violet, “it may be cancer at the cardiac end of the stomach. I don’t say it is. But it may be. That would account for the absence of appetite﻿—and for other symptoms.” In the moonlight he saw Violet wiping her eyes. “Come, come, Mrs. Earlforward, you mustn’t give way.”
“It’s not that,” Violet spluttered, who was crying at the thought that she had consistently misjudged Henry for many months past. Not from miserliness, but from illness, had he been refusing to eat. He *could* not eat normally.

===Chinese===

可他现在好多了，好得多了。他觉出一阵奇妙的好转，连他自己都吃惊。无论如何，医院是不进的。这桩事业太浩大、太凶险。一进去，几时出得来？何况护士是顶顶凶的恶霸。进了医院他就是任人摆布。再说生意呢？要垮的。样样都会乱了套。家务呢？他一松手，宅里准干出惊天动地的蠢事。他的生意他管得住，他的家他也管得住；换了谁都管不住。况且，进医院本就没有一条站得住的理由。至于洗胃——若单为这个，给他点芥末、一壶温水，他保两分钟办完这桩把戏。医生分明是想无中生有。他们全是一路货。女人也是一路货。他还当薇奥莱特不比旁的女人。他看错了！她慌了神——不然她绝不会半夜三更去请医生。夜诊医生必定收双倍。还有这炉火，又呛又烤！他看见自己置身于一场浩大而普遍的疯狂与合谋之中，独他一人守着寻常的常识与诚实。他觉出全世界都同他作对；可他斗得过全世界。他对自己天性根基里那股坚硬的力气，怀有完满的信心。
随后他发觉那两位已出了屋。可他不记得看见他们走的。埃尔西回来了，脸上一抹黑，来看火的进展——火已不再冒烟。
「你们太太呢，我的姑娘？」
「在楼梯口同医生说话呢，先生。」
「您瞧，」医生正低声对薇奥莱特说，「可能是胃贲门端的癌。我没说一定是。但可能是。没有胃口——还有旁的症状——都能对上号。」月光里，他看见薇奥莱特在拭眼。「嗐，嗐，厄尔福沃德太太，可不能垮。」
「不为这个。」薇奥莱特呜咽着说。她哭，是想起了这许多个月来，她一直在错怪亨利。他拒不吃东西，不是出于吝啬，而是出于疾病。他是*吃不下*啊。

===Original===
