He was a stricken man, and to herself she had been accusing him of the meanest avarice and the lowest stupidity. She now in a flash acquitted him on every charge, and made him perfect. His astounding secretiveness as to his condition she tried to attribute to a regard for her feelings.
“What are we to do? What am I to do?”
“Oh!” said Dr. Raste. “Don’t let that worry you. We’ll get him away all right tomorrow morning. I’ll come myself and fetch him.”
At the same moment they both saw the bedroom door open and the lank figure of the patient in his blue-grey nightshirt emerge. The light was behind him, and threw his shadow across them. Elsie stood scared in the background.
“It’s not the slightest use you two standing chattering there,” Henry murmured bitterly. “I’m not going into a hospital, so you may as well know it.”
“Oh, Henry!”
“Better get back to bed, Mr. Earlforward,” said the doctor rather grimly and coldly.
“I’m going back to bed. I don’t need you or anybody else to tell me I oughtn’t to be out here. I’m going back to bed.” And he limped back to bed triumphant.
Dr. Raste, who thought that he had nothing to learn about the strange possibilities of human behaviour, discovered that he had been mistaken. He could not hide that he was somewhat impressed. He again assured Violet that it would be all right in the morning, but he was not very convincing. As for Violet, since Dr. Raste was a little man, she did not consider that he had much chance, morally, against her husband, who was unlike all other men, and, indeed, the most formidable man on earth.

===Chinese===

他是个沉疴在身的人；而她心里，却一直控着他最卑下的贪婪、最低下的愚蠢。此刻她霜时间撤回了全部控罪，把他造成了完人。他对自己病情那骇人的隐瞒，她竭力归之于顾惜她的感受。
「咱们怎么办？我怎么办？」
「嗐！」拉斯特医生说。「这个交给我。明儿早上咱们准能把送走。我亲自来接他。」
同一刻，两人都看见卧室门开了，病人穿着蓝灰睡衣那瘦长的身影走了出来。灯光在他背后，把他的影子投到他们身上。埃尔西吓得立在后头。
「你们两个站在那儿嚼舌根，半点用也没有。」亨利苦涩地喃喃。「我不进医院，你们趁早知道。」
「哦，亨利！」
「还是回床上去吧，厄尔福沃德先生。」医生说，相当冷硬。
「我正要回床上去。用不着你、也用不着任何人来告诉我我不该出来。我回床上去。」说罢他一瘸一拐凯旋般回到床上。
拉斯特医生原以为自己对于人类行为的种种怪可能已无课可学；这会儿发现看走了眼。他掩饰不住，多少有些被镇住。他再次向薇奥莱特担保明早一切顺当，可话说得不太叫人信。至于薇奥莱特——拉斯特医生是个小个子，她不认为他在精神上能有多大胜算，去对付她丈夫；她丈夫不类世间一切男子，实在是天底下最可畏的人。

## IV Out of the Rain / 四 避雨
===Original===

Mrs. Perkins’s boy, who lived with Mrs. Perkins in the house next door to Elsie’s old home in Riceyman Square, and who had a chivalric regard for Elsie, fortunately happened to be out in the Square. In the darkness he was engaged in amorous dialectic with a girl of his own age﻿—fourteen or fifteen﻿—and they were both imperfectly sheltering under the eve of an outhouse (church property) at the northeast corner of the churchyard. Their voices were raised from time to time, and Elsie recognized his as she approached the house. Mrs. Perkins’s boy wore over his head a sack which he had irregularly borrowed for the night from the express parcel company in the tails of whose vans he spent about twelve hours a day hanging on to a piece of string suspended from the van roof. That he had energy left in the evening to practise savagely-delicate sentimental backchat in the rain was proof enough of a somewhat remarkable quality of “brightness.”
Elsie had chosen him for her mission because he was hardened to the world and thoroughly accustomed to the enterprise of affronting entrance-halls and claiming the attention of the guardians thereof. She now called to him across the roadway in an assured, commanding tone which indicated that she knew him to be her slave and that, in spite of her advanced years, she could more than hold her own with him against any chit in the Square. There was an aspect of Elsie’s individuality which no living person knew except Mrs. Perkins’s boy. He went hurrying to her.
“I want you to run down to the hospital with this letter and be sure to tell the porter it is to be given to Mrs. Earlforward tonight. She’s in there. And here’s sixpence for you, and I’ll lend you my umbrella and I’ll get it again from your mammy tomorrow morning; but you must just walk to the Steps with me first because I don’t want to get wet.”
“Right-o, Elsie!” he agreed in his rough, breaking voice, and louder: “So long, Nell!”
“Put it in your pocket now,” Elsie said, handing him the letter. “No; don’t take the keys.” She was still carrying Mr. Earlforward’s bunch of keys.
The boy insisted on taking the umbrella, which gave him almost as much happiness as the sixpence. Never before had he had the opportunity to show off with an umbrella. He wished that he could get rid of the sack, which did not at all match the umbrella’s glory.
“Here, hold on!”

===Chinese===

珀金斯太太的儿子——他和珀金斯太太住在莱斯门广场埃尔西老家隔壁的房子里，对埃尔西怀着一种骑士式的情意——碰巧正待在广场上，真是万幸。黑暗里，他正和一个同龄的女孩——十四五岁——进行着情爱的论辩，两人挤在教堂墓地东北角一间杂屋（教堂的产业）的屋檐下，遮得并不严实。两人的嗓门不时拔高，埃尔西走近房子时听出了他的声音。珀金斯太太的儿子头上罩着一条麻袋——那是他夜间从快运包裹公司「非正规」借来的；他每天约有十二个钟头吊在那家公司货车的车尾，手里攥着一根从车顶垂下来的绳子。到了晚上他竟还有力气在雨里演习那种粗野又精细的打情骂俏，足见他身上有一种相当了不起的「机灵」劲儿。
埃尔西挑他来办这桩差事，是因为他久经世故，对闯门厅、唤起门厅主人注意这门营生早已惯熟。这时她隔着马路喊他，声气笃定、带着号令的意味——那声气表明：她知道他是她的奴仆；也表明：尽管她年长几岁，对上广场里的哪个小丫头，她都能稳稳占他上风。埃尔西性情中有一副面目，世上除了珀金斯太太的儿子，没有第二个人知道。他急急忙忙朝她跑去。
「我要你拿着这封信跑一趟医院，务必告诉门房，今晚就得交到厄尔福沃德太太手里。她住在里头。这儿给你六便士，伞借你撑，明儿早上我再去你妈那儿取。不过你得先陪我走到台阶那儿，我不想淋湿。」
「好嘞，埃尔西！」他用粗嘎的、正变声的嗓子应道，又提高嗓门：「回见，内尔！」
「信这就揣进兜里。」埃尔西把信递给他。「别——钥匙不给你。」她还提着厄尔福沃德先生那一串钥匙呢。
那男孩非拿伞不可，这伞带给他的快活几乎不亚于那六便士。他此前从没有过撑伞招摇的机会。他真想甩掉那条麻袋——它跟伞的气派太不相称了。
「哎，等等！」

===Original===

He stopped her and threw the sack over the railings into his mother’s area. They walked together towards the Steps.
“Your Joe’s been asking for you tonight,” he said suddenly.
“My Joe!” She stood still, then leaned against the railings.
“Here! Come *on*!” he adjured her, nervously sniggering in a cheeky way to hide the emotion in him caused by hers.
Elsie obeyed.
“How do you know?”
“Nell just told me. It’s all about.”
“Where d’e call?”
“Hocketts’s.”
“What’d they tell him?”
“Told him where you was living, I suppose.”
“D’you know when he was inquiring?”
“Oh, some time tonight, I s’pose.”
“Now you hurry with that letter, Jerry,” she said at the shop-door. Mrs. Perkins’s boy sailed round the corner into King’s Cross Road with the umbrella on high.
Elsie had the feeling that she had not herself spoken to Jerry at all, but that she had heard someone else speaking to him with her voice. And she was quite giddy between the influences of fear and of happiness. Her hands and feet were very cold. All kinds of memories and hopes which she had murdered in cold blood and buried deep came rushing and thronging out of their graves, intensely alive, and overwhelmed her mind. The anarchy within her was such that she had to think painfully before she could even command her fingers to open the shop-door.
Entering from the street, you had to cross the full length of the shop to the wall between it and the office in order to turn on the electric light. As Elsie passed gropingly between the bays of shelves she thought that she heard a sound of movement, and then the question struck and shook her: “Was the door latched or unlatched when I opened it?” She could not be sure, so uncertain and clumsy had been her hands. She dared not, for a moment, light the shop lest she should see something sinister or something that she wanted too much to see.
Turning the switch at last, she looked and explored with apprehensive eyes all of the shop that could be seen from the office doorway. Nothing! But the recesses of the bays nearest the front of the shop were hidden from her. She listened. Not a sound within the shop, and outside only the customary sounds which she never noticed unless attentively listening. She would go upstairs. She would extinguish the light and go upstairs. No! She could not, anyhow, leave the shop. She must wait.

===Chinese===

他拦住她，把麻袋越过栏杆扔进他母亲家的地下采光井里。两人一同朝台阶走去。
「你的乔今儿晚上一直在打听你。」他忽然说。
「我的乔！」她站住了，跟着靠在栏杆上。
「嗐！快走哇！」他催她，放肆地嘿嘿窃笑，好掩饰她惹起他心头的激动。
埃尔西依了他。
「你怎么知道的？」
「内尔刚告诉我的。都传开了。」
「他在哪儿打听来着？」
「霍基特家。」
「他们跟他说什么了？」
「大概告诉他你住哪儿了吧。」
「你知道他是什么时候问的吗？」
「哦，今儿晚上什么时候吧，我猜。」
「好了，赶紧送信去吧，杰里。」她在店门口说。珀金斯太太的儿子高擎着伞，神气十足地拐过街角，进了国王十字路。
埃尔西只觉得自己压根儿不是在同杰里说话，倒像是听见另一个人用她的嗓音在跟他讲。恐惧和幸福两下夹攻，她头晕目眩。她的手脚冰凉。种种记忆和指望——那些被她狠心杀死、深深埋掉的——这会儿都活蹦乱跳地从坟里涌出来、挤出来，漫过了她的头脑。她心里乱作一团，连吩咐手指去开店门，都得先苦苦想上一想。
从街上进来，得穿过整个店堂，走到店堂与账房间相隔的那面墙跟前，才能打开电灯。埃尔西在一排排货架间摸索着往前走，仿佛听见一阵响动，跟着一个问题劈头把她震住：「我方才开门的时候，门到底是闩着的还是没闩着的？」她拿不准，那双手实在太慌太笨。有一瞬她不敢开灯，怕看见什么凶险的东西，或是她太想看见的什么东西。
终于拧开开关，她提心吊胆地用眼睛搜寻账房门口望得见的整个店堂。什么也没有！可最靠店门前部那几排货架的暗处，她望不见。她侧耳听。店里一点声息也没有，外头只有那些惯常的响动——不留神去听是从来察觉不到的。她要上楼去。她要灭了灯上楼去。不！她无论如何走不开店堂。她得等。

===Original===

She must open the door and look forth at short intervals to see if Joe was coming. She must even leave the door ajar for him. He was bound to come sooner or later. He knew where she was, and it was impossible that he should not come. She heard a very faint noise, which sounded through the shop and in her ears like the discharge of a gun or the herald of an earthquake. Then a silence equally terrifying! The faint noise appeared to come from the bay at the end of which was the window giving on King’s Cross Road. She could see about half, perhaps more, of this bay, but not all. She must go and look. Her skin crept and tingled. The shop was now for her peopled with invisible menaces. Mr. Earlforward was so forgotten that he might have been dead a hundred years. She must go and look. She did go and look. Her heart faltered horribly. There was indeed a heap of something lying under the side-window.
“Joe!” she cried, but in a whisper, lest by some infernal magic Mr. Earlforward up in his bedroom should overhear.
Joe was a lump of feeble life enveloped in loose, wet garments. His hat had fallen on the floor and was wetting it. He had grown a thin beard. Elsie knelt down by him and took his head in her arms and kissed his pale face; her rich lips found his dry and shrivelled up. He recognized her without apparently looking at her. She knew this by the responsiveness of his lips.
“I’m very thirsty,” he murmured in his deep voice, which to hear again thrilled her. (Strange that, wet to the skin, he should be thirsty!)
Though she knew that he was ill, and perhaps very ill, she felt happier in that moment than she had ever felt. Happiness, exultant and ecstatic, rushed over her, into her, permeating and surrounding her. She cared for nothing save that she had him. She had no curiosity as to what he had been doing, what sufferings he had experienced, how his illness had come about, what his illness was. She lived exclusively in the moment. She did not even trouble about his thirst. Then gradually a poignant yet sweet remorse grew in her because, a year ago, before his vanishing, she had treated him harshly. She had acted for the best in the interests of his welfare, but was it right to be implacable, as she had been implacable, towards a victim such as he unquestionably was? Would it not have been better to ruin and kill him with kindness and surrender?

她得隔一小会儿就开开门往外望望，看乔来了没有。她甚至该为他把门虚掩着留条缝。他迟早一定要来的。他知道她在哪儿，他不可能不来。她听见一声极轻的响动，那声音穿过店堂、落进她耳朵，好似一声枪响，又像大地震的前奏。随后是一片同样骇人的寂静！那轻响似乎来自尽头那排货架——排尾是对着国王十字路的橱窗。这排货架她望得见一半，兴许一半还多，但望不全。她得过去看看。她皮肤发麻，一阵阵刺痛。此刻这店堂在她眼里挤满了看不见的凶险。厄尔福沃德先生早被忘得干干净净，仿佛死了一百年了。她得过去看看。她真的过去看了。她的心可怕地一颤。侧窗底下，当真瘫着一堆什么。
「乔！」她喊了出来，却是气声，生怕楼上卧室里的厄尔福沃德先生借了什么邪魔法听见。
乔是一团裹在松垮湿衣裳里的孱弱的生命。他的帽子掉在地板上，正洇湿着地板。他蓄起了稀疏的胡子。埃尔西在他身边跪下，把他的头搂进臂弯，吻他苍白的脸；她丰润的嘴唇碰到的，是干瘪枯皱的唇。他分明没有看她，却认出了她。这一点，她是从他嘴唇的回应知道的。
「我渴得很。」他用他那低沉的嗓音喃喃说——再度听见这嗓音，让她浑身震颤。（怪了，浑身湿透，居然还会渴！）
她明知他病了，兴许病得很重，可这一刻她感到比生平任何时候都快乐。狂喜的、出神的幸福漫过她、涌进她，浸透她、围拢她。她什么都不在乎了，只在乎他回到了她手里。他在外头做了什么、吃了什么苦、病是怎么得的、得的是什么病——她一概不好奇。她只活在眼前这一刻。连他的渴，她都顾不上操心。随后，一种又痛又甜的悔意渐渐在她心里生出来：一年前，他失踪之前，她待他太狠了。她原是为他好、为他的前程打算，可对他这样一个不折不扣的受难者，像她那样毫不留情，真的对吗？当初若用温柔和顺从把他惯坏、断送，岂不更好？

===Original===

For Elsie kindness had a quality which justified it for its own sake, whatever the consequences of it might be. And then she began to regret keenly that she had destroyed his letter; she would have liked to be able to show it to him to prove her constancy. Supposing he were to ask her if she had received it, what she had done with it. Could she endure the shame of answering: “I burnt it”?
“I’m so thirsty,” he repeated. He was a man of one idea.
“Stay there,” she whispered softly, squeezing him, and damping her dress and cheeks before loosing him.
She ran noiselessly upstairs and came back with a small jug of cold water from the kitchen. As seemingly he could not clasp the handle, she held the jug to his lips. He swallowed the water in large, eager gulps.
“Wait a bit now,” she said, when he had drunk half of it, and pulled the jug away from him. After twenty or thirty seconds he drank the rest and sighed.
“Can you walk, Joe? Can you stand?”
He shook his head slowly.
“I dropped down giddy.﻿ ﻿… Door was unlatched. I came in out of the rain and dropped down giddy.”
She ran upstairs again, lit her candle, and set it on the floor by her bedroom door. When she had descended once more she saw that the candle threw a very faint light all the way down the two flights of stairs to the back of the shop. She seized Joe in her arms﻿—she was very strong from continual hard manual labour, and he was very thin﻿—and carried him up to her room, and, because he was wet, put him on the floor there. Breathless for a minute, she brought in the candle and closed and locked the door. (She locked it against nobody, but she locked it.)
She was nurse now, and he her patient. She began to undress him, and then stopped and hurried down to the bathroom, where Mr. Earlforward’s weekly clean grey flannel shirt lay newly ironed. She stole the shirt. Then, having secured her door again, she finished undressing the patient, taking every stitch off him, and rubbing him dry with her towel, and rubbed the ends of his hair nearly dry, and got the shirt over his shoulders, and turned down the bed, and lifted him into her bed, and covered him up, and threw on the bedclothes the very garments which in the early morning she had used for Mrs. Earlforward’s comforting.

在埃尔西看来，温柔本身就有一种质地，单凭这质地它便是天经地义，管它后果如何。跟着她又痛悔起来，悔自己毁了他那封信；她多想能把信拿出来给他看，证明自己的坚贞。倘若他问起她收到信没有、把信怎么处置了，她受得住那句羞人的回答吗——「我把它烧了」？
「我渴得厉害。」他又说。他是个一根筋的人。
「待着别动。」她轻轻耳语，紧紧搂了他一把，衣裙和脸颊都浸湿了，才放开他。
她轻手轻脚跑上楼，从厨房拿来一小罐凉水。他似乎握不住罐把儿，她就捧着罐送到他唇边。他大口大口、急切地吞咽。
「先停一停。」他喝到一半，她说着把罐子从他嘴边拉开。过了二三十秒，他把剩下的喝干，叹了口气。
「你能走吗，乔？能站起来吗？」
他缓缓摇头。
「我一发晕就栽倒了。……门没闩。我进来避雨，一发晕就栽倒了。」
她又跑上楼，点起蜡烛，搁在卧室门边的地板上。再下来时，她看见烛光幽幽地一路照亮两段楼梯，直照到店堂后部。她把乔拦腰抱起——常年累月的粗重活儿练得她力气很大，而他又瘦得很——抱进她的房间，因为他浑身湿透，便把他搁在地板上。她喘了一分钟的气，端进蜡烛，关上门，上了锁。（她防谁呢，谁也不防，可她就是锁了。）
她如今是看护，他是她的病人。她动手给他脱衣裳，脱到一半停下，赶忙下楼到浴室去——厄尔福沃德先生每星期换的那件干净灰法兰绒衬衫，刚熨好，正搁在那儿。她把衬衫偷了来。然后重新锁好门，把病人脱了个精光，用自己的毛巾替他擦干身子，把他发梢擦得半干，给他套上衬衫，掀开床被，把他抱上自己的床，掖好被子，又把清早给厄尔福沃德太太暖身用过的那几件衣裳，一股脑儿抛在被面上。

===Original===

There he lay in her bed, and nobody on earth except those two knew that he was in her room with the door locked to keep out the whole world. It was a wondrous, palpitating secret, the most wonderful secret that any woman had ever enjoyed in the history of love. She knelt by the bed and kissed him again and again. He smiled; then a spasm of pain passed over his face.
“What’s the matter with you, Joe, darling? What is it you’ve got?” she asked gently, made blissful by his smile and alarmed by his evident discomfort.
“I ache﻿—all over me. I’m cold.” His voice was extremely weak.
She ran over various diseases in her mind and thought of rheumatic fever. She had not the least idea what rheumatic fever was, but she had always understood that it was exceedingly serious.
“I shall light a fire,” she said, announcing this terrific decision as though it was quite an everyday matter for a servant, having put a “follower” in her own room, to light a fire for him and burn up her employer’s precious coal.
On the way downstairs to steal a bucket of coal she thought: “I’d better just make sure of the old gentleman,” and went into the principal bedroom and turned on the light. Mr. Earlforward seemed to be neither worse nor better. She was reassured as to him. He looked at her intently, but could not see through her body the glowing secret in her heart.
“You all right, sir?” she asked.
He nodded.
“Going to bed?”
“Oh, no! Not yet!” she smiled easily. “Not for a long time.”
“What’s all that wet on your apron, Elsie?”
She was not a bit disconcerted.
“Oh, that’s nothing, sir,” she said, and turned out the light before departing.
“Here! I say, Elsie!”
“Can’t stop now, sir. I’m that busy with things.” She spoke to him negligently, as a stronger power to a weaker﻿—it was very queer!﻿—and went out and shut the door with a smart click.
The grate and flue in her room were utterly unaccustomed to fires; it is conceivable that they had never before felt a fire. But they performed their functions with the ardour of neophytes, and very soon Mr. Earlforward’s coal was blazing furiously in the hearth and the room stiflingly, exquisitely hot﻿—while Mr. Earlforward, all unconscious of the infamy above, kept himself warm by bedclothes and the pride of economy alone. And a little later Elsie was administering to Joe her master’s invalid food. The tale of her thefts was lengthening hour by hour.

===Chinese===

他就这样躺在她的床上；普天之下，除了他们两个，没有一个人知道他在她房里，房门上了锁，把整个世界锁在门外。这是一个奇妙的、突突乱跳的秘密，是爱情有史以来女人享有的最美妙的秘密。她跪在床边，一遍又一遍地吻他。他笑了笑；随后一阵疼痛掠过他的脸。
「你怎么了，乔，亲爱的？你到底得了什么病？」她轻声问，他的笑让她飘飘然，他明显的难受又让她心惊。
「疼——浑身都疼。我冷。」他的声音微弱极了。
她在脑子里把各种病过了一遍，想到了风湿热。风湿热是什么，她一无所知，可她一向听说这病凶险得很。
「我要生个火。」她说，宣布这项惊人的决定时，那口气仿佛女仆人把「情郎」领进自己房里、为他生火、烧掉东家宝贵的煤，本是家常便饭。
下楼去偷一桶煤的路上，她想着：「顶好去瞧瞧那位老先生。」便走进正房卧室，开了灯。厄尔福沃德先生似乎不见坏也不见好。她对他放了心。他目不转睛地瞧着她，却看不透她的身子，看不透她心里那团火热的秘密。
「您没事吧，先生？」她问。
他点点头。
「这就去睡了？」
「哦，不！还早呢！」她从容地笑笑。「还早着呢。」
「你围裙上怎么湿了一大片，埃尔西？」
她一点不慌。
「哦，没什么，先生。」她说着，临走前把灯关了。
「哎！我说，埃尔西！」
「这会儿可停不得，先生。我忙得很。」她跟他说话的口气漫不经心，像强者对弱者——真是怪事！——说罢出去，利落地咔哒一声带上了门。
她房里的炉栅和烟道根本没惯于生火；可以想见，它们此前从没感受过炉火。可它们以新手的热忱各司其职，不多时，厄尔福沃德先生的煤便在炉膛里熊熊烧旺，屋里热得逼人、热得妙极——而楼上的厄尔福沃德先生，浑然不知头顶正在发生的罪孽，只靠被褥和节俭的自豪感独自取暖。又过了一小会儿，埃尔西把主人的病号饭喂给乔吃。她的偷窃清单，一个钟头一个钟头地越拉越长。

## XI Prison / 十一 监狱
===Original===

“Joe,” inquired Elsie, “where’s your papers?”
She had brought his clothes﻿—dry, folded, and possibly wearable﻿—back into her bedroom. She had found nothing in the pockets of the suit except some cigarette-card portraits of famous footballers, a charred pipe, three French sous, and a broken jackknife. These articles, the raiment, and a pair of battered shoes which she had pushed under the bed and forgotten, seemed to be all that Joe had to show for more than twenty years of strenuous and dangerous life on earth﻿—much less even than Elsie could show. The paucity of his possessions did not trouble her, and scarcely surprised her, for she knew that very many unmarried men, with no incentive to accumulate what they could immediately squander in personal use, had no more reserves than Joe; but the absence of the sacred “papers” disturbed her. Every man in her world could, when it came to the point, produce papers of some sort from somewhere﻿—army-discharge, pension documents, testimonials, birth-certificate, etc., etc. Even the tramps who flitted in and out of Rowton House had their papers to which they rightly attached the greatest importance. No man in Elsie’s world could get far along without papers, unless specially protected by heaven; and, sooner or later﻿—generally sooner than later﻿—heaven grew tired of protecting.
All day Elsie had been awaiting an opportunity to speak to Joe about his papers. The opportunity had now come. Mr. Earlforward could be left for an hour or so. Joe was apparently in less pain. The two bedrooms were tidied up. Both men had been fed. Joe had had more quinine. She could not sponge him again till the morrow. She herself had drunk two cups of tea, and eaten the last contents of the larder. She had lighted a new candle﻿—the last candle﻿—in the candlestick. She had brought coal and mended the fire. The next morning she would have a great deal to do and to arrange﻿—getting money, marketing, seeing the doctor and Mrs. Belrose, discussing the funeral with Mr. Earlforward﻿—terrible anxieties﻿—but for the present she was free.
Joe made no answer. He seemed to be trying to frame sentences. She encouraged him with a repetition:
“Where’s your papers? I can’t find ’em nowhere. You haven’t lost them, have ye?” Her brow contracted in apprehension.
“I sold ’em,” said Joe, in his deep, vibrating and yet feeble voice. He looked away.
“Sold ’em, Joe?

===Chinese===

「乔，」埃尔西问，「你的证件呢？」
她把他的衣裳——干的，叠好的，兴许还能穿——拿回了她的卧室。她在这套衣服的口袋里，除了几张著名球星的香烟卡片、一只烧焦的烟斗、三枚法国苏币和一把断了的小折刀，什么也没找到。这些物件，加上那身衣裳，加上一双她塞到床底下就忘了的破鞋，似乎就是乔在世上二十多年奔波冒险生涯的全部家当——甚至比埃尔西的还少。他东西之少并不让她不安，也不大让她吃惊，因为她知道，许多没成家的男人，既然没有攒下什么供眼前个人挥霍的动力，家底也就跟乔差不多；可那神圣的「证件」不见了，让她心神不宁。她那个世界里，人人到了节骨眼上都能从什么地方掏出点什么证件来——退伍证、抚恤金文件、保荐信、出生证，等等等等。连罗顿宿夜所里进进出出的流浪汉都有他们的证件，并且理应极其看重。埃尔西的世界里，没有证件的人走不远，除非老天爷特别保佑；而早晚——通常早多于晚——老天爷会保佑得厌倦。
埃尔西等了一整天，等一个机会跟乔说证件的事。机会现在来了。厄尔福沃德先生可以撂下一个钟头左右。乔看上去疼得轻了些。两间卧室都收拾停当。两个男人都喂过饭了。乔又服了奎宁。到明天以前不能再给他擦身了。她自己喝了两杯茶，吃完了食橱里最后一点东西。她在烛台上点了一支新蜡烛——最后一支。她添了煤，把火拢好。明天早晨她有一大堆事要办要安排——弄钱、采买、看大夫、见贝尔罗斯太太、跟厄尔福沃德先生商量丧事——可怕的愁事——可眼下她是自由的。
乔没作声。他像是在努力组织句子。她重复了一遍鼓励他：
「证件哪儿去了？我哪儿都找不着。你没弄丢吧，嗯？」她的眉头担忧地皱起来。
「我卖了，」乔用他那深沉、震颤而又虚弱的嗓音说。他望向别处。
「卖了，乔？

===Original===

Ye never sold ’em!”
“Yes I have, I tell ye. I sold ’em yesterday morning.”
“But, Joey﻿—”
“I sold ’em yesterday morning to a man as came to meet a man as came out of Pentonville same time as me.”
“Pentonville! Joe, d’ye mean ye’ve been to prison?” He nodded. “What a shame!” she exclaimed in protest, not at his having done anything wicked enough to send him to prison, but at the police having been wicked enough to send him to prison. She assumed instinctively and positively that he was an innocent victim of the ruthless blue men whom some people know only as pilots of perambulators across busy streets.
“There was no option, ye know, so I had fourteen days.”
She dropped on her knees at the bedside, and put her left arm under his neck and threw her right arm over his waist, and with it felt again the familiar shape of his waist through the bedclothes, and gazed into his homely, ugly face upon which soft, dark hair﻿—a beard on the chin﻿—was sprouting. This faith and tenderness made Joe cry.
“Tell me,” she murmured, scarcely hoping that he would succeed in any narrative.
“Oh, it’s nothin’,” Joe replied gloomily. “Armistice Day, ye know. I had my afternoon, and I went out.”
“Were ye in a place, Joe?”
“I had a part-time place in Oxford Street﻿—carrying coal upstairs, and cleaning brasses and sweeping and errands. And a bed. Yes, in the basement. Sort of a watchman. Doctor he give me a testimonial. Least, he sent it me when I wrote and asked him.” (No doubt whatever that she had been unjust to that doctor!) “I went down to Piccadilly to see the sights, and when it was about dark I see our old divisional general in a damn big car with two young ladies. There was a block, ye see, in Piccadilly Circus, and he was stopped by the kerb where them flower-girls are, ye know, by the fountain, and I was standing there as close as I am to you, Elsie. We used to call him the Slaughterer. That was how we called him. We never called him nothin’ else. And there he was with his two rows o’ ribbons and his flash women, perhaps they weren’t flash, and I didn’t like the look of his face﻿—hard, ye know. Cruel. We knowed him, we did. And then I thought of the two minutes’ silence, and hats off and stand at ’tention, and the Cenotaph, and it made me laugh. I laughed at him through the glass. And he didn’t like it, he didn’t. I was as close to him as I am to you, ye see.
