就在这时，埃尔西还没把她潮湿的膝盖从潮湿的地板上抬起来，门外响起一阵非常尖利、非常专横的敲击。
「天哪！那是谁？」
「是铺子的门，」埃尔西说。
「我去。」阿布太太把程序定得相当愉快。她愉快，是因为起居室连同其他房间都收拾完了，到了随时能给可能的买主——买她这铺面和生意的人——观看的状态；她自己并不打算住这间起居室。她还愉快，是因为一个狂乱、愚蠢、却又不全愚蠢的念头：敲铺子门的是厄尔福沃德先生，他给自己编了个什么荒唐的、男人式的借口，好当晚再来一趟。哪怕这么早，她对厄尔福沃德先生已经有了她的想法。她身上那个不死的少女举着一支蜡烛跑下楼，开了铺子的门。她一开门，一个男人粗鲁地往铺子里撞——不是厄尔福沃德先生；一个年轻人，燃烧的眼睛里透着危险的神色，手势里透着阴暗的激动。
「你要干什么？」她质问，努力坚定地控制局面，没能成功。
年轻人瞥了她一眼。她发觉他攦着一把撕破的伞，衣裳湿透。她听见外面的大雨。
「这么晚了你不能进来，」她补一句。「铺子关门了。」
她示意他走。她甚至动手往外推他；纯属莽撞。她想：
「我干吗要这样？他可能袭击我。」
年轻人非但不走，反而扔了伞，扑向那把大切肉刀——她刚给埃尔西切完火腿、留在柜台上的那把。就在那一瞬，阿布太太彻底拿定主意，不再有半点摇摆：她要卖掉这地方，立刻卖，能卖几个钱卖几个钱。此前几个顾客的阴相已经让她有点害怕了。她想起了克勒肯维尔那桩大谋杀案。她明白自己跑到克勒肯维尔来打头就是多么愚蠢。那男人把切肉刀在他头顶和她头顶上挥舞。
「埃尔西在哪儿？」他咆哮着，凶残得要杀人。
阿布

===Original===

Arb began dimly to understand.
“This comes of taking charwomen you don’t know,” she said pathetically to herself. “And yet I could have sworn by that girl.”
Then a strong light shone in the doorway leading to the back room. Elsie stood there holding the wall-lamp in her hand. As soon as he caught sight of her the man, still brandishing the knife, ran desperately towards her. She hesitated and then retreated a little. The man plunged into the room and banged the door.
After that Mrs. Arb heard not a sound. She was nonplussed, helpless and panic-stricken. Ah! If the late Mr. Arb had been alive, how he would have handled the affair! Not by force, for he had never been physically strong. But by skill, by adroitness, by rapid chicane. Only she could not imagine precisely *what* the late Mr. Arb would have done in his unique and powerful sagacity. She was overwhelmed by a sudden and final sense of the folly, the tragedy, of solitary existence for a woman like her. She had wisdom, energy, initiative, moral strength, but there were things that women could do and things that women could not do; and a woman who was used to a man needed a man for all sorts of purposes, and she resolved passionately that she would not live alone another day longer than she could help.
This resolve, however, did not mitigate her loneliness in the candle-lit shop with the shut door in front of her hiding dreadful matters and the rain pelting on the flagstones of Riceyman Steps. She looked timidly forth; a policeman might by Heaven’s mercy be passing. If not, she must run in the wet, as she was, to the police-station. She then noticed a faint light in Mr. Earlforward’s shop, and dashed across. Through the window she could see Mr. Earlforward walking in his shop with a candle in his hand. She tattooed wildly on the window. A tramcar thundered down King’s Cross Road, tremendously heedless of murders. After a brief, terrible interval the lock of Mr. Earlforward’s portal grated, and Mr. Earlforward appeared blandly in the doorway holding the candle.
“Oh, Mr. Earlforward!” she cried, and stepped within, and clutched his sleeve and told him what had occurred. And as she poured out the words, and Mr. Earlforward kept apparently all his self-possession and bland calm, an exquisite and intense feeling of relief filled her whole being.
“I’ll come over,” said Mr. Earlforward. “Rather wet, isn’t it?”

===Chinese===

太太开始隐约明白了。
「这就是雇了不相识的女清洁工的下场，」她凄然对自己说。「可我原先真可以为那姑娘起誓。」
随后，通往后屋的那道门里亮起一片强光。埃尔西站在那儿，手里举着壁灯。男人一看见她，仍旧挥着刀，发疯似的朝她冲去。她迟疑了一下，朝后退了退。男人冲进屋子，砰地关上了门。
此后阿布太太一个声音也没听见。她没了主意，孤立无援，惊恐万状。啊！要是阿布先生还在，他会怎么处置这件事！不是靠力气，他体力从来不行。靠的是手腕，是机变，是急智巧计。只是她想象不出，阿布先生凭他那份独一无二的、强大的睿智，究竟会做什么。一种突如其来的、尘埃落定的感觉将她压倒：像她这样的女人孤身度日，是何等的愚，何等的悲。她有智慧，有干劲，有决断，有道德力量，可女人有女人做得来的事，也有女人做不来的事；一个用惯了男人的女人，方方面面都需要一个男人；她热烈地拿定主意，但凡多一天能免，她绝不多独居一天。
然而这个决心并未减轻她在烛光铺子里的孤独——面前的门关着，门后藏着可怕的事，雨点噼里啦啦砸着赖斯曼台阶的石板。她怯生生向外张望；一位警察也许正蒙上天垂怜路过。若没有，她就得冒雨跑去警察局，就这样一身去。这时她注意到厄尔福沃德先生铺子里有一点微光，便冲了过去。隔着橱窗她看见厄尔福沃德先生手里举着蜡烛在铺子里走动。她疯了一样擂窗户。一辆有轨电车轰隆隆碾过国王十字路，对谋杀案全然置之度外。一段短暂而可怕的间隔之后，厄尔福沃德先生门上的锁咔啦一响，厄尔福沃德先生端着蜡烛，安然出现在门口。
「哦，厄尔福沃德先生！」她喊道，一步跨进去，抓住他的袖子，把事情讲了。她滔滔说着，而厄尔福沃德先生显然保持着全部的镇定与安详，一种精微而强烈的宽慰充满她的全身。
「我过去，」厄尔福沃德先生说。「雨挺大，是不是？」

===Original===

He cut a fine figure in the eyes of Mrs. Arb. He owed his prestige at that moment, however, not to any real ability to decide immediately and courageously upon the right, effective course to follow, but to the simple fact that his reactions were very slow. Mr. Earlforward was always afraid after the event. He limped vigorously into the dangers of Mrs. Arb’s dwelling with his placidity undisturbed by the realization of those dangers. And he had no conception of what he should do. Mrs. Arb followed timorously.
The door into Mrs. Arb’s back room was now wide open; the lamp near the carving-knife burnt on the white table there. Also the candle was still burning in the shop, but the umbrella had vanished from the shop floor. The back room was empty. No symptom of murder, nor even of a struggle! Only the brief, faint rumble of an Underground train could be heard and felt in the silence.
“Perhaps he’s chased her upstairs.”
“I’ll go and see. Anyhow, he’s left the knife behind him.” Mr. Earlforward picked up the carving-knife, and thereby further impressed Mrs. Arb.
“Take the lamp,” said Mrs. Arb.
“Nobody up here!” he called from the first floor. Mrs. Arb ascended. Together they looked into each room.
“She’s taken her jacket!” exclaimed Mrs. Arb, noticing the empty peg behind the door when they came down again to the back room.
“Ah! That’s better,” Mr. Earlforward commented, expelling breath.
“I’ve left my candle lighted,” he said a moment later. “I’ll go and blow it out.”
“But﻿—”
“Oh! I’m coming back. I’m coming back.”
While he was gone Mrs. Arb had a momentary lapse into terror. Suppose﻿—! She glimpsed again the savage and primeval passion half-disclosed in the gestures and the glance of the young man, hints of forces uncontrollable, terrific and fatal.
“I expect he’s that young fellow that’s running after her,” said Mr. Earlforward when he returned. “Seems he’s had shell-shock! So I heard. She’ll have to leave him alone﻿—that’s clear!” He was glad to think that he had found a new argument to help him to persuade Elsie not to desert him.
“She seemed to be so *respectable*!” observed Mrs. Arb.
“Well, she is!”
“Poor girl!” sighed Mrs. Arb; she felt a genuine, perturbing compassion for Elsie. “Ought I to go and tell the police, Mr. Earlforward?”
“If I were you I shouldn’t have the police meddling. It’s all right.”
“Well, anyhow, I can’t pass the night here by myself.

===Chinese===

在阿布太太眼里他形象伟岸。不过他此刻的威望，并非得自什么立刻、勇敢地选定正确有效行动路线的真实能力，而是得自一个简单的事实：他的反应非常慢。厄尔福沃德先生总是在事后害怕。他大步瘸着走进阿布太太住宅的危险，安然之态未因意识到那些危险而受丝毫搅扰。而他全然不知道自己该做什么。阿布太太怯怯地跟着。
通往阿布太太后屋的门此刻敞开着；切肉刀旁边的灯还在那边白桌上烧着。铺子里的蜡烛也还亮着，但伞已从铺子的地板上消失了。后屋空了。没有凶杀的迹象，连挣扎的迹象都没有！寂静里只听得见、只感觉得到地下火车一阵短暂而低沉的轰隆。
「也许他把她追到楼上去了。」
「我上去看看。不管怎样，他把刀留下了。」厄尔福沃德先生拾起切肉刀，就此进一步镇住了阿布太太。
「拿着灯，」阿布太太说。
「楼上没人！」他从二楼喊。阿布太太上去。两人逐屋查看。
「她的外套拿走了！」阿布太太喊道——再下到后屋时她注意到门后空了的挂钩。
「啊！那就好了，」厄尔福沃德先生评点，吐出一口气。
「我的蜡烛还点着呢，」他过了一会儿说。「我去吹掉。」
「可是——」
「哦！我回来。我回来。」
他离开的当口，阿布太太一时又坠入恐惧。万一——！她又矘见那年轻男人的手势和目光里半揭半露的野蛮而原始的激情，窥见那种种不可驾驭的、可怖而致命的力量。
「我估摸他就是缠她的那个小子，」厄尔福沃德先生回来时说。「好像他有弹震症！我听说的。她得躲开他——这明摆着！」他很高兴想到自己找着了一个新论据，好劝埃尔西别弃他而去。
「她看上去那么规矩！」阿布太太说。
「嗯，她是规矩的！」
「可怜的姑娘！」阿布太太叹道；她感到一阵真切的、心乱的怜悯。「我该去报告警察吗，厄尔福沃德先生？」
「我要是你，就不让警察掺和。没事了。」
「嗯，反正我一个人在这儿过不了夜。

===Original===

No, I can’t. And that’s flat!” She smiled almost comically.
“You go off to bed,” said Mr. Earlforward, with a magnificent wave of the hand. “I’ll make myself comfortable in this rocking-chair. I’ll stop till daylight.”
Mrs. Arb said that she couldn’t think of such a thing, and that he was too kind. He mastered her. Then she said she would put a bit of coal on the fire.
“You needn’t.” He stopped her. “I’ll go across and get my overcoat and a quilt, and lock up there. It’ll be all right. It’ll be all right.”
He reappeared with his overcoat on and the quilt a little rain-spotted. Mrs. Arb was wearing a long thick mantle.
“What’s this?” he asked. “What’s the meaning of this?”
“I couldn’t leave you to sit up by yourself. I couldn’t, really. I’m going to sit up too.”

不，过不了。就这么定了！」她几乎是滑稽地笑了笑。
「你去睡吧，」厄尔福沃德先生说着，豪迈地一挥手。「我在这把摇椅里把自己安顿舒服。我待到天亮。」

## II The Customer / 二 顾客
===Original===

The shop had one window in King’s Cross Road, but the entrance, with another window, was in Riceyman Steps. The King’s Cross Road window held only cheap editions, in their paper jackets, of popular modern novels, such as those of Ethel M. Dell, Charles Garvice, Zane Grey, Florence Barclay, Nat Gould, and Gene Stratton Porter. The side window was set out with old books, first editions, illustrated editions, and complete library editions in calf or morocco of renowned and serious writers, whose works, indispensable to the collections of self-respecting book-gentlemen (as distinguished from bookmen), have passed through decades of criticism into the impregnable paradise of eternal esteem. The side window was bound to attract the attention of collectors and bibliomaniacs. It seemed strangely, even fatally, out of place in that dingy and sordid neighbourhood where existence was a dangerous and difficult adventure in almost frantic quest of food, drink and shelter, where the familiar and beloved landmarks were public-houses, and where the immense majority of the population read nothing but sporting prognostications and results, and, on Sunday mornings, accounts of bloody crimes and juicy sexual irregularities.
Nevertheless, the shop was, in fact, well placed in Riceyman Steps. It had a picturesque air, and Riceyman Steps also had a picturesque air, with all its outworn shabbiness, grime and decay. The steps leading up to Riceyman Square, the glimpse of the Square at the top, with its church bearing a massive cross on the west front, the curious perpendicular effects of the tall, blind, ochreish houses﻿—all these touched the imagination of every man who had in his composition any unusually strong admixture of the universal human passion﻿—love of the past. The shop reinforced the appeal of its environment. The shop was in its right appropriate place. To the secret race of collectors always ravenously desiring to get something for much less than its real value, the window in Riceyman Steps was irresistible. And all manner of people, including book-collectors, passed along King’s Cross Road in the course of a day. And all the collectors upon catching sight of the shop exclaimed in their hearts: “What a queer spot for a bookshop! Bargains!﻿ ﻿…” Moreover, the business was of old date and therefore had firmly established connections quite extra-local.

===Chinese===

铺子在国王十字路上有一扇橱窗，但入口连同另一扇窗在赖斯曼台阶。国王十字路的橱窗只摆平装廉价版流行小说，带纸封套，诸如埃塞尔·M·德尔、查尔斯·加维斯、赞恩·格雷、弗洛伦斯·巴克利、纳特·古尔德、吉恩·斯特拉顿·波特的作品。侧窗陈列的是旧书、初版本、插图本，以及著名严肃作家的小牛皮或摩洛哥皮全集中版——那些作品，凡自尊的书籍绅士（区别于书贾）的收藏均不可或缺，历经数十年的批评，已进入永恒敬仰的攻不破的乐园。侧窗必然吸引收藏家和书痴的注意。在那个卑污穷陋的地段——在那里，生存是一场为吃喝住处几乎发狂地奔命的凶险艰难的冒险；在那里，人们熟悉而心爱的地标是酒馆；在那里，绝大多数人只读赛马的预测和结果，星期天早晨外加血腥罪案和多汁的桃色越轨——这扇窗显得古怪地、甚至致命地不合时宜。
然而，这铺子其实摆在赖斯曼台阶摆对了地方。它有一种古色古香的气派，赖斯曼台阶也有一种古色古香的气派，连同它全部的陈旧褴褛、污垢与朽败。通向赖斯曼广场的台阶，台阶顶上广场的一瞥，连同它西门顶着沉重十字架的教堂，那些高大、无窗、土黄色房子奇崛的垂直效果——凡此种种，都触动着每一个血液里异乎寻常强烈地掺着那普遍人类激情——对往昔之爱——的人的想象。铺子强化了它环境的感染力。铺子待在了它恰当的地方。对收藏家这支秘密种族——永远贪婪地渴望以远低于实值的价格得手——来说，赖斯曼台阶的那扇窗无法抗拒。而形形色色的人，包括书籍收藏家，一天里总要经过国王十字路。所有收藏家一矘见这铺子，心里都要惊叹：「多怪的铺位！便宜货！……」何况这生意年代久远，因而建立了牢固的、远超本地的联系。

===Original===

Scores of knowing persons knew about it, and were proud of their knowledge. “What!” they would say with affected surprise to acquaintances of their own tastes. “You don’t know Riceyman Steps, King’s Cross Road? Best hunting-ground in London!” The name “Riceyman” on a signboard, whose paint had been flaking off for twenty years, also enhanced the prestige of the shop, for it proved ancient local associations. Riceyman must be of the true ancient blood of Clerkenwell.
The customer, with his hands behind him and his legs somewhat apart, was staring at a case of calf-bindings. A short, carefully dressed man, dapper and alert, he had the air neither of a bookman nor of a member of the upper-middle class.
“Sorry to keep you waiting. I just had to slip out, and I’ve nobody else here,” said the bookseller quietly and courteously, but with no trace of obsequiousness.
“Not at all!” replied the customer. “I was very interested in the books here.”
The bookseller, like many shopkeepers a fairly sure judge of people, perceived instantly that the customer must have acquired deportment from somewhere after adolescence, together with the art of dressing. There was abruptness in his voice, and the fact was that he had learnt manners above his original station in a strange place﻿—Palestine, under Allenby.
“I suppose you haven’t got such a thing as a Shakespeare in stock; I mean a pretty good one?”
“What sort of a Shakespeare? I’ve got a number of Shakespeares.”
“Well, I don’t quite know.﻿ ﻿… I’ve been thinking for a long time I ought to have a Shakespeare.”
“Illustrated?” asked the bookseller, who had now accurately summed up his client as one who might know something of the world, but who was a simpleton in regard to books.
“I really haven’t thought.” The customer gave a slight good-humoured snigger. “I suppose it would be nice to have pictures to look at.”
“I have a good clean Boydell, and a Dalziel. But perhaps they’d be rather big.”
“Um!”
“You can’t hold them, except on a desk or on your knee.”
“Ah! That wouldn’t do! Oh, not at all!” The customer, who was nonplussed by the names mentioned, snatched at the opportunity given to decline them.
“I’ve got a nice little edition in eight volumes, very handy, with outline drawings by Flaxman, and nicely printed. You don’t often see it. Not like any other Shakespeare I know of. Quite cheap too.”
“Um!”

===Chinese===

几十个懂行的人知道它，并以他们的内行为傲。「什么！」他们会对自己的同道故作惊讶地说。「你不知道国王十字路的赖斯曼台阶？全伦敦最好的猎场！」招牌上「赖斯曼」的名字——那漆剥落了二十年——也增添了铺子的威望，因为它证明了古老的本地渊源。赖斯曼必是克勒肯维尔真正的古老血裔。
那位顾客，双手背在身后，两腿略分开，正盯着一只小牛皮精装的书柜。一个矮个子、衣着一丝不苟的男人，精悍而机警，既没有书贾的气派，也没有中产阶级上层的气派。
「抱歉让您久等。我刚不得不出去一趟，店里又没别人，」书商平静而客气地说，毫无谄媚之态。
「哪里！」顾客答。「我对这儿的书很感兴趣。」
书商像许多店主一样识人相当准，立刻看出：这位顾客的仪态必是成年以后从什么地方学来的，连同穿衣的艺术。他的嗓音里有突兀，事实是，他在一个奇怪的地方学会了超出他原有身份的礼貌——巴勒斯坦，艾伦比麾下。
「我想您手里没有比如一部莎士比亚吧；我是说一部像样的？」
「什么样的莎士比亚？我有好些部莎士比亚。」
「嗯，我说不好。……我早就想，我该有一部莎士比亚。」
「插图本吗？」书商问；此刻他已准确判定来客是个可能颇识世事、但对书而言是门外汉的雏儿。
「我真没想过。」顾客发出一声小小的、好脾气的窃笑。「我想，有画儿看看总归好。」
「我有一部干净的好博伊德尔，一部达尔齐尔。不过它们恐怕太大了。」
「嗯！」
「除了搁桌上或膝上，您捧不住它们。」
「啊！那不行！哦，可不行！」被那几个名字弄得发懵的顾客，赶紧抓住递来的机会回绝了它们。
「我有一部漂亮的小开本，八卷，非常趁手，带弗拉克斯曼的白描插图，印得也好。您不常见到它。跟我知道的任何别的莎士比亚都不一样。也相当便宜。」
「嗯！」

===Original===

“I’ll see if I can put my hand on it.”
The shop was full of bays formed by bookshelves protruding at right-angles from the walls. The first bay was well lighted and tidy; but the others, as they receded into the gloomy backward of the shop, were darker and darker and untidier and untidier. The effect was of mysterious and vast populations of books imprisoned forever in everlasting shade, chained, deprived of air and sun and movement, hopeless, resigned, martyrized. The bookseller stepped over piles of cast books into the farthest bay, which was carpeted a foot thick with a disorder of volumes, and lighted a candle.
“You don’t use the electric light in that corner,” said the client, briskly following. He pointed to a dust-covered lamp in the grimy ceiling.
“Fuse gone. They do go,” the bookseller answered blandly; and the blandness was not in the least impaired by his private thought that the customer’s remark came near to impudence. Searching, he went on: “We’re not quite straight here yet. The truth is, we haven’t been straight since 1914.”
“Dear me! Five years!”
Another piece of good-humoured cheek.
“I suppose you couldn’t step in tomorrow?” the bookseller suggested, after considerable groping and spilling of tallow.
“Afraid not,” said the customer with polite reluctance. “Very busy﻿ ﻿… I was just passing and it struck me.”
“The Globe edition is very good, you know﻿ ﻿… Standard text. Macmillans. Nothing better *of the sort*. I could sell you that for three-and-six.”
“Sounds promising,” said the customer brightly.
The bookseller blew out the candle and dusted one hand with the other.
“Of course it’s not illustrated.”
“Oh, well, after all, a Shakespeare’s for *reading*, isn’t it?” said the customer, for whom Shakespeare was a volume, not a man.
While the bookseller was wrapping up the green Globe Shakespeare in a creased bit of brown paper with an addressed label on it﻿—he put the label inside﻿—the customer cleared his throat and said with a nervous laugh:
“I think you employ here a young charwoman, don’t you?”
The bookseller looked up in mild surprise, peering. He was startled and alarmed, but his feelings seldom appeared on his face.
“I do.” He thought: “What is this inquisitive fellow getting at? It’s not what I call manners, anyhow.”
“Her name’s Elsie, I think. I don’t know her surname.”
The bookseller went on with his packing and said naught.

===Chinese===

「我看看能不能摸到它。」
铺子里满是书架从墙壁直角凸出形成的一格一格。第一格灯亮而整齐；但越往铺子幽暗的后部退去，一格比一格暗，一格比一格乱。那效果是神秘而浩瀚的书群，永远囚禁在永恒的幽暗里，上了锁链，被剥夺了空气、阳光和活动，绝望，认命，受着殉道。书商跨过一摞摞废弃的书，走进最深的一格——那里的书乱糟糟铺了一英尺厚，像铺了地毯——点起一支蜡烛。
「那个角落您不用电灯，」顾客敏捷地跟进来，指着油腻天花板上那盏积灰的灯。
「保险丝断了。它们总是断。」书商安然回答；这安然没有因为他心里那句「这话近于放肆」而减损分毫。他一边找一边继续说：「我们这儿还没理顺。实话说，从1914年起就没顺过。」
「哎呀！五年！」
又一句好脾气的无礼。
「您明天不能进来一趟吗？」书商摸索半天、洒了几滴烛油之后提议。
「怕不行，」顾客礼貌地表示遗憾。「太忙……我路过，一时起意。」
「环球版（Globe edition）很不错，您知道……标准文本。麦克米伦出的。同类里没有更好的。三先令六便士我就能卖给您。」
「听着有戏，」顾客明亮地说。
书商吹灭蜡烛，一只手在另一只手上掸了掸。
「当然，它没有插图。」
「哦，也是，说到底莎士比亚是拿来读的，不是吗？」顾客说——对他而言莎士比亚是一卷书，不是一个人。
书商正把绿色环球版莎士比亚用一张带地址标签的皱棕色纸包起来——他把标签翻在里面——顾客清了清嗓子，神经质地笑笑，说：
「我想您这儿雇了一位年轻的女清洁工，是吧？」
书商抬头，温和地惊讶着，眯眼望。他又惊又慌，但他的情绪很少上脸。
「是雇了一位。」他想：「这多事的家伙想干什么？反正这不是我说的礼貌。」
「她叫埃尔西，我想。姓我不知道。」
书商继续打包，一言不发。

===Original===

“As I’m here I thought I might as well ask you,” the customer continued with a fresh nervous laugh. “I ought to explain that my name’s Raste, Dr. Raste, of Myddelton Square. Dare say you’ve heard of me. From *your* name your family belongs to the district?”
“Yes,” agreed the bookseller. “I do.”
He was very proud of the name Riceyman, and he did not explain that it was the name only of his deceased uncle, and that his own name was Earlforward.
“I’ve got a lad in my service,” the doctor continued. “Shell-shock case. He’s improving, but I find he’s running after this girl Elsie. Quite OK, of course. Most respectable. Only it’s putting him off his work, and I just thought as I happened to be in here you wouldn’t mind me asking you about her. Is she a good girl? I’d like him to marry﻿—if it’s the right sort. Might do him a lot of good.”
“She’s right enough,” answered the bookseller calmly and indifferently. “I’ve nothing against her.”
“Had her long?”
“Oh, some time.”
The bookseller said no more. Beneath his impassive and courteous exterior he hid a sudden spasm of profound agitation. The next minute Dr. Raste departed, but immediately returned.
“Afraid your books outside are getting a bit wet,” he cried from the doorway.
“Thank you. Thank you,” said the bookseller mildly and unperturbed, thinking: “He must be a managing and interfering kind of man. Can’t I run my own business?”
Some booksellers kept waterproof covers for their outside display, but this one did not. He had found in practice that a few drops of rain did no harm to low-priced volumes.

===Chinese===

「既然来了，我想不妨问问您，」顾客继续说，又神经质地笑笑。「我得自我介绍一下，我姓拉斯特，拉斯特医生，米德尔顿广场的。您想必听说过我。从您的招牌看，您家是本地的老户？」
「是，」书商附和。「我听说过。」
他非常为「赖斯曼」这个名字自豪，他不曾解释这只是他亡舅的名字，他自己的名字是厄尔福沃德。
「我家里用着一个小伙子，」医生继续。「弹震症。正在见好，可我发现他在缠这位埃尔西姑娘。完全正常，当然。很规矩。只是这耽误他干活，我想正好到这儿来，您不会介意我问问她的情况。她是个好姑娘吗？我倒愿意他结婚——如果是合适的那类。或许对他大有好处。」
「她挺好，」书商平静而淡漠地回答。「我没说她不好。」
「用她很久了吗？」
「哦，有一阵了。」
书商不再多说。在他不动声色的客气外表底下，藏着突发的一阵深沉的激动。过了一分钟拉斯特医生走了，可随即又回来了。
「您外头的书怕是要淋湿了，」他在门口喊。
「谢谢。谢谢，」书商温和地说，不动声色，心里想：「他准是个爱支使人、爱管闲事的家伙。我自己的生意我自己不会做吗？」
有些书商给外展的书备有防雨布罩，这一位没有。他从经验里发现，几滴雨伤不了低价书。

## I The Day Before / 一 前一天
===Original===

Cytherea reigned in Mr. Earlforward’s office behind the shop﻿—invisible, but she was there﻿—probably reclining﻿—ask not how!﻿—on the full red lips (which fascinated Mrs. Arb) of Mr. Earlforward. It was just after four o’clock in the January following their first acquaintance. They sat on opposite sides of Henry’s desk, with the electric light extravagantly burning above them. At the front of the shop the day was expiring in faint gleams of grey twilight. Dirt was nothing; disorder was nothing; Mr. Earlforward loved. For weeks he had been steadfastly intending to put the place to rights for his bride, and he had not put it to rights. Dirt and disorder were repugnant to Mrs. Arb, but she had said not a word. She would not interfere or even suggest, before the time. She knew her place; she was a bit prim. The time was approaching, and she could wait.
“I suppose we can use that ring,” said Henry, pointing to the wedding ring on Mrs. Arb’s hand, which lay on the desk like the defenceless treasure of an invaded city.
Despite a recent experience, Mrs. Arb was startled by this remark delivered in a tone so easy, benevolent and matter-of-fact. The recent experience had consisted in Mr. Earlforward’s bland ultimatum, after a discussion in which Mrs. Arb had womanishly and prettily favoured a religious ceremony, that they would be married at a Registry because it was on the whole cheaper. Upon that point she had taken pleasure in yielding to him. So long as you were genuinely married, the method had only a secondary importance. She admitted﻿—to herself﻿—that in desiring the church she might have been conventional, superstitious. She was eager to yield, as some women are eager to be beaten. Morbidity, of course! But not wholly. Self-preservation was in it, as well as voluptuousness. Mr. Earlforward’s individuality frightened while enchanting her. She found she could cure the fright by intense acquiescence. And why not acquiesce? He was her fate. She would grasp her fate with both hands.
And there was this point: if he was her fate, she was his; she had already been married once, whereas he was an innocent; he had to learn. She saw an advantage there. Her day was coming﻿—at least, she persuaded herself that it was.
Thus the question of the wedding ceremony had been quite satisfactorily dissolved; and so well that Mrs. Arb now scorned the notion of marriage in a church.

===Chinese===

库忒瑞亚（Cytherea）君临着铺子后面厄尔福沃德先生的公事房——看不见，可她在那儿——多半斜偃着——别问怎么偃！——在厄尔福沃德先生那两片（迷住了阿布太太的）丰厚红唇上。这是他们结识后的第二年一月，刚过四点。两人分坐在亨利的书桌两侧，头顶上电灯奢侈地亮着。铺子前面，白昼正在灰色暮光的微明里咽气。肮脏不算什么；杂乱不算什么；厄尔福沃德先生恋爱了。一连几个星期，他一直打定主意要为新娘把这地方收拾停当，可他没收拾。肮脏和杂乱令阿布太太反感，可她一言未发。时候未到，她不肯干涉，连提也不提。她懂得自己的分寸；她有点拘谨。时候快到了，她等得起。
「我想这枚戒指咱们可以照旧用，」亨利说，指着阿布太太手上那枚结婚戒指——它搁在书桌上，像一座被攻陷城池的无助珍宝。
尽管新近有过一番历练，听到这句用如此轻松、和善、就事论事的语气说出的话，阿布太太还是吃了一惊。那番历练是指：在一番讨论——阿布太太以女人气的好看姿态属意宗教仪式——之后，厄尔福沃德先生温和地下了最后通牒：他们将在登记处结婚，因为总体上便宜些。在那一点上，她乐于顺从他。只要你们货真价实地结了婚，方式只有次要的重要性。她向自己承认：想要教堂，她也许是落了俗套、失于迷信。她巴不得顺从，正如有些女人巴不得挨打。病态，当然！但不全是。里头有自保，也有甘美。厄尔福沃德先生的个性让她着迷，也让她害怕。她发现，用彻底的默从可以治好这害怕。为什么不默从呢？他是她的命。她要用双手抓住她的命。
还有这一层：他是她的命，她也是他的命；她结过一次婚，而他是头一遭；他还得学。她在那儿看见了优势。她的日子会来的——至少，她说服自己会来。
婚礼仪式的问题就这样圆满消解了；消解得如此之好，以至于阿布太太如今藐视在教堂结婚的念头。

===Original===

But the incident of the ring touched her closer; it touched the aboriginal cave-woman in the very heart of her. Do you know, she had faintly suspected that to purchase a wedding ring formed no part of his programme! An absurd, an impossible suspicion! How could he espouse without a ring? But there the suspicion had lain! She ought to have been revolted by the idea of a second husband marrying her with the ring of the first. However, she was not. Mr. Earlforward’s natural, casual tone precluded that. And she answered quietly, as it were hypnotized, with a smile:
“We can’t use this. It won’t come off.”
She displayed the finger. Obviously the ring would not pass the joint. Mrs. Arb was slim, but she had been slimmer.
He said:
“But you can’t be married with that *on*. You can’t wear two.” (Something of the cave-creature in him also!)
“I know. But I was going to have it filed off tomorrow morning. There wouldn’t be time to have it made larger.”
He took the supine hand and thrilled it.
“I tell you what,” said he. “What carat is it?”
“Eighteen.”
“Soft!” he murmured. “I’ve got a little file. I’ll file it off now. I’m rather good at odd jobs. Oh no, I shan’t hurt you! I wouldn’t hurt you for anything.”
He found the file, after some search, in a drawer of his desk.
“It must feel like this to be manicured,” she said, with a slight, nervous giggle, when again he held her hand in his, and began to operate with the file.
He had not boasted; he was indeed rather good at odd jobs. Such delicate, small movements! Such patience! He was standing over her. She was his prisoner, and the ray of the bulb blazed down on the timorous yielded hand. At the finish the skin was scarcely perceptibly abraded. He pulled apart the ends of the severed band and removed it.
“Soft as butter!” he smiled. “Now lend me that other ring of yours, will you? For size, you know. And I’ll just slip across to Joas’s in Farringdon Road. Shan’t be long. Will you look after the shop while I’m gone? If anyone comes in and there’s any difficulty, ask ’em to wait. But all the prices are marked. I’ll leave the light on in the shop. You won’t feel lonely.”
“Oh, but﻿—!” she protested. Leave her by herself in his house﻿—and without the protection of the ring! And before marriage! What would people think?
“Well, Elsie’ll be here in a minute. So there’s nothing to worry over.”

===Chinese===

可戒指这事碰她碰得更深；它碰到了她心底那个原始的穴居女人。你可知道，她曾经隐约疑心：买一枚结婚戒指根本不在他的计划之内！一个荒唐的、不可能的疑心！没有戒指他怎么娶亲？可那疑心就是一直蹲在那儿！第二个丈夫用第一个丈夫的戒指娶她，这念头本该让她作呕。可她并不。厄尔福沃德先生那种自然、随口的语气封了这条路。于是她像被催眠了似的，安静地、带着微笑回答：
「这枚不能用。它取不下来。」
她伸出手指示看。显然，戒指过不了指节。阿布太太苗条，可她从前更苗条。
他说：
「可戴着它没法结婚啊。总不能戴两枚。」（他身上也有几分穴居生物的性子！）
「我知道。可我本来打算明天早上找人锉掉。改大是来不及了。」
他攦住那只仰着的手，让它一颤。
「我告诉你，」他说。「这是几开的？」
「十八开。」
「软！」他喃喃道。「我有把小锉刀。我现在就锉下来。我干零碎活儿还挺在行。哦不，我不会弄疼你的！我怎么也不会弄疼你。」
找了一阵之后，他从书桌抽屉里找到了锉刀。
「做美甲大概就是这种感觉吧，」当他又把她的手握在手里、开始用锉刀动工时，她带着一点紧张的傻笑说。
他没有吹牛；他干零碎活儿确实挺在行。多么精细的小动作！多么有耐心！他箭身在她上方。她是他的囚徒，灯泡的光柱照在那只驯顺的、微微发抖的手上。完工时，皮肤几乎没有擦破。他把锉断的指环两头掰开，取了下来。
「软得像黄油！」他微笑。「现在把你另一枚戒指借我用用，好吗？为了比对尺寸，你知道。我这就到法灵顿路乔斯家去一趟。去不了多久。我不在的时候你照看一下铺子好吗？要是有人进来有什么难处，请他们等一等。不过所有价钱都标着呢。我把铺子的灯留着。你不会觉得孤单的。」
「哦，可是——！」她抗议。把她一个人留在他家里——还没有戒指护身！而且还没结婚！人家会怎么想？
「嗯，埃尔西马上就到。所以没什么可担心的。」

===Original===

He spoke most soothingly, as to an irrational child. “I’d better see to it tonight. And they close at six, same as me﻿—except the pawnbroking. No time to lose!” He was gone.
She was saved from too much reflection by the entry of Elsie. At the sight of Elsie Mrs. Arb’s demeanour immediately became normal﻿—that is to say, the strange enchantment which had held her was dissipated, blown away. She was no longer morbid; she was not supine. Her body resumed all its active little movements, her glance its authority, cheerfulness, liveliness and variety. She rose from the chair, smoothed her dress, and was ready to deal with the universe.
“Oh, Elsie! So you’ve come! Mr. Earlforward was expecting you. He’s just slipped out on urgent business for a minute or two, and he said you’d be in to attend to customers, and I must say I didn’t much fancy being left here alone, because you see﻿—But, of course, business must be attended to. We all know that, don’t we?”
She gave a poke to the dull embers of the stove which warmed the shop in winter; Mr. Earlforward rarely replenished it after four o’clock; he liked it to be just out at closing time.
“Yes’m.”
Elsie, although wearing her best jacket and hat, and looking rather Sundayish, had carried﻿—not easily﻿—into the shop a sizeable tin trunk with thin handles that cut uncomfortably into the hands. This box contained her late husband’s medals, and all that was hers, including some very strange things. The french-polisher’s wife, by now quite accustomed to having three infants instead of two, had procured for herself a pleasant little change from the monotony of home-life by helping Elsie to transport the trunk from Riceyman Square to Mr. Earlforward’s shop-door. The depositing of the dented trunk on the uneven floor of the shop constituted Elsie’s “moving in.”
“I’ll take this upstairs now, shall I, m’m?” Elsie suggested, somewhat timidly, because she was beginning a new life and didn’t quite know how she stood.
“Well, it certainly mustn’t be here when Mr. Earlforward returns,” said Mrs. Arb gravely.
Elsie fully concurred. Masters of households ought not to be offended by the quasi-obscene sight of the private belongings of servants.
“No! You can’t carry it up by yourself. You might hurt yourself. You never know. Come, come, Elsie!” as Elsie protested. “Do you suppose I’ve never helped to carry a box upstairs before?

===Chinese===

他的口吻极尽抚慰，像哄一个不讲理的孩子。「我最好今晚就去办。他们六点关门，跟我一样——典当除外。一刻也不能耽误！」他走了。
埃尔西的到来救了她，免得她胡思乱想。一见埃尔西，阿布太太的做派立刻恢复了正常——也就是说，一直攦住她的那种奇怪的着魔消散了，吹跑了。她不再病态；她不再瘫软。她的身子恢复了全部活跃的小动作，她的目光恢复了权威、欢快、活泼与多变。她从椅子上起身，抻平衣裙，准备好打理宇宙了。
「哦，埃尔西！你来了！厄尔福沃德先生正等你呢。他刚因急事出去一两分钟，他说你会进来照应顾客，说真的，把我一个人留在这儿我可不大乐意，因为你知道——不过当然了，生意总要有人照管。这我们都懂，是不是？」
她捅了捅炉子里暗淡的余烬——那炉子冬天给铺子供暖；四点以后厄尔福沃德先生很少再添煤；他喜欢它赶在打烊时刚好熄灭。
「好的，太太。」
埃尔西虽然穿着她最好的外套和帽子，一副过礼拜天的模样，却（并不轻松地）把一只不小的铁皮箱提进了铺子——箱子的细提手勒得手生疼。箱子里装着她亡夫的勋章，和她全部的家当，包括一些非常古怪的东西。那个法国抛光工的妻子——如今对家里是三个娃娃而不是两个已经相当习惯——帮埃尔西把箱子从赖斯曼广场抬到厄尔福沃德先生铺子门口，为自己从单调的家庭生活里换得一小段愉快的调剂。把这只瘪塌塌的箱子放在高低不平的铺板上，就构成了埃尔西的「搬家入户」。
「我现在就把它搬上楼去，行吗，太太？」埃尔西试探着提议，因为她正开始一种新生活，不大清楚自己的站位。
「嗯，厄尔福沃德先生回来之前它可千万别在这儿，」阿布太太正色说。
埃尔西完全赞成。主人家的老爷，不该被仆人私人物件那种近乎有伤风化的景象冒犯。
「不！你一个人搬不上去的。你会伤着自己的。这谁说得准。得了，得了，埃尔西！」埃尔西还在推辞。「你以为我从前没帮着搬过箱子上楼？

===Original===

Now take the other handle, do! Where’s your umbrella? I know you’ve got one.”
“It’s coming tomorrow, ’m. I’ve lent it.”
Mrs. Arb was extremely cheerful, kindly and energetic over the affair of the trunk, and Elsie extremely apologetic.
“Now nip your apron on and come down as quick as you can﻿—there might be a customer. You must remember I’m not mistress here until tomorrow. I’m only a visitor.” Thus spoke Mrs. Arb gaily and a little breathless at the door of the small bedroom which Elsie was to share with a vast collection of various sermons in eighty volumes, some State Trials in twenty volumes, and a lot of other piled sensationalism.
When Elsie, still impressed by the fact of having a new home and by Mrs. Arb’s benevolent demeanour, came rather self-consciously downstairs in a perfectly new apron (bought for this great occasion), Mrs. Arb went to the foot of the stairs to meet her, and employing a confidential and mysterious tone, said:
“Now don’t forget all I told you about that cleaning business tomorrow, will you?”
“Oh, no, ’m. I suppose it will be all right?” Elsie’s brow puckered with conscientiousness.
Mrs. Arb laughed amiably.
“What do you mean, my girl﻿—‘it’ll be all right’? You must remember that when I come back tomorrow I come back Mrs. Earlforward. And you’ll call me ‘Mrs. Earlforward’ too.”
“I’d sooner call you mum, ’m, if it’s all the same to you.”
“Of course. But when you’re speaking *about* me.”
“I shall have to get into it, ’m.”
“Now I expect Mr. Earlforward’s settled your wages with you?”
“No, ’m.”
“Not said anything at all?”
“No, ’m. But it’ll be all right.”
Mrs. Arb was once again amazed at Henry’s marvellous faculty for letting things go.
“Oh, well, perhaps he was leaving it to me, though I’ve nothing to do with this house till tomorrow. Now, what wages do you want, Elsie?”
“I prefer to leave it to you, ’m,” said Elsie diffidently.
“Well, of course, Elsie, being a ‘general’ is a very different thing from being a char. You have a good home and all your food. And a regular situation. No going about from one place to another and being told you aren’t wanted today, or aren’t wanted tomorrow, and only half a day the next day and so on and so on! A regular place. No worries about shall I or shan’t I earn my day’s wage today.﻿ ﻿… You see, don’t you?”
“Oh yes, ’m.”
“I’ll just show you what I cut out of the *West London Observer* yesterday.”

===Chinese===

来，抓住另一头提手，快！你的伞呢？我知道你有伞。」
「明天到，太太。我借出去了。」
在箱子这件事上，阿布太太极其欢快、和气、干劲十足，埃尔西则一个劲儿道歉。
「现在把围裙系上，尽快下来——说不定有顾客。你得记住，到明天我才不是这家的主妇。今天我不过是个访客。」阿布太太在小卧室门口这样说，语气轻快，微微气喘——埃尔西将同整整八十卷各式布道文集、二十卷《国家审案录》以及一大堆别的耸动文学共享这间卧室。
埃尔西换了条全新的围裙（为这个盛大场合买的），仍怀着「有了新家」的震动和阿布太太慈惠做派的印象，有点拘谨地下楼来；阿布太太走到楼梯脚下迎她，用一种说体己话的、神秘的口吻说：
「明天大扫除那档子事，我嘱咐你的话可别忘了，嗯？」
「忘不了，太太。我想都会妥当的？」埃尔西尽责地皱起眉头。
阿布太太和蔼地笑了。
「你这话什么意思，我的姑娘——『会妥当的』？你得记住，明天我回来，就是厄尔福沃德太太了。你也得叫我『厄尔福沃德太太』。」
「要是您不介意，太太，我宁愿叫您太太（mum）。」
「当然可以。不过你说起我的时候呢。」
「我会慢慢习惯的，太太。」
「对了，我想厄尔福沃德先生跟你把工钱讲定了吧？」
「没有，太太。」
「一点儿都没提？」
「没有，太太。不过会妥当的。」
阿布太太再一次对亨利那种放任自流的本事叹为观止。
「哦，好吧，也许他是留给我办，虽说这房子到明天以前跟我毫不相干。那么，你想要多少工钱，埃尔西？」
「我宁愿听您的，太太，」埃尔西怯怯地说。
「嗯，当然，埃尔西，当『全活女仆』跟当清洁工可是两码事。你有个好住处，包吃。还是个固定差事。不用东家跑到西家，人家告诉你今天不用来、明天不用来、后天只用半天，如此等等！一个固定的地方。不用愁我今天到底挣不挣得着这一天的工钱。……你明白，是不是？」
「哦明白，太太。」
「我给你看我昨天从《西伦敦观察报》上剪下来的。」

===Original===

She drew her purse from her pocket, and from the purse an advertisement of a Domestic Servants’ Agency, offering innumerable places. “ ‘Generals £20 to £25 a year,’ ” she read. “Suppose you start with £20? Of course it’s very high, but wages are high in these days. I don’t know why. But they are. And we have to put up with it.”
“Very well, ’m,” Elsie agreed gratefully.
Twenty pounds seemed a big lump of money to her, and she could not divide by fifty-two. Besides, there it was, printed in the paper! No arguing against that. The two talked about washing and the kitchen and the household utensils which Mrs. Arb had abstracted from the schedule of possessions sold to the purchaser of the business opposite. Elsie sold a couple of books. During this transaction Mrs. Arb retired to the office, and after it she refused to take charge of the money which Elsie dutifully offered to her.
“Elsie, haven’t I just told you I’m not mistress here? You must give the money to your master.”
Then Mr. Earlforward returned; and Mrs. Arb gave Elsie a sign to withdraw upstairs; and Elsie, having placed the money on the paper containing the titles of the sold books, went discreetly upstairs.
“I’ve taken on myself to settle that woman’s wages,” said Mrs. Arb, while Henry was removing his overcoat in the back room. “She told me you hadn’t said anything.”
“No, I hadn’t.”
“Well. I’ve settled twenty pounds a year.”
“Eight shillings a week. Rather less. Anyhow, it’s better than half a crown every morning of your life for half a day’s work.”
“Did you give her half a crown? I only used to give her two shillings. Did you give her any food?”
“Certainly not.”
“Neither did I. Unless she stayed late.”
Mrs. Arb felt upon her Mr. Earlforward’s glance of passionate admiration, and slipped into the enchantment again. She was very content; she was absurdly content. The fact was that Mr. Earlforward had been under the delusion of having driven a unique bargain with Elsie in the matter of wages. For he knew that the recognized monstrous rate was five shillings a day *and* food. And here this miraculous creature, so gentle, submissive and girlish, had beaten him by sixpence a half-day. What a woman! What a wife! She had every quality. He gloated over her.﻿ ﻿… He sat on the desk by her chair, boyishly to watch her girlishness.

===Chinese===

她从口袋里摸出钱包，又从钱包里掏出一页家庭佣工介绍所的广告，上面职位多不胜数。「『全活女仆年薪20镑至25镑，』」她念道。「就按20镑起吧？当然这很高，可如今工价就是高。我不知道为什么。可事实如此。咱们也只好忍着。」
「好的，太太，」埃尔西感激地同意。
二十镑在她看来是一大块钱，她不会拿它除以五十二。再说，白纸黑字印在报上呢！这没法抬杠。两人又谈了洗衣、厨房和家什——那些家什是阿布太太从卖给对街生意买主的财产清单里扣下来的。埃尔西卖掉了两本书。这笔交易期间阿布太太退到公事房去了，交易完她也不肯接埃尔西恭恭敬敬奉上的钱。
「埃尔西，我不是刚告诉过你，我还不是这家的主妇吗？钱得交给你家主人。」
随后厄尔福沃德先生回来了；阿布太太示意埃尔西退到楼上去；埃尔西把钱包在写着卖掉书名的纸上，识趣地上楼去了。
「我自作主张把那女人的工钱定下了，」亨利在后屋脱大衣的当口，阿布太太说。「她告诉我你一个字没提。」
「是，我没提。」
「嗯。我定为一年二十镑。」
「一星期八先令。还略少。不管怎样，总好过每天早上半个克郎买半天活。」
「你给她半个克郎？我从前只给两先令。你管不管她饭？」
「当然不。」
「我也不管。除非她待得晚。」
阿布太太感到厄尔福沃德先生那道热烈倾慕的目光落在自己身上，又滑进了那着魔里。她非常满足；满足得荒唐。事实是，厄尔福沃德先生一直沉浸在一种错觉里，以为在工钱问题上同埃尔西做成了一笔独一无二的买卖。因为他知道，公认的高得离谱的行价是每天五先令外加饭食。而这位奇迹般的造物，如此温柔、如此顺从、如此少女气，硬是每个半天赢了他六便士。怎样的女人！怎样的太太！她样样都占。他把她饱览了个够。……他坐到书桌上，挨着她的椅子，孩子气地欣赏她的少女气。

===Original===

Then he interrupted the tête-à-tête to go and turn off the light in the shop﻿—because the light in the office gave sufficient illumination to show that the shop was open. And he called out to Elsie:
“Elsie, come down and bring the bookstand inside. It ought to have been brought in before. It’s quite dark﻿—long since.﻿ ﻿… Oh! She won’t look *this* way,” he murmured, with a shrug in answer to Mrs. Arb’s girlish alarm as he sat down on the desk by her once more.
“Now here’s the ring I’ve got.” He pulled from his waistcoat pocket a hoop of glittering gold. “And here’s your finger-ring﻿—keeper, do you call it? See! They’re exactly the same size. It’s a very good ring, and it’ll last much longer than the old one. Harder. Nine carat. Looks better too, *I* think.”
Mrs. Arb, examining the ring, kept a smiling, constrained silence. The nine carat was a blow to her. But, of course, he was right; he was quite right. He put the new ring back in his pocket.
“But where’s my old wedding ring?”
“Oh, I sold that to Joas. Flinty fellow, but I don’t mind telling you I sold it to him for six and sixpence more than what I paid for this one.” He spoke, very low﻿—because of Elsie, with a contented and proud calm, his little eyes fixed on her. “I suppose that six and six is by right yours. Here it is.” And he handed her the six and sixpence.
“Oh, that’s all right,” said Mrs. Arb weakly, as if to indicate that he could keep the money.
“Oh, no!” said he. “Right’s right.”
She put the coins in her purse. Then she said it was time for her to be “going across.” (Part of the bargain with the purchaser of her business was that he should provide her with a room and food until the day of the wedding.)
“I hope you’ll slip in again tonight,” he urged.
“Not tonight, Henry. *It’s the night before.* It wouldn’t be quite nice.”
He yielded. They discussed all the arrangements for the morrow. As they were leaving the back room side by side, Henry switched off the light. Elsie had completed her task and gone upstairs. Total darkness﻿—for a few moments! Mrs. Arb felt Henry’s rich lips on hers. She was sensible of the mystery of the overcrowded shop stretching from bay to bay in front of her to the gradually appearing yellow twilight from the gas-lamp of Riceyman Steps.

===Chinese===

随后他中断了这番密谈，去关掉铺子里的灯——因为公事房的灯足以照亮表明铺子在营业。他朝楼上喊埃尔西：
「埃尔西，下来把书摊搬进来。早该搬进来了。天全黑了——早黑了。……哦！她不会朝这边看的，」他在她身边重新坐上书桌时咕哝道，耸了耸肩，回应阿布太太少女式的惊慌。
「瞧，这是我买的戒指。」他从马甲口袋里拽出一圈金光闪闪的指环。「这是你的指环——keeper，你们这么叫吧？看！尺寸一模一样。这戒指很好，比旧的那枚耐用得多。更硬。九开。我看，成色也更好看。」
阿布太太端详着戒指，维持着微笑的、拘谨的沉默。这九开对她是一记打击。可当然，他是对的；他完全对。他把新戒指收回口袋。
「可我原来的结婚戒指呢？」
「哦，我卖给乔斯了。那家伙硬得很，可我不妨告诉你，我卖得的价比买这枚多出六先令六便士。」他说得很低——因为埃尔西在——带着一种心满意足的、骄傲的安详，小眼睛盯住她。「我想那六先令六便士按理是你的。给。」他把六先令六便士递给她。
「哦，没关系的，」阿布太太有气无力地说，意思是这钱他可以留着。
「哦，不行！」他说。「该是谁的就是谁的。」
她把硬币放进钱包。然后她说她该「过那边」去了。（同生意买主谈定的条件之一，是买主要供她一间房和伙食，直到婚礼那天。）
「希望你今夜再溜过来，」他怂恿。
「今夜不行，亨利。这是前夜。不太像话。」
他让步了。他们把明天的种种安排全数商议了一遍。两人并肩离开后屋时，亨利关了灯。埃尔西干完了活上了楼。一团的黑——有那么几瞬！阿布太太感到亨利丰厚的嘴唇贴上了她的。她感觉到那间拥挤不堪的铺子的神秘，从她面前一格一格铺向赖斯曼台阶煤气灯那儿渐渐显现的黄色微光。

===Original===

She abandoned herself, in an ecstasy that was perhaps less, perhaps more, than what is called happiness, to the agitating uncertainties of their joint future. Useless for her to recall to herself her mature years, her experience, her force, her sagacity. She was no better than a raw girl under his kiss. Well, it was a loving kiss. He worshipped the ground she trod on, as the saying was. A point in her favour!
He switched on the light.

===Chinese===

她把自己交了出去，沉入一种或许低于、或许高于所谓幸福的狂喜，交给她和他共同前途那令人心颤的无常。让她去想自己已届中年、自己的阅历、自己的魄力、自己的明达，全是白搭。在他的吻底下，她不比一个生丫头强多少。也罢，这是个多情的吻。俗话说，他拜倒在她走过的地上。这是她的一分！
他拧亮了灯。

## V The Gift / 五 礼物
===Original===

Mrs. Arb was listening to a customer and giving change.
“ ‘And when you’ve got children of your own,’ she said, ‘and when you’ve got children of your own,’ that was her remark,” the customer, an insecurely fat woman, was saying.
“Just so,” Mrs. Arb agreed, handing the change and pushing a little parcel across the counter. She ignored Mr. Earlforward completely. He stood near the door, while the fat customer repeated once more what some third person had remarked upon a certain occasion. The customer’s accent was noticeably vulgar in contrast with Mrs. Arb’s. Mrs. Arb was indeed very “well spoken.” And she contrasted not merely with the customer but with the shop.
There were dozens of such little shops in and near King’s Cross Road. The stock, and also the ornamentation, of the shop came chiefly from the wholesalers of advertised goods made up into universally recognizable packets. Several kinds of tea in large quantities, and picturesque, bright tea-signs all around the shop. Several kinds of chocolate, in several kinds of fancy polished-wood glazed stands. (But the chocolate of one maker was in the stand of another.) All manner of patent foods, liquid and solid, each guaranteed to give strength. Two competitors in margarine. Scores of paper bags of flour. Some loaves; two hams, cut into. A milk-churn in the middle of the shop. Tinned fruits. Tinned fish. Tinned meats. And in the linoleum-lined window the cakes and bonbons which entitled the shop to style itself “confectioner’s.” Dirty ceiling; uneven dark wood floor; frowsy, mysterious corners; a shabby counter covered with linoleum in black-and-white check, like the bottom of the window. One chair; one small, round, iron table. No cash-desk. No writing apparatus of any sort. A smell of bread, ham and biscuits. A poor little shop, showing no individuality, no enterprise, no imagination, no potentiality of reasonable profits. A shop which saved the shopkeeper from the trouble of thinking for himself. The inevitable result of big advertising, and kept up to the average mark by the constant visitation of hurried commercial travellers and collectors who had the magic to extract money out of empty tills.
And Mrs. Arb, thin, bright, cheerful, with scintillating eyes; in a neat check dress and a fairly clean white apron! Yes, she was bright, she was cheerful, she had a keen face.

===Chinese===

阿布太太正听一位顾客说话，一边找零。
「『等你自己有了孩子，』她说，『等你自己有了孩子，』她就是这么说的，」顾客——一个胖得不牢靠的女人——正说着。
「正是，」阿布太太附和，递过零钱，把一个小纸包从柜台上推过去。她全然不理会厄尔福沃德先生。他站在门边，胖顾客则把某个第三者在某个场合说的话又复述了一遍。那顾客的口音明显粗俗，同阿布太太形成对照。阿布太太确实非常「会说话」。而她不仅同顾客形成对照，也同铺子形成对照。
这样的小铺子，国王十字路内外有几十家。铺子的货品和装点主要来自批发商——广告商品，装成一眼认得出的包装。好几种茶叶，大量地堆着，铺子四周围着花花绿绿的鲜艳茶牌。好几种巧克力，摆在好几种花哨的抛光木亮格架上。（不过这一家的巧克力攦在了那一家的架上。）形形色色的专利食品，液体和固体，每一种都保证给力。人造黄油两家对头。几十袋纸袋面粉。几条面包；两只开了膛的火腿。铺子正中一只奶桶。罐头水果。罐头鱼。罐头肉。而在衬着油毡的橱窗里，是糕点和糖果——铺子赖以自称「糖果店」的资格。肮脏的天花板；高低不平的深色木地板；油腻而神秘的角落；一张寒酸的柜台，覆着黑白格油毡，同橱窗底一样。一把椅子；一张小圆铁桌。没有银钱柜。没有任何书写家什。一股面包、火腿和饼干的气味。一间可怜的小铺子，不见个性，不见进取，不见想象，不见合理利润的潜能。一间替店主省去自主动脑筋之劳的铺子。大广告的必然产物，并靠匆匆来往的旅行推销员和收款员频频光顾维持及格线——那些人自有从空钱柜里变出钱的魔法。
而阿布太太，苗条、明亮、欢快，眼睛闪闪发光；一身整洁的格子裙，一条相当干净的白围裙！是的，她明亮，她欢快，她有一张敏锐的脸。

===Original===

Perhaps that was what had attracted Mr. Earlforward, who was used to neither cheerfulness nor brightness. Yet he thought: “It would have been just about the same if she’d been a gloomy woman.” Perhaps he had been attracted because she had life, energy, downrightness, masterfulness.
“Good evening, Mr. Earlforward. And what can I do for you?” She greeted him suddenly, vivaciously, as the fat customer departed.
She knew him, then! She knew his real name. She knew that his name did not accord with the sign over his shop. Her welcoming smile inspired him, as alcohol would have inspired him had he ever tasted it. He was uplifted to a higher plane of existence. And also, secretly, he was a little bit flurried; but his demeanour did not betray this. A clock struck rapidly in some room behind the shop, and at the sound Mrs. Arb sprang from behind the counter, shut and locked the shop door, and drew down its blind for a sign to the world that business was over for the day. She had a fine movement with her. In getting out of her way Mr. Earlforward strove to conceal his limp as much as possible.
“I thought I’d just look in about that cookery-book you wanted,” said he.
“It’s very kind of you I’m sure,” said she. “But I really don’t think I shall need it.”
“Oh!”
“No! I think I shall get rid of this business. There’s no doing anything with it.”
“I’m sorry to hear that,” said Mr. Earlforward. And he was.
“It isn’t as if I didn’t enjoy it﻿—at first. Quite a pleasant change for me to take something in hand. My husband died two years ago and left me nicely off, and I’ve been withering up ever since, till this came along. It’s no life, being a widow at my age. But I couldn’t stand this either, for long. There’s no *bounce* to this business, if you understand what I mean. It’s like hitting a cushion.”
“You’ve soon decided.”
“I haven’t decided. But I’m thinking about it.﻿ ﻿… You see, it’s a *queer* neighbourhood.”
“Queer?” He was shocked, perhaps a little hurt, but his calm tone disclosed nothing of that. He had a desire to explain to Mrs. Arb at great length that the neighbourhood was one of almost unique interest.
“Well, you know what I mean. You see, I come from Fulham﻿—Chelsea you might call it. I’m not saying that when I lived in this shop before﻿—eighteen years ago, is it?﻿—I’m not saying I thought it was a queer neighbourhood then.

===Chinese===

也许正是这一点吸引了厄尔福沃德先生——他对明亮和欢快都不习惯。可他又想：「她哪怕是个阴沉沉的女人，结果也会差不多。」也许他之所以被吸引，是因为她有生气，有干劲，有直率，有霸气。
「晚上好，厄尔福沃德先生。我能为您效什么劳？」胖顾客一走，她突然活泼地招呼他。
这么说她认得他！她知道他的真名。她知道他的名字同他铺子上方的招牌对不上。她那个欢迎的微笑鼓舞了他，如同酒——假如他尝过酒的话——会鼓舞他那样。他被托举到存在的一个更高层面。同时，暗地里，他有点慌乱；可他的做派没有泄露这一点。铺后某个房间里一座钟急促地敲响，一听这声音，阿布太太从柜台后一跃而出，关上铺门落了锁，拉下门上的软帘，昭告世界本日营业结束。她的动作漂亮。给她让路的厄尔福沃德先生，竭力尽可能遮掩他的瘸腿。
「我想着为您要的那本烹饪书过来一趟，」他说。
「您真是太好了，真的，」她说。「可我真觉得我用不着它了。」
「哦！」
「不用了！我想我要把这铺子脱手。这生意没法弄。」
「听您这么说我很难过，」厄尔福沃德先生说。他是真难过。
「倒不是我干得不乐意——起初挺乐意。亲自经管点什么，对我是挺愉快的变化。我丈夫两年前去世，留下一份不错的产业，打那以后我就一直枯萎着，直到这铺子来。我这样的年纪当寡妇，不算生活。可这个我也长受不了。这生意没有劲儿（bounce），您懂我的意思吗。就像打枕头。」
「您这么快就决定了。」
「我没决定。可我在考虑。……您瞧，这地段怪（queer）。」
「怪？」他震惊了，也许还有点受伤，可他平静的语气一点没显出来。他很想向阿布太太长篇大论地解释，这地段几乎是独一无二的有趣。
「嗯，您知道我的意思。您瞧，我从富勒姆来——也可以叫切尔西。我不是说从前我住这铺子的时候——十八年前了吧？——我不是说我那时觉得这地段怪。

===Original===

I didn’t﻿—and I was here for over a year, too. But I do now.”
“I must confess it hasn’t struck me as *queer*.”
“You know this King’s Cross Road?” Mrs. Arb proceeded with increased ardour. “You know it? You’ve walked all along it?”
“Yes.”
“So have I. Oh! I’ve looked about me. Is there a single theatre in it? Is there one music-hall? Is there one dance-hall? Is there one picture theatre? Is there one nice little restaurant? Or a teashop where a nice person could go if she’d a mind?﻿ ﻿… And yet it’s a very important street; it’s full of people all day. And you can walk for miles round here and see *nothing*. And the dirt and untidiness! Well, I thought Fulham was dirty. Now look at this Riceyman Square place, up behind those funny steps! I walked *through* there. And I lay there isn’t one house in it﻿—not one﻿—without a broken window! The fact is, the people about here don’t want things nice and *kept*.﻿ ﻿… I’m not meaning you﻿—certainly not! But people in general. And they don’t want anything fresh, either. They only want all the nasty old things they’ve always had, same as pigs. And yet I must say I admire pigs, in a way. Oh, dear!” She laughed, as if at herself, a tinkling laugh, and looked down, with her steady agreeable hand still on the door.
Twice before she had looked down. It was more than coyness, better than coyness, more genuinely exciting. When she laughed her face crinkled up very pleasantly. She had energy. All the time her body made little movements. Her glance varied, scintillating, darkling. Her tone ceaselessly varied. And she had authority. She was a masterful woman, but masterful in a broad-minded, genial manner. She was experienced, and had learnt from experience. She must be over forty.﻿ ﻿… And still, somehow girlish! Best of all, she was original; she had a point of view. She could see. Mr. Earlforward hated Clerkenwell to be damned. Yet he liked her to damn it.﻿ ﻿… And how natural she was, dignified, but not ceremonious, willing to be friends at once! He repeated to himself that from the first sight of her he had known her to be a highly remarkable creature.
“I brought the book along,” he said, prudently avoiding argument. She took it amiably from him, and out of politeness inspected it again.
“You shall have it for ninepence. And you might be needing it after all, you know.”

===Chinese===

我不觉得——我那时候还待了一年多呢。可我现在觉得了。」
「我得承认我没觉得它怪。」
「您了解这条国王十字路吗？」阿布太太越发激昂地继续。「您了解吗？您从头到尾走过吗？」
「走过。」
「我也走过。哦！我四下里瞧过。这条街上有一家戏院吗？有一家杂耍场吗？有一家舞厅吗？有一家电影院吗？有一家像样的小饭馆吗？或者有一家体面人想去就去得起的茶馆吗？……可这是一条很重要的街；整天都是人。您可以在这儿周围走上几英里，什么也看不见。还有那脏、那乱！嗯，我原以为富勒姆够脏了。再瞧瞧这赖斯曼广场，在那些滑稽台阶的后面上头！我打那儿穿过去走过。我敢打赌里面没有一幢房子——一幢也没有——不带破窗的！事实是，这一带的人不要东西漂亮、不要东西被人爱惜（kept）。……我不是说您——当然不是说您！是说一般人。他们也不要任何新鲜的。他们只要他们一向有过的那些旧烂玩意儿，跟猪一样。不过话说回来，在某种意义上我还真佩服猪。哎呀！」她笑了，像笑自己，笑声叮咚，垂下目光，那只沉稳可亲的手还搭在门上。
此前她已经垂过两次目光。这不止是娇羞，好过娇羞，更真正地令人心动。她笑起来的时候，脸非常好看地皱出纹来。她有精力。她的身子自始至终做着小动作。她的目光变幻不定，闪亮，又转暗。她的声调不停地变。而她有权威。她是个霸气的女人，但霸得开朗、和气。她有阅历，并从阅历中学了东西。她必已过四十。……可不知怎么，还有少女气！最好的是，她有原创性；她有自己的看法。她看得见。厄尔福沃德先生最恨人诅咒克勒肯维尔。可他喜欢听她诅咒。……而她多么自然，有尊严，而不拘礼节，随时愿意立刻交朋友！他对自己反复说，从看见她的头一眼起他就知道，她是一个高度非凡的造物。
「我把书带来了，」他说，谨慎地避开争论。她和气地接过书，出于礼貌又翻检了一遍。
「您九便士拿去吧。说不定您到底还是用得着的，您知道。」

===Original===

With her face still bent towards *Snacks and Titbits* she raised her eyes to his eyes﻿—it seemed roguishly.
“I might! I might!” She shut the book with a smart snap. “But I won’t go beyond sixpence, thank you all the same. And not as I don’t think it’s very kind of you to bring it over.”
What a woman! What a woman! She was rapidly becoming the most brilliant, attractive, competent, and comfortable woman on earth; and Mr. Earlforward was rapidly becoming a hero, a knight, a madman capable of sublime deeds. He felt an heroical impulse such as he had never felt. He fought it, and was beaten.
“See here,” he said quietly, and with unconscious grandeur. “We’re neighbours. I’ll make you a present of the book.”
Did she say, as a silly little creature would have said: “Oh, no! I couldn’t possibly. I really couldn’t”? Not a bit. She said simply:
“It’s most kind of you, Mr. Earlforward. It really is. Of course I accept it with pleasure. Thank you.”
And she looked down, like a girl who has received a necklace and clasped it on her neck. Yes, she looked down. The moment was marvellous to Mr. Earlforward.
“But I do think you’re a little hard on Riceyman Square,” he said, as she unlocked the door for his departure.
She replied gaily and firmly: “Not one house without a broken pane!” She insisted and held out her hand.
“Well, we must see one day,” said he.
She nodded.
“And if there is,” she said, “I shall pay you a shilling for the book. That’s fair.”
She shook hands. Mr. Earlforward crossed the space between her shop and his with perfect calmness, and as he approached his door he took from his pocket with the mechanical movement of regular habit a shining key.

===Chinese===

她的脸还箭在《点心与小吃》（Snacks and Titbits）上，眼睛却抬起来对上他的眼睛——带点促狭。
「说不定！说不定！」她利落地啪一声合上书。「可我不出六便士以上了，还是多谢您。倒不是我不知您把它送过来是好意。」
怎样的女人！怎样的女人！她正在迅速变成世上最明亮、最迷人、最干练、最妥帖的女人；厄尔福沃德先生则正在迅速变成英雄、骑士、一个干得出崇高事迹的疯子。他感到一阵从未有过的英雄冲动。他抵抗了，被击溃了。
「听我说，」他平静地说，带着无意识的庄严。「咱们是街坊。这书我送您了。」
她可曾像傻小女人那样说「哦，不！我不能要。真的不能」？一点没有。她简单地说：
「您太客气了，厄尔福沃德先生。真的。我当然乐意收下。谢谢。」
随后她垂下目光，像一个收下项链并把它戴上脖子的姑娘。是的，她垂下了目光。这一刻对厄尔福沃德先生妙不可言。
「可我确实觉得您对赖斯曼广场太苛刻了，」他趁她开锁送客时说。
她欢快而坚定地回答：「没有一幢房子不带一块破玻璃！」她坚持，伸出手来。
「嗯，咱们哪天得瞧瞧，」他说。
她点点头。
「要真有，」她说，「这书我付您一先令。这才公平。」
她握了手。厄尔福沃德先生以完全的镇定穿过她铺子和他铺子之间的空地，走近自家门口时，他出于常年习惯的机械动作，从口袋里掏出一把亮晶晶的钥匙。

## IX The Kiss / 九 吻
===Original===

That night Elsie sat in the parlour (as she still to herself called the dining room) by the gas-fire which she had lighted on her own responsibility. An act and a situation which a few days earlier, two days earlier, would have been inconceivable to her! But Joe’s clothes had refused to dry in the kitchen; the gas-ring there was incapable of drawing the water out of them in the damp weather. Now they were dry; some of them were folded on a chair; upon these were laid the braces which she had given to him on his birthday, and which evidently he had worn ever since. To Elsie now these soiled and frayed braces had a magic vital quality. They seemed, far more than the clothes, to have derived from him some of his individuality, to be a detached part of him; she was sewing a button on the lifeless old trousers, and she had taken the button, and the thimble, needle, and thread from Mrs. Earlforward’s cardboard sewing-box in the left-hand drawer of the sideboard. She was working with the tools of a dead lady. At moments this irked and frightened her; at other moments she thought that what must be must be, and that, anyhow, the clothes ought to be put in order; and she could not go upstairs and disturb Joe by searching for her own apparatus﻿—which certainly did not comprise trouser buttons. She tried to be natural and not to look ahead. She would not, for instance, dwell upon the apparently insoluble problem of arranging a proper funeral for Mrs. Earlforward. How could she, the servant, do anything towards that? She dared not leave her patients. She knew nothing about the organization of funerals. She had never even been to a funeral. She had no knowledge of possible relatives of the Earlforwards.
“Tomorrow! Tomorrow! Not till tomorrow﻿—all that!” she said doggedly.
But she failed to push away everything. In the midst of her great grief for the death of Mrs. Earlforward (a perfect woman and a martyr) the selfish thought of her own future haunted her and would not be dismissed. Would Joe ever again wear those clothes which she was mending? He had taken some Bovril (Mr. Earlforward also), but she could not persuade herself that he was really better. She was terror-struck by the varied possibilities attending his death. A dead man secretly in her bed! What a plight for her! She determined afresh to confide the secret of Joe to Mrs. Belrose tomorrow morning.

===Chinese===

那天夜里，埃尔西坐在客厅里（她心里仍把饭厅叫客厅），守着她自作主张点起的煤气炉。这举动、这处境，要在几天前、两天前，对她都是不可想象的！可乔的衣裳在厨房里死活不肯干；那儿的小煤气环在这潮湿天气里没本事把衣裳里的水抽出来。现在干了；有几件叠在椅子上；上面摙着那副背带——她生日那天送他的，而他显然从那以后一直戴着。此刻在埃尔西眼里，这副脏兮兮、磨毛了的背带有一种神奇的、活的品质。比起衣裳来，它们远远更像是分得了他的一部分个性，是他分离出来的一块；她正给那条没有生命的旧裤子钉扣子，扣子、顶针、针线都是从厄尔福沃德太太的硬纸板针线盒里拿的——针线盒在餐具柜左手抽屉里。她用着一位亡故太太的家什。这有时让她别扭、心惊；有时她又想，该怎样就怎样吧，再说衣裳总该拾摄齐整；她也不能上楼去翻找自己的针线包、搅扰乔——何况那里面肯定没有裤扣。她努力自然些，不去朝前想。比如，她就不去细想那个显然无解的难题：怎样给厄尔福沃德太太办一场像样的葬礼。她一个做仆人的，能做什么呢？她不敢掠下她的两个病人。她对办丧事的组织一无所知。她连一场葬礼都没参加过。她对厄尔福沃德家可能有什么亲戚也一无所知。
「明天！明天！都等到明天——那一切！」她倔强地说。
可她没能把一切推开。在对厄尔福沃德太太之死（一个完人，一个殉道者）的巨大悲恸中央，关于她自己前途的自私念头纠缠着她，赶不走。乔还会再穿上这些她正缝补的衣裳吗？他喝了些保维牛肉汁（厄尔福沃德先生也喝了），可她没法说服自己他真的好些了。他死的种种可能把她吓坏了。一个死人秘密地躺在她床上！她将落到什么田地！她重新拿定主意，明天一早就把乔的秘密吐给贝尔罗斯太太。

===Original===

Not that the mere inconveniences of death deeply troubled her. No! In truth they were naught. Or rather, if he died they would have absolutely no importance to her compared with the death itself. Having found Joe, was she to lose him again? She could not face such a prospect.﻿ ﻿…
And then Mr. Earlforward. She was beginning to be convinced that the master really was better. He had taken the Bovril. He had opened one or two of his letters. The shock of the news about Mrs. Earlforward, instead of shattering him to pieces, had strengthened him, morally if not physically. He might recover﻿—he was an amazing man! And, of course, she desired him to recover. Could she wish anyone’s death? She could not be so cruel, so wicked! And yet, and yet, if he lived, she was his slave forever; she was a captive with no hope of escape. A slave, either bowed down by sorrow for the death of Joe, or fatally desolated by the eternal reflection that Joe was alive and she could not have him because of her promise to Mr. Earlforward! She saw no hope; she made no reserves in the interpretation of her vow to the master. She could not see that circumstances inevitably, if slowly, alter cases.
She yawned heavily in extreme exhaustion.
Then her ear caught a faint, cautious tapping below. All trembling she crept downstairs. Jerry was at the shop-door. In the turmoil of distress she had forgotten that she had commanded him to call for orders. She was glad to have someone to talk to for a little while, and she brought him into the office. She saw in front of her, on the opposite side of the desk, a young lad who had most surprisingly and touchingly put on his best clothes for important events. Also he had washed himself. Also he was smoking a cigarette.
Jerry, who was thin and pinched in the face, saw in front of him an ample and splendid young woman﻿—not very young to him, for his notion of youthfulness was rather narrow, but much younger than his mother, though much older than Nell, his fancy of the Square, whose years did correspond with his notion of youthfulness. Elsie was slightly taller than himself. He thought she had the nicest, kindest face he had ever seen. He loved her brow when she frowned in doubt or anxiety; for him even her aprons were different from any other woman’s aprons.

===Chinese===

倒不是说死亡的种种麻烦本身让她深深烦恼。不！实在说，那些不算什么。或者说，他若死了，同死亡本身相比，那些对她将全无分量。好不容易找回了乔，难道又要失去他？这样的前景她无法面对。……
再说厄尔福沃德先生。她开始相信，主人真的好些了。他喝了保维。他拆开了一两封信。厄尔福沃德太太的消息那一击，没有把他击碎，反倒把他击强了——如果说不是身体上，也是道德上。他兴许会好起来——他是个了不起的人！当然，她盼着他好起来。她能盼谁死吗？她不能那么狠、那么坏！可是，可是，他若活着，她就永远是他的奴隶；她是个再无脱身的希望的囚徒。做奴隶——要么被乔的死压弯，要么被那永恒的念头致命地荒芜着：乔活着，而因为她对厄尔福沃德先生的承诺，她得不到他！她看不见希望；她对自己向主人立下的誓言不作任何保留的解释。她看不出，情势纵然缓慢、却必然地改变着事态。
她在极度的疲惫里打了个大大的哈欠。
随后她的耳朵捕捉到楼下一阵轻轻的、小心翼翼的叨门。她浑身发抖地蹑手下楼。是杰里，在铺子门口。愁乱之中她忘了自己吩咐过他来听差遣。有人可以说一会儿话，她挺高兴，便把他领进公事房。她看见书桌对面站着一个少年，为了大事件最惊人、最动人地穿上了他最好的衣裳。他还洗了脸。还抽着一支烟。
杰里——脸瘦而尖——看见面前一位丰满而漂亮的年轻女子——在他眼里不算很年轻，因为他对「年轻」的概念相当窄，但比他母亲年轻得多，虽说比内尔大得多——内尔，他在广场上属意的姑娘，年岁倒是同他的年轻概念相符。埃尔西比他略高。他觉得她有他见过的最好、最和善的脸。她皱眉发愁的时候，他爱看她那额头；对他来说，连她的围裙都跟任何别的女人的围裙不一样。

===Original===

He was precocious, in love as in other matters, but he did not love Elsie, did not aspire to love her. She was above him, out of his reach; he went in awe of her; he liked to feel that she was his tyrant. She was the most romantic, mysterious, and beautiful of all women and girls. Elsie very well understood his attitude towards her.
“I thought I might want yer to run down to the hospital for me, Jerry my boy,” she said. “But I shan’t now. Mrs. Earlforward died this afternoon.”
“It’s all over the Square,” said Jerry, spitting negligently into the dark fireplace, and pushing his cap further back on his head.
Elsie saw that he did not understand death.
“Yes,” said she, “I suppose it is.” She said no more, because of the uselessness of talking about death to a simple-minded youth like Jerry.
“It was very nice of you to bring me my umbrella like that,” she said.
“Oh!” said he, falsely scornful of himself. “It was easiest for me to bring it along like that.”
He had been standing with his legs apart; at this point he sat down familiarly and put his elbows on the desk and his jaw in his hands; the cigarette hung loosely in his very mobile lips. They were silent; Jerry was proud and happy, and had nothing in particular to say about it. Elsie had too much to say to be able to talk.
“Then ye haven’t got anything for me to do?” he asked.
“No, I haven’t.”
“Oo!” He was disappointed.
“But I might have tomorrow. You’ll be off at two o’clock tomorrow, won’t yer?”
“That’s me.”
“Very well then.” She rose.
Jerry was extraordinarily uplifted by this brief sojourn alone with Elsie in the private office of T. T. Riceyman’s. He felt that he was more of a grown man than ten thousand cigarettes and oaths and backchat with fragile virgins in the Square could make him. He sprang from the chair.
“Give me a kiss, Elsie,” he blurted out audaciously. He was frightened by his own cheek.
“Jerry Perkins!” Elsie admonished him. “Aren’t ye ashamed of yerself? Mrs. Earlforward dead! And them two so ill upstairs!”
“What two?” Jerry asked, rather to cover his confusion than from curiosity.
“I mean Mr. Earlforward,” said Elsie. She was not abashed at her slip. With Jerry she had a grandiose role to play, and no contretemps could spoil her performance.
Jerry guessed instantly that she had got Joe hidden in the house, but he never breathed a word of it.

===Chinese===

他早熟，在爱情上同在别的事情上一样，可他并不爱埃尔西，也不敢指望爱她。她高他一等，够不着；他对她存着敬畏；他喜欢感到她是他的暴君。她是所有女人和姑娘里最浪漫、最神秘、最美的。埃尔西非常明白他对她的态度。
「我本来想，兴许要你替我跑一趟医院，杰里我的小伙子，」她说。「可现在不用了。厄尔福沃德太太今天下午去世了。」
「广场上都传遍了，」杰里说着，满不在乎地朝黑洞洞的壁炉啐了一口，把帽子又往后推了推。
埃尔西看出他不懂得死亡。
「是，」她说，「我想是传遍了。」她不再多说，因为同杰里这样心简单的少年谈死亡没用。
「你那样把伞给我送来，真是太好了，」她说。
「哦！」他说，对自己假装不屑。「顺路捎来最省事。」
他一直叉腿站着；这时他熟不拘礼地坐下，两肘支在书桌上，下巴搁在手里；香烟松松地吊在他极灵活的嘴唇上。两人沉默；杰里又骄傲又快活，而且没什么特别要说的。埃尔西要说的话太多，反倒说不出。
「这么说，没什么要我干的？」他问。
「没有。」
「哦！」他失望了。
「可明天兴许有。明天你两点下班，是吧？」
「是我。」
「那就这么定了。」她起身。
在 T. T. 赖斯曼的私人公事房里同埃尔西单独相处了这一小会儿，杰里觉得异乎寻常地振奋。他感到自己成了大人——比抽一万支烟、发一万句誓、同广场上那些脆弱的黄花闺女斗一万句嘴更能让他成为大人。他从椅子上一跃而起。
「亲我一个，埃尔西，」他大胆地脱口而出。他被自己的厚脸皮吓了一跳。
「杰里·珀金斯！」埃尔西训他。「你不害臊吗？厄尔福沃德太太死了！楼上那两位还病得那么重！」
「哪两位？」杰里问，多半是为了遮窘，倒不是好奇。
「我是说厄尔福沃德先生，」埃尔西说。她不为自己的失言发窘。在杰里面前她要扮演一个高贵的角色，什么意外都败坏不了她的表演。
杰里瞬间猜到了她把乔藏在了屋里，可他一个字也不吐。

===Original===

He even tried to look stupid and uncomprehending, which was difficult for him.
“Aren’t ye ashamed of yerself?” she solemnly repeated.
He moved towards the door. Elsie’s glance followed him. She was sorry for him. She wanted to be good to somebody. She could not help Mr. Earlforward. She could do very little for Joe. Mrs. Earlforward was dead, and she could so easily give Jerry delight.
“Here!” she said.
He turned. She kissed him quietly but fully. There were no reservations in her kiss. Jerry, being too startled by unexpected joy, could not give the kiss back. He lost his nerve and went off so absorbed in his sensations that he forgot even to thank the sweet benefactress. In the Square his behaviour to the attendant Nell was witheringly curt. Nell did not know that she now had to cope with a genuine adult.

他甚至试着装出又傻又不懂的样子——这对他不容易。

他朝门口走。埃尔西的目光跟着他。她为他难过。她想对什么人好一点。她帮不了厄尔福沃德先生。她能为乔做的也很少。厄尔福沃德太太死了，而她可以轻而易举地给杰里快乐。
「过来！」她说。
他转过身。她安安静静但又结结实实地吻了他。她的吻毫无保留。杰里被这意外之喜惊得太狠，没能把吻回敬过去。他慌了神，走了，整个人沉浸在感觉里，连谢都没谢这位甜蜜的恩人。到了广场上，他对女招待内尔的态度冷得扎人。内尔不知道，她如今要应付的是一个真正的大人了。

## III The Message of Violet / 三 薇奥莱特的口信
===Original===

“I’m raging in my heart! I’m raging in my heart!” Elsie said to herself. “It makes me gnash my teeth!” And she did gnash her teeth all alone in the steadily darkening shop. “I’m that *ashamed*!” she said out loud.
The origin of her expostulation was Mr. Earlforward’s obstinacy. She was humiliated on his behalf by his stupidity, and on her own behalf by her failure to get him to the hospital. The incident would certainly become common knowledge, and ignominy would fall upon T. T. Riceyman’s. What preoccupied her was less the danger to her employer’s health, and perhaps life, than the moral and social aspects of the matter. She would have liked to give her master a good shaking. She was losing her fear of the dread Mr. Earlforward; she was freely criticizing and condemning him, and, indeed, was almost ready to execute him﻿—she who, under the continuous suggestion of Mrs. Earlforward, had hitherto fatalistically and uncritically accepted his decrees and decisions as the decrees and decisions of Almighty God. He had argued with her; he had defended himself against her; he had shown tiny glimpses of an apprehension that she might somehow be capable of forcing him to go to the hospital against his will. He had lifted her to be nearly equal with him. The relations between them could never be the same again. Elsie had a kind of intoxication.
“Well, anyway, something’s got to be done,” she said, with a violent gesture.
She rushed for her tools and utensils, she found a rough apron and tied it tightly with a hard, viciously-drawn knot over her white one, and began to clean the shop. If seen by nobody else the shop was seen by her, and she could no longer stand the sight of its filth. She ranged about like a beast of prey. She picked up the letters from the floor and ran with them into the office and dashed them on to the desk. And at that moment a postman outside inconsiderately dropped several more letters through the flap. “Of course you *would*!” Elsie angrily protested, and picked them up and ran with them into the office and dashed them on to the desk.
“Oh! This is no use!” she muttered, after a minute or so of sweeping in the gloom, and she turned on the electric lights. Only two sound lamps were now left in the shop, and one in the office. She turned them all on﻿—the one in the office from sheer naughtiness. “I’ll see about his electric light!”

===Chinese===

「我心里直冒火！我心里直冒火！」埃尔西对自己说。「气得我磨牙！」她也确实独自在那渐渐黑下去的铺子里磨着牙。「丢死人了！」她大声说。
她这番抗议的由头，是厄尔福沃德先生的顽固。他的愚蠢让她替他蒙羞，她没能送他进医院又让她替自己蒙羞。这件事必然要传得尽人皆知，耻辱将落到 T. T. 赖斯曼铺子的头上。她心里盘桓的，与其说是对雇主健康乃至性命之忧，不如说是这件事的道德面和社会面。她真想狠狠摇一摇她的主人。她对那位可畏的厄尔福沃德先生正在失去恐惧；她放肆地批评他、谴责他，而且实在差不多要处决他了——她这个人，此前在厄尔福沃德太太不断的暗示下，一直宿命论地、不加批评地把他的法令和决断当作全能上帝的法令和决断来接受。他同她争辩过；他针对她为自己辩护过；他偶尔闪出一丝担忧，怕她兴许有本事违背他的意愿强送他进医院。他把她抬举到几乎同他平起平坐。两人之间的关系再也回不到从前了。埃尔西有一种醉意。
「不管怎样，总得想个办法，」她说，配着一个激烈的手势。
她冲过去取她的家什，找出一条粗布围裙，在白围裙外面狠狠打了个死结系紧，开始打扫铺子。就算没有别人看见，铺子她自己看得见，那污秽她已经看不下去了。她像一头猛兽似地巡行。她把地板上的信捡起来，跑进公事房，啪地摙在书桌上。就在这当口，外头的邮差不顾大体又从投信口塞进来几封信。「你偏来！」埃尔西怒冲冲地抗议，捡起来，跑进公事房，摙在书桌上。
「哦！这样不行！」在昏暗里扫了约莫一分钟之后她啸哝，把电灯全拧亮了。铺子里如今只剩两只好灯泡，公事房一只。她全开了——公事房那只纯属淘气。「他的电灯我来管！

===Original===

she said to herself. “I’ll burn his electric light for him﻿—see if I don’t!” She was punishing him as she cleaned the shop with an energy and a thoroughness unexampled in the annals of charing. This was the same woman who a short while ago had trembled because she had eaten a bit of raw bacon without authority. And when, having finished the shop, she assaulted the office, she drowned the floor in dust-laying water, and she rubbed his desk and especially his safe with a ferocity calculated to flay them. For there was not only his obstinacy and his stupidity﻿—there was his brutality. “Then more fool her!” he had exclaimed about his wife, soon to be martyrized by an “operation.” And he had said nothing else.
Then Elsie began to think of Dr. Raste. Of course, she had been mistaken about Dr. Raste. On the pavement in front of his house he had been very harsh, with his rules about what he ought to do and what he ought not to do. And before that, long before that, when he had given a careless look at her in the house in Riceyman Square upon the occasion of Joe’s attack on her﻿—well, he hadn’t seemed very human. A finicking sort of man﻿—that was what she called him﻿—standoffish, stony. And yet he had got out of bed in the middle of the night for the old miser, and he must have known he could never screw much money out of him. And fancy the doctor coming with a taxi himself to take away the master! Elsie had never heard of such a thing. And him taking the mistress instead! It was wonderful. And still more wonderful was the arrival of his little girl﻿—a little queen she was, and knew her way about. And he’d arranged things at the hospital, too. (Oh! As she reflected, her humiliation at the failure to “manage” Mr. Earlforward was intensified. She could scarcely bear to think of it.) No doubt at all she had been mistaken about Dr. Raste. Joe had always praised Dr. Raste, and she had been putting Joe down for a simpleton, as indeed he was; but in this matter Joe had been right and she wrong. In repentance, or in penance, she extinguished the two lights in the shop, which she was not using; her mind worked in odd ways, but it had practical logic. The cleaning done, she doffed the rough apron.
She was somewhat out of breath, and she seated herself in the master’s chair at his desk. An audacious proceeding, but who could say her nay?

===Chinese===

」她对自己说。「他的电灯我替他烧——瞧我敢不敢！」她一边以清洁女工史上未有的干劲和彻底打扫铺子，一边这样惩罚他。就是这个女人，不久前还因为未经许可吃了一小片生熏肉而发抖。打扫完铺子，她又攻打公事房：她把除尘水泼了满地，她擦他的书桌、尤其是他的保险柜，那股狠劲足以把它们的皮都擦掉。因为不光有他的顽固和他的愚蠢——还有他的狠心。「那她更傻！」他提起妻子——即将被「手术」殉道的那位——时竟那样喊。此外再无一言。
随后埃尔西想起拉斯特医生。当然，她错看了拉斯特医生。在他家门前的便道上，他曾非常严厉，满口他该做什么不该做什么的规矩。再早，早得多，在赖斯曼广场那所房子里、乔袭击她的那次，他曾漫不经心朝她看过一眼——嗯，他那时显得不太像人。一个矫情的男人——她是这么叫他的——冷淡，铁石。可他竟为那老守财奴半夜里爬起床，而且他一定明白，从老头身上他决挤不出几个钱。再想想，医生竟亲自带着出租车来接主人！埃尔西从没听说过这样的事。而他接走的竟是太太！真是了不起。更了不起的是他小闺女的到来——一位小小的女王，门儿清。医院那边也是他安排的。（哦！她一路想，她没能「搞定」厄尔福沃德先生的耻辱就一路加深。她几乎不敢想它。）毫无疑问，她错看了拉斯特医生。乔一向称赞拉斯特医生，而她一直把乔当傻子看——他也确实是傻子；可这件事上乔对她错。出于悔改，或者补赎，她灭掉了铺子里她用不着的两盏灯；她脑子运转的路数古怪，却自有实用的逻辑。打扫完毕，她解下粗布围裙。
她微微喘着气，在主人的椅子上、他的书桌前坐了下来。一个胆大包天的举动，可谁能说她半个不字？

===Original===

She looked startlingly out of place in the sacred chair as she gazed absently at the sacred desk. The mere fact that nobody could say her nay filled her with sadness. Tragedy pressed down upon her. Life was incomprehensible, and she saw no relief anywhere in the world. That man upstairs might be dying, probably was dying. And no one knew what was his disease, and no one could help him without his permission. He lay over the shop-ceiling there, and there was nothing to be done. As for mistress, the case of her mistress touched her even more closely. Mistress was a woman, and she was a woman. She had known a dozen such cases. Women fought their invisible enemy for a time. Then they dropped, and they were swept off to a hospital, and the next thing you heard they were dead.﻿ ﻿… Mrs. Earlforward alone in a hospital﻿—all rules and regulations! And her husband very ill in bed at home here! Nobody to say a word to Mrs. Earlforward about home, and she fretting her heart away because of master, and the operation tomorrow morning and all! *He* was very ill, and people were often queer while they were ill. They weren’t rightly responsible; you couldn’t really blame them, could you? He must be terribly worried about everything. It was a pity he was obstinate, but there you were. Elsie was overwhelmed with affliction, misery, anguish. Her features were most painfully decomposed under the lamp.
But when Mr. Earlforward, answering her tap at the bedroom-door, roused himself to make a fresh and more desperate defence against a powerful antagonist who was determined to force him to act contrary to his inclination and his judgment, he saw, as soon as his eyes had recovered from the dazzle of the sudden light, a smiling, kind and acquiescent face. His relief was intense, and it flowered into gratitude. He thought: “She promised she would never desert me, and she won’t.” He was weak from his malady and from lack of nourishment; he was in pain; he had convinced himself that he was better, but he could not deny that he was still very ill﻿—and Elsie was all he had. She could make his existence heaven or hell; he perceived that she meant to make it as nearly heaven as she could. She was not going to bully him. She had no intention of disputing his decision about the hospital business. She had accepted her moral defeat, and accepted it without reserve and without ill will.

===Chinese===

她坐在那把神圣的椅子里，心不在焉地凝视着神圣的书桌，格格不入得刺眼。只消「谁也不能说她半个不字」这一桩事实，就让她充满悲伤。悲剧压在她身上。生活不可解，而她在世上任何地方都看不见解救。楼上那个男人兴许在死，多半在死。没人知道他得的是什么病，没他的准许谁也帮不了他。他就躺在铺子天花板的上头，而毫无办法。至于太太，她女主人的病例贴她更近。太太是女人，她也是女人。这样的病例她见过一打。女人们同那看不见的敌人搏斗一阵。然后倒下，被扫进医院，跟着你听说的就是她死了。……厄尔福沃德太太独自一人在医院里——满眼的规矩章程！而她丈夫病重在床，在家里这儿！没有一个人对厄尔福沃德太太提半个字家里的事，她正为主人揪心，明早就是手术，还有这一切！他是病得很重，人病着常常会反常。他们算不得真正负责；你不能真怪他们，对不对？他一定为一切都愁坏了。可惜他顽固，可事情就这样。埃尔西被愁苦、悲惨、绞痛压倒了。灯下，她的五官痛苦地散了架。
可当厄尔福沃德先生应着她卧室门上的叨击振作起来、准备对抗那位打定主意逼他违反自己意愿和判断行事的强大对手、作一番新的更绝望的防守时——他一见她在骤亮灯光中那副脸：微笑的、和善的、顺从的。他的宽慰是深切的，并开成了感激。他想：「她答应过永不弃我，她就不会。」他因病和营养缺乏而虚弱；他在疼；他已说服自己他好些了，可他无法否认他仍旧病得很重——而埃尔西是他的一切。她能把他的存在变成天堂或地狱；他看出她打算把它尽量变成天堂。她不打算欺压他。她无意争执医院那件事上的决定。她接受了她的道义失败，毫无保留、毫无恶意地接受了。

===Original===

She was bringing liquid food for him, in an attractive white basin. He had, as usual, little desire for food, but the sight of the basin and the gleaming spoon on the old lacquer tray tempted him, and he reflected that even an abortive attempt at a meal would provide a change in the awful monotony of his day. Moreover, he wanted to oblige her.
As, angelically smiling, she walked round the bed to his side and stood close to him, a veil fell from his eyes, and for the first time he saw her, not as a charwoman turned servant, but as a girl charged with energetic life; and her benevolence had rendered her beautiful. He envied her healthy vigour. He relied on it. The moment was delicious in the silent and curst house.
“I’ll try,” he said pleasantly, raising his body up and gazing at her.
“Why!” she exclaimed. “If you haven’t been making your bed!”
No disapproval in her voice. No warning as to the evil consequences of this mad escapade of making his bed.
“Any more letters?” he inquired, after he had swallowed a mouthful.
“I believe there was one,” she answered vivaciously. “Shall I run and get it for you?” Down she ran and picked up a letter at random off the desk in the office. And she brought back also a sheet of notepaper and an envelope, a millboard portfolio and a pencil.
“What’s all that?” he asked mildly, opening the letter.
“Well, you want to write to missis, don’t you?”
“Um?” he murmured as he read the letter, affecting not to have heard her. He was ashamed and self-conscious because he had not himself had the idea of writing to Violet.
“You’ll be sending a note to missis at the hospital. It’ll give her a good lift-up to hear from you.”
“Yes,” he said. “I was going to write.”
“Here! I’ll take that letter. You can do with some of this food. I shouldn’t like you to let it get cold.” She stayed near him and held a corner of the insecure tray firmly. “You can’t take any more? All right.”
She removed the tray, and replaced it by the portfolio which was to serve as a writing-desk on the bed. It was always marvellous to Elsie to see the ease with which her master wrote. She admired. And she was almost happy because she had resolved to smile cheerfully and give in to him and do the best she could for him on his own lines and be an angel.
“Shall I read you what I’ve written?” he suggested, with a sudden upward glance.
“Oh, sir!”

===Chinese===

她端着流食来了，盛在一只好看的白盆里。他照例不大想吃，可那白盆和旧漆托盘上亮闪闪的勺子让他动了心，他寻思，哪怕是失败的一顿，也能给他这可怕的单调一天换个花样。何况，他愿意让她高兴。
她天使般微笑着绕过床走到他那一侧，站得离他近近的，这时他眼睛上落下一层帷幕，他头一回看见了她——不是一个清洁工出身的女仆，而是一个浑身充盈着蓬勃生命的姑娘；她的慈善使她变美了。他羡慕她健康的元气。他依赖它。在这沉默而乖戾的宅子里，这一刻甘美。
「我试试，」他愉快地说，抬起身子望着她。
「哎呀！」她喊。「您不是自己铺了床嘛！」
她的语气里没有不以为然。没有一句警告，说铺床这桩疯狂妄举会有什么恶果。
「还有信吗？」他咽下一口之后问。
「好像有一封，」她活泼地答。「要我跑去给您拿来吗？」她跑下去，从公事房书桌上随手捡起一封信。她还带回一张信纸、一个信封、一块硬纸板夹子和一支铅笔。
「这都是干什么？」他温和地问，拆着信。
「嗯，您要给太太写信，不是吗？」
「嗯？」他读着信啸哝，装作没听见。他又羞又窘，因为给薇奥莱特写信这个念头不是他自己起的。
「您要给医院的太太捎张条子去。收到您的信，对她是一大提气。」
「是，」他说。「我正要写。」
「来！那封信给我。这个您多少吃一点。可别让它凉了。」她守在旁边，牢牢扶住那只不稳的托盘的一角。「再也吃不下了？好。」
她撤走托盘，换上那块硬纸板夹子，权当床上的写字台。主人写字的那种轻省，埃尔西看着始终是奇迹。她叹服。她几乎快活起来，因为她已拿定主意：要笑盈盈的，顺着他，按他自己的路子尽量待他好，做一个天使。
「我写的要不要念给您听？」他突然抬眼提议。
「哦，先生！」

===Original===

The astounding, the incredible flattery overthrew her completely. He would read to her what he had written to the mistress, doubtless for her approval. She blushed.

> “ ‘My dear Wife﻿—
> As you may guess, I am torn with anxiety about you. It was a severe shock when Elsie told me the doctor had taken you off to the hospital without a moment’s delay. However, I know you are very brave and have an excellent constitution, and I feel sure that before a week is out you will be feeling better than you have done for months. And, of course, the hospital is a very good one, one of the best in London, if not the best. It has been established for nearly eight hundred years. If it was only to be under the same roof as you I should have come to the hospital myself today, but I feel so much better that really it is not necessary, and I feel sure that if you were here to see me you would agree with me. There is the business to be thought of. I am glad to say that Elsie is looking after me splendidly, but, of course, that does not surprise me. Now, my dear Violet, you must get better quickly for my sake as well as your own. Be of good courage and do not worry about me. My little illness is nothing. It is your illness that has made me realize that.
> —Your loving Husband,
> H. Earlforward.’ ”

===Chinese===

这惊人的、难以置信的抬举把她彻底击倒了。他要把写给太太的信念给她听——多半是要她过目认可。她红了脸。

> 「『亲爱的妻子——
> 你可以想见，我为你忧心如焚。埃尔西告诉我医生毫不迟疑地把你送进了医院，这对我是一记重击。不过我知道你非常勇敢，体质极好，我确信不出一星期你就会觉得比几个月来都好。当然，那家医院非常好，是伦敦最好的之一——如果不是最好的。它创建至今差不多八百年了。哪怕只是为了同你在一个屋顶下，我今天自己也该去医院；可我感觉好多了，实在没有必要，而且我确信，你若在这儿见到我，你也会同意我的看法。生意也要有人着想。我很欣慰地说，埃尔西把我照应得极好——当然，这一点也不让我意外。好了，亲爱的薇奥莱特，为了我也为了你自己，你必须赶快好起来。鼓起勇气，别为我操心。我的小病不算什么。正是你的病让我明白了这一点。
> ——爱你的丈夫，
> H. 厄尔福沃德。』」

===Original===

He read the letter in a calm and even but weak voice, addressed the envelope, and then lay back on the pillows. (He was now﻿—since he had made the bed﻿—using Violet’s pillow as well as his own.) He did not finish his food. He left Elsie to fold the letter, stick it in the envelope, and lick and fasten the envelope. She did these things with a sense of the honour bestowed upon her. It was a wonderful letter, and he had written it right off. No hesitation. And it was so nice and thoughtful; and how it explained everything. She had to believe for a moment that her master really was better. The expressions about herself touched her deeply, and yet somehow she would have preferred them not to be there. What touched her most, however, was the mere thought of the fact that once, and not so long ago either, her master had been a solitary single man, never troubling himself about women and no prospect of such; and here he was wrapped up in one, and everything so respectable and nice.﻿ ﻿… But he was very ill. His lips and cheeks were awful. Elsie recalled vividly the full rich red lips he once had.
She had moved away from the bed, taking the basin and putting it on the chest of drawers. The contents of her master’s pockets were on the chest of drawers, where he laid them every night, in order better to fold his carefully creased clothes.
“I do fancy I haven’t got any money,” she said diffidently, after a little while.
“Why, it isn’t your wages day﻿—you don’t mean?”
“Oh, no, sir.”
She had deposited nearly all her cash in the Post Office Savings Bank during her afternoon out, and the bit kept in hand had gone to pay for the unused taxi.
“Why, Elsie! You must be a rich woman,” said Mr. Earlforward. “What with your wages and your pension!” He spoke without looking at her, in a rather dreamy tone, but certainly interested.
“Well, sir,” Elsie replied, “it’s like this. I give my pension to my mother. She’s a widow, same as me, and she can’t fend for herself.”
“All of it? Your mother?”
“Yes, sir.”
“How much is your pension?”
“Twenty-eight shillings and elevenpence a week, sir.”
“Well, well.” Mr. Earlforward said no more. He had often thought about her war pension, but never about any possible mother or other relative. He had never heard mention of her mother.

===Chinese===

他用平静、均匀然而虚弱的嗓音读完信，写好信封，随后躺回枕头上。（他现在——既然铺了床——用上了他自己的枕头，也用上了薇奥莱特的。）他没有把饭吃完。他把信留给埃尔西折、插进信封、舐上封好。她做这些时怀着受宠若惊之感。一封出色的信，一挥而就。没有迟疑。又周到又体贴；把一切都解释得妥妥帖帖。她一时间不能不信主人真的好些了。那些写到她的话深深感动了她，可不知怎么，她倒宁愿没有那些话。最让她感动的，其实是单单这一个念头：就在不久以前，她的主人还是个孤零零的单身汉，从不为女人操心，也毫无那样的指望；而如今他整个儿缠在一个女人身上，一切又这么体面、这么美满。……可他病得很重。他的嘴唇和脸颊吓人。埃尔西清清楚楚地想起他从前那两片丰厚红润的嘴唇。
她已从床边走开，端着白盆把它放在五斗柜上。主人衣袋里的东西在五斗柜上——他每晚都摆在那儿，好把仔细压出褶的衣裳叠得更平整。
「我想我手头是一点钱都没有了，」过了一小会儿她怯怯地说。
「哎呀，还没到你领工钱的日子——你不是那个意思吧？」
「哦，不是，先生。」
她下午歇班时把几乎全部现钱存进了邮政储蓄银行，手头留的那点付了没用上的出租车钱。
「哎呀，埃尔西！你准是个有钱的女人，」厄尔福沃德先生说。「又有工钱又有抚恤金！」他说这话时没看她，声调如梦，兴致却实在是有的。
「嗯，先生，」埃尔西答，「是这样。我的抚恤金给我母亲。她跟我一样是寡妇，她自己没法谋生。」
「全给她？你母亲？」
「是的，先生。」
「你的抚恤金是多少？」
「一星期二十八先令十一便士，先生。」
「嗯，嗯。」厄尔福沃德先生不再说了。他常常想起她的战争抚恤金，却从没想过她可能有个母亲或别的什么亲戚。他从没听人提起过她母亲。

===Original===

He thought how odd it was that for years she had been giving away a whole pension and nobody knew about it in Riceyman Steps.
“Could you let me have sixpence, sir?” Elsie meekly asked, coming to the point of her remark concerning money.
“Sixpence? What do you want sixpence for? You surely aren’t thinking of buying food tonight!” Mr. Earlforward, who had been lying on his right side, turned with a nervous movement on to his back and frowned at Elsie.
“I wanted it to give to Mrs. Perkins’s boy in the Square to take your letter down to missis at the hospital.” In spite of herself she felt guilty of a betrayal of Mr. Earlforward’s financial interests.
“What next?” he said firmly. “You must run down with it yourself. Won’t take you long. I shall be all right.”
“I don’t like leaving you, sir. That’s all.”
“You get off with it at once, my girl.”
She was reduced to the servant again, she who had just been at the high level of a confidante. The invalid turned again to his right side and pushed his nose into the pillow, shutting his eyes to indicate that he had had enough of words and desired to sleep. His keys were on the chest of drawers and several other things, including three toothpicks, but not money. He seldom went to bed with money in his pockets.
Elsie, with a swift gesture, silently picked up the bunch of keys and left the room, a criminal; she had no intention of taking the letter to the hospital herself. She went downstairs quite cheerful; she still felt happier because she had been smiling and benevolent and yielding after her mood of revolt, and because the letter to Mrs. Earlforward was her own idea. In the office she knelt in front of Mr. Earlforward’s safe. No fear accompanied the sense of power which she felt. There was nobody to spy upon her, to order her to do one thing, to forbid her to do another. Her omnipotence outside the bedroom could not be disputed.
