“But aren’t you going to eat any of it?”
“No, ’m,” with solemnity. “But *you* must.﻿ ﻿… I’ll just taste this white part,” she added, picking up a bit of icing from the desk.
The married pair ate.
“I think I’ll go now, ’m, if you’ll excuse me,” said Elsie. “But I’ll just sweep up in the shop here first.” She was standing in the doorway.
They heard her with hand-brush and dustpan collecting the scattered food of the Orient. She peeped in at the door again.
“Good night, ’m. Good night, sir.”

===Chinese===

「这是我送的一件礼物，」埃尔西红着脸继续，把纸包递上。
「我们真是非常感激，」厄尔福沃德太太接过纸包说。「到后屋来，埃尔西，我来打开。挺沉的。不，我还是别拎着绳提了。」
于是在公事房里，割断细绳，揭开牛皮纸和薄纸，露出一只盒子；打开盒子，露出一块结婚蛋糕——确实不大，但货真价实。算上那只鞋、那些米和这块蛋糕，埃尔西以她灵魂的盛大气度，在这场婚礼上必是花了一笔巨款，必是施展了罗斯柴尔德式的豪奢——而这一切只因为她信婚礼仪式古来规矩的功德。唯有她见过厄尔福沃德太太做新娘、厄尔福沃德先生做新郎，而她信念的魔法，逼得这对当事人也把自己看成了新娘和新郎。
「好了，埃尔西，」薇奥莱特脱口而出——她深深动了情——「我真不知道说什么好。太意外了，我不知道怎么谢你。快去拿刀来，咱们把它切开。」
「必须切，」埃尔西说，又是那位女祭司，随后顺从地跑去拿刀。
「哎呀，哎呀！……哎呀，哎呀！」亨利喃喃，目瞪口呆，脸红得比他妻子红过的还厉害。两人心乱得不敢对视。
「您来切，太太，」埃尔西拿着刀和一只平盘回来说。
厄尔福沃德太太把蛋糕搁在盘上，朝蛋糕锯下去。她得用尽全力才能穿透那层棕的；顶上的糖霜应声碎裂，碎屑在书桌上四处飞溅。
「好了，埃尔西，这一片是你的，」薇奥莱特端起盘子说。
「谢谢您，太太。可我得把我那片留着。我楼上有只小盒子装它。」
「可你一点不吃吗？」
「不吃，太太，」庄重地说。「可您必须吃。……我就尝尝这点白的，」她添一句，从书桌上拈起一小块糖霜。
这对已婚者吃了。
「我想我现在该走了，太太，请您原谅，」埃尔西说。「不过我先把这些米扫一扫。」她站在门口。
他们听见她用扫帚和簸箕收集那散落的东方食粮。她又朝门里张望。
「晚安，太太。晚安，先生。」

===Original===

She saluted them with a benignant grin in which was a surprising little touch of naughtiness. And then they heard her receding footfalls as she ascended cautiously the dark flights of stairs and entered into her inviolable private life on the top floor.
“It would never have done not to eat it,” said Violet.
“No,” Henry agreed.
“She’s a wonder, that girl is! You could have knocked me down with a feather.”
“Yes.”
“I wonder where she bought it.”
“Must have gone up to King’s Cross. Or down to Holborn. King’s Cross more likely. Yesterday. In her dinner-hour.”
“I’m hungry,” said Violet.
And it was a fact that they had had no evening meal, seeing that they had expressly announced their intention of “eating out” on that great day.
“So must you be, my dear,” said Violet.
There they were, alone together on the ground-floor, with one electric bulb in the back room and one other, needlessly, lighting the middle part of the cleansed and pleasant shop. They could afford to be young and to live perilously, madly, absurdly. They lost control of themselves, and gloried in so doing. The cake was a danger to existence. It had the consistency of marble, the richness of molasses, the mysteriousness of the enigma of the universe. It seemed unconquerable. It seemed more fatal than daggers or gelignite. But they attacked it. Fortunately, neither of them knew the inner meaning of indigestion. When Henry had taken the last slice, Violet exclaimed like a child:
“Oh, just one tiny piece more!” And with burning eyes she bent down and bit off a morsel from the slice in Henry’s hand.
“I am living!” shouted an unheard voice in Henry’s soul.

===Chinese===

她朝他们行礼，一脸慈祥的咧嘴笑，笑里带着惊人的一点儿淘气。随后他们听见她渐远的脚步，谨慎地登上黑暗的一层层楼梯，走进她顶层那份不可侵犯的私生活。
「不吃掉它可不行，」薇奥莱特说。
「不行，」亨利同意。
「她真是个奇人，那姑娘！一根羽毛就能把我打倒。」
「是。」
「不知她哪儿买的。」
「准是去了国王十字。要不就是霍尔本那头。国王十字更可能。昨天。趁她午饭那一个钟头。」
「我饿了，」薇奥莱特说。
事实是，他们那晚什么都没吃——因为这大喜的日子他们明明宣布过要「下馆子」的。
「你也准饿了，亲爱的，」薇奥莱特说。
两人就这样，单独在底楼，后屋一只灯泡，另一只——多此一举地——照亮着焕然一新的宜人铺子的中段。他们享受得起年轻，享受得起活得危险、活得疯狂、活得荒唐。他们失去了自持，并引以为荣。那蛋糕是对存在的威胁。它有大理石的坚实、糖蜜的浓甜、宇宙之谜的神秘。它似不可征服。它比匕首和炸药更致命。可他们向它发起了进攻。所幸，两人都不知道消化不良的内在含义。亨利拿下最后一片时，薇奥莱特像个孩子似的喊：
「哦，再来小小一块！」她眼睛发亮，弯腰从亨利手里的那片上咬下一小口。
「我活着！」亨利灵魂里一个无人听见的声音高喊。

## I The Promise / 一 承诺
===Original===

Elsie was forgetting to fasten the shop door. With a little start at her own negligence she secured both the bolt and the lock. She thought suddenly of the days﻿—only a year away, yet far, far off in the deceiving distances of time﻿—when Mr. Earlforward and she had the place to themselves. Mrs. Earlforward had come, and Mrs. Earlforward had gone, and now Elsie had sole charge﻿—had far more responsibility and more power than ever before. The strangeness of quite simple events awed her. Nor did the chill of the thin brass handle of the milk-can in her hand protect her against the mysterious spell of the enigma of life.
She “knew” that the shop would never open again as T. T. Riceyman’s. She “knew” that either Mr. or Mrs. Earlforward would die, and perhaps both; and she was very sad because she felt sorry for them, not because she felt sorry for herself. In the days previous to the amazing advent of Mrs. Earlforward Elsie had had Joe. Joe was definitely vanished from her existence. Nothing else in her own existence greatly mattered to her. She would probably lose a good situation; but she was well aware, beneath her diffidence and modesty, that by virtue of the knowledge which she had acquired from Mrs. Earlforward she could very easily get a fresh situation, and from the material point of view a better one. Professionally she had one secret ambition, to be able to say to a prospective employer that she could “wait at table.” There would be something grand about that, but she saw no chance of learning such an intricate and rare business. She had never seen anybody wait at table. In the little pewed eating-houses to which once or twice Joe had taken her, or she had taken Joe, the landlady or a girl brought the food to you and took your plate away, and whisked crumbs on to the floor and asked you what else you wanted; but she felt sure that that was not waiting at table, nor anything like it.﻿ ﻿… So the ideas ran on in her mind﻿—scores of them following one another in the space of a few seconds, until she shut off the stream with a murmured: “I’m a nice one, I am!” The solitary daemonic figure of Mr. Earlforward, fast in bed, was drawing her upstairs. And the shop was keeping her in the shop. And the plight of Mrs. Earlforward was pulling her away towards St. Bartholomew’s Hospital.

===Chinese===

埃尔西忘了闩铺门。她为自己的疏忽微微一惊，把门闩和锁都弄妥了。她猛然想起那些日子——才过去一年，却在时间那骗人的远景里远之又远——那时厄尔福沃德先生和她两个人守着这地方。厄尔福沃德太太来了，厄尔福沃德太太又走了，如今埃尔西独自当家——责任和权柄都远胜从前。简单事件之离奇让她肃然。手里牛奶罐那枚薄黄铜提手的凉意，也护不住她不受生命之谜那道魔咒的侵扰。
她「知道」这铺子再也不会作为 T. T. 赖斯曼的铺子开门了。她「知道」厄尔福沃德先生和太太必有一个会死，也许两个都会；她非常悲伤，是因为她为他们难过，不是为她自己难过。在厄尔福沃德太太那惊人降临之前的日子，埃尔西还有乔。乔已从她的存在里确然消逝。她自己的存在里再没有什么大不了的东西。她多半会丢掉一份好差事；可在她那怯懦谦卑底下，她深知：凭着从厄尔福沃德太太那里学来的本事，她能轻而易举再找一份新差事，而且从物质观点看更好的一份。职业上她有一个秘密的抱负：能对未来的雇主说，她会「伺候上桌」。那可真气派，可她看不见学会这门精深稀有行当的机会。她从没见过谁伺候上桌。在乔带她去过一两次、或她带乔去过的那种带卡座的小饭铺里，老板娘或一个姑娘把饭菜端给你，把盘子撤走，把面包屑掸到地上，问你还想要什么；可她确信那不叫伺候上桌，连边都不沾。……这些念头就这样在她脑子里跑下去——几秒钟里几十个鱼贯而出，直到她啸囔一句把这条溪流闸住：「我这个人，真是的！」厄尔福沃德先生那孤零零的魔鬼似的身影，困在床上，正把她往楼上拽。而铺子把她留在铺子里。而厄尔福沃德太太的处境，把她拽向圣巴塞洛缪医院。

===Original===

And there she stood like a regular hard-faced silly, thinking about waiting at table! She must go to Mr. Earlforward instantly, and tell him what had happened.
When she reached the first-floor she said to herself that she might as well take the milk into the kitchen first, and when she reached the kitchen she remembered poor Mrs. Earlforward’s bulbs. The precious bulbs had been neglected. Out of kindness to Mrs. Earlforward she went at once and watered the soil in which they were buried, and put the pots out on the windowsill. It was an act of piety, not of faith, for Elsie had no belief in the future of those bulbs. Indeed, she counted them among the inexplicable caprices of employers. If you wanted a plant, why not buy one that you could see, instead of interring an onion in a lot of dirt? Still, for Mrs. Earlforward’s sake, she took great pains over the supposed welfare of the bulbs. And yet﻿—it must be admitted, however reluctantly﻿—her motive in so meticulously cherishing the bulbs was by no means pure. She was afraid of the imminent interview with Mr. Earlforward, and was delaying it. If she had been sure of herself in regard to Mr. Earlforward, she would not have spent one second on the bulbs; she would have disdained them utterly.
Mr. Earlforward was somewhat animated.
“I didn’t sleep much the first part of the night,” he said, “but I must have had some good sleeps this morning.”
Elsie thought he was a little better, but he still looked very ill indeed. His pallor was terrible, and his eyes confessed that he knew he was very ill. He was forlorn in the disordered and soiled bed; and the untidy room, with its morsel of dying fire, was forlorn.
“Well,” said Elsie nervously, in a tone as if she was repeating a fact with which both of them were familiar, “well, so missis has gone to the hospital!”
She had told him. She trembled for his exclamation and his questions. He made no sound, no movement. Elsie felt extremely uncomfortable. She would have preferred any reply to this silence. She was bound to continue.
“Yes. Missis was that ill that when doctor came for you he took *her* off instead. I told her I’d see after you properly till you was fetched too, sir.” She gave no further details. “I’m that sorry, sir,” she said.
Mr. Earlforward maintained his silence. He did not seem to desire any details. He just lay on his back and stared up at the ceiling.

===Chinese===

她却站在那儿，活像个铁着脸的傻瓜，想着什么伺候上桌！她必须立刻去见厄尔福沃德先生，告诉他出了什么事。
到了二楼，她心想不妨先把牛奶送进厨房；到了厨房，她又想起可怜的厄尔福沃德太太的球根。宝贵的球根一直没人管。出于对厄尔福沃德太太的好意，她立刻过去，给埋着球根的土浇了水，把花盆搬到窗台上。这是虔敬之举，不是信心之举，因为埃尔西对那些球根的前途没有信念。说实话，她把它们归入雇主们不可解的任性。你要养花，为什么不买一盆看得见的，偏要把一颗洋葱埋进一堆土里？话虽如此，看厄尔福沃德太太的面子，她还是煞费苦心照料这球根。然而——必须承认，再不情愿也得承认——她这样精心疼爱球根，动机绝不纯。她怕同厄尔福沃德先生那场迫近的谈话，在拖延。倘若她在厄尔福沃德先生面前有把握，她一秒钟也不会花在球根上；她会全然地蔑视它们。
厄尔福沃德先生略有生气。
「上半夜没怎么睡，」他说，「可今天早上准是睡了几觉好觉。」
埃尔西觉得他好些了，可他看上去实在仍病得很重。他的苍白可怕，他的眼睛承认他知道自己的重病。他在凌乱污渍的床上孤苦；凌乱的屋子，连同那一小撮垂死的炉火，也孤苦。
「嗯，」埃尔西紧张地说，那口气像在复述一件两人都熟悉的事实，「嗯，太太进医院了！」
她说出来了。她为他即将发出的惊呼和追问发抖。他没有声音，没有动作。埃尔西感到极其不自在。她宁愿得到任何回答，也不愿这沉默。她只得说下去。
「是的。太太病得那样重，大夫来接您的时候，反倒把她接走了。我告诉她，在您也被接走之前，我会好好照看您，先生。」她没讲更多细节。「我真难过，先生，」她说。
厄尔福沃德先生保持着沉默。他似乎不想要任何细节。他只是仰卧着，盯住天花板。

===Original===

The expression on his hollowed face, now the face of a man of seventy, drew tears to Elsie’s eyes, and she had difficulty in restraining a sob. The aspect of her employer and of the room, the realization of the emptiness of the rest of the house, the thought of Mrs. Earlforward snatched away into the mysterious and formidable interior of the legendary hospital, were intolerable to Elsie, who horribly surmised that “they” must be cutting up the unconscious form of her once lively and impulsive mistress. To relieve the tension which was overpowering her Elsie began to straighten the rumpled eiderdown.
“I’ll run and make you some of that arrowroot, sir,” she said. “You must have something, so it’s no use you﻿—”
Mr. Earlforward said nothing; then his head dropped on one side, and his eyes met hers.
“Elsie,” he murmured plaintively, “you won’t desert me?”
“Of course not, sir. But the doctor’s coming for you.”
“Never!” Mr. Earlforward insisted, ignoring her last sentence. “You’ll never desert me?”
“Of course not, sir.” His weakness gave her strength.
In order to continue in activity, she went to mend the fire.
“Let it out,” said Mr. Earlforward. “I’m too hot.”
She desisted, well knowing that he was not too hot, but that he hated to see good coal consumed in a grate where it had never been consumed before. From pity she must humour him. What did it matter whether the fire was in or out?﻿—the doctor would be coming for him very soon. Then a flicker of thought for herself: after the departure of Mr. Earlforward, would she have to stay and mind the place till something else happened, or would she be told to go, and let the place mind itself? Very probably she would be told to stay. She opened the door.
“Where are you going now?”
“I was just going to make your arrowroot, sir. That was what missis was giving you. At least, it looks like arrowroot.”
“Come here. I want to talk to you. Have you opened the shop?”
“No, sir.”
A long pause.
“Bring me up the letters, and let me have my glasses.”
He had accepted, in his practical, compromising philosophy, the impressive fact that the shop had not been and would not be opened.
Without saying anything Elsie went downstairs into the shadowy shop. A dozen or so letters lay on the floor. “I’ll give him two or three to quiet him,” she thought, counting him now as a baby. She picked up three envelopes at random.

===Chinese===

他凹陷的脸上——如今是一张七十岁人的脸——那副神情，把泪引到埃尔西眼里，她好不容易才忍住抽噎。雇主的模样和屋子的模样，对宅子其余部分空空如也的意识，对厄尔福沃德太太被掳进那传奇医院神秘而可畏的内部的念头，都让埃尔西受不了；她骇然猜想，「他们」准是在解剖她那曾经活泼冲动的主母失去知觉的躯体。为了解开压倒她的紧张，埃尔西动手抻平皱乱的鸭绒被。
「我这就去给您弄点竹芋粉，先生，」她说。「您总得吃点什么，所以您再——也没用。」
厄尔福沃德先生没说话；随后他的头歪向一边，眼睛对上了她的眼睛。
「埃尔西，」他哀怨地喃喃，「你不会丢下我吧？」
「当然不会，先生。可大夫要来接您的。」
「绝不！」厄尔福沃德先生坚持，不理会她后一句。「你永远不会丢下我？」
「当然不会，先生。」他的软弱给了她力量。
为了继续有事做，她去拢炉火。
「让它灭，」厄尔福沃德先生说。「我太热。」
她住了手，心里明白他不是太热，而是不忍看好煤在一个从来没有烧过煤的炉膛里烧掉。出于怜悯，她必须顺着。火生着还是灭着，有什么要紧？——大夫很快就来接他了。接着闪过一丝为自己打的算盘：厄尔福沃德先生走后，她要不要留下来照看这地方，等下一步？还是会被辞掉，让这地方自己照看自己？多半会叫她留下。她开了门。
「这会儿去哪儿？」
「这就去给您弄竹芋粉，先生。太太一直给您喝的就是这个。至少，看着像竹芋粉。」
「过来。我要同你说话。铺子开了没有？」
「没有，先生。」
长长的停顿。
「把信给我拿上来，把我的眼镜给我。」
他以他那实际的、通融的哲学，接受了铺子没有开、也不会再开这个惊人的事实。
埃尔西一言不发下楼进了昏暗的铺子。十几封信躺在地板上。「我给他两三封，安安他，」她想，如今把他算作一个婴孩。她随手捡起三个信封。

===Original===

“He’d better not have them all,” she thought. The others she left lying. She had no concern whatever as to the possible business importance of any of the correspondence. Her sole concern, apart from the sickroom, was the condition of the shop. Ought she to clean it, or ought she to “let it go”? She wanted to clean it, because it was obviously fast returning to its original state of filth. On the other hand, while cleaning it she might be neglecting her master. None but herself had the power to decide which course should be taken. She perceived that she was mistress. Naively she enjoyed the strange sensation of authority, but the responsibility of authority dismayed her.
“Are these all?” Mr. Earlforward asked indifferently, as she put the three letters into his limp, shiny hand.
“Yes, sir,” she said without compunction.
He allowed the letters to slip out of his hand on to the eiderdown. She was just a little afraid of being alone with him.

===Chinese===

「最好别全给他，」她想。其余的留在原地。这些信件里任何一封可能有什么生意上的要紧，她毫不关心。除了病室，她唯一关心的是铺子的状况。她该打扫它呢，还是「由它去」？她想打扫，因为它显然在飞快回到它本来的污秽状态。另一方面，打扫的时候她也许就冷落了主人。该走哪条路，只有她自己有权决定。她意识到自己是主妇了。她天真地享受着权威的奇异感受，可权威的责任让她发慌。
「就这些？」她把三封信放进他那只绵软发亮的手里时，厄尔福沃德先生漠然问。
「是，先生，」她说，毫无愧色。
他任信从手里滑落到鸭绒被上。单独同他待在一起，她有一点点怕。

## II The Refusal / 二 拒绝
===Original===

In the early dusk of the afternoon, about four o’clock, there was a banging on the shop-door, and the short bark of a dog, who evidently considered himself entitled to help in whatever affair was afoot. Elsie was upstairs. During the morning several persons, incapable of understanding that when a shop is shut it is shut, had banged on the door, and at last Elsie, by means of two tin tacks, had affixed to the door﻿—without a word to her master﻿—a dirty old card on which she had scrawled in large pencilled letters the succinct announcement, “Closed.” This had put an end to banging. But now more banging!
“The doctor!” Elsie exclaimed, and ran down.
Not the doctor, but a lanky and elegant little girl accompanied by a fox-terrier, stood at the door. As soon as the door opened and she saw Elsie the little girl blushed. The fact was that this was her very first entry into the world of affairs, and she felt both extremely nervous and extremely anxious not to show her nervousness to a servant. The dog, of course, suffered.
“Be quiet, sir!” she said very emphatically to the restless creature, addressing him as a gentleman, and the next minute catching him a clout on his hard head. “Papa can’t come, and he told me to say﻿—”
“Will you please step inside, Miss Raste?” Elsie suggested.
Nobody was about, but Elsie with a servant’s imitativeness had acquired her mistress’s passion for keeping private business private. The little girl, reassured by the respectful formality of her reception, stepped inside with some dignity, and the dog, too tardily following, got himself nipped in the closing door and yelped.
“Serves you right!” said Miss Raste; and to apologetic Elsie: “Oh, not at all! It’s all his own fault.﻿ ﻿… Papa says he’s so busy he can’t come himself, but you are to get Mr. Earlforward ready to go to the hospital, and wrap him up well; and while you’re doing that I am to walk towards King’s Cross and get a taxi for you. I may have to go all the way to King’s Cross,” Miss Raste added proudly and eagerly. “But it will be all right. I got a taxi for papa yesterday; it was driving towards our Square, but I stopped it and got in, and told the chauffeur to drive me to our house﻿—not *very* far, of course. Papa said I should be quite all right, and he’s teaching me to be self-reliant and all that.” Miss Raste gave a little snigger. “Jack! You naughty boy!”

===Chinese===

午后薄暮时分，四点钟光景，铺门上一阵擂动，还伴着一声短促的狗吠——那狗显然认为自己有份参与眼下的任何事情。埃尔西在楼上。一上午，好几个人——他们不能理解铺子关了就是关了——擂过门；最后埃尔西用两枚小图钉，往门上钉了一张脏旧卡片——没跟主人说——用铅笔大大地草写了简洁的告示：「歇业」。擂门就此止息。可现在又擂！
「大夫！」埃尔西喊了一声，跑下楼去。
不是大夫，是一个瘦长优雅的小姑娘，带着一条猎狐梗，站在门口。门一开、一看见埃尔西，小姑娘就红了脸。事实是，这是她头一回进入实务的世界，她又紧张得不行，又急着不肯在一个仆人面前露出紧张。狗，自然，遭了殃。
「安静，先生！」她斩钉截铁地对那不安分的小东西说，以绅士相称，下一分钟又朝它坚硬的脑壳拍了一记。「爸爸来不了，他叫我说——」
「请进来说好吗，拉斯特小姐（Miss Raste）？」埃尔西建议。
四下无人，可埃尔西以仆人的模仿性，继承了主母把私事关起门来办的热情。小姑娘受了这恭敬而正式的接待的鼓舞，颇有威仪地走进来；狗迟了一步跟进来，被关拢的门夹了一下，唯地叫了。
「活该！」拉斯特小姐说；又对致歉的埃尔西：「哦，不客气！全是他自找的。……爸爸说他忙得来不了，可你们要把厄尔福沃德先生拾摄好去医院，给他裹严实；你们拾摄的时候，我朝国王十字走，给你们叫出租车。我说不定得一路走到国王十字呢，」拉斯特小姐骄傲而热切地补充。「可没问题的。昨天我就给爸爸叫到过一辆出租车；它正朝我们广场开，可我拦下它上了车，叫司机开到我们家——当然不算很远。爸爸说我完全没问题，他正教我自立什么的。」拉斯特小姐小小地窃笑一声。「杰克！你这个坏孩子！」

===Original===

Jack was examining in detail the correspondence which Elsie had neglected and told lies about. At his mistress’s protest he ran off into the obscure hinterland of the shop to stake out a claim there.
“And after I’ve got you the taxi I am to walk home. Oh, and papa said I was to say you were to tell Mr. Earlforward that Mrs. Earlforward will have an operation tomorrow morning.”
Miss Raste was encouraged to be entirely confidential, to withhold nothing even about herself, by the confidence-inspiring and kindly aspect of Elsie’s face. She thought almost ecstatically to herself: “How nice it would be to have *her* for a servant! She’s heaps nicer than Clara.” But she had some doubt about the correctness of Elsie’s style in aprons.
“Oh dear! Oh dear!” Elsie murmured.
“And they’ll be expecting Mr. Earlforward at Bart’s. It’s all arranged.”
Having impinged momentarily upon a drab tragedy of Clerkenwell and taken a considerable fancy to Elsie, and having imperiously summoned her dog, Miss Raste, who was being educated to leave Clerkenwell one day and disdain it, departed on her mission with a demeanour in which the princess and the filly were mingled.
“What’s the matter? What have you turned the light on for?” Mr. Earlforward demanded when Elsie, much agitated, entered the bedroom. “What *is* the matter?”
Elsie tried to compose her face.
“How do you feel now, sir?” she asked, serpent-like in spite of her simplicity and nervousness.
“I feel decidedly better. In fact, I was almost thinking of getting up.”
“Oh! That’s good. Because the doctor’s sending a taxi for you, and I am to take you to the hospital at once. Here’s all your things.” She fingered a loaded chair. “And while you’re putting ’em on I’ll just run upstairs and get my things.”
“Is the doctor here?” Henry cautiously inquired.
“No, sir. He says he’s too busy. But he’s sent his little girl.”
“Well, I’m not going to the hospital. Why should I go to the hospital?” Mr. Earlforward exclaimed with peevish, rather shrill obstinacy.
She had “known” he would refuse to go to the hospital. She was beaten from the start.
“But you said you *would* go to the hospital, sir.”
“When did I say I would go to the hospital?”
“You said so to missis, sir.”
“And who told *you*?”
“Missis, sir.”
“Yes, but I didn’t know then that your mistress would have to go. The place can’t be left without both of us.

===Chinese===

杰克正在细细检查那些埃尔西没收拾、还为之撒了谎的信件。受了主人的呵斥，它跑进铺子幽暗的腹地，去圈占一块领地。
「我把出租车给你们叫到之后，就自己走回家。哦，还有，爸爸叫我说，你们要告诉厄尔福沃德先生，厄尔福沃德太太明天早上要做手术。」
埃尔西那张脸能给人信心、又和气，拉斯特小姐受了鼓励，全然推心置腹，连自己的事也不瞒。她心里近乎狂喜地想：「家里要是用上她这样的仆人该多好！她比克拉拉好多啦。」不过她对埃尔西围裙的式样是否得体，有点疑心。
「哎呀！哎呀！」埃尔西喃喃。
「巴茨那边会等厄尔福沃德先生的。全都安排好了。」
这位小姐——她受的教育就是有朝一日要离开克勒肯维尔并鄙夷克勒肯维尔——在这克勒肯维尔的灰色悲剧上短暂地触了一下，对埃尔西生出了相当的好感，又威严地召唤了她的狗，随后出发执行任务，那做派里公主与小母马兼而有之。
「怎么了？开灯干什么？」埃尔西激动万分地进卧室时，厄尔福沃德先生质问。「到底怎么了？」
埃尔西努力摆正脸色。
「您这会儿觉得怎么样，先生？」她问，虽单纯虽紧张，却蛇一般地绕。
「我觉得明显好多了。说实话，我差点打算起床了。」
「哦！那太好了。因为大夫给您派了辆出租车，我得马上送您去医院。您的衣裳都在这儿。」她摸了摸那张堆满东西的椅子。「您穿的时候，我上楼去拿我的东西。」
「大夫来了？」亨利谨慎地探问。
「没有，先生。他说太忙。可他派了他的小闺女来。」
「哼，我不去医院。我干吗去医院？」厄尔福沃德先生喊，带着病态的、近乎尖利的顽固。
她本来就「知道」他会拒绝去医院。她从一开头就败了。
「可您说过您肯去医院的，先生。」
「我什么时候说过肯去医院？」
「您对太太说的，先生。」
「谁告诉你的？」
「太太，先生。」
「是，可我当时不知道你家太太也得去。两个人都走了，这地方总不能没人。

===Original===

You aren’t expecting I should leave *this* place in your charge. Besides, I’m not really ill. Hospital! I never heard of such a thing. I should like to know what I’ve got﻿—to be packed off to a hospital! I should feel a perfect fool there. I’m not going. And you can tell everybody I’m not going.” He rolled over and hid his face from Elsie, and kept on muttering, feeble-fierce. He had no weapon of defence except his irrational obstinacy; but it was sufficient, and he knew it was sufficient, against the entire organized world. If he had had an infectious disease the authorities would have had the right to carry him off by force; but he had no infectious disease, and therefore was impregnable.
“Now, it’s no use you standing there, Elsie. I’m not going. You think because I’m ill you can do what you like, do you? I’ll show you!”
Elsie could see the perspiration on his brow. He looked desperate. He was a child, a sick man, a spoilt darling, a martyr to anguish and pain, a tiger hunted and turning ferociously on his pursuers. His mind as much as his body was poisoned. Elsie said quietly:
“Missis is to have an operation tomorrow morning, sir.”
A silence. Then, savagely:
“Is she? Then more fool her!”
Elsie extinguished the light, shut the door and descended the stairs, wondering what brilliant people, clever people, people of resource and brains, would have done in her place.
When Miss Raste came back with the taxi in the gathering night, having accomplished a marvellous Odyssey and pretending grandly that what she had done was nothing at all, it was Elsie who blushed in confusion.
“I can’t get him to go to the hospital, Miss Raste. No, I can’t!”
“Oh!” observed Miss Raste uncertainly. “Well, shall I tell papa that?”
“Yes, please.﻿ ﻿… Do *what* I will!”
“I’m afraid the taxi will have to be paid. I’ve left Jack in it. He’s so naughty. A shilling I saw on the dial. But, of course, there’s the tip.”
Elsie hurried upstairs to her own room and brought down one and twopence of her own money. Another minute and she had locked herself up alone once again with her master.

===Chinese===

你总不是指望我把这地方交给你照管吧。再说我又不是真病。医院！我没听说过这种事。我倒想知道我得了什么——要被打发进医院！我在那儿会像个十足的傻瓜。我不去。你可以告诉所有人，我不去。」他翻过身去，把脸避开埃尔西，不停地咕哝，虚弱而凶狠。他除了那不讲理的顽固之外没有别的武器；可这就够了，而且他知道这就够了——对抗整个组织起来的世界。他若得的是传染病，当局有权强行抬走他；可他没得传染病，因而是攻不破的。
「行了，你站在那儿也没用，埃尔西。我不去。你以为我病了，就可以随心所欲了，是不是？我要让你瞧瞧！」
埃尔西看得见他额头上的汗。他一脸绝境。他是个孩子，是个病人，是个被宠坏的心肝，是个痛苦与疼痛的殉道者，是一头被追急了、凶猛回身扑向猎人的老虎。他的头脑同他的身体一样中了毒。埃尔西平静地说：
「太太明天早上要做手术，先生。」
一阵沉默。随后，凶狠地：
「是吗？那她更傻！」
埃尔西灭了灯，带上门，下楼去了，一路想着：换了那些聪明人、有本事的人、有头脑有办法的人，处在她的位子上会怎么办。
拉斯特小姐在渐浓的夜色里带着出租车回来时——她完成了一场了不起的奥德赛，又豪迈地装作自己干的根本不算什么——脸红语塞的倒是埃尔西。
「我劝不动他去医院，拉斯特小姐。劝不动！」
「哦！」拉斯特小姐没把握地说。「那，要告诉爸爸吗？」
「是的，请。……我是一点法子也没有了！」
「出租车怕是得付钱。我把杰克留在车上了。他太闹。表上我看到是一先令。当然，还有小费。」
埃尔西急急上楼去自己屋里，拿下来一先令两便士她自己的钱。又过了一分钟，她再次把自己同主人单独锁在了一起。

## X The Safe / 十 保险柜
===Original===

Not a sound in the house; nor outside the house. Not a clock nor a watch going in the house. Mr. Earlforward had listened interminably to get the time from the church, but without success. He knew only from the prolonged silence of the street that the hour must be very late. “Work!” he murmured to himself in the vast airless desert and void created by the death of Violet. “That’s the one thing﻿—the one thing.” His faculty for compromising with destiny aroused itself for a supreme achievement. It was invincible. He would not think himself into hell or madness or inanition by yielding feebly to the frightful grief caused by the snatching away of that unique woman so solicitous about him, so sensible, so vivacious, so agreeable, so energetic, so enterprising, so ready to adopt his ideas﻿—and yet so independent. Her little tantrums﻿—how exquisite, girlish! There had always been a girl in her. The memory of her girlishness desolated him more than anything.
“Insufficient nourishment”? No! It could not have been that. Had he ever, on any occasion, in the faintest degree, discouraged her from satisfying her appetite? Or criticized her housekeeping accounts? No! Never had he interfered. Moreover she had plenty of money of her own and the absolutely unfettered use of it. He would give her such a funeral as had not been seen in Clerkenwell for many a year. The cost, of course, might be charged to her estate, but he would not allow that﻿—though, of course, it would all be the same in the end.
He could not bear to lie in the bed which she had shared with him. The feel of the empty half of it, when he passed his hand slowly over the lower blanket in the dark, tortured him intolerably, and yet he must somehow keep on passing his hand over it. Futile and sick indulgence! He got out of bed, drew aside the curtains and drew up the blind. He could not see the moon, but it was lighting the roofs opposite, and its light and that of the gas-lamp lit the room sufficiently to reveal all the principal features of it. Animated by the mighty power of his resolution to withstand fate he felt strong﻿—he *was* strong. His cold legs were quite steady. Yes, though he still had a dull pain, the attack of indigestion was declining. He had successfully taken Bovril. To work, seated at his desk, could not tire him, and ought to do him good.
A queer affair, that indigestion!

===Chinese===

宅子里一点声音也没有；宅子外也没有。屋里没有一座钟、一只表在走。厄尔福沃德先生没完没了地听，想从教堂那儿得知时间，没有成功。他只能从街道长久的沉寂推断，时辰必已很晚。「干活！」他在薇奥莱特之死造成的浩大无风的荒漠与空虚里对自己喃喃。「就这一件——就这一件。」他与命运妥协的本领振作起来，要作一次最高的成就。它是不可战胜的。他不肯因软弱地屈从于那可怕的悲恸而把自己想进地狱、想成疯狂、想成枯槁——那悲恸来自那个独一无二的女人被夺走：她那样挂念他，那样明理，那样活泼，那样可人，那样有干劲，那样乐于采纳他的观念——然而又那样独立。她那些小脾气——多么精美，多么少女！她身上始终有个姑娘。对她少女时代的记忆，比什么都更让他荒芜。
「营养不足」？不！不会是那个。他可曾在任何场合、以最轻微的程度，劝阻过她满足食欲？或挑剔过她的持家账目？不！他从没干涉过。何况她自己的钱有的是，而且绝对不受拘束地使用。他要给她办一场克勒肯维尔多年未见的葬礼。费用固然可以记在她遗产的账上，可他不会允许——尽管结局当然全是一样。
他受不了躺在她同他共享过的床上。黑暗里他缓缓把手抚过下层毛毯，那空了一半的触感折磨他到不可忍受，可他又偏偏忍不住一直把手抚过去。徒劳而病态的沉溺！他下了床，拉开窗帘，拉起软帘。他看不见月亮，可月亮正照亮对面的屋顶，它的光同煤气灯的光把屋子照得足以显出全部主要陈设。被抵挡命运这一决心的巨力所激励，他觉得自己强壮——他确实强壮。他冰凉的两腿相当稳。是的，虽然还有钝痛，消化不良的发作正在退去。他已成功喝下了保维。坐到书桌前干活，累不着他，只该对他有益。
一件怪事，那消化不良！

===Original===

He had never suffered from indigestion until the day after his wedding night, when he had eaten so immoderately of Elsie’s bride-cake. The bride-cake seemed to have been the determining cause, or perhaps it was merely the occasion, of some change in his system. (But naturally he had said nothing of it.) However, he was now better. A little pain in the old spot﻿—no more.
He opened the wardrobe to get his new shirt and new suit, and saw in the pale gloom Violet’s garments arranged on their trays. The sight of them shook him terribly. He must assuredly save himself by the labour of reconstituting his existence. It was impossible for him to remain in the bedroom. He dressed himself in the new clothes, putting a muffler round his neck instead of a collar. Then he filled his pockets with his personal belongings from the top of the chest of drawers. None was missing. He picked up the pile of correspondence, which he had laid neatly on the pedestal. He could walk without discomfort. He must work. The grim intention to work was irresistibly monopolizing his mind, and driving all else out of it. He left the bedroom﻿—a deed in itself.
On the landing, as he looked upwards, he could see light under Elsie’s bedroom door. The candles that girl must be burning! He would correct her. Should he? Supposing she rebelled! Elsie had changed; he did not quite know where he was with her; and he did not want to lose her. She was his mainstay in the world. Still, it would never do to be afraid of correcting a servant. He would correct her. He would knock at her door and tell her﻿—not for the first time. He mounted two steps, but his legs nearly failed him. He could walk downstairs but not up. Besides, if she knew that he was out of bed there might be trouble, and he wished to avoid trouble. Therefore, he turned and limped downstairs into the shop and lit it.
To see the shop was like revisiting after an immense period the land of his youth. He recognized one by one the landmarks. Here was the loaded bookstand, with its pair of castors, which *she* had devised. The shop was like a mausoleum of trade. His trade had ceased. It had to be brought back to life, galvanized into activity. Could he do it? He must and he would do it. He was capable of the intensest effort. His very sorrow was inspiring him. On the floor at the entrance lay some neglected correspondence which bore footmarks.

===Chinese===

他从不曾消化不良，直到新婚次日的那个白天——那天他把埃尔西的结婚蛋糕吃了个没够。结婚蛋糕似乎成了他体内某种变化的决定性原因，或者只是它的契机。（可他自然只字未提。）不过，他现在好些了。老地方还有点疼——仅此而已。
他打开衣柜取新衬衫和新西装，在浅浅的幽暗里看见薇奥莱特的衣裳在托板上摆放齐整。这景象把他狠狠摇撼。他必须笃定地靠重构自己的存在这一劳动来拯救自己。他不可能再待在这间卧室。他穿上新衣裳，脖子上围了条围巾代替领子。随后他把五斗柜顶上的随身物件装满口袋。一样不缺。他拾起那摞信件——他先前齐整地摙在柜座上的。他走路已无不适。他必须干活。干活这个冷峻的意图不容抗拒地垄断了他的头脑，把其余一切都驱赶出去。他离开了卧室——这本身已是一桩壮举。
在楼梯口，他朝上一望，能看见埃尔西卧室门下的光。那姑娘烧掉的蜡烛！他要训她。训吗？万一她反了呢！埃尔西变了；他有点摸不准同她的关系了；他又不想失去她。她是他在这世上的支柱。话虽如此，怕了不敢训仆人，这可万万不行。他要训她。他要敲她的门告诉她——不是头一回了。他上了两级，可两腿几乎不支。他能下楼，上不了楼。再说，她若知道他下了床，兴许有麻烦，而他要避免麻烦。于是他转身，瘸着下楼进了铺子，把灯点亮。
看这铺子，像阔别了一整个纪元之后重访他青年时代的国土。他一处一处认出地标。这儿是那座带一对小脚轮的载重书摊——她设计的。铺子像一座交易的陵墓。他的交易终止了。必须把它救活，给它通上电让它活动起来。他办得到吗？他必须办到，也必定办到。他有能力作最剧烈的努力。他的悲恸本身就在激励他。入口处的地板上，躺着被冷落的、印有脚印的信件。

===Original===

Servants were astounding. Elsie had been too negligent even to pick the letters up. She probably never would have picked them up. She would have trod and trod them into the dirty boards﻿—demands for books, offers of books, possibly cheques﻿—the stuff itself of trade. He picked them up with difficulty, and padded into the office, which also he lit. Cold! He shivered.
“I’m not entirely cured yet,” he thought, and began to doubt himself. The fire was prepared﻿—Violet’s influence again. Fires had never been laid in advance till she came. He put a match to the fire and felt better. Undecided, he stroked his cheek. Stubble! How long was it since he had shaved? His face must look a pretty sight. Happily there was no mirror in either the office or the shop, so that he could not inspect himself. Work! Work! Memories were insinuating themselves anew in his mind. He must repulse them. Fancy her running off like that, without a word of goodbye, to the hospital, and now she was irrevocably gone! It was incredible, monstrous, the most sinister piece of devil’s magic that ever happened.﻿ ﻿… Chloroform. The knife. Fibroid growth.﻿ ﻿… Dead. Vanished. She with her vivacity and her optimism.﻿ ﻿… He was fatigued. The pain had recurred. It was very bad. Perhaps he had been ill-advised to come downstairs, for he could not get upstairs again. He cautiously skirted the desk, holding on to it, and sat in his chair. Work! Work! The reconstruction of his life!
He fingered the letters. With one of them was a cheque, and it must go into the safe for the night. He would endorse it tomorrow. Never endorse a cheque till you paid it into the bank, for an endorsed cheque might be the prey of thieves. He bent down sideways to his safe with a certain pleasure. *Her* safe was upstairs in the bathroom. He would have to obtain her keys and open it and examine its contents. He took his own keys from his pocket, and, not very easily, unlocked his safe, and swung forward its door. The familiar act soothed him. The sublime spectacle of the safe, sole symbol of security in a world of perils, enheartened him. After all﻿ ﻿…
Then he noticed that the silver bag was not precisely in its customary spot on the ledge over the nest of drawers. He started in alarm and clutched at the bag. It was not tied with his knot. He unloosed it and felt crumpled paper within it. “6d”!

===Chinese===

仆人们真是了不得。埃尔西疏懒到连信也不捾。她多半永远不会捾。她会把它们一脚一脚踩进肮脏的板缝里——索书的、售书的，说不定还有支票——生意本身的原料。他艰难地把它们拾起来，轻步走进公事房，把那儿也点亮。冷！他打了个寒噤。
「我还没全好，」他想，开始怀疑自己。炉膛里火已备好——又是薇奥莱特的影响。她来之前，火是从不预先摆好的。他划了根火柴点火，觉得好受了些。他犹疑着，摸了摸脸颊。胡茬！他有多久没刮脸了？他的脸一定没法看。幸而公事房和铺子里都没有镜子，他没法自检。干活！干活！记忆正重新潜入他的头脑。他必须击退它们。想想她竟那样跑掉了，一句再见也没有，去了医院，而如今她一去不返！难以置信，骇人听闻，旷古未有的最阴森的魔鬼魔法。……氯仿。手术刀。纤维瘤。……死了。消逝了。她，连同她的活泼与她的乐观。……他疲乏了。疼痛又来了。很糟。或许他下楼来是失策，因为他再也上不了楼了。他小心地贴着书桌绕，扶着桌沿，坐进他的椅子。干活！干活！重构他的生活！
他摩挲着那些信。其中一封附着一支票，得进保险柜过夜。他明天再背书。支票在付进银行之前绝不要背书，因为背了书的支票会成窃贼的猎物。他带着几分快意朝侧面弯下身去开他的保险柜。她的保险柜在楼上浴室里。他将不得不取来她的钥匙，打开它，清点里面的内容。他从口袋里掏出自己的钥匙，不太利落地开了柜门，把门荡开。这个熟悉的动作抚慰了他。保险柜那庄严的景象——险恶世界里安全的第一象征——给他壮了胆。毕竟……
随后他注意到，银币袋没有分毫不差地待在抽屉窝上方搁板的老位置上。他惊跳起来，一把攦住袋子。系的不是他的结。他解开，摸到里面一团皱纸。「6d」！

===Original===

Elsie’s clumsy handwriting, which he knew so well from having seen it now and then on little lists of sales on the backs of envelopes! No! It was not the loss of sixpence that affected him. He could have borne that. What so profoundly, so formidably, shocked him was the fact that Elsie had surreptitiously taken his keys, rifled the safe, and returned the keys﻿—and smiled on him and nursed him! There was no security at all in the world of perils. The foundations of faith had been destroyed. Elsie!
But in the agony of the crisis he did not forget his wife. He moaned aloud:
“What would Violet have thought? What would my poor Violet have thought of this?”
His splendid fortitude, his superhuman courage to recreate his existence over the ruins of it and to defy fate, were broken down. Life was bigger, more cruel, more awful than he had imagined.

===Chinese===

埃尔西那笨拙的笔迹——他见得熟了，在信封背面那些小小的售书单上，时不时就见到！不！让他失态的不是那六便士的损失。那个他受得住。深深地震骇他的、骇人到不可名状的是这个事实：埃尔西偷偷拿了他的钥匙，撬了保险柜，又把钥匙放回——还朝他笑，还护理他！这险恶的世界里根本没有安全可言。信仰的地基被捣毁了。埃尔西！
可在这场危机的绞痛中他没有忘记妻子。他出声呜咽：
「薇奥莱特会怎么想？我可怜的薇奥莱特会怎么想这个？」
他那份堂皇的坚忍，那份在废墟上重建自己存在、公然蔑视命运的超人勇气，垮掉了。生活比他想象的更大、更残忍、更可怕。

## VI The Second Refusal / 六 第二次拒绝
===Original===

She was running up the Steps (not as early as she hoped, owing to a quick succession of requisitions from her two patients at the last moment) to find a messenger in the Square to dispatch for the doctor, when a sharp “Hai! Hai!” from behind caused her to turn. The summons came from Dr. Raste, who had appeared round the corner from King’s Cross Road. Elsie ran back and unlocked the shop-door. The ink of her scrawled notice of closure to the public had been weeping freely in the weather of the last twenty-four hours.
“You were leaving your patient, Elsie,” said the doctor, in a prim, impartial voice, expressing neither disapproval nor approval nor anything, but just holding up the mere fact for her consideration.
She explained.
“He’s worse, of course,” the doctor remarked, his tone not asking for confirmation﻿—almost forbidding it.
He was impenetrable; or, as Elsie thought: “You couldn’t make anything out of him.” He might be tired; he might not be tired. He might have been roused from his bed at 2 a.m.; he might have slept excellently in perfect tranquillity. You didn’t know; you never would know. The secrets of the night were locked up in that trimly dressed bosom. He was the doctor, exclusively. But one thing showed him human; he had once again disturbed the sequence of his daily programme in order to visit T. T. Riceyman’s.
They passed through the shop, on whose floor more letters were lying. At the door of Mr. Earlforward’s bedroom, the doctor paused and murmured:
“I’d better hear what you’ve got to say before I go in.”
She took him to the dining room, where he sat down on a dusty chair. To Elsie’s mind the dining room was in a disgraceful state, and indeed, though the shop and office had not yet seriously deteriorated from last night’s terrific cleansing, the only presentable rooms in the house were the two bedrooms. All the rest was as neglected and forlorn as a pet animal forgotten in the stress of a great and prolonged crisis. Elsie, standing, gave her report, which the doctor received like a magistrate. She wanted to ask about Mrs. Earlforward, but it was not proper for her to ask questions. Nor could she frame any formula of words in which to broach to the steely little doctor the immense fact of Joe’s presence in the building.
“Been to bed?” he inquired coldly.
“Oh no, sir!”
“Had any sleep?”
“Oh no, sir!”
“Not for two nights, eh?”

===Chinese===

她正跑上台阶（没有她希望的那么早，因为最后一刻她的两位病人接连提出了几项要求），要去广场找个人捐信去请大夫，身后一声尖利的「嘿！嘿！」让她回过头。召唤来自拉斯特医生，他刚从国王十字路那边的拐角出现。埃尔西跑回去开了铺门。她那张写给公众的潦草歇业告示，墨水在这二十四小时的天气里淌得不轻。
「你把病人掠下了，埃尔西，」医生说，口吻拘谨而不偏不倚，既不表不满也不表赞同，什么也不表，只是把单纯的事实摆出来供她考虑。
她解释了。
「他当然更糟了，」医生评点，语气不求证实——简直禁止证实。
他深不可测；或者如埃尔西所想：「你从他身上什么也套不出来。」他也许累了；也许不累。他也许凌晨两点被人从床上叫起来过；也许在彻底的安宁里睡得极好。你不知道；你永远也不会知道。夜的秘密锁在那收拾得整整齐齐的胸怀里。他只是医生，纯乎其纯的医生。只有一件事显出他的人性：为了到 T. T. 赖斯曼家来，他又一次打乱了自己日程的顺序。
他们穿过铺子，地板上又躺着信。在厄尔福沃德先生卧室门口，医生停下，喃喃道：
「我进去之前，最好先听听你要说什么。」
她把他领进饭厅，他在一把积灰的椅子上坐下。在埃尔西心里，饭厅处在一个丢人的状态；确实，虽说铺子和公事房还没有从昨夜那场骇人的大清洗中严重退化，全宅拿得出手的只有两间卧室。其余的一切都被冷落、被遗弃，像一场浩大而漫长的危机的重压之下被忘掉的宠物。埃尔西站着做汇报，医生像治安法官一样受理。她想打听厄尔福沃德太太，可她发问不合规矩。她也组织不出任何一种措辞，好把乔在这宅子里的巨大事实向这位钢打的小医生开口。
「上床睡了没有？」他冷冷地问。
「哦没有，先生！」
「睡了会儿没有？」
「哦没有，先生！」
「两夜没睡了，嗯？」

===Original===

“No, sir﻿—well, nothing to mention.”
When at length they passed into the bedroom, Elsie was shocked at the condition of the sickbed. She had left it unimpeachably smooth, tidy and rectangular; it was now tossed and deranged into a horrible confusion, as though it had not been made for days, as though for days the patient had been carrying on in it a continuous battle with some powerful enemy. And in the midst of it lay Mr. Earlforward (whom also she had just “put to rights,” and who after her tending had somehow not seemed to be very ill), unkempt, hot, wild-eyed, parchment-skinned, emaciated, desiccated, creased, anxious, at bay, nearly desperate, mumbling to himself. Yet the moment he caught sight of the doctor he altered his demeanour, becoming calm, still, and even a little sprightly. The change was pathetic in its failure to deceive; and it was also heroic.
“Well, my friend,” the doctor greeted him, staccato, with his characteristic faint, nervous snigger at the end of a phrase.
“You’re here very early, doctor,” said Mr. Earlforward composedly. “At least it seems to me early.” He did not know the time; nor Elsie either; not a timepiece in the house was going, and the church-clock bell was too familiar to be noticed unless listened for.
“Thought you might like to know something about your wife,” said Dr. Raste, raising his voice. He made no reference at all to Henry’s exasperating refusal to go to the hospital on the previous day. “They tell me at the hospital that a fibroid growth is her trouble. I suspected it.”
“Where?”
“Matrix.” The doctor glanced at Elsie as if to say: “You don’t know what that word means.” She didn’t, but she divined well enough Mrs. Earlforward’s trouble. “Change of life. No children,” the doctor went on tersely, and nodded several times. Mr. Earlforward merely gazed at him with his little burning eyes. “There’ll be an operation this morning. Hope it’ll be all right. It ought to be. An otherwise healthy subject. Yes. Hold this in your mouth, will you?”
He inserted a clinical thermometer between Mr. Earlforward’s white, crinkled lips, took hold of the patient’s wrist and pulled out his watch.
“Appears you can’t retain your food,” he said, after he had put the watch back. “Comes up exactly as it goes down. Mechanical. You’re very strong.” He withdrew the thermometer, held it up to the light, washed it, restored it to its case.

===Chinese===

「没有，先生——嗯，不值一提。」
等他们终于进了卧室，埃尔西被病床的状况吓了一跳。她离开时它平整、齐整、有棱有角，无可指摘；现在它被搅得、翻得乱成一团可怕，仿佛几天没铺过，仿佛病人几天来在床上同某个强大的敌人进行着一场不间断的搏斗。在这乱阵中央躺着厄尔福沃德先生（她也才刚把他「拾掇停当」，经她伺候之后他不知怎么并不显得病得很重），蓬头垢面，滚烫，眼睛狂乱，皮肤如羊皮纸，消瘦，干瘪，满脸褶皱，焦虑，走投无路，近乎绝望，喃喃自语。可他一看见医生，立刻改了做派，变得平静、安稳，甚至还有点轻快。这变化骗不了人，可怜；却也英勇。
「好啊，我的朋友，」医生向他打招呼，一顿一顿的，句尾带着他特有的那种轻微紧张的窃笑。
「您来得很早，大夫，」厄尔福沃德先生安详地说。「至少我觉得早。」他不知道时间；埃尔西也不知道；全宅没有一只计时器在走，教堂的钟声太熟了，不特意去听就察觉不到。
「想着您许愿听听您太太的消息，」拉斯特医生说，提高了嗓音。对亨利昨天那个气死人的拒入医院，他只字未提。「医院告诉我，她的毛病是纤维瘤。我早疑心是它。」
「在哪儿？」
「子宫。」医生矘了埃尔西一眼，仿佛说：「这词什么意思你不懂。」她是不懂，可厄尔福沃德太太的病她猜得相当准。「更年期。没有孩子，」医生简短地继续，点了几下头。厄尔福沃德先生只是用他那双燃烧的小眼睛盯着他。「今天上午手术。希望顺利。应该顺利。除此以外是个健康的体质。是。把这个含在嘴里，好吗？」
他把一支体温计插进厄尔福沃德先生发白起皱的嘴唇间，捏住病人的手腕，掏出表。
「看来你存不住食物，」他收回表之后说。「下去什么样，上来还什么样。机械性的。你非常结实。」他抽出体温计，举到光里看了看，洗了洗，收回套管。

===Original===

“Well, we know what’s the matter with your wife, but *I* shouldn’t like to say what’s the matter with you﻿—yet. I’m not a specialist.” He uttered the phrase with a peculiar intonation, not entirely condemning specialists, but putting them in their place, regarding them very critically and rather condescendingly, as befitting one whose field of work and knowledge was the whole boundless realm of human pathology. “You’ll have to be put under observation, watched for a bit, and X-rayed. You can’t possibly be nursed properly here, though I’m sure Elsie’s doing her best. And there’s another great advantage of your being in hospital. You’ll know how Mrs. Earlforward’s going on. You can’t expect ’em to be sending up here every ten minutes to tell you. Nor telegraph either. Something else to do, hospitals have!” Another faint snigger. “If you’ll come now, I mean in half an hour or so, I’ve arranged to get you there in comfort. It’s all fixed.” (He did not say how.) “I hear you can walk about, and you made your bed yesterday. Now, Elsie, you must﻿—”
“I won’t go to the hospital,” Mr. Earlforward coldly interrupted him. “I don’t mind having a private nurse here. But I won’t go to the hospital.”
The doctor laughed easily.
“Oh, but you must! And one nurse wouldn’t be enough. You’ll need two. And even then it would be absolutely no good. You can’t be X-rayed here, for instance. It’s no use me telling you how ill you are, because you know as well as I do how ill you are.”
The battle was joined. Dr. Raste, in addition to being exasperated, had been piqued by the reports of his patient’s singular obstinacy; he had now positively determined to get him into the hospital, and it was this resolve that had prompted him to give special attention to Mr. Earlforward’s case, disorganizing all his general work in favour of it. He could not allow himself to be beaten by the inexplicable caprice of a patient who in all other respects had struck him as a man of more than ordinary sound sagacity, though of a somewhat miserly disposition; and the caprice was the more enigmatic in that to enter the hospital would be by far the cheapest way of treating the illness.
Mr. Earlforward’s obstinacy, on the other hand, was exasperated and strengthened by the disdainful reception given to his marvellous, his perfectly reckless suggestion about having a private nurse.

===Chinese===

「嗯，你太太的毛病我们知道了，可你的毛病——我还不想说。我不是专科医生。」他说这句话用了一种特别的腔调，不完全否定专科医生，但给他们定了位，用非常批判、相当俯就的眼光看他们——这符合这样一位人物：他的工作与知识的领域，是人类病理学那整个无垠的疆域。「你得接受观察，看护一阵子，做 X 光。在这儿没法得到妥当的护理，尽管我确信埃尔西尽了力。你住院还有一大好处。你能知道厄尔福沃德太太的进展。你可不能指望他们每十分钟派人上来告诉你。也不能打电报。医院还有别的事要做！」又是轻轻一声窃笑。「你要是现在走——我是说半个钟头左右——我安排好了，让你舒舒服服过去。全定妥了。」（怎么定妥的他没说。）「我听说你能走动，昨天还自己铺了床。好了，埃尔西，你必须——」
「我不去医院，」厄尔福沃德先生冷冷地打断他。「我不在乎在这儿请一个私人护士。可我不去医院。」
医生轻松地笑了。
「哦，可你必须去！而且一个护士不够。你得两个。就算那样也绝对没用。比如，在这儿没法照 X 光。我不必告诉你你病得多重，因为你和我一样清楚你病得多重。」
战斗打响了。拉斯特医生除了气恼，还被病人那反常顽固的传闻激着；他如今拿定主意要把他弄进医院，正是这个决心促使他对厄尔福沃德先生的病例特殊关照，打乱了自己全部日常工作来偏向它。他不能容许自己败给一个病人莫名其妙的任性——这病人在其余各方面给他的印象都是个智力过常、只是有点守财奴气质的人；而这任性更加费解的是：住院恰恰是治病最便宜的方式。
而厄尔福沃德先生的顽固，则被对他那个绝妙的、不惜血本的私人护士建议的轻慢接待激得更旺、更强。

===Original===

These people were ridiculously concerned about his health. They had their own ideas. He had his. He had offered an extremely generous compromise﻿—a compromise which would cost him a pot of money﻿—and it had not even been discussed; the wonder of it had in no way been recognized. Well, on the whole he was glad that the suggestion had not been approved. He withdrew it. He had only made it because he felt﻿—doubtless in undue apprehension﻿—that he was not yet beginning to progress towards recovery. He admitted to himself, for example, that whereas on the previous day he had been interested in his business, today his business was a matter of indifference to him. That, he knew, was not a good sign. But, then, tomorrow would certainly show some improvement. Indigestion﻿—and he was suffering from nothing but acute indigestion﻿—invariably did yield to a policy of starvation. As for hospitals, he had always had a horror of hospitals since once, in his insurance days, he had paid a visit to a fellow-clerk confined in a fever-ward. The vision of the huge, long, bare room, with its rows of beds and serried pain and distress, the draughts through the open windows, the rise and fall of the thunder of traffic outside, the semi-military bearing of the nurses, the wholesaleness of the affair, the absence of privacy, the complete subjection of the helpless patients, the inelasticity of regulations, the crushing of individuality: this dreadful vision had ineffaceably impressed itself on his imagination﻿—the imagination of an extreme individualist with a passion for living his own life free of the obligation to justify it or explain it. He had recalled the vision hundreds of times﻿—and never mentioned it to a soul. He did not intend to die of his illness; he knew that he would not die of it, but he convinced himself that he would prefer anything, even death, to incarceration in a great hospital. Were he wrenched by force out of his bed, he would kick and struggle to the very last, and his captors should be stricken with the fear of killing him while trying in their misguided zeal to save him. He read correctly the pertinacity in the doctor’s face. But he had never encountered a pertinacity stronger than his own, and illness had not weakened it, rather the reverse; his pertinacity had become morbid.

===Chinese===

这些人可笑地惦记他的健康。他们有他们的想法。他有他的。他提出过一个极其慷慨的折中——一个要花他一大罐钱的折中——竟然连讨论都不讨论；它的惊人之处全然没被认识到。嗯，总体上他倒庆幸这建议没被采纳。他收回它。他提它只是因为他感到——无疑是过度的忧虑——自己还没有开始向痊愈进展。比如他向自己承认，前一天他还关心他的生意，今天他的生意对他已无所谓了。他知道，这不是好征兆。可是，明天肯定会见好。消化不良——他害的不过是急性消化不良——在饥饿疗法下无不屈服。至于医院，他素来怕医院，打保险行那会儿起；有一回他去看望一位隔离在热病病房的同事。那间巨大、狭长、光秃的屋子，一排排的床和密密麻麻的疼痛与苦难，敞窗的穿堂风，外面车流轰鸣的起落，护士们半军事的做派，整批处理的派头，私生活的荡然无存，无助病人彻底的从属，章程的铁面无情，个性的被碾碎：这幅可怕的图景不可磨灭地烙在了他的想象上——一个极端个人主义者的想象，此人毕生的热情就是过自己的日子，不负解释它的义务。这幅图景他回想了几百遍——从未对一个人提起。他不打算死于这场病；他知道他不会死于它，可他说服自己：他宁可要任何东西，哪怕死，也不要被关进一家大医院。倘若他被强行从床上拖走，他会挣扎踢打到最后一息，而抓他的人将怀着恐惧——怕在误导的热情救他的过程中弄死他。他读得懂医生脸上那份执拗。可他从没遇到过比自己更强的执拗，病也没能削弱它，反倒相反；他的执拗已经病态了。

===Original===

“I don’t think I’ll go into a hospital, doctor,” he said quietly, turning his face away. The words were mild, the resolution invincible. The doctor crossed over to look him in the face. Their eyes met in fierce hostility. The doctor was beaten.
“Very well,” said he, with bitter calm. “If you won’t, you won’t. There is nothing else for me to do here. I must ask you to be good enough to get another adviser. And”﻿—he transfixed Elsie with a censorious gaze, as though Elsie was to blame﻿—“and, please remember that if the worst comes to the worst, I shall certainly refuse to give a certificate.”
“A certificate, sir?” Elsie faltered.
“Yes. A certificate of the cause of death. There would have to be an inquest,” he explained, with implacable and calculated cruelty.
But Mr. Earlforward only laughed﻿—a short, dry, sardonic laugh. The sun shone into the silent room and upon the tumbled bed and the sick, triumphant man, and made them more terrible than midnight could have made them. The doctor, with the pompous solemnity of a little man conscious of rectitude, slowly picked up his hat from the chest of drawers.
“But what am I to do?” Elsie appealed.
“My good woman, I don’t know. I wish I did. All I know is, I’ve done what I could; and I can’t take the private affairs of all Clerkenwell on my shoulders. I’ve other urgent cases to attend to.” A faint snigger, which his will was too late to suppress!
“Elsie’ll be all right,” muttered Mr. Earlforward. “Elsie’ll never desert me, Elsie won’t. She promised me.”
The doctor walked majestically out of the room, followed by Elsie.

===Chinese===

「我想我不进医院了，大夫，」他平静地说，把脸转开。话是温和的，决心是不可战胜的。医生走过去，直视他的脸。两双眼睛在凶狠的敌意中相撞。医生败了。
「很好，」他带着苦涩的平静说。「你不去就不去。我在此地再无事可做。只好请你费心另请高明。还有」——他一道责备的目光钉住埃尔西，仿佛都怪埃尔西——「还有，请记住，万一到了最坏的那一步，我一定拒绝出证明。」
「证明，先生？」埃尔西嘌嚅。
「是的。死因证明。那就要验尸了，」他解释道，带着不饶人的、蓄意的残忍。
可厄尔福沃德先生只是笑——一声短促、干巴巴、讥诮的笑。太阳照进寂静的房间，照在乱床上、照在那个病着而得胜的人身上，比午夜更能使他们可怕。医生以一个小人物自觉正直的堂皇庄严，慢慢从五斗柜上拾起帽子。
「可我该怎么办呢？」埃尔西哀告。
「我的好女人，我不知道。我要是知道就好了。我只知道，我能做的都做了；我不能把全克勒肯维尔的私事都扛在我肩上。我还有别的急症要处理。」轻轻一声窃笑——他的意志想拦已来不及了！
「埃尔西不会有事的，」厄尔福沃德先生喃喃。「埃尔西不会丢下我，埃尔西不会。她答应过我。」
医生威严地走出房间，埃尔西跟在后面。

## V The Two Patients / 两个病人
===Original===

Towards four o’clock in the morning Joe woke up from a short sleep and suddenly put questions to Elsie about his safety in that strange house, and also he inquired whose bed he was in.
“You’re in my bed, Joe,” she answered, kneeling again by the bedside, so as to have her face close to his and to whisper more intimately; and she told him the situation of the household and how her mistress had been carried to the hospital for an operation, and how her master was laid up with an unascertained disease, and how she alone had effective power in the house.
Then Joe began excitedly to talk of his adventures in the past twelve months, and she perceived that a change for the worse had come over him and that he was very ill. Both his voice and his glance indicated some development of the malady.
“Don’t tell me now, Joe dear,” she stopped him. “I want to hear it all, but you must rest now. Tomorrow, after you’ve had another good sleep. I must just go and look at Mr. Earlforward for a minute.”
She offered him a drink of water and left him, less to look at Mr. Earlforward than in order to give him an opportunity to calm himself, if that was possible. She knew that in certain moods solitude was best for him, ill or well. And she went down the dark stairs to the other bedroom, which was nearly as cold as the ice-cold stairs.
Mr. Earlforward also was worse. He seemed to be in a fever, yet looked like a corpse. Her arrival clearly gave him deep relief; he upbraided her for neglecting him; but somewhat timidly and cautiously, as one who feels himself liable to reprisals which could not be resisted. Elsie stayed with him and tended him for a quarter of an hour, and then went to the kitchen, which the extravagant gas-ring was gently keeping warm, while it warmed water and tried to dry Joe’s miserable clothes.
Elsie had to think. Both men under her charge were seriously ill, and she knew not what was the matter with either of them. Supposing that one of them died on her hands before the morning, or that both of them died! All her bliss at the reappearance of Joe had vanished. She had horrible thoughts, thoughts of which she was ashamed but which she could not dismiss. If anyone was to die she wanted it to be Mr. Earlforward. More, she could not help wishing that Mr. Earlforward would in any case die. She had solemnly promised Mr.

===Chinese===

凌晨将近四点钟，乔从短暂的睡眠中醒来，忽然向埃尔西问起自己在这陌生宅子里是否安全，又问这是谁的床。
「你躺在我的床上呢，乔，」她答道，又跪到床边，好让自己的脸凑近他，可以更亲密地低语。她把家里的情形讲给他听：女主人怎样被送进医院动手术，男主人怎样害了一场查不出名目的病躺着不起，又讲她怎样独自一个在家里掌着实权。
于是乔兴奋地讲起他过去一年的种种遭遇，她则看出他的情形恶化了，病得很重。他的嗓音和目光都显出病症又有了新的发展。
「现在别讲给我听，亲爱的乔，」她拦住他。「我都想听，可你现在必须歇着。等到明天，等你又好好睡过一觉再说。我得去看看厄尔福沃德先生，就去一下。」
她给他喝了点水，便离开了他——与其说是去看厄尔福沃德先生，不如说是想给他一个机会让自己平静下来，如果那是可能的话。她知道他在某些心绪之下，病也罢不病也罢，独处于他最好。她走下黑暗的楼梯，到另一间卧室去，那里差不多同冰冷的楼梯一样寒气逼人。
厄尔福沃德先生也加重了。他像在发烧，模样却似一具尸首。她的到来分明叫他大大松了口气；他责备她怠慢了自己，却责备得有点怯生生、小心翼翼，像一个人自觉挡不住人家可能的报复。埃尔西陪着他照料了一刻钟，然后到厨房去——那阔气的煤气灶正温和地烘着厨房，一面烧着热水，一面竭力烘干乔那身寒碜的衣裳。
埃尔西不得不思前想后。她照管的两个男人都病得厉害，而两个人害的是什么病她一概不知。倘若其中一个天亮以前死在她手上呢，或者两个都死了呢！乔重新出现带给她的全部欢欣都消散了。她起了可怕的念头——她自己都羞于承认、却又挥之不去的念头。若是注定要死一个，她巴不得死的是厄尔福沃德先生。岂止如此，她竟忍不住盼着厄尔福沃德先生无论如何都死了才好。她郑重答应过

===Original===

Earlforward never to desert him, and a promise was a promise. If he lived, and “anything happened” to Mrs. Earlforward, she was a prisoner for life. And if Joe lived Mr. Earlforward would never agree to her marrying him and having him in the house with her, as would assuredly be necessary, having regard to Joe’s health. Whereas with Mr. Earlforward out of the way she would be her own mistress and could easily assume full charge of Joe. Strange that so angelically kind and unselfish a creature could think so murderously; but think thus she did.
Further, the double responsibility which impulsively she had assumed weighed upon her with a crushing weight. Never had that always anxious brow been so puckered up with anxiety and hesitancy as now. Ought the doctor to be instantly summoned? But she could not fetch him herself; she dared not even leave her patients long enough to let her run over to the Square and rouse one of her friends there. And, moreover, she had a curious compunction about disturbing the doctor two nights in succession, and this compunction somehow counted in the balance against even men’s lives! She simply did not know what to do. She desperately needed counsel, and could not get it. On the whole she considered that the doctor should be sent for. Many scores, perhaps hundreds, of people were sleeping within a hundred yards of her. Was there not one among them to whom she could appeal? She returned to Joe. He was talking in his sleep. She went to the window, opened it, and gazed out.
A lengthy perspective of the backyards of the houses in King’s Cross Road stretched out before her; a pattern of dark walls﻿—wall, yard, wall, yard, wall, yard﻿—and the joint masonries of every pair of dwellings jutting out at regular intervals in back rooms additional to the oblongs of the houses. The sky was clear, a full moon had dimmed the stars; and fine weather, which would have been a boon to the day, was being wasted on the unconscious night. The moonlight glinted here and there on window-glass. Every upper window marked a bedroom. And in every bedroom were souls awake or asleep. Not a window lit, except one at the end of the vista. Perhaps behind that window somebody was suffering and somebody watching. Or it might be only that somebody was rising to an interminable, laborious day. The heavy night of the town oppressed Elsie dreadfully.

===Chinese===

厄尔福沃德先生决不离开他，而答应的事就是答应的事。他若活着，而厄尔福沃德太太又「出了什么事」，她便终身是个囚徒。而乔若活着，厄尔福沃德先生决不会答应她嫁给他、把他接进屋里同住——鉴于乔的身体，这又是万不可少的。可要是厄尔福沃德先生不在了，她便自己做得了自己的主，轻而易举地把乔整个儿接管过来。真奇怪，这样天使般仁慈无私的人，竟能起这样狠毒的杀心；可她偏偏就是这样想了。
再者，她一时冲动担下的双重责任，此刻以千钧之力压在她身上。她那永远锁着愁的眉头，从来不曾像此刻这样被忧愁和踌躇绞得这样紧。要不要立刻去请大夫？可她不能亲自去请；她甚至不敢离开病人久到足以跑到广场那边唤醒她的一位朋友。何况，接连两夜打扰大夫，她心头有一种说不出的过意不去，而这分过意不去，在天平上竟压得连人命都不足重了！她简直不知如何是好。她迫切需要有人指点，却求告无门。总起来想，她觉得大夫还是应当去请。方圆百步之内睡着几十人，兴许几百人。其中就没有一个她可以求助的人么？她回到乔那里。他在说着梦话。她走到窗前，推窗望去。
眼前展开一长列国王十字路上那些屋子的后院；一式深色的墙——墙、院、墙、院、墙、院——每一对住宅的合用墙皮按着均匀的间隔凸出去，在方匣子似的屋身之外多出一间间后房。天空明净，一轮满月把星光照得黯淡；明日的好天气——白天原是求之不得的——正白白抛掷给毫无知觉的黑夜。月光这里那里在窗玻璃上闪亮。每一扇楼上窗口都意味着一间卧室。每一间卧室里都有睡着或醒着的灵魂。没有一扇窗点着灯，只尽头处有一扇亮着。那扇窗后头，也许有人正在受苦、有人正在守着。又也许不过是有人起身，去面对漫无尽头的劳碌的一天。城里的沉沉黑夜压得埃尔西透不过气来。

===Original===

She had noticed that a little dog kennelled in the yard of the very next house to T. T. Riceyman’s was fitfully moaning and yapping. Then a light flickered into a steady gleam behind a window of this same house, less than a dozen feet away, with an uncanny effect upon Elsie. The light waned to nothing, and shortly afterwards the back door opened, and the figure of a young woman in a loose gown, with unbound hair, was silhouetted against the radiance of a candle within the house. Across the tiny backyard of T. T.’s Elsie could plainly see the woman, whose appearance was totally unfamiliar to her. A soul living close to her perhaps for months and years, and she did not know her from Eve! Elsie wanted to call out to her, but dared not. A pretty face, the woman had, only it was hard, exasperated, angry. The woman advanced menacingly upon the young, chained dog, and the next moment there was one sharp yell, followed by a diminuendo succession of yells. “That’ll learn ye to keep people awake all night,” Elsie heard a thin, inimical voice say. The woman returned to the house. The dog began again to yap and moan. The woman ran out in a fury, picked up the animal and flung it savagely into the kennel. Elsie could hear the thud of its soft body against the wood. She shrank back, feeling sick. The woman retired from her victory; the door was locked; the light showed once more at the bedroom window, and went out; the infant dog, as cold and solitary as ever, and not in the least comprehending the intention of the treatment which it had received, issued from the kennel and resumed its yapping and moaning.
“Poor little thing!” murmured the ingenuous Elsie, and shut the window.
No! She could not send anybody at all for the doctor. Common sense came to her aid. She must wait till morning. A few hours, and it would be full day. And the risk of a disaster in those few hours was exceedingly small. She must not be a silly, frightened little fool. Joe was still talking in his restless sleep. She quickly made up the fire, and then revisited Mr. Earlforward, who also was asleep and talking. After a moment she fetched a comb and went to the kitchen, washed her face and hands in warm water, took down her blue-black hair, combed it and did it up. And she put on a clean apron. She had to look nice and fresh for her patients when the next day should start.

===Chinese===

她早已留意到，紧挨着「T.T.莱斯门」的那户人家院里，一条小狗断断续续地呜咽、狺狺地叫。随后，十几英尺外那同一户人家的一扇窗后，一点光摇曳着凝成一束稳定的亮，叫埃尔西心里发毛。那点光渐渐暗下去熄了，不多时后门开了，一个披着宽松晨衣、头发放散的年轻女人的身影，衬着屋里烛光映出来。隔着T.T.莱斯门这方小小的后院，埃尔西可以把那女人看得清清楚楚——一张全然陌生的脸。一个也许同她比邻住了几月几年的灵魂，她竟素不相识！埃尔西想朝她喊一声，却不敢。那女人有张漂亮脸蛋，只是又硬、又躁、又怒气冲冲。她气势汹汹地朝那条拴着的小狗逼过去，紧接着一声尖利的惨叫，随后是一声低似一声、渐渐消歇的连续惨叫。「叫你再闹得人一夜不得睡，」埃尔西听见一个尖细的、含着敌意的声音说。那女人回屋去了。狗又叫起来，呜呜咽咽。女人怒气冲冲地奔出来，拎起那小东西，狠狠往狗窝里一掼。埃尔西听得见它柔软的身子撞在木头上的闷响。她缩回身子，只觉恶心。那女人得胜回朝；门上了锁；卧房的窗又一次透出光来，随即熄灭；那条幼犬，依旧又冷又孤单，全然不解自己挨的这顿摆布是什么意思，从窝里钻出来，重新呜呜咽咽地叫。
「怪可怜的小东西！」天真烂漫的埃尔西喃喃说着，关上了窗。
不！她根本不能派任何人去请大夫。常识出来帮了她的忙。她必须等到天亮。再过几个钟头就是大白天了。而在这几个钟头里出大乱子的风险微乎其微。她可不能做个又傻又怕的小傻瓜。乔还在不安的睡梦里说着话。她赶紧给炉子添了火，又去看了一趟厄尔福沃德先生——他也睡着，也在说梦话。过了一会儿她拿了一把梳子到厨房去，用温水洗了脸和手，放下乌黑的头发，梳好了再盘起来。又系上一条干净的围裙。等新的一天开始时，她得清清爽爽、齐齐整整地见她那两个病人。

===Original===

For her night and day were now the same; her existence had become continuous﻿—no break in consciousness﻿—it ran on and on and on. She did not feel tired. On the contrary, she felt intensely alive and energetic and observant, and had no desire for sleep. And her greed seemed to have left her.

===Chinese===

因为此刻黑夜白昼于她已是一样；她的存在变成了连续不断的一条——知觉没有间歇——一直向前流啊、流啊、流啊。她不觉得累。相反，她觉得自己生机勃勃、劲头十足、事事看得真切，毫无睡意。而她那份嘴馋似乎也丢掉了。

## VII Under an Umbrella / 伞下
===Original===

The entrance-gates to the yard of Daphut, the builder and stonemason, which lay between Mrs. Arb’s shop and the steps proper, were set back a little from the general frontage of the north side of Riceyman Steps, so that there was a corner at that point sheltered from east and northeast winds. In this corner stood a young man under an old umbrella; his clothes were such as would have entitled him to the newspaper reporter’s description, “respectably dressed”﻿—no better. His back was against the blind wall of Mrs. Arb’s. It was raining again, with a squally wind, but the wind being in the northeast the young man was only getting spotted with rain. A young woman ran out of Mrs. Arb’s and joined him. She placed herself close to him, touching him, breast to breast; it was the natural and rational thing to do, and also she had to receive as much protection as possible from the umbrella. The girl was wearing all Elsie’s clothes. Elsie’s sack-apron covered her head and shoulders like a bridal veil. But she was not Mrs. Arb’s Elsie nor Mr. Earlforward’s! She was not the drudge. She had suddenly become a celestial visitant. The attributes of such an unearthly being were in her shining face and in the solace of her little bodily movements; and her extraordinary mean and ugly apparel could not impair them in the least. The man, slowly, hesitatingly, put one arm round her waist﻿—the other was occupied with the umbrella. She yielded her waist to him, and looked up at the man, and he looked down at her. Not a word. Then he said in a deep voice:
“Where’s your hat﻿—and things?”
He said this as one who apprehended calamity.
“I haven’t finished yet,” she answered gently. “I’m that sorry.”
“How long shall you be?”
“I don’t know, Joe. She’s all by herself, and she begged and prayed me to stop on and help her. She’s all by herself, and strange to it. And I couldn’t find it in my heart to refuse. You have to do what’s right, haven’t you?”
The man’s chin fell in a sort of sulky and despairing gloom; but he said nothing; he was not a facile talker, even on his best days. She took the umbrella from him without altering its position.
“Put both arms round me, and hold me tight,” she murmured.
He obeyed, reluctantly, tardily, but in the end fiercely. After a long pause he said:
“And my birthday and all!”
“I know! I know!” she cried. “Oh, Joe! It can’t be helped!”

===Chinese===

建筑商兼石匠达法特的院子大门，位于阿尔布太太的铺子与台阶本身之间，比莱斯门台阶北侧的临街面略微缩进一些，于是那里形成一个不受东风、东北风侵袭的角落。一个年轻人打着一把旧伞，站在这个角落里；他那身衣裳，够得上报馆记者笔下「衣履整洁」四个字——也仅止于此。他背抵着阿尔布太太铺子那面无窗的墙。又下起雨来，风一阵阵狂吹，好在风在东北，年轻人身上不过溅了些雨点。
一个年轻姑娘从阿尔布太太铺子里跑出来，到了他身边。她紧贴着他，胸脯贴着胸脯；这原是最自然最合理的姿势，何况她还须从那把伞底下得到尽可能多的遮蔽。这姑娘从头到脚穿的都是埃尔西的衣裳。埃尔西那件罩衫式围裙罩在她头上肩上，宛如一层婚纱。可她已不是阿尔布太太的埃尔西，也不是厄尔福沃德先生的埃尔西了！她不是那个苦役似的老妈子。她倏然变成了一位天外来客。这种超凡生灵的品性，全在她那张发光的脸上，全在她小小的身体动作所给予的慰藉里；她那身寒酸得出了奇的装束丝毫折损不了它们。
男人迟疑地、慢慢地伸出一条胳膊搂住她的腰——另一条胳膊要打伞。她把腰交给他，仰起脸望着男人，男人低头望着她。一句话也没有。半晌，他才低沉地说：
「你的帽子——还有那些东西呢？」
他说这话的口气，像一个人预感到了大祸临头。
「还没弄完呢，」她轻声答道。「我心里那个过意不去。」
「还得多久？」
「我不知道，乔。她孤零零一个人，求了又求，求我留下来帮帮她。她孤零零一个人，又生疏得很。我实在狠不下心拒绝。人总得做该做的事，不是么？」
男人的下巴垂了下去，一副怏怏的、绝望的阴沉；可他什么也没说；他本来就不是个会说话的人，即使在他最顺的日子。她接过那把伞，位置一动没动。
「两只胳膊都搂着我，搂紧些，」她低声说。
他照办了，勉勉强强，慢慢腾腾，末了却是狠狠地搂紧。好一阵沉默之后，他说：
「还赶上我的生日！」
「我知道！我知道！」她喊道。「唉，乔！没法子呀！」

===Original===

He had many arguments, and good ones, against her decision; but he could not utter them. He never could argue. She just gazed up at him softly. Tears began to run down his cheeks.
“Now, now!” she soothed him. With her free hand she worked up the tail of her apron between them, and, while still fast in his clutch, wiped his eyes delicately. She kissed him, keeping her lips on his. She kissed him until she knew from the feel of his muscles everywhere that the warm soft contact with her had begun to dissolve his resentment. Then she withdrew her lips and kissed him again, differently. They stood motionless in the dark corner under the umbrella, and the rain pattered dully on the umbrella and dropped off the umbrella and round them, and pattered with a brighter sound on the flagstones of Riceyman Steps. A few people passed at intervals up and down the steps. But the clasped pair ignored them; and the wayfarers did not look twice, nor even smile at the lovers, who, in fact, were making love as honest love is made by lovers whose sole drawing rooms and sofas are the street.
“Look here, Joe,” Elsie whispered. “I want you to go home now. But you must call at Smithson’s on yer way﻿—they don’t close till nine o’clock﻿—and get them braces as I’m giving you for a birthday present. I see ’em still in the window this morning. I should have slipped in and bought ’em then, but I was on an errand for Mr. Earlforward, and, besides, I didn’t like to, somehow, without you, and me with my apron on too. But you must buy ’em tonight so as you can wear ’em tomorrow. I want to say to myself tomorrow morning, ‘He’s wearing them braces.’ I’ve brought you the money.” She loosed one of his hands from her waist, got at the silver in her pocket, and inserted it into his breast pocket. “You promise me, Joe? It’s a fair and square promise?”
He made no reply.
“You promise me, darling Joe?” she insisted.
He nodded; he could not speak in his desolation and in his servitude to her. She smiled her lovely thanks for his obedience.
“Now let me see ye start off,” she cajoled him. “I know ye. I know what *you’ll* do if I don’t see you start with me own eyes.”
“Then it’s tomorrow night?” he said gruffly.
She nodded. They kissed again. Elsie pushed him away, and then stood watching until he had vanished round the corner of the disused Mission Hall into King’s Cross Road.

===Chinese===

他有许多道理可以驳她的决定，而且都是好道理；可他说不出口。他从来就不会讲道理。她只是柔柔地仰望着他。泪水开始顺着他的双颊往下淌。
「好了，好了！」她抚慰他。她腾出一只手，把围裙的边角从两人中间抽上来，趁着还被紧紧搂在他怀里，轻轻替他擦眼睛。
她吻他，唇贴着他的唇不放。她吻了又吻，直到从周身肌肉的感觉里知道，同她温软的接触已开始消融他的怨气。这时她收回嘴唇，又换了个样子再吻他。
两人在伞下黑黢黢的角落里一动不动地站着，雨单调地敲着伞面，从伞上滚落，落在他们四周，又以更清脆的声响敲着莱斯门台阶的石板。不时有人沿台阶上上下下。可相拥的两个人全不理会；过路的人也不多看一眼，甚至不朝这对恋人笑一笑——他们谈情说爱的样子，正同那些唯一的客厅和沙发就是街头的情人们诚实的恋爱一个样。
「听我说，乔，」埃尔西悄声道。「我要你现在就回家去。可你路上一定得去史密森铺子一趟——他们九点才打烊——把我送你做生日礼物的那副背带买来。今儿早上我还看见它摆在橱窗里呢。本来我该溜进去买了的，可我正替厄尔福沃德先生跑腿，再说，不知怎么的，不跟你一块儿我不想去，何况我还系着围裙。可你今儿晚上一定得买来，明儿才好穿上。明儿早上我要自己跟自己说：『他穿上那背带了。』钱我给你带来了。」她把他搂着她腰的一只手解开，掏出衣袋里的银币，塞进他胸前的口袋。「你答应我，乔？规规矩矩答应我？」
他没有作声。
「你答应我，亲爱的乔？」她追问。
他点了点头；他凄惶到了极处，又甘心做她的奴仆，说不出话来。她微微一笑，笑出她那可爱的谢意，谢他的依顺。
「这就让我瞧着你动身，」她哄他。「我可知道你。不亲眼看你就此走了，我知道*你*会干出什么来。」
「那就是明儿晚上？」他粗声粗气地说。
她点头。两人又吻了一回。埃尔西把他推开，然后站在那里望着，直到他的身影绕过废弃的布道堂拐角、进了国王十字路，看不见了。

===Original===

She stood watching, indeed, for some moments after that. She was crying.
“My word!” said Mrs. Arb vivaciously. “I was beginning to wonder if you meant to come back, after all. You’ve been that long your tea’ll be cold. Here’s the ham, and very nice it is too.”

===Chinese===

其实，他又消失之后，她还站着望了好一会儿。她在哭。
「哎哟！」阿尔布太太活泼泼地说。「我都开始琢磗你到底还回不回来了呢。去了这么半天，你的茶都要凉了。火腿在这儿，可真是好火腿。」

## IV Vacuum / 真空
===Original===

For potent municipal or administrative reasons the tramcar carrying Henry and his bride would not stop at Riceyman Steps, but it stopped fifty yards farther down the road. As Henry was whisked thunderingly past his home and the future nest of his love, he glimpsed in the Steps such a spectacle as put a strain on the credibility of his eyes. Only on the rarest occasions do men refuse to believe their eyes; they are much more likely to allow themselves to be deluded by their deceitful eyes. The vision was come and gone in a moment, and Henry, who had great confidence in his eyes, did, in fact, accept, though with difficulty, their report, which was to the effect that a considerable crowd had collected in front of his house, that the house was blazing with light, and that forms resembling engines, with serpentine hose rising therefrom, stood between the shop-door and the multitude of spectators.
“Did you see that?” he demanded, sharply but calmly.
“See what, dear?” said Violet, self-consciously.
“The house is on fire.”
“Oh, no! It *can’t* be on fire.”
A strange colloquy! It seemed unreal to him. And the strangest thing was that he did not honestly think the house was on fire. He did not know what to think. But he suspected his angel of some celestial scheming against him; and he considered that she was beginning rather early and that his first business must be to set her in the true, wifely path. Suspicion is a wonderful collector of evidence in its own support. He recalled her agitation when he had decided to tear up the programme for the day and go home earlier; the agitation had soon passed, but during the journey to Clerkenwell it had certainly recurred, increasing somewhat as they neared the destination. Also he recalled her private chat with Elsie before leaving in the morning. At the time he had attached no significance to that whispered interview, but now it suddenly took on a most sinister aspect.
An amazing fellow was Henry. As he hurried, without a word, from the tram to the house he carefully maintained his limp, and in pushing through the crowd he was careful to avoid any appearance of astonishment or alarm. At any rate, the engines, both throbbing, were too small to be fire-engines, there were no brass helmets or policemen about, and the house was not on fire. What distressed him was the insane expenditure of electricity that was going on.

===Chinese===

出于市政或行政当局强有力的理由，载着亨利和他新娘的有轨电车不在莱斯门台阶停站，而是在再往下五十码的地方才停。电车轰隆隆地一掠而过，把亨利载过他的家、载过他未来爱巢的所在，就在这一瞥之间，他在台阶那里看见了一幅叫人不敢相信自己眼睛的景象。人只有在最罕见的场合才肯不相信自己的眼睛；更常见的倒是任凭那骗人的眼睛把自己蒙了过去。那景象一闪即逝。亨利向来极信自己的眼睛，这一回也果真——尽管颇费力气——接受了它们的报告：他家门前聚起了一大群人，整座宅子灯火通明，几台像引擎似的东西立在店门与看热闹的人群之间，蛇一样的皮管从那上头翘起来。
「你看见了么？」他问，语气尖锐而平静。
「看见什么，亲爱的？」薇奥莱特说，有点心虚。
「房子着火了。」
「哦，不会的！*绝不可能*着火。」
好奇怪的一段对话！他听着都像不真的。而最奇怪的是，他心里其实并不认为房子着了火。他不知道该怎么想。只是他疑心他的天使在对他施展什么天上的计谋；并且他觉得她起手得未免太早，他的头一件大事便是把她引上贤妻的正路。猜疑这东西，搜集起于自己有利的证据来本领惊人。他记起当他决定撕掉当天的节目单提早回家时她的那份激动；激动不久便平息了，可在来克拉肯韦尔的一路上它分明又回来了，越临近目的地越见涨。他还记起早上出门前她同埃尔西的私语。当时他并未把那场咬耳朵的密谈放在心上，此刻它却忽然露出一片凶兆。
亨利真是个了不得的人物。他从电车匆匆走向宅子，一路一言不发，还仔细保持着他的跛行；从人群中挤过去时，又处处留神不露一点惊讶或慌张的神色。不管怎样，那两台突突颤动的机器个头太小，不是消防引擎；四周没有戴铜盔的消防员，也没有警察；房子也没有着火。叫他痛心的是眼下这场丧心病狂的电费开销。

===Original===

And why was the shop open? The day being Saturday it ought to have been closed hours ago.
He strode over a hosepipe into his establishment. One side of the place looked just as if it had been newly papered and painted, and all the books on that side shone like books that had been dusted and vaselined with extreme care daily for months; almost the whole of the ceiling was nearly white, and the remainder of it was magically whitening under a wide-mouthed brass nozzle that a workman who stood on a pair of steps was applying to it. And Henry heard a swishing sound as of the in-drawing of wind. He went forward mechanically into his private room, which, quite unbelievably, was as clean as a new pin. No grime, no dust anywhere! And not a book displaced. The books which ordinarily lay on the floor still lay on the floor, and even the floor planks looked as if they had been planed or sandpapered. He dropped into a chair.
“Darling, how pale you are!” murmured Violet, bending to him. “*This is my wedding present to you. I wanted it to be a surprise, but you’ve gone and spoilt it all with coming back home so soon!* And I couldn’t stop you.”
He did not realize for weeks the grandeur of his wife’s act, which had outraged a thrifty instinct in her nearly as powerful as his own. But he realized at once the initiative and the talent for organized execution which had rendered her plan successful. How had she managed to accomplish the affair without betraying to him the slightest hint of what she was about? A prodigious performance! And she had suborned the faithful Elsie, too!
He could not like the cleanliness. He had been robbed of something. And the place had lost its look of home; it was bare, inhospitable, and he was a stranger in it.
“How much is it to cost?” he breathed.
“Well,” Violet answered hesitatingly, “of course, vacuum-cleaning isn’t what you’d call cheap. But it saves so much labour and wear-and-tear and inconvenience that it pays for itself over and over again. And you know I can’t *stand* dirt. And when a thing’s got to be done I’m one of those that must get it over and have *done* with it. And it’s my little present to you. Shall I rub your knee with some Zam-buk? I have some.”
“How much is it to cost?” he repeated.
“Well, it ought by rights only to cost ten pounds for the whole job.”
“Ten pounds!”
“Yes.

===Chinese===

还有，铺子怎么开着门？这天是星期六，早该关张好几个钟头了。
他跨过一根皮管，大步走进他的店堂。店堂的一面看去就像刚裱糊粉刷过似的，那一面的书全在发亮，像几个月来天天被人精心掸尘抹油的书；天花板几乎整片都白了，剩下的一角在一个工人的宽口铜喷嘴底下正奇妙地继续变白，那工人站在一副梯子上操作。亨利听见一阵呼呼声，像风往里吸。他失魂落魄地走进自己的内容室——干净得崭新一枚针，简直叫人不能相信。哪儿也没有污垢，没有灰尘！一本书也没挪动。平日摊在地上的书照旧摊在地上，连地板的木板都像刚刨过或刚用砂纸打过。他一屁股跌坐进椅子里。
「亲爱的，你脸色多白呀！」薇奥莱特俯身向他，喃喃说。「*这是我送你的新婚礼物。我本想给你个意外之喜，可你偏这么早就回了家，全给揽了！*我又拦不住你。」
过了好几个星期他才领会他妻子这番壮举的伟大——这壮举蹂躏了她自己身上那节俭的天性，那天性几乎同他的一样强。可他立刻就领会了那份首创的魄力和组织执行的才干，全靠这些她的计划才得成功。她怎么竟能把这件事办成，而不向他泄露半点风声？了不得的手段！而且还把忠心的埃尔西也收买通了！
他喜欢不起这份洁净。他被抢走了什么东西。这地方失去了家的模样；它光秃秃的、冷冰冰的，他在里面成了个陌生人。
「这要花多少钱？」他屏着气问。
「这个嘛，」薇奥莱特迟疑着答道，「真空吸尘当然说不上便宜。可它省下的工夫、损耗和麻烦那么多，等于一遍一遍地把自己本钱挣回来。你知道我*受不了*脏。事情既然非做不可，我就是这样的人：非一口气做完、*彻底*了结不可。这算我送你的一件小礼物。要不要我给你膝盖上擦点赞布克药膏？我带着呢。」
「这要花多少钱？」他又问一遍。
「呃，按理说，整个儿做下来只要十镑。」
「十镑！」
「是呀。」

===Original===

Only as I wanted it done in a great hurry, I knew that would mean two machines instead of one; and besides that, the men expect overtime pay for Saturday afternoons. I’m afraid it’ll cost thirteen or fourteen pounds. But think how nice it’s going to be. Look at this room. You wouldn’t know it.”
“Fourteen pounds!”
The wages of a morning charwoman for over three months! Squandered in a few hours! The potentialities of Violet’s energetic brain frightened him.
“You aren’t vexed, I’m sure!” said Violet.
“Of course I’m not,” he replied blandly, admitting the nobility of her motives and the startling efficiency of her methods.
“Perhaps I ought to have told you.”
“Yes.”
“But, you see, I wanted it to be a surprise for you.”
He walked back into the shop and thence outside.
“What do you do with the dirt?” he inquired of one of the men in charge of the machines.
“Oh, we take it away, sir. We shan’t leave any mess about.”
“Do you sell it? Do you get anything for it?”

===Chinese===

「只是因为我要他们赶着做完，我知道那就得用两台机器，不是一台；再说，星期六下午工人们还要加班费。恐怕得花十三四镑。可你想想这以后多好。瞧瞧这间屋子。你都要认不得它了。」
「十四镑！」
这是打杂女工三个多月的工钱！几个钟头里就挥霍掉了！薇奥莱特那颗精力充沛的脑袋所蕴藏的能耐，叫他害怕。
「你不会惼的，我知道！」薇奥莱特说。
「我当然不惼，」他温和地答道，一面承认她动机的高尚、她手段的惊人高效。
「也许我该先告诉你的。」
「是的。」
「可是你要知道，我想给你个意外之喜呀。」
他走回店堂，又从店堂走到外头。
「这些脏东西你们怎么处理？」他向一个管机器的工人打听。
「哦，我们运走，先生。不会留下一点儿脏。」
「你们卖它么？能卖得出钱么？」

## IX Violet’s Victory / 薇奥莱特的胜利
===Original===

“How do you feel, my girl?” Henry asked.
They lay again in bed together. Before leaving the doctor had given, with casualness, certain instructions, not apparently important, which Violet had carried out, having understood that there was no immediate danger to her husband and also that there was nothing immediately to be done. Dr. Raste’s final remarks, as he departed, had had a sardonic tone, almost cynical, which had at first abraded Violet’s sensitiveness; but later she had said to herself: “After all, with a patient like Henry, what *can* you expect a doctor to do?” And she had accepted, and begun to share, the doctor’s attitude. A patient might be very seriously ill, he might be dying of cancer, and yet by his callous and stupid obstinacy alienate your sympathies from him. Human sympathies were as precarious as that! She admitted it. A few minutes earlier she had lifted Henry to a pedestal of perfection. Now she dashed him down from it. “I know I oughtn’t to feel as I do, but I *do* feel as I do.” And she even confirmed herself in harshness. She had sent Elsie to bed for the few remaining hours of the night. She had undressed once more and got into bed herself.
The light of the fire played faintly at intervals on the astonished ceiling, and sometimes shafts of moonlight could be discerned through an aperture in the thick, drawn curtains. Behind the curtains the blind could be heard now and then answering restlessly to the north breeze. The room was so warm that the necessity to keep the bedclothes over the shoulders and up to the chin had disappeared. Violet had a strange sense of luxury. “And why shouldn’t we have a fire *every* night?” she thought, and added, somewhat afraid of the extravagance of the proposition: “Well, anyhow, *some* nights﻿—when it’s very cold.” She gave no reply to Henry’s question about her health.
Henry felt much better. He had scarcely any pain at the spot which the doctor had indicated; he was as sure as ever that he had done right in refusing to enter a hospital, and as determined as ever that he never would enter a hospital. None the less, he was disturbed; he was a bit frightened of trouble in the bed. He had noted his wife’s face before she turned the light out, and seen rare and unmistakable signs in it. His illness was not now the important matter, nor her illness either.

===Chinese===

「你感觉怎么样，我的姑娘？」亨利问。
两人又一起躺在床上。临走前，医生漫不经心地吩咐了几句，看去不要紧，薇奥莱特都照办了；她明白丈夫眼下没有性命之忧，也明白眼下无医可施。拉斯特医生临别时最后那几句话带着讥诮，近乎冷嘲，起初磨得薇奥莱特心里生疼；可后来她对自己说：「说到底，摊上亨利这样的病人，还能指望医生*怎么*样呢？」于是她接受了医生的态度，还渐渐同他共享了这态度。一个病人可以病得极重，可以眼看死于癌症，却仍旧用他那冷酷而愚蠢的固执，把你的怜悯从他自己身上赶开。人的怜悯竟是这样靠不住的东西！她承认了。几分钟之前，她刚把亨利抬上完美的宝座。现在又把他从上面捯了下来。「我知道我不该这样想，可我*偏*就这样想。」她甚至存心让自己狠下心去。夜色剩下的几个钟头，她已打发埃尔西去睡了。她又脱了衣裳，自己上了床。
炉火的光一阵一阵淡淡地映在那惊愕的天花板上；厚厚的拉拢的窗帘偶尔露一道缝，漏进几股月光。窗帘后面，百叶窗时不时不安地应和着北风。屋里暖和得很，被子非得盖到肩头、拉到下巴的那种必要已经没有了。薇奥莱特有一种奇怪的奢侈感。「咱们干吗不*天天*夜里生火呢？」她想，随即又被这提议的挥霍吓着了似的，补上一句：「呃，反正，*偶尔*几夜——顶冷的时候。」对亨利那句问她安好的话，她没有答。
亨利觉得好多了。医生指过的那个地方几乎不疼了；他一如既往地确信自己拒进医院做得对，也一如既往地打定主意永不进医院。然而他心里不安；他有点怕床上出乱子。熄灯之前他留意过妻子的脸色，看出了一些罕见而错不了的端倪。此刻要紧的已不是他的病，也不是她的病。

===Original===

The important matter was their sentimental relations. He knew that he had estranged her. Convinced of the justice of his own cause and of the folly of doctors and wives, he was yet apprehensive and had somehow a quite illogical conviction of guilt. Violet had wanted to act against his best interests, and yet he must try to appease her! It was more important to appease her than to get well!
Dr. Raste, or anybody else, looking at the couple lying beneath Violet’s splendid eiderdown (which still by contrast intensified the dowdiness and shabbiness of the rest of the room) would have seen merely a middle-aged man and a middle-aged woman with haggard faces worn by illness, fatigue, privations and fear. But Henry did not picture himself and Violet thus; nor Violet herself and Henry. Henry did not feel middle-aged. He did not feel himself to be any particular age. His interest in life and in his own existence had not diminished during the enormous length of time which had elapsed since he first came into Riceyman Steps as a young man. In his heart he felt no older than on that first night. He did not feel that he now in the least corresponded to his youthful conception of a middle-aged man. He did not feel that he was as old as other men whom he knew to be of about his own age. He thought that he alone had mysteriously remained young among his generation. For him his grey hairs had no significance; they were an accident. Then in regard to his notion of Violet. He knew that all women were alike, but with one exception﻿—Violet. Women were women, and Violet was thrice a woman. He was aware of her age arithmetically, for he had seen her birth-certificate. But in practice she was a girl﻿—well, perhaps a little more than a girl, but not much more. And she had for him a romantic quality perceptible in no other woman. He admired certain efficiencies in her, but he could not have said why she was so important to him, nor why he was vaguely afraid of her frown﻿—why it was so urgent for him to stand well with her. He could defeat her in battle. He had more common sense than she had, more authority, a surer grasp of things; he could see farther; he was more straightforward. In fact, a superior being! Further, she had crossed him, sided with the doctor against him, made him resentful. Therefore, if justice reigned, she ought to be placating him.

===Chinese===

要紧的是他们两人之间的情分。他知道他把她疏远了。他一边坚信自己理直、坚信医生和妻子们的糊涂，一边却又惴惴不安，心里莫名生出一种全然不合逻辑的罪愆。明明是薇奥莱特要作践他的切身利益，到头来却得他去安抚她！安抚她竟比养病还要紧！
拉斯特医生，或者随便什么人，看着躺在薇奥莱特那床华贵鸭绒被底下的这一对（那床被子一衬，屋里其余的一切愈发显得寒碜破旧），看见的不过是两个中年人，一张男人脸一张女人脸，都被疾病、劳累、匮乏和恐惧折磨得憔悴不堪。可亨利并不这样看待自己同薇奥莱特；薇奥莱特自己看待亨利和自己也不是这样。亨利不觉得自己是中年。他觉得自己没有年纪。打他年轻时初到莱斯门台阶起，过去了那么漫长的一大段岁月，他对生活、对自己这条命的兴趣却未见衰减。在他心里，他同头一夜一样年轻。他觉得自己同年轻时想象中的「中年人」毫不相干。他觉得自己不像那些他知道与他同龄的人那样老。他以为整整一代人里，只有他一个神秘地保住了青春。至于他的白头发，那不算数；那是意外。
再说他心目中的薇奥莱特。他知道天下女人一个样，只有一个例外——薇奥莱特。女人就是女人，而薇奥莱特是三倍的女人。她的年纪，他从算术上知道，因为他见过她的出生证明。可在实际里她是个姑娘——好吧，也许比姑娘多一点，可多不到哪儿去。并且她于他有一种浪漫的品质，别的女人身上觅不见的。他佩服她某些干练之处，可他说不出她为什么对他这样要紧，也说不清自己为什么隐隐怕她皱眉——为什么在她跟前得宠竟是这样急切的一件事。交起锋来他打得过她。他比她见识多，比她有威严，把事情握得更牢；他看得更远；他更直来直去。总而言之，是个高一等的人！况且，是她顶撞了他，是她同医生联手对付他，是他满腹委屈。所以，倘若天下有公道，该是她来安抚他才对。

===Original===

Instead, he was anxious to placate her.
And, on her part, Violet saw in Henry a man not of any age, simply a man: egotistic, ruthless, childish, naughty, illogical, incalculable, the supreme worry of her life; a destroyer of happiness; a man indefensible for his misdeeds, but very powerful and inexplicably romantic, different from all other men whatsoever. She hated him; her resentment against him was very keen, and yet she wanted to fondle him, physically and spiritually; and this desire maintained itself not without success in opposition to all her grievances, and, compared to it, her sufferings and his had but a minor consequence.
“Well, how do you feel?” he repeated.
The repetition aroused Violet’s courage. She paused before speaking, and in the pause she matured a magnificent, a sublime enterprise of attack. She had a feeling akin to inspiration. She flouted his illness, his tremendous power, her own weakness and pain. She did not care what happened. No risk could check her.
“You don’t care how I am!” she began quietly and bitterly. “Did you show the slightest interest in me all yesterday? Not one bit. You thought only of yourself. You pretended you were ill. Well, if you weren’t, why couldn’t you think about me? But you were ill. Not that that excuses you! However ill I was, I should be thinking about you all the time. But I say you were ill, and I say it again. You only told me a lot of lies about yourself, one lie after another. Why *do* you keep yourself to yourself?
