It’s an insult to me, all this hiding, and you know it. I suppose you think I’m not good enough to be told! I can tell you one thing, and I’ve said it before, and this is the last time I ever shall say it﻿—you’ve taught me to sew my mouth up, too; that’s what you’ve done with your everlasting secrecy. I always said you’re the most selfish and cruel man that ever was. You’re ill, and the doctor says you ought to go to a hospital﻿—and you won’t. Why? Doesn’t everybody go into a hospital some time or another? A hospital’s not good for you﻿—that’s it. It suits you better to stop here and be nursed night and day by your wife. Don’t matter how ill *I* am! I’ve got to nurse you *and* look after the shop as well. It’ll kill me; but a fat lot you care about that.

===Chinese===

偏偏是他急巴巴地要去安抚她。
而在薇奥莱特那方面，她看亨利，看见的不是一个有年纪的男人，干脆就是一个男人：自顾自、狠心肠、孩子气、浑、不讲理、摸不透，她此生头一桩的心病；一个专毁人幸福的东西；一个所作所为无可辩护、却又极有力量、说不明白地带着浪漫、同普天下别的男人全都两样的男人。她恨他；对他的怨气重得很，可她又想疼他，身上疼、心里也疼；这分心愿顶着满腔的委屈硬是撑住了，还撑得不无成绩；同它一比，她的苦、他的苦，都算不得什么了。
「呃，你感觉怎么样？」他又问了一遍。
这一问反倒唤起了薇奥莱特的胆气。她开口之前先顿了顿，就在这一顿里，她酝酿成了一场堂皇的、轰轰烈烈的进攻大业。她有一种近乎神启的感觉。她把他的病、他的威权、她自己的虚弱和疼痛统统不放在眼里。管它出什么事。什么风险也拦不住她了。
「你才不管我好不好呢！」她开了腔，平静而辛辣。「昨天一整天，你可曾把我放在心上一点点？一点儿也没有。你心里只有你自己。你装病。哼，你要真没病，怎么就不能想想我？可你也真病了。病了也不饶你！我就算病得再狠，我心里也时时刻刻想着你。我说你病了，我再说一遍：你病了。可你只拿一箩筐一箩筐的谎话来搪塞我，一个谎接着一个谎。你*干吗*什么都不肯对我说？这样遮遮掩掩，就是打我的脸，你自己清楚。你当我配不上听实话是不是！有一句话我告诉你，这话我从前说过，这是最后一回说——你也教会了我把嘴缝上了；你那没完没了的守口如瓶，就落了这么个结果。我早就说过，你是天下顶自私、顶狠心的男人。你病了，医生说你该进医院——你就是不去。为什么？谁一辈子不进一两回医院？医院对你没好处——就是这么回事。你待在家里，叫老婆日日夜夜伺候着，才更合你的意。*我*病成什么样不要紧！我得伺候你，*还得*照管铺子。这要我的命；可你才不管它死不死呢。

===Original===

And if you hadn’t deceived me and told me a lot of lies you might have been all right by this time, because I should have had the doctor in earlier, and we should have known where we were then. But how was I to know how ill you are? How was I to know I’d married a liar besides a miser?”
Henry interjected quietly:
“I told you long ago that the reason I didn’t eat was because I’d got indigestion. But you wouldn’t believe me.”
Violet’s voice rose:
“Oh, you did, did you? Yes, you did tell me once. You needn’t think I don’t remember. It was that night I cooked a beautiful bit of steak for you, and you wouldn’t touch it. Yes, you did tell me, and it *was* the truth, and I didn’t believe it. And you were glad I didn’t believe it. You didn’t want me to believe it. You’re very knowing, Henry, aren’t you? You say a thing once, and then it’s been said, it’s finished with. And then afterwards you can always say: ‘But I told you.’ And you’re always so polite! As if that made any difference! I wish to God often you weren’t so polite. My first husband wasn’t very polite, and I’ve known the time when he’s laid his hand on me, knocked me about﻿—yes, and more than once. I was young then. Disgusting, *you*’d call it. And I’ve never told a soul before; not likely. But what I say is I’d sooner be knocked about a bit and know what my man’s really thinking about than live with a locked-up, cast-iron safe like you! Yes, a hundred times sooner. There’s worse things than a blow, and every woman knows it. Well, you won’t go to the hospital! That’s all right. You won’t go and you won’t go. But I shall go to the hospital! The doctor’ll tell me to go, and the words won’t be out of his mouth before I shall be gone. I can feel here what’s coming to me. I shall go, and I shall leave you with your Elsie, that eats you out of house and home. She was here before I came. I’m only a stranger. You pretend to be very stiff and all that with her, but you and her understand each other, and I’m only a stranger coming between you. Are you asleep?”
“No.”
Violet rose up and slipped out of bed. Henry heard the sound of her crying. She seemed to rush at the fire. She poked it furiously, not because it needed poking, but because she needed relief.
“Come back to bed, Vi,” said Henry kindly.

===Chinese===

「你要是没哄我、没拿谎话堆我，这会儿早好了，因为我早该把医生请来了，咱们也早知道局面到了哪一步。可我怎么知道你病得这么重？我怎么知道我嫁的除了一个吝啬鬼，还是一个扯谎精？」
亨利轻轻插了一句：
「我早告诉过你，我不吃饭是因为消化不良。可你不信。」
薇奥莱特嗓门高了：
「哦，你告诉过，是么？对，你是跟我说过一回。你别当我记不得。就是那我给你煎了好好一块牛排的晚上，你碰都不肯碰。对，你是说过，那也*是*真话，我没信。你没信着才好呢。你压根就不要我信。你鬼得很，亨利，是不是？一句话说一遍，就算交代过了，就算完了。往后你随时可以说：『我可告诉过你。』你还老是这样客气！好像客气能顶什么用！我常常祷告上帝，巴不得你别这么客气。我头一个男人就不大客气，他动手动脚、打过我——对，还不止一回。那时候我年轻。恶心，*照你*的说法。这事我从前没跟一个人吐露过；也不会吐露。可我的话是：宁可挨几下打，知道自己的男人心里到底想什么，也强似守着你这么一座上了锁的铁保险箱！对，强一百倍。天底下还有比挨打更坏的事呢，哪个女人都知道。好，你不进医院！随你。你不去就是不去。可我要进医院！医生一开口叫我去，他话音没落我人就走了。我这里就觉出自己身上要出什么事。我要走，我要把你丢给你的埃尔西，那个吃得你倾家荡产的埃尔西。她比我先来的。我不过是个外人。你对她装得一本正经什么的，可你跟她心照不宣，我不过是夹在你们中间的外人。你睡着了么？」
「没有。」
薇奥莱特爬起来，溜下了床。亨利听见她哭的声音。她像朝炉火扑过去似的，狠狠地捅那炉子——不是炉子要捅，是她要泄一泄。
「回床上来吧，薇，」亨利和气地说。

===Original===

She dropped the poker with a clatter on the fender, and Henry saw her, a white creature, moving towards him round by his side of the bed. She bent over him.
“Why should I come back to bed?” she asked angrily, her voice thickened and obscured by sobs. “Why should I come back to bed? You’re ill. You’ve got no strength, and haven’t had for weeks. What do you want me to come back to bed for?”
He felt her fingers digging into the softness of his armpits. He felt her face nearer his. She mastered herself.
“Listen to me, Henry Earlforward,” she said in a low, restrained, trembling voice: “You’ll go into that hospital tomorrow morning. You’ll go into that hospital. You’ll *go* into it when the doctor comes to fetch you. Or, if you don’t, I’ll﻿—I’ll﻿—I’ll﻿—”
He felt her lips on his in a savage, embittered and passionate kiss. She was heroical; he a pygmy﻿—crushed by her might. He was afraid and enchanted.
“No,” he thought, “there never was another like her.”
“Will you, will you, will you, will you?” she insisted ruthlessly, and her voice was smothered in his lips.
“Very well. I’ll go.”
Her body fell limp upon his. She was not sobbing now, but feebly and softly weeping. With a sudden movement she stood upright, then ran to the door, just as she was, fumbled for the knob in the darkness, and rushed out of the room, banging the door after her with a noise that formidably resounded through the whole house. Her victory was more than she could bear.

===Chinese===

通条当嘟一声掉在炉挡上，亨利看见一个白生生的身影绕过床他那一边，朝他挪过来。她朝他俯下身。
「我干吗要回床上来？」她怒气冲冲地问，嗓音被呜咽堵得又重又浊。「我干吗要回床上来？你病着。你一丝力气也没有，几个礼拜都没有了。你要我回床上干什么？」
他觉出她的手指抠进他腋下的软肉里。他觉出她的脸凑近了他的脸。她把自己的劲收住了。
「你听着，亨利·厄尔福沃德，」她低低地说，压着劲，声音发颤：「明儿早上你进医院去。你进那所医院去。等医生来接你，你就*给我*进去。你要是不去，我就——我就——我就——」
他觉出她的唇压上他的唇，一个凶狠的、怨愤的、火热的吻。她是英雄；他是侏儒——被她的神威碾平了。他又怕又醉。
「不，」他想，「天下再没有第二个像她这样的了。」
「你肯不肯，肯不肯，肯不肯，肯不肯？」她毫不留情地追问，声音闷在他的唇里。
「好吧。我去。」
她的身子软软地縍在他身上。这会儿她不抽噎了，只是低低地、轻轻地淌着泪。她猛地一下直起身，就那样一丝不挂地奔到门口，在黑地里摸到门钮，冲出房去，随手把门砰地一带，一声巨响震彻了整座宅子。这胜利大得她自己受不住了。

## III Waxworks / 蜡像馆
===Original===

As Henry and Violet approached the turnstile, Henry murmured to Violet:
“How much is it? How much is it?”
“One and three, including tax,” Violet murmured in reply.
Half a crown for the two was less than he had feared, but he felt in his trouser-pocket and half a crown was more than he had there, and he slowly pulled out of his breast-pocket an old Treasury-note case. The total expenses of the wedding ceremony at the Registry had been considerable; he seemed to have been disbursing the whole time since they left Clerkenwell for the marriage and honeymoon (which, according to arrangement, was to be limited to one day).
The wedding breakfast﻿—two covers﻿—at the magnificent, many-floored, music-enlivened, swarming Lyons’ establishment in Oxford Street had been﻿—he was prepared to believe﻿—relatively cheap, and there were no tips, and everything was very good and splendid; but really the bill amounted to a lot of money in the judgment of a man who for years had never spent more than sixpence on a meal outside his own home, and whom the mere appearance of luxury frightened. Throughout the wedding breakfast he had indeed been scared by the gilding, the carving, the seemingly careless profusion, the noise, and the vastness of the throng which flung its money about in futile extravagance; he had been unable to dismiss the disturbing notion that England was decadent, and the structure of English society threatened by a canker similar to the canker which had destroyed Gibbon’s Rome. Ten shillings and sevenpence for a single repast for two persons! It was fantastic. He had resolved that this should be the last pleasure excursion into the West End. Meanwhile, he was on his honeymoon, and he must conduct himself and his purse with the chivalry which a loved woman would naturally, if foolishly, expect.
It was after the wedding breakfast that Violet had, in true feminine capriciousness, suddenly suggested that they should go to Madame Tussaud’s waxworks before the visit to the gorgeous cinema in Kingsway, which was the pièce de résistance of the day’s programme. She had never seen Madame Tussaud’s (nor had he), and she was sure it must be a very nice place; and they had plenty of time for it. All her life she had longed to see Madame Tussaud’s, but somehow﻿ ﻿… etc. Not that he needed too much persuading. No!

===Chinese===

亨利和薇奥莱特走近旋转栅门时，亨利朝薇奥莱特低声说：
「多少钱一位？多少钱一位？」
「一先令三便士，税钱在内，」薇奥莱特低声答道。
两个人两先令六便士，比他怕的数目小；可他往裤袋里一摸，两先令六便士比袋里的多。于是他从胸前口袋里慢慢掏出一只旧钞票夹。在登记所行婚礼，各项花销已经可观；自打离开克拉肯韦尔来办喜事、度蜜月（照安排，蜜月以一天为限）以来，他仿佛一直在往外掏钱。
喜宴——两位——设在牛津街那家富丽堂皇、层层叠叠、乐声盈耳、人声鼎沸的里昂斯大饭馆，他愿意相信算是便宜的，不用给小费，样样又好又体面；可是照一个多年来在外头吃一餐从不超过六便士、见了奢华的排场就心里发虚的人来评判，这笔账实在是一大注钱。整个喜宴期间，他着实被那些烫金、雕花、看似漫不经心的奢华、喧闹，还有那一大群把钱胡乱朝外抛的人群吓着了；他摆脱不了一个叫人心烦的念头：英国在堕落，英国社会的肌体正受着一种痼疾的侵蚀，同当年毁掉吉本笔下罗马的那痼疾一模一样。两个人区区一顿饭，十先令七便士！真是天方夜谭。他拿定主意，这回进西区游乐，是最后一回。不过眼下他在度蜜月，他必得拿出骑士风度来掌管自己的举止和钱袋——一个被人爱的女人，就算糊涂些，也自然会盼着这份风度。
喜宴之后，薇奥莱特出于女人天生的任性，忽然提议，去国王大道那家富丽堂皇的电影院之前——那是全天节目里的压轴戏——先去看看杜莎夫人蜡像馆。她从没见过杜莎夫人蜡像馆（他也没见过），她断定那准是个很好的去处；时间又充裕得很。她一辈子都向往着看看杜莎夫人蜡像馆，只是不知怎么……如此等等。倒也不用人费太多口舌劝他。不用！

===Original===

He liked, he adored, the girlishness in that vivacious but dignified and mature creature, so soberly dressed (save for the exciting red flowers in her dark hat). In consenting to gratify her whim he had the sensations of a young millionaire clasping emerald necklaces round the divine necks of stage-favourites. After all, it was only for one day. And she had spoken truly in saying that they had plenty of time. The programme was not to end till late. Previous to their departure from Riceyman Steps on the wedding journey he had seen Violet call aside Elsie (who was left in charge of the shop), and he doubted not that she had been enjoining the girl to retire to bed before her employers’ return. A nice thoughtfulness on Violet’s part.
Withal, as he extracted a pound note from his case, he suffered agony﻿—and she was watching him with her bright eyes. It was a new pound note. The paper was white and substantial; not a crease in it. The dim watermarks whispered genuineness. The green and brown of the design were more beautiful than any picture. The majestic representation of the Houses of Parliament on the back gave assurance that the solidity of the whole realm was behind that note. The thing was as lovely and touching as a young virgin daughter. Could he abandon it forever to the cold, harsh world?
“Here! Give it me,” said Violet sympathetically, and took it out of his hand. What was she going to do with it?
“I’ve got change,” she added, with a smile, her face crinkling pleasantly.
He was relieved. His agony was soothed. At any rate the note was saved for the present; it was staying in the shelter of the family. He felt very grateful. But why should she have taken the note from him?
“Thank you, ma’am,” said the uniformed turnstile-man, with almost eager politeness as Violet put down half a crown. The character of the place had been established at once by the well-trained attendant.
“I’m sure it’s a very nice place,” Violet observed. She was a judge, too. Henry agreed with her.
There was a spacious Victorianism about the interior, and especially about the ornate, branching staircase, which pleased both of them. Crowds moving to and fro! Crowds of plain people; no fashion, no distinction; but respectable people, solid people; no riffraff, no wastrels, adventurers, flighty persons.
“It *is* a very nice place,” Violet repeated.

===Chinese===

这个活泼然而端庄成熟的人儿，衣着这样朴素（只有她那顶深色帽子上的红花儿叫人心跳），他喜欢、他爱煞了她身上那点少女气。答应依了她这番兴致，他得到的感受，好比一个年轻的百万富翁把翡翠项链往名伶们神圣的颈子上戴。毕竟不过一天。她说时间充裕，也没说错。节目不到深夜不散。动身离开莱斯门台阶来度蜜月之前，他看见薇奥莱特把埃尔西——铺子留给她照管——拉到一边叮嘱，他毫不怀疑她是嘱咐姑娘在东家回来之前先上床歇着。薇奥莱特想得真周到。
然而，从夹子里抽出一张一镑钞票时，他痛苦极了——她正睁着亮晶晶的眼睛望着他。那是张崭新的一镑钞。纸白而挺实，一道折痕也没有。淡淡的水印低语着它的真。票面上绿褐两色的花纹比什么画都美。背面威严的国会大厦图样叫人放心：整个王国的殷实都垫在这张票子后头。这东西又可爱又惹人怜，像一位年少的闺女。他忍心把它永远抛给这个冷冰冰、硬邦邦的世界么？
「来！给我，」薇奥莱特体贴地说，从他手里接了过去。她要拿它怎么样呢？
「我带着零钱呢，」她笑着补了一句，脸愉快地皱起细纹。
他松了口气。他的痛苦平息了。这张钞票眼下总算保住了；它还留在家的庇护里。他心里很感激。可她为什么要把钞票从他手里拿过去呢？
「谢谢您，太太，」穿制服的检票员说；薇奥莱特放下两先令六便士，他那股客气几乎是热切的。这地方的品格，登时就由这位训练有素的侍者立定了。
「这准是个好去处，」薇奥莱特说。她也是个识货的。亨利深以为然。
馆内一派维多利亚式的宽敞气象，尤其是那道雕饰华美、节节分岔的大楼梯，叫两人都欢喜。人流来来往往！都是些普通百姓；没有时髦人物，没有显贵；可是正派的、扎实的人；没有地痞、废料、冒险家、轻浮男女。
「这*真是*个好去处，」薇奥莱特又说了一遍。

===Original===

“And they’re much better than audiences at cinemas, I must say.”
Of course, she went through the common experience of mistaking a wax figure for a human being, and called herself a silly. Suddenly she clutched Henry’s arm. The clutch gave him a new, delightful sensation of owning and being owned, and also of being a protector.
“Oh, dear!” she exclaimed in alarm. “It gave me quite a turn.”
“What did?”
“I thought he was a wax figure, that young man there by the settee. I looked at him for ever so long, and he didn’t move; and then he moved! I wouldn’t like to come here alone. No! That I wouldn’t!” Thereupon, with a glance of trust, she loosed Henry.
For perhaps a couple of decades Henry had not been even moderately interested in any woman, and for over a decade not interested at all; he had been absorbed in his secret passion. And now, after a sort of Rip van Winkle sleep, he was on his honeymoon, and in full realization of the wonderfulness of being married. He felt himself to be exalted into some realm of romance surpassing his dreams. The very place was romantic and uplifted him. He blossomed slowly, late, but he blossomed. And in the crowds he was truly alone with this magical woman. He did not, then, want to kiss her. He would save the kissing. He would wait for it; he was a patient man, and enjoyed the exercise of patience. Quite unperturbed, he was convinced, and rightly, that none in the ingenuous crowds could guess the situation of himself and Violet. Such a staid, quiet, commonplace couple. He savoured with the most intense satisfaction that they were deceiving all the simple creatures who surrounded them. He laughed at youth, scorned it. Then his eye caught a sign, “Cinematograph Hall.” Ha! Was that a device to conjure extra sixpences and shillings from the unwary? He seemed to crouch in alarm, like a startled hare. But the entrance to the Cinematograph Hall was wide and had no barriers. The Cinematograph Hall was free. They walked into it. A board said, to empty seats, “Next performance four o’clock.”
“We must see that,” he told Violet urgently. She answered that they certainly must, and thereupon, Henry having looked at his watch, they turned into the Hall of Tableaux.
A restful and yet impressive affair, these reconstitutions of dramatic episodes in English history.

===Chinese===

「再说了，这儿的人也比电影院里的强多了，我得说。」
当然，她也经历了人人都有的一遭，把蜡人当成了活人，还笑自己傻。忽然她一把抓住亨利的胳膊。这一抓给了他一种新的、叫人心醉的感受：占有着谁，又被谁占有着，而且还是个保护人。
「哎呀！」她惊叫。「吓了我一大跳。」
「什么吓着你了？」
「我还当他是个蜡人呢，长沙发边上的那个年轻人。我瞧了他老半天，他一动不动；后来他动了！我才不要一个人上这儿来呢。才不！」说罢，她信赖地望了亨利一眼，松开了手。
大约有二十年，亨利对任何女人都不曾有过中等程度的兴趣；十几年以来更是毫无兴趣；他一心沉在他的那桩秘密癖好里。而如今，一场瑞普·凡·温克尔式的长睡之后，他度上了蜜月，充分领略着结婚的美妙。他觉得自己被搜升到一个超过梦想的浪漫境界。连这地方都是浪漫的，把他托高了。他开花开得迟、开得慢，可到底是开了。在人群之中，他同这个有魔力的女人真正地独处着。这一刻他还不想吻她。他要留着这个吻。他要等；他是个有耐性的人，还以练这耐性为乐。
他泰然自若，并且深信——信得也对——天真诚朴的人群里没有一个人猜得出他同薇奥莱特的情形。这么一对沉静的、安分的、毫不起眼的夫妻！他们把周围那些单纯的生灵全蒙在鼓里，他品唂着这滋味，心满意足到了极点。他笑话青春，看不起青春。
忽然他的眼睛扫见一块牌子：「电影放映厅」。哈！那是不是又一个从不留神的人腰包里变出额外的六便士、先令来的机关？他像是吃了一惊，缩了一下，如一只受惊的野兔。可电影放映厅的入口敞着，没有栅栏。电影放映厅是免费的。两人走了进去。一块牌子朝着空座位说：「下一场四点。」
「这场咱们务必看，」他急切地对薇奥莱特说。她答道当然要看；于是亨利看了看表，两人转身进了历史场景厅。
这些英国历史戏剧性场面的复原，看着安闲，却又叫人心折。

===Original===

And there was no disturbing preciosity in the attitude of the sightseers, who did not care a fig what “art” was, to whom, indeed, it would never have occurred to employ such a queer word as “art” even in their thoughts. Nor did they worry themselves about composition, lighting, or the theory of the right relation of subject to treatment. Nor did they criticize at all. They accepted, and if they could not accept they spared their brains the unhealthy excitement of trying to discover why they could not accept. They just left the matter and passed on. A poor-spirited lot, with not the slightest taste for hitting back against the challenge of the artist. But anyhow they had the wit to put art in its place and keep it there. What interested them was the stories told by the tableaux, and what interested them in the stories told was the “human” side, not the historic importance. King John signing Magna Charta under the menace of his bold barons, and so laying the foundation stone of British liberty? No! The picture could not move them. But the death of Nelson, Gordon’s last stand, the slip of a girl Victoria getting the news of her accession, the execution of Mary Queen of Scots? Yes! Hundred percent successes every one. Violet shed a diamond tear at sight of the last. Violet said:
“They do say, seeing’s believing.”
She was fully persuaded at last that English history really had happened. Henry’s demeanour was more reserved, and a little condescending. He said kindly that the tableaux were very clever, as they were. And he smiled to himself at Violet’s womanish simplicity﻿—and liked her the better for it, because it increased her charm and gave to himself a secret superiority.
What all the sightseers did completely react to was the distorting mirrors, which induced a never-ceasing loud tinkle and guffaw of mirth through the entire afternoon. Violet laughed like anything at the horrid reflection of herself.
“Well,” she giggled, “they do say you wouldn’t know yourself if you met yourself in the street. I can believe it.”
Rather subtle, that, thought Henry, as he smiled blandly at her truly surprising gaiety. He hurried her away to the cinematograph. The hall was full. He had never in his life been to a picture-theatre. Why should he have gone? He had never felt the craving for “amusement.”

===Chinese===

看客们的态度里也没有那种叫人心烦的风雅讲究；他们压根不管「艺术」是什么玩意儿，说实在的，就连在心里，他们也决计想不起用「艺术」这么个古怪字眼。他们也不为构图、布光或者题材与手法应当如何搭配的理论操心。他们也全不挑剔。他们接受；接受不了的，也不让脑筋去受那份不健康的兴奋，去琢磨为什么接受不了。他们把事情一撂，走过去。
一帮没脾气的角色，艺术家下战书，他们毫无回手之意。可是不管怎样，他们有那点聪明，把艺术安置在它该在的地方，并且叫它待在那儿。他们感兴趣的是场景讲的故事；而故事里叫他们感兴趣的是「人」的那一面，不是历史上的分量。
约翰王在跋扈男爵们的威逼之下签署大宪章、就此奠下英国自由的基石么？不！这幅画打动不了他们。可是纳尔逊之死、戈登的最后顽抗、黄毛丫头维多利亚初闻自己登基的消息、苏格兰女王玛丽的断头？行！个个百分之百地叫座。看到最后一幕，薇奥莱特滚下一滴钻石似的泪珠。
薇奥莱特说：
「人家不是说么，眼见为实。」
她这才彻底信了：英国历史当真是发生过的事。亨利的态度矝持些，还带点屈尊。他和气地说，这些场景做得巧妙，确实巧妙。他暗暗好笑薇奥莱特这女人家的天真——却因此更喜欢她，因为这天真添了她的妩媚，又给了他自己一种秘而不宣的优越。
全体看客都毫无保留地起了反应的，是那些哈哈镜；整个下午，它们引来一阵接一阵放肆的哄笑。薇奥莱特照见镜中自己那副可怕的样子，笑得不可开交。
「喏，」她咯咯笑着说，「人家不是说么，你要在街上撞见自己，怕还认不得呢。我信。」
这话倒有点儿微妙，亨利想，一面朝她那着实惊人的快活温和地笑着。他催她快去看电影。放映厅满了。他有生以来从没进过电影院。他去干什么呢？他从来不曾渴望过「消遣」。

===Original===

He knew just what cinemas were and how they worked, but he did not lust after them. By long discipline he had strictly confined his curiosity to certain fields. But now that the cinema lay gratis to his hand he suddenly burned with a desire to judge it. He refrained from confessing to Violet that he had never been to a picture-theatre. As he had already decided that the cinema was a somewhat childish business, he found nothing in the show to affect this verdict. While it was proceeding he explained the mechanism to Violet, and also he gave her glimpses of the history of Madame Tussaud’s, which he had picked up from books about London. Violet was impressed; and, as she had seen many films far more sensational than those now exhibited, she copied his indifference. Nevertheless, Henry would not leave until the performance was quite finished. He had a curiously illogical idea in his head that although he had paid nothing he must get his full money’s worth.
It was in the upper galleries, amid vast waxen groups of monarchs, princes, princesses, statesmen, murderers, soldiers, footballers and pugilists (Violet favoured the queens and princesses) that, to the accompaniment of music from a bright red-coated orchestra, a new ordeal arose for Henry.
“I wonder where the Chamber of Horrors is,” said Violet. “We haven’t come across it yet, have we?”
An attendant indicated a turnstile leading to special rooms﻿—admittance eightpence, tax included. Henry was hurt; Madame Tussaud’s fell heavily in his esteem, despite the free cinematograph. It was a scheme to empty the pockets of a confiding public.
“Oh!” exclaimed Violet, dashed also. She was in a difficult position. She wanted as much as Henry to keep down costs, but at the same time she wanted her admired mate to behave in a grand and reckless manner suitable to the occasion.
Meeting her glance, Henry hesitated. Was there to be no end to disbursements? His secret passion fought against his love. He turned pale; he could not speak; he was himself amazed at the power of his passion. Full of fine intentions, he dared not affront the monster. Then, his throat dry and constricted, he said blandly, with an invisible gesture of the most magnificent and extravagant heroism:
“I hardly think we ought to consider expense on a day like this.”
And the monster recoiled, and Henry wiped his brow. Violet paid the one and fourpence.

===Chinese===

电影院是什么东西、怎么运转，他知道得清清楚楚，只是不馋它。多年的修炼，他早已把好奇心严严地圈在几块自留地里。可如今电影白白地送到他手边，他忽然烧起一股欲望，要来评它一评。他对薇奥莱特瞒下了自己从未进过电影院这一层。他既已断定电影是桩孩子气的事业，放映中便看不出任何东西足以动摇这个判词。放映的时候，他给薇奥莱特讲解机械原理，还捎带讲了几段杜莎夫人蜡像馆的历史，那是他从几本讲伦敦的书里捾来的。薇奥莱特大为折服；又因为她看过的片子比眼下放的轰动得多，便学着他的淡漠。尽管如此，亨利还是非等放映完了个整体才肯走。他脑袋里有一个古怪的不合逻辑的念头：虽然一个子儿没花，他也非把这票钱看够本不可。
在楼上的陈列厅里，置身于君王、亲王、公主、政治家、杀人犯、军人、足球运动员和拳击手们成片成片的蜡像中间（薇奥莱特偏爱王后和公主），伴着一支红号衣乐队的音乐，一场新的磨难又落到亨利头上。
「不知恐怖屋在哪儿，」薇奥莱特说。「咱们还没撞见它呢，是不是？」
一名侍者指了指通向专室的旋转栅门——门票八便士，税钱在内。亨利受伤了；杜莎夫人在他心里的分量陡然跌落，尽管电影是白看的。这是一个把轻信大众的口袋掏空的阴谋。
「哦！」薇奥莱特也叫了一声，同样泄了气。她的处境为难。她同亨利一样想省钱，可同时她又盼着她敬爱的伴侣拿出一副配得上这个日子的、大手大脚的堂皇气派。
迎着她的目光，亨利踌躇了。这掏钱难道没有个完？他的秘密癖好同他的爱情打起仗来。他脸色发白，说不出话；他自己也惊骇于这癖好的伟力。他满心怀着美好的意愿，却不敢冒犯那头怪物。随后，喉咙又干又紧，他淡淡地说，同时做出一个谁也看不见的、最豪迈最挥霍的英雄手势：
「我想，这样的日子，咱们不大该计较花钱。」
那怪物缩了回去，亨利抹了抹额头。薇奥莱特付了一先令四便士。

===Original===

They entered into a new and more recondite world. Relics of Napoleon did not attract them, but a notice at the head of a descending flight of steps fascinatingly read, “*Downstairs to the Chamber of Horrors.*” The granite steps presented a grim and awe-inspiring appearance; they might have been the steps into hell. Violet shivered and clutched Henry’s arm again.
“No, no!” she whispered in agitation. “I couldn’t face it. I couldn’t.”
“But we’ve paid, my dear,” said Henry, gently protesting.
He, the strong male, took command of the morbidly affected, clinging woman, and led her down the steps. Her arm kept saying to him: “I am in your charge. Nobody but you could have persuaded me into this adventure.﻿ ﻿…” Docks full of criminals of the deepest dye. The genuine jury-box from the original Old Bailey. Recumbent figures in frightful opium dens. Reconstitutions of illustrious murder scenes, with glasses of champagne and packs of cards on the tables, and siren women on chairs. Wonderful past all wondering! Violet was enthralled. Quickly she grew calmer, but she never relaxed her hold on him. The souvenirs of incredible crimes somehow sharpened the edge of his feeling for her and inflamed the romance. He remembered with delicious pain how his longing for this unparalleled Violet had made him unhappy night and day for weeks, how it had seemed impossible that she could ever be his, this incarnation of the very spirit of vivacity, brightness, energy, dominance.﻿ ﻿… And now he dominated her. She attached herself to him, wound round him, the ivy to his oak. She was not young. And thank God she was not young. A nice spectacle he would have made, gallivanting round at the short skirts of some girlish thing! She was ideal, and she was his. The exquisite thought ran to and fro in his head all the time.
“What murder *can* that be?” she demanded in front of a kitchen interior. She had identified the others.
Close by was a lady with a catalogue.
“Would you mind telling me what crime this is supposed to be, madam?” Henry politely asked, raising his hat. The lady looked at him with a malignant expression.
“Can’t you buy a catalogue for yourself?”
“Vulgar, nasty creature!” muttered Violet.
Henry said nothing, made no sign. They walked away. He knew that he ought to have bought a catalogue at the start, but he had not bought one, and now he could not. No! He could not.

===Chinese===

他们走进一个新的、更深奥的世界。拿破仑的遗物引不起他们的兴致，可是一段下行石阶顶端的告示牌诱人地写着：「*由此下楼，通往恐怖屋。*」花岗石的台阶显出一副阴森骇人的相貌；那简直可以是一步步下到地狱去的台阶。薇奥莱特打了个寒噤，又抓住了亨利的胳膊。
「不，不！」她激动地低语。「我不敢看。我不敢。」
「可是钱咱们已经付了呀，亲爱的，」亨利温柔地分辩。
他，强有力的男性，接管了这害着恐怖病、寸步不离的女人，领她走下台阶。她的胳膊一路对他说：「我在你手里。除了你，谁也劝不动我来冒这个险。……」一排排押解座，坐满十恶不赦的罪人。老贝利法庭原装的真陪审席。骇人鸦片馆里横陈的人形。名噪一时的凶案现场的复原：桌上摆着一杯杯香槟、一副副纸牌，椅子上坐着妖冶的女人。神奇得超乎神奇！薇奥莱特被迷住了。她很快镇静下来，可是揪着他的一只手始终没有松过。
这些难以置信的罪行的纪念物，不知怎么磨利了他对她的情愫的锋刃，把浪漫烧得更旺。他带着甜丝丝的痛楚记起来：对这无双的薇奥莱特的渴慕，怎样叫他几星期日日夜夜地不安，这个活泼、明亮、精力、威风四样精神的化身，怎样看上去绝无可能归他所有。……而如今是他辖着她。她依傍他，缠着他，藤之于橡。她不年轻。她也谢天谢地不年轻。要不然，追在什么黄毛丫头的短裙子后头转，他成什么体统！她是理想的，而且是他的人。这个精美的念头在他脑子里跑来跑去，一刻不停。
「这*到底*是哪桩凶案呀？」她站在一处厨房内景前问。别的她都已认出来了。
近旁站着一位拿着目录册的太太。
「可否请您告诉我这幕是什么案子，夫人？」亨利举了举帽子，客气地问。那太太恶狠狠地瞪了他一眼。
「你自己不会买一本目录么？」
「粗坯，坏东西！」薇奥莱特咕哝。
亨利一言不发，不动声色。两人走开了。他明知开头就该买一本目录，可他没买，如今更不能买了。不能！他不能了。

===Original===

The situation was dreadful, but Violet enchantingly eased it.
“Everything ought to be labelled,” she said. “However﻿—” And she began to talk cheerfully as if nothing had happened.
They passed along a corridor and through a turnstile, and were once again in the less sensational Hall of Tableaux, and they heard the tinkling, unbridled laughter of girls surveying themselves in the distorting mirrors. Henry limped noticeably. Violet led the way through the restaurant towards the main hall. Tea laid on spotless tables. Jam in saucers on the tables. Natty, pretty and smiling waitresses.
“I could do with a cup of tea. Oh! And there’s jam!” exclaimed Violet.
Henry was shocked. More expense. Must they be eating all day? Nevertheless, they sat down.
“I’m afraid I’m about done for,” said Henry sadly, disheartened. “My knee.”
His knee was not troubling him in the least, but a desperate plan for cutting short the honeymoon and going home had seized him. He had decided that the one cure for him was to be at home alone with her. He had had enough, more than enough, of the licence of the West End. He wanted tranquillity. He wanted to know where he was.
“Your knee. Oh, Henry! I’m so sorry. What can we do?”
“We can go home,” he replied succinctly.
“But the big cinema, and all that?”
“Well, we’ve seen one. I feel I should like to be at home.”
“Oh, but﻿—!”
Violet was strangely disturbed. He could not understand her agitation. Surely they could visit the big cinema another night. He was determined. He said to himself that he must either go home or go mad. The monster had come back upon him in ruthless might. To placate the monster he must at any cost bear Violet down. He did bear her down, and she surrendered with a soft and deferential amiability which further endeared her to him. They partook of tea and jam; she discharged the bill, and they departed.
“I don’t want to be bothered with my lameness on my wedding day,” he said, wistfully smiling, as they got out into the street.

===Chinese===

这局面糟透了，可薇奥莱特施施然把它岔开了。
「样样东西都该挂上牌子才是，」她说。「不过——」于是她高高兴兴地扯起别的话来，仿佛什么也没发生过。
他们沿走廊走过一道旋转栅门，回到那不太惊耸的历史场景厅，又听见姑娘们对着哈哈镜顾影时那叮铃铃、无拘无束的笑声。亨利跛得明显了。薇奥莱特领路穿过餐厅朝正厅去。洁白的桌上摆好了茶。小碟里盛着果酱。女侍者们利落、漂亮、笑盈盈。
「我倒想喝杯茶。哎呀！还有果酱呢！」薇奥莱特喊道。
亨利吓了一跳。又是一注开销。难道他们要吃一整天么？话虽如此，两人还是坐下了。
「我怕是不中用了，」亨利悲戚地、意气消沉地说。「我的膝盖。」
他的膝盖其实一点儿也不闹他；可是一个斩截的计划搜住了他：截短蜜月，回家去。他已断定，唯一的对症之药就是回家同她单独待着。西区这放纵，他受够了，受得不能再够了。他要清静。他要知道自己在哪儿。
「你的膝盖。唉，亨利！真对不住。这可怎么办呢？」
「回家去，」他简截地答。
「可是那家大电影院，还有那些节目呢？」
「呃，电影咱们看过一家了。我觉得我还是想回家。」
「哦，可是——！」
薇奥莱特莫名地慌乱起来。他不明白她激动什么。那家大电影院，改天夜里总去得成的。他是铁了心了。他对自己说：不回家，就发疯。那怪物挟着无情的伟力又扑回他身上。要安抚这怪物，他不惜任何代价压倒薇奥莱特。他也真把她压倒了；她柔顺地、带着恭谨的和气降服了，这越发叫她得他的欢心。两人用了茶和果酱；她付了账，两人离去。
「结婚的日子，我可不想叫这条瘸腿败兴，」走到街上，他怅然微笑着说。
